\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n!\cdot 2^{n}}\sum_{\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}=\pm 1}\sup_{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in B}\mathrm{q}\biggl(P\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{k}v_{k}\Bigr)\biggr)

(2.1.35)

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{n}}{n!\cdot 2^{n}}\sum_{\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}=\pm 1}\sup_{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in B}\mathrm{q}\biggl(P\Bigl(\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{k}v_{k}}_{\in B}\Bigr)\biggr)

(2.1.36)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{n}}{n!}\cdot\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v)\bigr),

(2.1.37)

which is the desired inequality.

\ch@irxboxempty

The constant
n!/n^{n}
we picked up during the passage from the multilinear mapping to
the polynomial is best possible, see
[
50
, Example 1.39]
for a
comprehensive analysis. As the notation suggests, one is typically interested in
bounded subsets
B
in (
2.1.34
). We will prove in
Lemma
2.1.9
that, for bornological domains

V
, the finiteness of all expressions in (
2.1.34
) for bounded

B\subseteq V
is equivalent to the continuity of both
P
and
\widecheck{P}
. In
analogy to linear mappings, many notions of
continuity turn out to coincide for polynomials. The following is a minor
generalization of
[
50
, Prop. 1.11]
.

Proposition 2.1.8
(Continuity of Polynomials).

Let
P\colon V\longrightarrow W
be a homogeneous polynomial of degree
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
between locally convex spaces
V
and
W
. Then the following are
equivalent:

i.)

For every
v\in V
there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
v
such that the restriction
P\big|_{U}
is uniformly continuous.

ii.)

The polynomial
P
is continuous, i.e.
P\in\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
.

iii.)

The polynomial
P
is continuous at some
v\in V
.

iv.)

The polynomial
P
is continuous at zero.

v.)

For every continuous seminorm
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
there exist a constant
c>0
and continuous seminorm
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
such that

\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v)\bigr)\leq c\cdot\mathrm{p}(v)^{n}\qquad\textrm{for all $v\in V$.}

(2.1.38)

Proof
:

Clearly
\ref{item:PolynomialLocallyUniformlyContinuous}\implies\ref{item:PolynomialContinuous}\implies\ref{item:PolynomialContinuousSomewhere}\implies\ref{item:PolynomialContinuousAtZero}
.
Assume
iv.)
. Invoking the polarization
identity (
2.1.7
) once more, this means that
\widecheck{P}
is
continuous at zero, as well. Fix a continuous seminorm
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
.
Within the realm of locally
convex spaces, the continuity at zero translates to the continuity estimate

\mathrm{q}\bigl(\widecheck{P}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\bigr)\leq\mathrm{r}(v_{1})\cdots\mathrm{r}(v_{n})

(2.1.39)

for some
\mathrm{r}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and all
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
. Here we
use the fact that powers of seminorms in
\operatorname{cs}(V)
already generate the
topology of the finite Cartesian product
V^{n}
. Consider now the –
continuous! – composition
\mathrm{s}\coloneqq(\mathrm{q}\circ P)^{1/n}
. Let
v,w\in V
and
\lambda\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. By construction, we then
have
\mathrm{s}(\lambda v)=\lvert\lambda\rvert\cdot\mathrm{s}(v)
and by virtue of
(
2.1.17
) also

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}(v+w)^{n}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v+w)\bigr)

(2.1.40)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{q}\biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\widecheck{P}\bigl(\underbrace{v,\ldots,v}_{k\text{-times}},\underbrace{w,\ldots,w}_{(n-k)\text{-times}}\bigr)\biggr)

(2.1.41)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\mathrm{q}\bigl(\widecheck{P}\Bigl(\underbrace{v,\ldots,v}_{k\text{-times}},\underbrace{w,\ldots,w}_{(n-k)\text{-times}}\bigr)\Bigr)

(2.1.42)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\mathrm{r}(v)^{k}\cdot\mathrm{r}(w)^{n-k}

(2.1.43)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathrm{r}(v)+\mathrm{r}(w)\bigr)^{n}.

(2.1.44)

As this is not quite the triangle inequality, we are lead to define
\mathrm{p}\coloneqq\max\{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{q}\}
, which is now really a continuous
seminorm on
V
. By
construction,
\mathrm{p}
fulfils (
2.1.38
)
with
c\coloneqq 1
. Thus we have shown

v.)
and turn towards the converse
implication
v.)

\implies

iv.)
. As the collection of all unit cylinders

\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{q},r}(0)
for
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
and
r>0
generates the
locally convex topology of
W
, it suffices to check that their preimages are
zero neighbourhoods in
V
. Now, our assumption
(
2.1.38
) translates to

\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},r}(0)\subseteq P^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{q},cr^{n}}(0)\bigr),

and thus
P^{-1}(\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{q},cr^{n}}(0))
is indeed a neighbourhood of
0
. We have established

\ref{item:PolynomialLocallyUniformlyContinuous}\implies\ref{item:PolynomialContinuous}\implies\ref{item:PolynomialContinuousSomewhere}\implies\ref{item:PolynomialContinuousAtZero}\iff\ref{item:PolynomialContinuityEstimate}

(2.1.45)

and finally infer
i.)
from

iv.)
. To this end, fix again
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
and let
\mathrm{r}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
such
that (
2.1.39
)
holds. Given
v_{0}\in V
, we get for
v,w\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{r},1/2}(v_{0})

by using (
2.1.18
) and
(
2.1.39
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v)-P(w)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k}\mathrm{q}\Bigl(\widecheck{P}\bigl(\underbrace{w,\ldots,w}_{k\text{-times}},\underbrace{v-w,\ldots,v-w}_{(n-k)\text{-times}}\bigr)\Bigr)

(2.1.46)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k}\mathrm{r}(w)^{k}\cdot\mathrm{r}(v-w)^{n-k}

(2.1.47)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{r}(v-w)\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k}\mathrm{r}(w)^{k}

(2.1.48)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{r}(v-w)\cdot\bigl(1+\mathrm{r}(w)\bigr)^{n}

(2.1.49)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{r}(v-w)\cdot\bigl(3/2+\mathrm{r}(v_{0})\bigr)^{n},

(2.1.50)

where we have also used that
\mathrm{r}(v-w)\leq 1
by the triangle inequality. This estimate is uniform in the open cylinder
U\coloneqq\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{r},1/2}(v_{0})
and thus we have proved
i.)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Having to restrict to locally uniform continuity in

i.)
already appears for
V=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}=W
and
the quadratic polynomial
z^{2}
. For constant and linear mappings,
one may of course upgrade back to uniform continuity on all of
V
.

Recall that a mapping
f\colon V\longrightarrow W
between locally convex spaces
V
and
W
is called bounded if it maps bounded subsets of
V
to bounded subsets of
W
. This is equivalent to

??(f)\coloneqq\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr)<\infty

(2.1.51)

for all bounded subsets
B\subseteq V
and
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
. If
W
is
normed, we abbreviate
\mathrm{p}_{B,\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert}
to
\mathrm{p}_{B}
.
Mimicking the linear theory, we get the following relationship between
boundedness and continuity for polynomials.

Lemma 2.1.9
(Boundedness and Polynomials).

Let
P\colon V\longrightarrow W
be a polynomial between locally convex spaces
V
and
W
.

i.)

If
P
is continuous, then
P
is bounded.

ii.)

Let
V
be bornological.
9
9

9

If
U\subseteq V
is bornivorous, i.e. for every
bounded set
B\subseteq V
there is some
r>0
such that
B\subseteq rU
,
then it is a zero neighbourhood. See
[
166
, II. 7]
or

[
152
, Sec. 4.1]
.
If
P
is bounded, then it is continuous.

Proof
:

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
P
is
n
-homogeneous. Let
moreover
P
be continuous,
B\subseteq V
be bounded and
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
. By Proposition
2.1.8
,

v.)
there exist
c>0
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
such that (
2.1.38
) holds for all
v\in V
. As

B
is bounded, we have

\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{p}(v)<\infty,

(2.1.52)

and thus

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P)=\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v)\bigr)\leq c\cdot\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{p}(v)^{n}=c\cdot\bigl(\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{p}(v)\bigr)^{n}<\infty.

(2.1.53)

Consequently,
P
is indeed bounded. Turning to

ii.)
, we assume that
V
is
bornological and
P
is bounded. By
Proposition
2.1.8
,

iv.)
it suffices to check continuity at the
origin. To this end, let
U\subseteq W
be an absolutely convex zero
neighbourhood and
B\subseteq V
be bounded. By assumption, the image
P(B)\subseteq W
is bounded in
W
. Consequently, there is a radius
r>0
such that

P(B)\subseteq rU
, and thus

B\subseteq P^{-1}\bigl(P(B)\bigr)\subseteq P^{-1}(rU)=r^{n}\cdot P^{-1}(U).

This means that the preimage
P^{-1}(U)\subseteq V
is bornivorous. As
V
is
bornological, this implies that
P^{-1}(U)
is a zero neighbourhood, proving the
continuity of
P
at the origin in view of
Proposition
2.1.4
,

i.)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the sequel, we endow
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
and the polynomial algebra
\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}(V,W)

with the locally convex topology induced by the seminorms

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}
from (
2.1.51
). We indicate
this by
adding a subscript ??and call it the
topology
of uniform convergence on
bounded subsets
, the strong topology or just the
\beta
-topology. We are going to
study the properties of the resulting locally convex spaces ??and
\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{\bullet}(V,W)
in detail in
Section
2.4
, after establishing and thoroughly
discussing the notions of Gâteaux and Fréchet holomorphy.

2.2
Gâteaux Holomorphic Functions

The young man is also an idealist. He has yet to find out that what’s in the
public interest is not what the public is interested in.

The Truth
–
Terry Pratchett

Our presentation mostly follows
[
50
, Sec. 3.1]
, but provides some
additional algebraic considerations and technical details in various places. We begin
by fixing the terminology we shall use.

Definition 2.2.1
(Gâteaux Holomorphy).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
a mapping.

i.)

The map
f
is called Gâteaux differentiable at
v_{0}\in U
if for
every
v\in V
and
\varphi\in W^{\prime}
, the composition

z\quad\mapsto\quad\varphi\bigl(f(v_{0}+zv)\bigr)\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.2.1)

is holomorphic on some open neighbourhood of
z=0
in
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
.

ii.)

The map
f
is called Gâteaux holomorphic if it is Gâteaux
differentiable at all
v_{0}\in U
.

iii.)

The set of all Gâteaux holomorphic mappings from
U
to
W
is denoted by

??\coloneqq\bigl\{f\colon U\longrightarrow W\colon f\text{ is G\^{a}teaux holomorphic}\bigr\}.

(2.2.2)

We collect some first algebraic properties of the set of Gâteaux holomorphic functions.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces and
U\subseteq V
be open.

i.)

The set
\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
is a vector space with respect to the pointwise
operations.

ii.)

If
\mathscr{A}
an algebra, so is
\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,\mathscr{A})
with respect to
the pointwise operations.

iii.)

Sending open subsets
U^{\prime}\subseteq V
to
\mathscr{H}_{G}(U^{\prime},W)
yields a sheaf

\mathscr{H}_{G}(\,\cdot\,,W)
of vector spaces with restrictions given by the
restriction of functions.

iv.)

Every polynomial is Gâteaux holomorphic. In particular,

\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}(V,W)\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{G}(V,W).

(2.2.3)

Proof
:

The condition (
2.2.1
) is linear and so
i.)
and

ii.)
are
obvious. As we are dealing with functions, have defined Gâteaux holomorphy by
Gâteaux
differentiability at all points of the domain and the condition (
2.2.1
)
only depends on the values of
f
in a neighbourhood of
v_{0}
, it is clear that

\mathscr{H}_{G}(\,\cdot\,,W)
is a sheaf.
To see
iv.)
, let
P\colon V\longrightarrow W
be a
– not necessarily continuous –
n
-homogeneous polynomial,
\varphi\in W^{\prime}

and
v_{0}\in V
. By (
2.1.17
), we then get

\displaystyle\varphi\bigl(P(v_{0}+zv)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi\biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\widecheck{P}\bigl(\underbrace{v_{0},\ldots,v_{0}}_{k\text{-times}},\underbrace{zv,\ldots,zv}_{(n-k)\text{-times}}\bigr)\biggr)

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}z^{n-k}\cdot\varphi\Bigl(\widecheck{P}\bigl(\underbrace{v_{0},\ldots,v_{0}}_{k\text{-times}},\underbrace{v,\ldots,v}_{(n-k)\text{-times}}\bigr)\Bigr)

(2.2.5)

for
z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
and
v\in V
. As this is simply a complex polynomial of degree
n
with
respect to
z
, it is certainly holomorphic, proving
P\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V,W)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Remark 2.2.3
(Hartogs’ Theorem on separate holomorphy).

Recall
Hartogs’
10
10

10

Friedrich Moritz Hartogs (1874-1943) was a German Jewish
mathematician, who was ultimately discriminated against and killed by the
Nazi regime. He
proved several groundbreaking results concerning power series expansion and
singularities of holomorphic functions in several complex variables.

seminal
Theorem on separate holomorphy: If
f\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is such that

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\ni z_{j}\quad\mapsto\quad f(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C},

(2.2.6)

where the remaining entries of
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
are fixed, is holomorphic
for
j=1,\ldots,n
, then the full function
f
is holomorphic. This means that it is
totally differentiable with complex linear differential. In particular, this then implies
the continuity of
f
. The proof of this remarkable
result is based on Baire’s category theorem, which is used to infer the
boundedness of
f
on bounded sets. Having established this, one may then
construct local Taylor expansions for such functions by means of Cauchy
integrals, which implies the holomorphy. For a detailed discussion and the proof,
we refer to
[
110
, Thm. 1.2.5]
. The first major goal of this
section is to adapt this argument to construct Taylor expansions of Gâteaux
holomorphic functions as series over homogeneous polynomials,
culminating in Theorem
2.2.12
. One may think of these expansions
as power series in infinitely many variables, and historically this is indeed the
idea they make precise.

To this end, we first note the following: By virtue of Hartogs’ Theorem, we
may rephrase Gâteaux holomorphy of some mapping
f\colon V\longrightarrow W

as all restrictions
f\big|_{F}\colon F\longrightarrow W
of
f
to finite
dimensional subspaces being holomorphic. This yields the following Gâteaux
holomorphic version of Hartogs’ Theorem.

Proposition 2.2.4
(Hartogs’ Theorem for Gâteaux holomorphy).

Let
V_{1},V_{2},W
be locally convex spaces with open subsets
U_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
and
U_{2}\subseteq V_{2}
. Let moreover

f\colon U_{1}\times U_{2}\longrightarrow W

(2.2.7)

be such that both
f(v_{1},\,\cdot\,)
and
f(\,\cdot\,,v_{2})
are Gâteaux holomorphic for all vectors
v_{1}\in U_{1}
and
v_{2}\in U_{2}
. Then
f
is Gâteaux holomorphic.

Proof
:

If
F\subseteq V_{1}\times V_{2}
is finite dimensional, then so is

F^{\prime}\coloneqq\pi_{1}(F)\times\pi_{2}(F)

(2.2.8)

with the canonical linear projections
\pi_{1}\colon V_{1}\times V_{2}\longrightarrow V_{1}
and
\pi_{2}\colon V_{1}\times V_{2}\longrightarrow V_{2}
. By
assumption,
f\big|_{F^{\prime}}
is separately holomorphic and thus holomorphic by the
classical Hartogs’ Theorem. Consequently,
f
is Gâteaux holomorphic, as well.

\ch@irxboxempty

This exposes the natural topology of
\mathscr{H}_{G}(V,W)
as the one of

locally uniform convergence on finite dimensional subspaces
. However,
trying to recast this as a seminorm condition, one runs into the complication
that, in principle, the functions (
2.2.1
) corresponding to a sequence

(f_{n})_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
need not be defined on a common
domain. We rule out this inconvenience with a preparatory lemma.

Lemma 2.2.5
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
v_{0}\in V
.
Writing

U_{v}\coloneqq\bigl\{z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\colon v_{0}+zv\in U\bigr\}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.2.9)

for
v\in U
, the mapping

U_{v}\ni z\quad\mapsto\quad\varphi\bigl(f(v_{0}+zv)\bigr)\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.2.10)

is holomorphic for all
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
and
\varphi\in W^{\prime}
.

Proof
:

The crucial point is that we have assumed Gâteaux holomorphy and not just Gâteaux
differentiability at a single point. Fix
v_{0}\in U
,
z_{0}\in U_{v}
and
v\in V
.
We define

v_{1}\coloneqq v_{0}+z_{0}v

(2.2.11)

and denote the mappings from (
2.2.10
) corresponding to
v_{j}

by
g_{j}\colon U_{v}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
for
j=0,1
. Then

g_{0}(z)=\varphi\bigl(f(v_{0}+zv)\bigr)=\varphi\bigl(f(v_{1}+(z-z_{0})v)\bigr)=g_{1}(z-z_{0})

(2.2.12)

holds for all
z\in U_{v}
. But by assumption,
g_{1}
is holomorphic in a zero
neighbourhood and thus
g_{0}
is holomorphic in a neighbourhood of
z_{0}
. As
this holds for all
z_{0}\in U_{v}
, we have completed the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

The advantage of our, a priori less restrictive, definition is that in practice
one often checks the holomorphy of (
2.2.1
) by producing a local power
series expansion, whose convergence one then does not need to check on all of

U_{v}
. Conversely, Lemma
2.2.5
ensures that the
domains of (
2.2.1
) are intrinsic to the open set
U
and independent of
the particular function one considers. Indeed, in Theorem
2.2.12
,
we will ultimately see that a similar statement is true about Taylor expansions for
Gâteaux holomorphic functions on
U
.

Having dealt with this subtlety, we may establish the completeness of the set of
Gâteaux holomorphic functions, which in the sequel we will always endow with the
topology of locally uniform convergence on finite dimensional subspaces. A generating
system of seminorms is given by

\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)\ni f\quad\mapsto\quad\max_{v\in K}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr),

(2.2.13)

where we vary over compact sets
K\subseteq F\cap U
contained in finite dimensional
subspaces
F
of
V
intersected with the open set
U
and continuous
seminorms
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
.

Proposition 2.2.6
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
W
is complete, and let
U\subseteq V
be open. Then the space
\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
is complete with respect to the
topology of locally uniform convergence on finite dimensional subspaces. If
W
is
merely sequentially complete, the same is true for
\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
.

Proof
:

Let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
be a Cauchy net. As

W
is complete and the topology of locally uniform convergence on finite
dimensional subspaces is finer than the topology of pointwise convergence, we may
consider the pointwise limit

f\colon U\longrightarrow W,\quad f(v)\coloneqq\lim_{\alpha\in J}f_{\alpha}(v).

(2.2.14)

Fix
v_{0}\in U
,
v\in V
and
\varphi\in W^{\prime}
. By
Lemma
2.2.5
the net
(g_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}

given by

g_{\alpha}\colon U_{v}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad g_{\alpha}(v)\coloneqq\varphi\bigl(f_{\alpha}(v_{0}+zv)\bigr)

(2.2.15)

is well defined. By assumption,
(g_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
is Cauchy in

\mathscr{H}(U_{v})
and thus locally uniformly convergent to some
g\in\mathscr{H}(U_{v})
. By continuity of
\varphi
, it is clear that

g(v)=\varphi\bigl(f(v_{0}+zv)\bigr)\qquad\textrm{holds for all }v\in U_{v}.

(2.2.16)

We have thus established
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
and that
f_{\alpha}\rightarrow f
locally uniformly on finite dimensional subspaces.

\ch@irxboxempty

Using the Gâteaux holomorphy of polynomials it is easy to establish that power
series, i.e. pointwisely convergent series of homogeneous polynomials, are Gâteaux
holomorphic.

Corollary 2.2.7
.

Let
(P_{n})_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of polynomials
between locally convex spaces
V
and
W
such that
P_{n}
is homogeneous of
degree

n
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Assume moreover that the series

f(v)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)

(2.2.17)

converges in
W
for all
v
in some open subset
U\subseteq V
. Then the
resulting mapping

f\colon U\longrightarrow W

(2.2.18)

is Gâteaux holomorphic.

Proof
:

The idea is that the partial sums of (
2.2.17
) are Cauchy in

\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
. Indeed, let
F\subseteq V
be a finite dimensional
subspace. Then, by Proposition
2.2.2
,
iv.)

and Remark
2.2.3
, the restrictions

P_{n}\big|_{F}\colon F\longrightarrow W

(2.2.19)

are holomorphic for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Consequently, after choosing coordinates, they
are
globally
given by locally uniformly convergent power series on
F
. This
shows that the
partial sums of (
2.2.17
) are indeed Cauchy in

\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
. By Proposition
2.2.6
, the series
(
2.2.17
) thus converges to the pointwise limit
f
in

\mathscr{H}(U,\widehat{W})
, where
\widehat{W}
denotes the completion of
W
.
As by assumption, all necessary limits are already contained in
W
, i.e.
f

actually maps into
W
, this completes the proof. Note that by virtue of the
continuous dual
W^{\prime}
and the continuous dual

\widehat{W}^{\prime}
of the completion being essentially the same space,
11
11

11

By
means of restriction and the extension principle for locally uniformly continuous
mappings.

the co-restriction back to

W
does not interfere with Gâteaux differentiability.

\ch@irxboxempty

Another natural question is whether compositions of Gâteaux holomorphic functions are
again Gâteaux holomorphic. This turns out to be a bit more subtle: Indeed, the
condition (
2.2.1
) is linear and thus, at least a priori, incompatible with
precomposition with general maps. In Corollary
2.2.10
, we
will conversely prove that linearity suffices for good behaviour of the pullback.

Returning to the general problem in the guise of restrictions to finite dimensional
subspaces, see again Remark
2.2.3
, we run into a similar obstacle: Gâteaux
holomorphic functions

f\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\supseteq U\longrightarrow W

(2.2.20)

may already possess an infinite dimensional image, even when restricted to compact
subsets.
12
12

12

This is not a bug, but rather a feature: Otherwise, the theory of
vector-valued holomorphic functions would be rather dull.

Example 2.2.8
.

Let
V\coloneqq??
, the Banach space of zero sequences
indexed by
\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
, and
consider the standard basis vectors
e_{n}\in c_{0}
with
e_{n}(k)\coloneqq\delta_{n,k}
for
n,k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Write

??\coloneqq\bigl\{z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\colon\lvert z\rvert<1\bigr\}

(2.2.21)

for the open unit disk and consider the mapping
f\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{D}\longrightarrow c_{0}
,

f(z)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e_{n}\cdot z^{n}=\bigl(1,z,z^{2},\ldots\bigr),

(2.2.22)

which converges within the Banach
13
13

13

Stefan Banach (1892-1945) was a
Polish mathematician, and one of the founding fathers of modern functional
analysis. He survived both world wars, but tragically succumbed young to lung
cancer. Hugo Dionizy Steinhaus, who ultimately became Banach’s doctoral
advisor, remarked “Banach was my greatest scientific
discovery.”
space
c_{0}
, as

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\big\lVert e_{n}\cdot z^{n}\big\rVert_{\infty}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert z\rvert^{n}=\frac{1}{1-\lvert z\rvert}<\infty.

(2.2.23)

Note that the
n
-th term is induced by the
n
-linear mapping

L_{n}\bigl(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\bigr)\coloneqq z_{1}\cdots z_{n}\cdot e_{n}

(2.2.24)

and thus
f
is
Gâteaux holomorphic by Corollary
2.2.7
. Moreover, if
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{D}
are distinct, then the set
\{f(z_{1}),\ldots,f(z_{n})\}\subseteq c_{0}
is
linearly independent. Hence, the image of any smaller open disk

??=\{z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\colon\lvert z\rvert<r\}
with
0<r<1
is not contained within any finite dimensional
subspace of
c_{0}
.

Ruling out this behaviour facilitates a proof of the continuity of a composition. To avoid
complications regarding the openness of domains of definitions, we work with globally
defined functions.

Lemma 2.2.9
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{1},V_{2})
and
g\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{2},V_{3})
be Gâteaux
holomorphic maps between locally convex spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
and
V_{3}
. Assume
moreover that for every finite dimensional subspace
F_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
, the
restriction
f\big|_{F_{1}}
maps into a finite dimensional subspace
F_{2}

of
V_{2}
. Then
g\circ f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{1},V_{3})
. Moreover, the pullback

??\colon\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{2},V_{3})\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{1},V_{3}),\quad f^{*}g\coloneqq g\circ f

(2.2.25)

with
f
is a linear and continuous mapping.

Proof
:

We use the reformulation of Gâteaux holomorphy from Remark
2.2.3
. To
this end, let
F_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
be finite dimensional. By assumption, there
exists a corresponding finite dimensional subspace
F_{2}\subseteq V_{2}
with
f\big|_{F_{1}}\subseteq F_{2}
. Hence,

g\circ f\big|_{F_{1}}=g\big|_{F_{2}}\circ f\big|_{F_{1}}

(2.2.26)

is holomorphic by virtue of
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{1},V_{2})
and
g\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{2},V_{3})
. This proves

g\circ f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{1},V_{3}).

(2.2.27)

The continuity of (
2.2.25
) follows from the fact that
f

maps compact subsets of finite dimensional subspaces of
V_{1}
to compact
subsets of finite dimensional subspaces of
V_{2}
. Here, we use that
restrictions of
f
to finite dimensional subspaces of
V_{1}
are, in
particular, continuous as holomorphic maps.

\ch@irxboxempty

We take a closer look two particular cases with additional structure.

Corollary 2.2.10
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
be locally convex spaces and
L\colon V_{1}\longrightarrow V_{2}
be linear. Then the pullback

L^{*}\colon\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{2},V_{3})\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{1},V_{3}),\quad L^{*}f\coloneqq f\circ L

(2.2.28)

with
L
is a well defined and continuous mapping.

Proof
:

As a linear mapping,
L\big|_{F_{1}}
has finite dimensional image for any finite
dimensional subspace

F_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
. Hence, all claims follow from
Lemma
2.2.9
. The well definedness
of (
2.2.28
) may also be readily verified directly.
Indeed, we have

\varphi\bigl(L^{*}f(v_{0}+zv)\bigr)=\varphi\bigl(f(Lv_{0}+zLv)\bigr)

(2.2.29)

for
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V_{2},V_{3})
,
v_{0},v\in V_{1}
,
z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
and
\varphi\in V_{3}^{\prime}
. Hence, Gâteaux differentiability of
f
at
Lv_{0}
implies Gâteaux
differentiability of
L^{*}f
at
v_{0}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

As a yet more particular case, we may consider
L\colon V\longrightarrow V
to be
the translation by some fixed
v_{0}\in V
, i.e.
Lv\coloneqq v+v_{0}
. This allows
one to restrict many considerations about Gâteaux holomorphic functions to
considerations around the origin. In particular, one gets to utilize
(
2.1.2
) freely when dealing with homogeneous polynomials. This matches
with (
2.1.17
), which states that the pullback
L^{*}P
of an

n
-homogeneous polynomial
P
is a linear combination of homogeneous
polynomials of degrees
0,\ldots,n
. Refining our methods, we may moreover
establish the following variant of Corollary
2.2.10
for

continuous
polynomials
and general domains of definitions.

Proposition 2.2.11
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
and
V_{3}
be locally convex spaces,
P\in\mathrm{Pol}(V_{1},V_{2})
be a
continuous polynomial and
U\subseteq V_{2}
be open. Then the pullback

P^{*}\colon\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,V_{3})\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{G}\bigl(P^{-1}(U),V_{3}\bigr),\quad P^{*}f\coloneqq f\circ P

(2.2.30)

with
P
is a well defined and continuous mapping.

Proof
:

By continuity of
P
, the preimage
P^{-1}(U)\subseteq V_{1}
is open and so we may
speak of the locally convex space
\mathscr{H}_{G}(P^{-1}(U),V_{3})
in the first
place. Let
F_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
be a finite dimensional subspace. Then

P\bigl(P^{-1}(U)\cap F_{1}\bigr)\subseteq U\cap P(F_{1})\subseteq P(F_{1}),

(2.2.31)

where
P(F_{1})
is contained in a finite-dimensional subspace
F_{2}
of
V_{2}
,
see again Proposition
2.1.5
,

iv.)
. Thus, if
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,V_{3})
,
then the composition

P^{*}f=P\circ f\colon F_{1}\cap P^{-1}(U)\longrightarrow V_{3}

is holomorphic. As
F_{1}
was arbitrary, this proves
P^{*}f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(P^{-1}(U),V_{3})
. Taking another look at the seminorms
(
2.2.13
), the continuity of
P^{*}
is now clear.

\ch@irxboxempty

We have gathered all ingredients to prove the existence of Taylor expansions for
Gâteaux holomorphic functions
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,V)
. The idea is that
restrictions of
f
to finite dimensional subspaces do possess such expansions by the
finite dimensional theory. Our derivation is notably different from the one presented

[
50
, Sec. 3.1]
and avoids using some of the heavier functional analytic
machinery such as Grothendieck’s
14
14

14

Alexander Grothendieck (1928-2014)
was, among many other astonishing achievements, the father of modern algebraic
geometry. He was awarded the Fields medal in 1966. After a dispute over military
funding, he withdrew from the public, while still pursuing both mathematical and
non-mathematical writing, and spent his final years secluded in a small village
at the foot of the Pyrenees.
Theorem on equicontinuity.

Theorem 2.2.12
(Taylor Expansions of Gâteaux holomorphic functions).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
open,
v_{0}\in U
and
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
. Denote the completion
of
W
by ??.
15
15

15

If
W
is not Hausdorff, we pass to its
Hausdorffization before completing.

i.)

There exist unique
n
-homogeneous polynomials
P_{n}\colon V\longrightarrow\widehat{W}
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and a balanced
16
16

16

For all
v\in B
and
t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
, we have
e^{\mathrm{i}t}\cdot v\in B
. Also referred to as circled in the
literature and some authors demand the condition for multiplications with any complex
number
z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{D}^{\mathrm{cl}}
.
zero neighbourhood
U_{0}\subseteq V
such that for all
v\in U_{0}

f(v_{0}+v)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v).

(2.2.32)

ii.)

If
U=U_{0}+v_{0}
for some balanced zero neighbourhood
U_{0}\subseteq V
, then (
2.2.32
) holds for all
v\in U_{0}
.

iii.)

The polynomials
P_{n}
are explicitly given by the vector-valued Cauchy integrals

P_{n}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)}{z^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\in\widehat{W}

(2.2.33)

for
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
v\in V
with
v_{0}+zv\in U
for all
z\in\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}
.

iv.)

The series (
2.2.32
) converges in

\mathscr{H}_{G}(U_{0},\widehat{W})
,
i.e. locally uniformly on the intersections of finite dimensional subspaces
with
U_{0}
.

Proof
:

By our considerations around Corollary
2.2.10
, it
suffices to work with
v_{0}=0
.
17
17

17

This will not simplify any of our
arguments, but at least ease the pain of bookkeeping.
Moreover, we use local
convexity of
V

to replace
U
with a balanced neighbourhood of zero. We begin by establishing the
uniqueness of the expansion. It suffices to prove that, assuming (
2.2.32
)
holds and
P_{n}(v)\neq 0
for some
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
v\in V
, then
f
is not the zero
function. Indeed, as
P_{n}(v)\neq 0
, there exists a
\varphi\in W^{\prime}
such that

\varphi(P_{n}(v))\neq 0
. Consider the auxiliary function
h\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
,

h(z)\coloneqq\varphi\bigl(f(zv)\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varphi\bigl(P_{k}(zv)\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}z^{k}\cdot\varphi\bigl(P_{k}(v)\bigr),

(2.2.34)

where the series converges by virtue of Gâteaux differentiability of
f
at

v_{0}
and the
continuity of
\varphi
. Taking another look, we notice that
h
is a power series
whose
n
-th coefficient is
{\varphi(P_{n}(v))\neq 0}
. Hence,
h
is not the zero
function, and thus the same is true for
f
.

We turn towards proving that choosing
P_{n}
as in
(
2.2.33
) yields
n
-homogeneous polynomials such that
(
2.2.32
) holds. To this end, we fix
v\in V
as well as

\varphi\in W^{\prime}
and check that the integrals
(
2.2.33
)
actually converge in the completion
\widehat{W}
of
W
. Indeed, if
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
, then we may estimate

\mathrm{q}\biggl(\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(zv)}{z^{n}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\biggr)\leq 2\pi\cdot\max_{z\in\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(zv)\bigr)<\infty,

as
f
is continuous on the one-dimensional subspace spanned by
v
. Sequential
completeness of
\widehat{W}
thus implies the convergence of
(
2.2.33
) as vector-valued Riemann integrals. By
absolute convexity of
U
, this yields functions

P_{n}\colon U\longrightarrow\widehat{W},\quad P_{n}(v)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(zv)}{z^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

(2.2.35)

The substitution
w\coloneqq\lambda\cdot z
and path independence of the integral yields

P_{n}(\lambda v)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(\lambda z\cdot v)}{z^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z=\frac{\lambda^{n}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\lambda\cdot\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(w\cdot v)}{w^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w=\lambda^{n}\cdot P_{n}(v)

(2.2.36)

for all
\lambda\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
with
\lambda v\in U
and we extend
P_{n}
to
V
by enforcing
this homogeneity, i.e. by setting

P_{n}(\lambda v)\coloneqq\lambda^{n}\cdot P_{n}(v)\qquad\textrm{for all $\lambda\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}$ and $v\in U$.}

(2.2.37)

As
U
is open and thus in particular absorbing, this indeed yields an

n
-homogeneous extension
P_{n}\colon V\longrightarrow\widehat{W}
. If
F\subseteq V
is a finite dimensional subspace, then the restriction
P_{n}\big|_{F}

is still
n
-homogeneous and, as
f
is holomorphic on
F
, also smooth. Euler’s
Theorem on homogeneity thus implies that
P_{n}
is a homogeneous polynomial of
degree
n
. That is, by Corollary
2.1.3
there exists a
unique corresponding symmetric
n
-linear mapping

L_{F}\coloneqq\widecheck{Q}_{n,F}\colon F^{n}\longrightarrow\widehat{W},

(2.2.38)

which we extend by zero to
V^{n}
. This yields a net
(L_{F})_{F}
of multilinear mappings

V^{n}\longrightarrow W
indexed by the finite dimensional subspaces of
V
endowed
with the direction of upwards set inclusion. If

F\cap F^{\prime}\neq\emptyset,\qquad\textrm{then}\quad L_{F}\Big|_{F\cap F^{\prime}}=L_{F^{\prime}}\Big|_{F\cap F^{\prime}}

(2.2.39)

by construction. Thus, this net is Cauchy within
the space
of all
n
-linear mappings, endowed with the topology of pointwise convergence. It
converges to some multilinear mapping
L\colon V\longrightarrow W
, which by
construction induces
P_{n}
. Thus,
P_{n}
is a polynomial itself. By Gâteaux holomorphy,
we moreover get the locally uniformly convergent power series expansion

\varphi\bigl(f(zv)\bigr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{\varphi(f(wv))}{w^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w=\varphi\biggl(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(wv)}{w^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w\biggr)

(2.2.40)

for any
\varphi^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
and
z\in U_{v}
, see again
(
2.2.9
) with
v_{0}\coloneqq 0
. As
W^{\prime}

separates the points of the completion
\widehat{W}
and by the Hahn-Banach
Theorem, this implies

f(zv)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(wv)}{w^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}w=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z^{n}\cdot P_{n}(v)

(2.2.41)

for
z\in U_{v}
, and the series converges locally uniformly. By construction,
(
2.2.32
) holds on all of
U
. By our choice of
U
, we have thus proved

ii.)
. If finally
F\subseteq V
is a finite dimensional subspace, then (
2.2.32
)
reduces
to the usual vector-valued Taylor expansion of
{f\big|_{F}\colon F\longrightarrow W}

by construction. In particular, it converges locally uniformly.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the process, we have proved the following ‘‘duck type’’
18
18

18

If it looks like a duck and quacks like a duck, then it is a duck.
lemma for polynomials.

Corollary 2.2.13
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces and
U\subseteq V
an open neighbourhood of zero.
Assume that
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,V)
is
n
-homogeneous in the sense of
(
2.1.2
) for some integer
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Then
f
is a polynomial of
homogeneity
n
.

Plugging in
v=0
in (
2.2.32
) and
(
2.2.33
) implies the validity of the mean value property for
Gâteaux holomorphic functions.

Corollary 2.2.14
(Mean value property).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
. Then

f(v_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f\bigl(v_{0}+re^{\mathrm{i}t}v\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)}{z}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

(2.2.42)

for any
r>0
and
v\in V
such that
v_{0}+re^{\mathrm{i}t}v\in U
for all
t\in[0,2\pi]
.

We call the expansion (
2.2.32
) the
Taylor expansion
of
f

around
v_{0}
and the
n
-homogeneous polynomials (
2.2.33
)
the
Taylor polynomials
of
f
around
v_{0}
. Whenever necessary, we will use the
more precise notation ??instead, but stick to the less
cumbersome
P_{n}

whenever we may. Taking another look at (
2.2.33
), we note that

P_{n}
takes values in the
first sequential
completion of

W
19
19

19

Which needs not at all be sequentially complete!
, i.e. one does not
need to pass to the full completion. As for holomorphic functions in finite
dimensions, our construction shows
that the natural domains of convergence only depend on the geometry of the domain and not
the particular function.
20
20

20

Analytic continuation is, of course, a much more
complicated endeavour.
We have already encountered this phenomenon in
Lemma
2.2.5
.

The case of
U=V
is particularly nice, as the Taylor expansion simply
represents
f
globally. Following esteemed tradition, we call functions
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,V)

entire
Gâteaux holomorphic functions. Taking into
account Corollary
2.2.7
there are the following equivalent
characterizations of entire Gâteaux holomorphic functions.

Corollary 2.2.15
(Entire Gâteaux holomorphic functions).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces and
f\colon V\longrightarrow W
be a
mapping. Then the following are equivalent:

i.)

The function
f
is an entire Gâteaux holomorphic function, i.e.
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V,W)
.

ii.)

For every
v_{0}\in V
there exist unique
n
-homogeneous polynomials

P_{n}\colon V\longrightarrow W
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
such that

f(v_{0}+v)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in V.

(2.2.43)

iii.)

There exists some
v_{0}\in V
and unique
n
-homogeneous polynomials

P_{n}\colon V\longrightarrow W
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
such that
(
2.2.43
) holds for all
v\in V
.

iv.)

There exist unique
n
-homogeneous polynomials
P_{n}\colon V\longrightarrow W
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
such that for all
v\in V
.

f(v)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v).

(2.2.44)

Taking a closer look at (
2.2.33
), we get the following
locally convex version of the Cauchy estimates, which will be instrumental for our
considerations in Section
2.5
and
Section
3.7
:

Proposition 2.2.16
(Locally convex Cauchy estimates,
[
50
, (3.12)]
).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
with Taylor expansion (
2.2.32
) around
v_{0}\in U
. Then, for every balanced subset
B\subseteq V
, we have

\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n}(v)\bigr)\leq r^{-n}\sup_{v\in v_{0}+rB}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr)

(2.2.45)

for all
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
,
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
r>0
with
v_{0}+rB\subseteq U
.

Proof
:

We use the Cauchy integrals (
2.2.33
) to estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n}(v)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{v\in B}\frac{1}{2\pi r^{n}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+re^{\mathrm{i}t}v)\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(2.2.46)

\displaystyle\leq r^{-n}\sup_{v\in B}\sup_{t\in[0,2\pi]}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+re^{\mathrm{i}t}v)\bigr)

(2.2.47)

\displaystyle\leq r^{-n}\sup_{v\in v_{0}+rB}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr)

(2.2.48)

for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
r>0
with
v_{0}+rB\subseteq U
.

\ch@irxboxempty

The locally convex Cauchy estimates facilitate several continuity statements regarding
the passage from holomorphic functions and expansion points towards Taylor
polynomials and their values. A first instance is the following:

Corollary 2.2.17
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
V
is Hausdorff,
U\subseteq V
be
absolutely convex,
v_{0}\in U
and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Then the linear mapping

P_{n,v_{0}}\colon\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{G}(V,W)

(2.2.49)

is continuous with respect to the topology of locally uniform convergence on finite
dimensional subspaces.

Proof
:

Let
F\subseteq V
be a finite dimensional subspace. Without loss of generality, we
may assume
v_{0}\in F
. Then
F\cap U\subseteq F
is an open neighbourhood of

v_{0}
and thus contains a compact ball
\mathrm{B}_{r}(v_{0})^{\mathrm{cl}}
with respect to some
auxiliary norm. The locally convex Cauchy estimates
(
2.2.45
) then yield

\sup_{v\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n,v_{0}}(v)\bigr)\leq\sup_{v\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(v_{0})}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr)

(2.2.50)

for all
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
. If now
K\subseteq F
is an arbitrary compact
set, then
K\subseteq\mathrm{B}_{R}(0)
for some
R>0
and thus
(
2.1.2
) and what we have already proved yield

\sup_{v\in K}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n,v_{0}}(v)\bigr)\leq\frac{R^{n}}{r^{n}}\sup_{v\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(v_{0})}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr).

(2.2.51)

Variation of
F
completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Having established the existence of Taylor expansions, we investigate how one can
relate
Gâteaux holomorphy to the existence of suitable differential quotients. Indeed, geometrically,
Gâteaux holomorphy corresponds to the existence of all
directional derivatives
in
every direction with complex homogeneous dependence on the direction. The following
adaptation of
[
50
, Lemma 3.3]
makes this precise in order one.

Corollary 2.2.18
(Gâteaux Differentiability vs. Directional Derivatives).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
a mapping. Then
f
is Gâteaux holomorphic if and only if the limit

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)-f(v_{0})}{z}

(2.2.52)

exists for all
v_{0}\in U
and
v\in V
in the completion ??of

W
. In this case, Taylor expanding

f(v_{0}+v)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)

(2.2.53)

around
v_{0}
, we get

P_{1}(v)=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)-f(v_{0})}{z}\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in V.

(2.2.54)

Proof
:

Assume that the limit (
2.2.52
) exists in the completion

\widehat{W}
of
W
for all
v_{0}\in U
and
v\in V
. We fix
v_{0}\in U
. Then
for every
\varphi\in W^{\prime}
, the limit

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\varphi\biggl(\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)-f(v_{0})}{z}\biggr)=\varphi\biggl(\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)-f(v_{0})}{z}\biggr)

(2.2.55)

exists by continuity. That is, the function (
2.2.1
) is complex
differentiable at zero. If now
\lambda\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is such that
v_{0}+\lambda v\in U
, then replacing
v_{0}
with
v_{0}+z\lambda
yields the complex
differentiability of

z\mapsto f\bigl((v_{0}+\lambda v)+zv\bigr)=f\bigl(v_{0}+(z+\lambda)v\bigr)

(2.2.56)

at the origin, which implies complex differentiability of (
2.2.1
)
at

\lambda
. Varying
\lambda
as above yields holomorphy of (
2.2.1
) in
an open neighbourhood of zero by openness of
U
and thus
f
is Gâteaux
differentiable at
v_{0}
.

Assume conversely that
f
is Gâteaux holomorphic and Taylor expand
f
around

v_{0}
with Taylor polynomials
P_{n}\colon V\longrightarrow\widehat{W}

such that (
2.2.32
) holds. Plugging the expansion into the
difference quotient yields then, if
v\in V
and
z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
with
v_{0}+zv\in U
,

\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)-f(v_{0})}{z}=\frac{1}{z}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P_{n}(zv)=P_{1}(v)+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}z^{n-1}P_{n}(v)\overset{z\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}P_{1}(v),

(2.2.57)

where the convergence is locally uniform on intersections of
U
with finite dimensional subspaces of
V
. In particular, the limit exists pointwisely, i.e. within
\widehat{W}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

If
W
is complete and Hausdorff, the limit (
2.2.52
) is unique and it is
customary to write

??\coloneqq\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)-f(v_{0})}{z}\qquad\textrm{for all $v_{0}\in U$ and $v\in V$.}

(2.2.58)

One calls
df(v_{0},\,\cdot\,)
the
Gâteaux derivative
of
f
at
v_{0}
. By
(
2.2.54
), Gâteaux
derivatives of functions between complex locally convex spaces always have a linear
dependence on the direction. This was first established in
[
199
]
in
the setting of Banach spaces.

Our “duck lemma” Corollary
2.2.13
applied to the
Taylor polynomials (
2.2.33
) may be viewed as the higher
order generalization of this observation.

Example 2.2.19
(A non-linear real Gâteaux derivative).

Notably, if one were to consider real differentiability instead, Gâteaux
derivatives need not be linear, already in finite dimensions. Indeed, consider

F\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{2}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{R},\quad F(x,y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{x^{3}}{x^{2}+y^{2}}\;&\textrm{for}\;(x,y)\neq 0,\\
0\;&\textrm{for}\;(x,y)=0.\end{cases}

(2.2.59)

At the origin, we have

\frac{F(tx,ty)-F(0,0)}{t}=\frac{1}{t}\cdot\frac{(tx)^{3}}{(tx)^{2}+(ty)^{2}}-0=\frac{x^{3}}{x^{2}+y^{2}}

(2.2.60)

for
(x,y)\neq 0
and
t\neq 0
, which clearly fails to be linear with respect to
(x,y)
.

Remark 2.2.20
(Gâteaux holomorphy and Continuity).

We provide an alternative proof of Proposition
2.2.2
,

iv.)
. Indeed, combining (
2.1.18
)
with Corollary
2.2.18
yields

dP(v_{0},v)=\frac{1}{z}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k}\widecheck{P}\bigl(\underbrace{v_{0},\ldots,v_{0}}_{k\text{-times}},\underbrace{zv,\ldots,zv}_{(n-k)\text{-times}}\bigr)\bigg|_{z=0}=n\widecheck{P}\bigl(v_{0},\underbrace{v,\ldots,v}_{(n-1)\text{-times}}\bigr)

(2.2.61)

as the Gâteaux derivative of
P
. As in real analysis of several variables,
existence of directional derivatives does not necessarily imply continuity, a
drastic instance of which follows from combining Proposition
2.2.2
,

iv.)
with
Corollary
2.1.7
: Every discontinuous
multilinear symmetric mapping induces discontinuous polynomials, which are
nevertheless Gâteaux holomorphic.

This completes our study of Gâteaux holomorphy and motivates the definition of Fréchet
holomorphy: We simply add the assumption of continuity.

2.3
Fréchet Holomorphic Functions

It’s still magic even if

you know how it’s done.

A Hat Full of
Sky
– Terry Pratchett

Our presentation is guided by the textbooks
[
49
, Ch. 2-3]
,

[
113
, Sec. 7-8]
,
[
50
, Sec. 3.1]
, again with
the added self-imposed enrichment of working with arbitrary open domains.

Definition 2.3.1
(Fréchet Holomorphy).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
a mapping.

i.)

The map
f
is called
Fréchet differentiable
at
v_{0}\in U
if
f
is Gâteaux
differentiable and continuous at
v_{0}
.

ii.)

The map
f
is called
Fréchet holomorphic
if it is Fréchet differentiable at
all

v_{0}\in U
.

iii.)

The set of all Fréchet holomorphic mappings from
U
to
W
is denoted by

??\coloneqq\bigl\{f\colon U\longrightarrow W\colon f\text{ is Fr\'{e}chet holomorphic}\bigr\}=\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)\cap\mathscr{C}(U,W),

(2.3.1)

where ??denotes the set of continuous functions between
topological spaces
X
and
Y
.

As
\mathscr{H}(U,W)
is defined as an intersection, we immediately get the following analogue of Proposition
2.2.2
for Fréchet holomorphic functions.

Proposition 2.3.2
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces and
U\subseteq V
be open.

i.)

The set
\mathscr{H}(U,W)
is a vector space with respect to the pointwise operations.

ii.)

If
\mathscr{A}
is a locally convex algebra, then the set

\mathscr{H}(U,\mathscr{A})
is a locally convex algebra with respect to the
pointwise operations and the subspace topology inherited from
the continuous mappings
\mathscr{C}(U,W)
.

iii.)

Sending open subsets
U^{\prime}\subseteq V
to
\mathscr{H}(U^{\prime},W)
yields a sheaf
\mathscr{H}(\,\cdot\,,W)
of vector spaces with restrictions given by the restriction of functions.

iv.)

We have

\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}(V,W)\subseteq\mathscr{H}(V,W).

(2.3.2)

Taking another look at the Cauchy integrals
(
2.2.33
), we get the following first regularity result for Taylor
polynomials of Fréchet holomorphic functions:

Corollary 2.3.3
(Continuity of Taylor polynomials).

Let
V
and
W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)
. Then the Taylor polynomials of
f
are continuous as
mappings

P_{n,\bullet}\colon U\times V\longrightarrow\widehat{W}

(2.3.3)

with respect to the product topology and all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. In particular,

P_{n,v_{0}}\in\mathrm{Pol}(V,\widehat{W})
for all
v_{0}\in U
.

Proof
:

Let
U_{0}
be the absolutely convex zero neighbourhood corresponding to the Taylor
expansion of
f
around
v_{0}
as in Theorem
2.2.12
,

i.)
and fix
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. Using the continuity of
addition, we find
another absolutely convex zero neighbourhood
U_{0}^{\prime}
such that
U_{0}^{\prime}+U_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{0}
. We define

U_{0}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq v_{0}+U_{0}^{\prime}.

(2.3.4)

Invoking (
2.2.33
) for the expansion point
v_{0}^{\prime}\in U_{0}^{\prime\prime}
, we may write

P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime}}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(v_{0}^{\prime}+zv)}{z^{n+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in U_{0}^{\prime},

(2.3.5)

as

v_{0}^{\prime}+zv\in U_{0}^{\prime}+U_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{0}\qquad\textrm{for all }z\in\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}.

(2.3.6)

In view of the continuity of
f
, this implies continuity of

P_{n,\bullet}\colon U_{0}^{\prime\prime}\times U_{0}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\widehat{W}.

(2.3.7)

But
P_{n,\bullet}
is polynomial with respect to its second argument and thus
Proposition
2.1.8
coupled with variation of
v_{0}
yields
the claim.

\ch@irxboxempty

Under which conditions pointwisely convergent series of continuous polynomials yield a
Fréchet holomorphic limit is much trickier, as the Taylor series
(
2.2.32
) derived from Gateaux holomorphy has no reason to converge
uniformly on any
open
zero neighbourhood. We will return to this problem in
Corollary
2.3.9
and Proposition
2.3.17
, once we
have established more technology. The latter provides a converse to
Corollary
2.3.3
, where the crucial assumption is the
continuity with respect to the
expansion
point.

Combining the continuity of Taylor polynomials for Fréchet differentiable mappings
with Theorem
2.2.12
and Corollary
2.2.7

moreover allows us to prove that Fréchet differentiability is, up to some technical
subtleties, stable under composition.

Theorem 2.3.4
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
be locally convex spaces,
U_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
be open and
v_{0}\in U
. Let moreover
f\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow V_{2}
be Fréchet differentiable at
v_{0}
,
U_{2}\subseteq V_{2}
an open neighbourhood
of
f(v_{0})
and
g\in\mathscr{H}(U_{2},V_{3})
. Then the composition
g\circ f
is
Fréchet differentiable at
v_{0}
.

Proof
:

By Theorem
2.2.12
there exist balanced neighbourhoods of zero

U_{0,1}\subseteq V_{1}
and
U_{0,2}\subseteq V_{2}
as well as polynomials

P_{n}\colon V_{1}\longrightarrow\widehat{V}_{2}
and
Q_{n}\colon V_{2}\longrightarrow\widehat{V}_{3}
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
such that

f(v_{0}+v)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)\quad\textrm{and}\quad g\bigl(f(v_{0})+w\bigr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Q_{n}(w)

(2.3.8)

for
v\in U_{0,1}
and
w\in U_{0,2}
. By
Proposition
2.1.8
, each of the
Q_{n}
is actually
locally uniformly continuous and thus maps Cauchy nets to Cauchy nets.
Consequently, it
admits a unique locally uniformly continuous extension to a polynomial
Q_{n}\colon\widehat{V}_{2}\longrightarrow\widehat{V}_{3}
and this in turn yields an extension
of
\widecheck{Q}_{n}
to
\widehat{V}_{2}\times\cdots\times\widehat{V}_{2}

for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. The auxiliary function

h\colon U_{0,1}\longrightarrow V_{2},\quad h(v)\coloneqq f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)

(2.3.9)

is continuous at zero by continuity of
f
at
v_{0}
and thus
U_{0}\coloneqq U_{0,1}\cap h^{-1}(U_{0,2})
constitutes an open neighbourhood of
v_{0}
.
Composing the Taylor expansions of
f
and
g
then yields

\bigl(g\circ f\bigr)(v_{0}+v)=g\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)\bigr)=g\biggl(f(v_{0})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)\biggr)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}Q_{m}\biggl(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)\biggr)

(2.3.10)

for
v\in U_{0}\subseteq U_{0,1}
, as
h(v)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)\in U_{0,2}
by construction. Note that, for fixed
v\in U_{0}
, this series
converges

unconditionally
, as it arises from a Taylor series in finite dimensions.
By Corollary
2.3.3
, each of the polynomials
Q_{n}
is
continuous, and by Corollary
2.1.7
the same is
true for the corresponding multilinear symmetric mapping
\widecheck{Q}_{n}
.
Consequently,

Q_{m}\biggl(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)\biggr)=\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}=1}^{\infty}\widecheck{Q}_{m}\bigl(P_{k_{1}}(v),\ldots,P_{k_{n}}(v)\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in U_{0}.

(2.3.11)

By the Polarization identity (
2.1.7
) and the fact that the
composition of two polynomials is once again a polynomial, each of the
compositions

v\mapsto\widecheck{Q}_{m}\bigl(P_{k_{1}}(v),\ldots,P_{k_{n}}(v)\bigr)

(2.3.12)

is thus a linear combination of homogeneous polynomials itself. By unconditional
convergence, we may thus rearrange the series into one indexed by their degrees,
which still converges pointwisely. Each term is then a pointwisely convergent
sequence of homogeneous polynomials and thus converges to a homogeneous
polynomial itself. By Corollary
2.2.7
,
this in turn implies that
g\circ f
is Gateaux differentiable at
v_{0}
. Finally,
as the continuity of
g\circ f
at
v_{0}
is clear, this completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the situation that
f
is a polynomial, i.e. the setting of
Proposition
2.2.11
, the complexity of the argument reduces
significantly. Indeed, then the inner sum is
finite
, and thus one does not
need to reorder the series.

Our next goal is a sufficient criterion for Fréchet holomorphy based on a
boundedness condition, which generalizes
Lemma
2.1.9
to Fréchet holomorphic
functions. Before establishing the abstract result, we convince ourselves that general
Fréchet holomorphic functions need, unlike continuous polynomials, not be bounded.

Example 2.3.5
(An unbounded holomorphic function).

Let
V\coloneqq??
be the space of absolutely
summable sequences indexed by
\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. We write
e_{n}\in\ell^{1}
for the
sequences with
??(k)\coloneqq\delta_{n,k}
for
n,k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. The dual
vectors

\varphi_{n}\colon\ell^{1}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\varphi_{n}(a)\coloneqq a_{n}

(2.3.13)

are continuous linear functionals with operator norm
\lVert\varphi_{n}\rVert=1
for
all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Define

f\colon\ell^{1}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad f(a)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{n}(a)^{n}.

(2.3.14)

Note that
f
is well defined, as for
a\in\ell^{1}
, there exists an index
N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
with
\lvert a_{n}\rvert\leq 1
for all
n\geq N
. Consequently,

\big\lvert f(a)\big\rvert\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{n}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{n}+\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\lvert a_{n}\rvert\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{n}+\lVert a\rVert_{1}<\infty.

By construction, the Taylor polynomials of
f
at the origin are given by

P_{n}=\varphi_{n}^{n}
, each of which is
n
-homogeneous and continuous. Thus
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(\ell^{1},\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
by Corollary
2.2.7
.
However,
f
is unbounded on every ball with radius
R>1
. Indeed, if
1<r<R
, then

\big\lvert f(re_{n})\big\rvert=r^{n}\qquad\textrm{for all }n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0},

which is unbounded for
n\rightarrow\infty
. Conversely, the function
f
is bounded
by
1
on the closed unit ball. This also illustrates that – in contrast to
polynomials – a
holomorphic function may behave wildly different on a set
S
and its rescalings
rS
.

Another related observation is that the Taylor series
(
2.3.14
) converges uniformly on the open ball

\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)\subseteq\ell^{1}
for any
r\leq 1
, but not on any ball with radius
R>1
. Indeed, every
sequence
a\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)
with
r\leq 1
fulfils
\lvert a_{n}\rvert<r
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. This yields the estimate

\bigg\lvert\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\varphi_{n}(a)^{n}\bigg\rvert\leq\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\big\lvert a_{n}\big\rvert^{n}<\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}r^{n}=\frac{r^{N}}{1-r}\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

(2.3.15)

for the remainder. As a uniform limit of continuous and bounded functions, our function
f
is thus continuous and bounded on
\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)
for abstract reasons. That is,
f\in\mathscr{H}(\mathrm{B}_{1}(0),\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
. We will later see that
f
is Fréchet holomorphic on all of
\ell^{1}
. Conversely, the unboundedness of
f
on
\mathrm{B}_{R}(0)
for all
R>1
implies that the Taylor series does not converge uniformly there. Regarding the situation on the unit sphere, we note

\bigg\lvert f(e_{N+1})-\sum_{n=0}^{N}a_{n}(e_{N+1})^{n}\bigg\rvert=1

(2.3.16)

for any
N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. In particular, the convergence is not uniform on the closed unit ball.

We proceed with a more or less obvious Lemma to develop an intuition on how to
establish a notion of “local boundedness”.

Lemma 2.3.6
(Local Boundedness).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces.

i.)

Let
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
be such that there exists an open set
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
such that
f(U^{\prime})\subseteq W
is bounded. Then the restriction
f\big|_{U^{\prime}}\colon U^{\prime}\longrightarrow W
is bounded.

ii.)

Let
P\colon V\longrightarrow W
be a polynomial such that there exists an
open zero neighbourhood
U
within
V
such that the restriction
P\big|_{U}
is
bounded. Then
P
is bounded.

Proof
:

Take objects as described in
i.)
and let
B\subseteq U^{\prime}
be bounded. Then also the image
f(B)\subseteq f(U^{\prime})\subseteq W
is bounded as a subset of a bounded set. For
ii.)
we may additionally assume that
P
is
n
-homogeneous in the sense of (
2.1.2
). If now
B\subseteq V
is bounded, then there exists some
r>0
such that
rB\subseteq U
and clearly
rB
is still bounded. Hence,

P(B)=\frac{1}{r^{n}}P(rB)

(2.3.17)

is bounded by boundedness of
P\big|_{U}
. We have proved boundedness of
P
.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the sequel, we call a function
f\colon U\longrightarrow W

locally
bounded at
v_{0}\in U
if the more restrictive condition in

i.)
is fulfilled, i.e.
v_{0}
has an open
neighbourhood
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
such that the image
f(U^{\prime})\subseteq W
is
bounded. Analogously,
f
is called
locally bounded
if it is locally
bounded at all
v_{0}\in U
. This yields the following sufficient criterion for
Fréchet holomorphy, which can be found in
[
50
, Prop. 3.7]
and
formalizes some of our observations from Example
2.3.5
.

Proposition 2.3.7
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
open and
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
.

i.)

If
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
is locally bounded, then it is Fréchet holomorphic.

ii.)

If
W
is normed and
f
is continuous
21
21

21

Say, by virtue of
Fréchet differentiability of
f
at
v_{0}
.
at
v_{0}\in U
, then
f

locally bounded at
v_{0}
.

Proof
:

Let
f
be locally bounded and
v_{0}\in U
. By assumption, we find an open neighbourhood
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
of zero such that
f(v_{0}+U^{\prime})\subseteq W
is bounded. By local convexity, we may assume that
U^{\prime}
is absolutely convex. That is, for every
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
we have

C_{\mathrm{q}}\coloneqq\sup_{v\in U^{\prime}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)\bigr)<\infty.

(2.3.18)

We prove that the Taylor series (
2.2.32
) of
f
around
v_{0}

converges uniformly on
v_{0}+U^{\prime}
, i.e. within the complete space
\mathscr{C}(v_{0}+U^{\prime},\widehat{W})
. Invoking the locally convex Cauchy
estimates (
2.2.45
), we get

\sup_{v\in U^{\prime}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n}(v)\bigr)\leq r^{-n}\cdot\sup_{v\in v_{0}+rU^{\prime}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr)\leq r^{-n}\cdot\sup_{v\in U^{\prime}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)\bigr)\leq r^{-n}\cdot C_{\mathrm{q}}

(2.3.19)

for
0<r<1
by absolute convexity of
U^{\prime}
. Hence,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n}(v)\bigr)\leq C_{\mathrm{q}}\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^{-n}=\frac{C_{\mathrm{q}}}{1-r}<\infty\qquad\textrm{for }0<r<1.

(2.3.20)

Thus, (
2.2.32
) converges in
\mathscr{C}(v_{0}+U^{\prime},\widehat{W})

and its limit
f
is continuous around
v_{0}
. Variation of
v_{0}
proves
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,V)
.

Let now conversely
W
be normed and assume
f
is continuous at
v_{0}\in U
.
The crucial point is that open norm balls
\mathrm{B}_{r}(v_{0})
are bounded open subsets of

W
. By continuity at
v_{0}
, the preimage

U^{\prime}\coloneqq f^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{B}_{1}(f(v_{0}))\bigr)

(2.3.21)

is an open neighbourhood of
v_{0}
in
U
. Consequently,

\big\lVert f(v)\big\rVert\leq\big\lVert f(v)-f(v_{0})\big\rVert+\big\lVert f(v_{0})\big\rVert<1+\big\lVert f(v_{0})\big\rVert

(2.3.22)

for all
v\in U^{\prime}
and thus
f(U^{\prime})\subseteq W
is indeed bounded.

\ch@irxboxempty

The condition that
W
is normed is essential for
ii.)
. Indeed, general locally convex spaces lack open bounded sets
22
22

22

The existence of an open bounded set is quite close to the topology coming from some norm. We will make this precise in Proposition
2.3.10
.
and so the condition may already fail for the
identity mapping
.

Example 2.3.8
(Fréchet holomorphic mappings need not be locally bounded).

For a concrete example, consider the space

??=\mathrm{Map}(\ch@airxmathbbm{N},\ch@airxmathbbm{C})=\prod_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.3.23)

of
all
complex sequences, endowed with the topology of pointwise convergence, i.e. the Cartesian product topology inherited from countably many copies
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. A subset

B=\prod_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}B_{n}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}

(2.3.24)

is bounded if and only if
B_{n}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is bounded for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. On the
other hand, we are dealing with the product topology: A nonempty set

U=\prod_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}U_{n}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}

(2.3.25)

is open if and only if almost all
23
23

23

All but finitely many.

U_{n}=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

and the remaining
U_{n}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
are open. As
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
itself is unbounded,
these two conditions are clearly incompatible. Hence, the identity mapping

\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N},

(2.3.26)

which is Fréchet holomorphic as a continuous linear map by
Proposition
2.3.2
,
iv.)
, is not locally bounded.

Using the ideas from Proposition
2.3.7
,

i.)
we may prove a variant of
Corollary
2.2.7
for Fréchet holomorphy.

Corollary 2.3.9
.

Let
P_{n}\in\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
be
n
-homogeneous polynomials between locally convex
spaces
V
and
W
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.
Assume moreover that for every
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)

there exists a zero neighbourhood
U\subseteq V
such that

C\coloneqq\sup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}\sup_{v\in U}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n}(v)\bigr)<\infty.

(2.3.27)

Then the power series

f\colon U\longrightarrow\widehat{W},\quad f(v)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)

(2.3.28)

defines a Fréchet holomorphic function.

Proof
:

Let
0<r<1
and note that, by homogeneity,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sup_{v\in rU}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n}(v)\bigr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^{n}\cdot\sup_{v\in U}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n}(v)\bigr)\leq\frac{C}{1-r}.

(2.3.29)

By assumption, we get such an estimate for all
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
. Thus, by
continuity of each
P_{n}
, this implies that the series
(
2.3.28
)
converges absolutely within the space
\mathscr{C}(rU,\widehat{W})
. Taking the
limit
r\uparrow 1
shows that this yields a continuous function
f\colon U\longrightarrow\widehat{W}
. As the series (
2.3.28
) converges
in particular pointwisely, Corollary
2.2.7
implies that
f

is Gâteaux holomorphic, as well. Thus,
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,\widehat{W})

as desired.

\ch@irxboxempty

Recall that a topological space
X
is called
T_{1}
-space
24
24

24

The
T
alludes to
the German “Trennungseigenschaft”, which translates to “separation axiom”, and was
coined in
[
180
]
. The idea
behind numbering them was that axiom
T_{k}
implies
T_{\ell}
whenever
k\geq\ell
. In
practice, this did not work out all too well due to a number competing inequivalent
definitions. For instance, the modern notion of
T_{3}
-space does not imply property

T_{2}
without also assuming
T_{1}
. Moreover, this has lead to amusing notions such as

T_{2\;1/2}
, and the full beauty of the arising taxonomy can be marvelled upon
in the wonderful

[
177
, Sec. I.2]
.
if for every pair of
distinct
points
x,y
within
X
, there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
x
such that
y\notin U
.

Proposition 2.3.10
(Kolmogorov’s normability criterion,
[
105
]
).

Let
V
be a topological vector space. Then there is a norm that induces the topology of
V
if and only if it is a
T_{1}
-space and admits a bounded convex zero neighbourhood.

Proof
:

We only prove the non-trivial implication. Let
V
be
T_{1}
and
U\subseteq V
a
bounded convex neighbourhood of zero. Consider the balanced hull

B\coloneqq\bigl\{\lambda v\colon v\in U,\,\lambda\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\,\lvert\lambda\rvert=1\bigr\}=\bigcup_{\lvert\lambda\rvert=1}\lambda\cdot U

(2.3.30)

of
U
. By continuity of the multiplication with scalars, each of
the sets
\lambda\cdot U
is open again, and hence also
B
is open. By
Lemma
2.0.1
the set
B
is moreover
bounded, as subsets of bounded sets are bounded. We study the associated
Minkowski functional

\mathrm{p}_{B}\colon V\longrightarrow[0,\infty),\quad\mathrm{p}_{B}(v)\coloneqq\inf\bigl\{r>0\colon v\in rB\bigr\}.

(2.3.31)

By openness of
B
, the Minkowski functional is well defined. As
B
is an absolutely
convex neighbourhood of zero,
\mathrm{p}_{B}
is a continuous seminorm by

[
152
, Thm. 3.7]
. We check that
\mathrm{p}_{B}
actually
defines a
norm. To this end, let
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
. As
V
is a
T_{1}
-space, we find a
zero neighbourhood
U^{\prime}\subseteq V
with
v\notin U^{\prime}
. By boundedness of
B
,
there moreover exists a radius
r>0
such that
rB\subseteq U^{\prime}
, i.e.
v\notin r^{\prime}B

for all
0<r^{\prime}\leq r
. Taking another look at (
2.3.31
),
this means

\mathrm{p}_{B}(v)\geq r>0
. Thus
\mathrm{p}_{B}
is indeed a norm and the
closure of
B
is the associated closed unit ball. It remains to establish that

\mathrm{p}_{B}
generates the topology of
V
. By continuity of
translations, it
suffices to prove that the open balls
\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)
associated to
\mathrm{p}_{B}

constitute a basis of zero neighbourhoods. Indeed, if
\widetilde{U}\subseteq V
is a zero
neighbourhood, then by boundedness of
B
, there exists a radius
r>0
such that

rB\subseteq\widetilde{U}
. By what we have shown, this implies
\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)\subseteq\widetilde{U}
. This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Using Kolmogorov’s
25
25

25

Andrey Nikolaevich Kolmogorov (1903-1987) was a Russian
mathematician and the father of the modern formulation of probability theory by means
of measure spaces. The first of his many major discoveries was an almost everywhere
divergent Fourier series
[
104
]
.
normability criterion,
we may infer that bounded subsets of
topological spaces are metrizable.

Corollary 2.3.11
.

Let
V
be a topological vector space with the
T_{1}
-property and
B\subseteq V
be
bounded. Then
B
, endowed with the subspace topology, is metrizable.

Proof
:

Let
W\subseteq V
be the vector space generated by
B
, endowed with the subspace topology. Then
W
is still
T_{1}
and
B\subseteq W
is a bounded open subset of
W
. By Proposition
2.3.10
, the topological vector space
W
is thus normable, say with norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
. The restriction of the corresponding metric to
B
provides the desired metric.

\ch@irxboxempty

Our proof of Proposition
2.3.10
moreover provides an
explicit formula for a norm if
B
is absolutely convex, namely its
Minkowski
26
26

26

Hermann Minkowski (1864-1909) was a Polish geometer. His
introduction of Minkowski spacetime provides an efficient and precise mathematical
framework for special relativity. This, in particular, builds a bridge between physics
and the field
of hyperbolic geometry.

functional. This is sometimes useful, hence we make it precise here.

Corollary 2.3.12
.

Let
V
be a topological vector space with the
T_{1}
-property and an open bounded absolutely convex subset
B\subseteq V
. Then the Minkowski functional

\mathrm{p}_{B}\colon V\longrightarrow[0,\infty),\quad\mathrm{p}_{B}(v)\coloneqq\inf\bigl\{r>0\colon v\in rB\bigr\}

(2.3.32)

associated to
B
constitutes a norm on
V
, which induces its topology.

After this topological detour, we return to the regularly scheduled study of Fréchet
holomorphic functions.

Remark 2.3.13
(Hartogs’ Theorem on separate holomorphy: Redux).

We revisit Remark
2.2.3
. In terms of Gâteaux and Fréchet
holomorphy, we may rephrase Hartogs’ Theorem as

\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{m})=\mathscr{H}(U,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{m})

(2.3.33)

for any
n,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
and open
U\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
. Remarkably, this still
holds true if the codomain is infinite dimensional. Early versions of this
observation are due to Dunford
[
60
]
, who studied the
problem in terms of the Gâteaux derivative from
Corollary
2.2.18
.

Proposition 2.3.14
.

Let
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
,
U\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
open and
W
a locally convex space. Then

\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)=\mathscr{H}(U,W).

(2.3.34)

Proof
:

Let
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
. We check local boundedness of
f
, as we may
then infer Fréchet holomorphy by means of
Proposition
2.3.7
. To this end, fix
v\in U

and consider some norm ball
U_{v}\coloneqq\mathrm{B}_{r}(v)
such that its closure

K
is contained within
U
. By (
2.3.33
), the composition
\varphi\circ f
is continuous for all

\varphi\in W^{\prime}
. Consequently, the images

\bigl(\varphi\circ f\bigr)(K)\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.3.35)

are compact and in particular bounded for all
\varphi\in W^{\prime}
. By the Hahn-Banach
Theorem, this is equivalent to the boundedness of
f(K)\subseteq W
. As

f(\mathrm{B}_{r}(v))\subseteq f(K)
, this implies that
f
is locally bounded at
v
. As
v

was arbitrary, we may thus apply Proposition
2.3.7

to complete the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

As a simple application, we note that the function from
Example
2.2.8
is continuous and thus Fréchet
holomorphic. Before endowing
\mathscr{H}(U,W)
with a locally convex topology of
its own, we investigate the connection between Fréchet holomorphy and
total
differentiability
.
27
27

27

Historically, this is also referred to as Fréchet
differentiability.
We have, however, given this phrase an ostensibly different meaning in
Definition
2.3.1
.
In Proposition
2.3.16
we shall prove that both approaches are
consistent whenever both concepts are applicable.
Recall that a mapping
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
defined on an open
subset
U
of a normed space
B
with values in a locally convex space
W
is called
totally differentiable at
v_{0}\in U
if there exists a continuous
complex

linear mapping

L\colon B\longrightarrow W
such that

\frac{f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})-Lv}{\lVert v\rVert}\overset{v\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0.

(2.3.36)

If one wants to generalize this condition to domains
U
within locally convex spaces, one runs into the principal problem that seminorms may possess non-trivial kernels. In some spaces, one may choose a continuous auxiliary norm, but these need not exist:

Example 2.3.15
(No continuous norms).

Consider the space
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}
of all complex sequences from
Example
2.3.8
, again endowed with the locally convex
topology of pointwise convergence. Let
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N})
be a continuous
seminorm. Then, by definition of the topology of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}
, there exists a finite set

\{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
and some
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}>0
such that

\mathrm{p}(\gamma)\leq c_{1}\cdot\big\lvert\gamma_{n_{1}}\big\rvert+\cdots+c_{k}\cdot\big\lvert\gamma_{n_{k}}\big\rvert\qquad\textrm{for all }\gamma\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}.

(2.3.37)

In particular, if
n\notin\{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\}
, then the sequence
e_{n}

with
e_{n}(m)=\delta_{m,n}
fulfils

0\leq\mathrm{p}(e_{n})\leq c_{1}\cdot\big\lvert e_{n}(n_{1})\big\rvert+\cdots+c_{k}\cdot\big\lvert e_{n}{n_{k}}\big\rvert=\mathrm{p}(\gamma)\leq c_{1}\cdot\delta_{n,n_{1}}+\cdots+c_{k}\cdot\delta_{n,n_{k}}=0.

(2.3.38)

Hence,
e_{n}\in??\mathrm{p}
and thus
\mathrm{p}
is not a
norm.

Without a straightforward generalization of (
2.3.36
) in sight, we nevertheless note the following classical result for Banach spaces, which we may extend to locally convex codomains without too much trouble.

Proposition 2.3.16
(Fréchet Holomorphy vs. Total Differentiability,
[
200
]
).

Let
B
be normed,
U\subseteq B
be open,
W
locally convex and
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
be a mapping. Then
f
is Fréchet differentiable at
v_{0}\in U
if and only if it is totally differentiable at
v_{0}
.

Proof
:

Assume first that
f
is Fréchet differentiable at
v_{0}\in U
, i.e. Gâteaux
differentiable and continuous at
v_{0}
. As the statement is local, we may assume
that
U
is some open ball around
v_{0}
. By Theorem
2.2.12
, we
then have

\frac{f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})-P_{1}(v)}{\lVert v\rVert}=\frac{1}{\lVert v\rVert}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\lVert v\rVert^{n-1}\cdot P_{n}\biggl(\frac{v}{\lVert v\rVert}\biggr)

(2.3.39)

for all
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
with
v_{0}+v\in U
, where
P_{n}
are the Taylor
polynomials of
f
at
v_{0}
. Let
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
. By continuity of

\mathrm{q}\circ f
at
v_{0}
, there exists a radius
r>0
such that

\mathrm{B}_{r}(v_{0})\subseteq U\quad\textrm{and}\quad C\coloneqq\sup_{v\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(v_{0})}\mathrm{q}(v)<\infty.

(2.3.40)

Invoking the locally convex Cauchy estimates
from Proposition
2.2.16
, we get

P_{n}\biggl(\frac{v}{\lVert v\rVert}\biggr)\leq r^{-n}\cdot\sup_{v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(v_{0})}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v^{\prime})\bigr)=\frac{C}{r^{n}}

(2.3.41)

for
v\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)
and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Consequently, if
v\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)
, then

\mathrm{q}\biggl(\frac{f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})-P_{1}(v)}{\lVert v\rVert}\biggr)\leq\frac{C}{r}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\biggl(\frac{\lVert v\rVert}{r}\biggr)^{n}=\lVert v\rVert\cdot\frac{C}{r^{2}}\frac{r}{r-\lVert v\rVert}\overset{v\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0\cdot\frac{C}{r^{2}}=0.

(2.3.42)

As this holds for all
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
, we have proved

\lim_{v\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})-P_{1}(v)}{\lVert v\rVert}=0

(2.3.43)

and thus
f
is totally differentiable at
v_{0}
with total derivative
P_{1}
, which is
indeed linear and continuous by a combination of Theorem
2.2.12

with Corollary
2.3.3
.

Assume conversely that
f
is totally differentiable at
v_{0}\in U
with total derivative

L\in??=\mathrm{Pol}^{1}(V,W).

(2.3.44)

Then also all restrictions
f\big|_{F}
of
f
to finite dimensional subspaces
F\subseteq V
are totally differentiable with total derivative
L\big|_{F}
and thus
holomorphic. Hence,
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
by Remark
2.2.3
. The
condition (
2.3.36
) moreover implies for any
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
that

\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})\bigr)\leq\mathrm{q}\bigl(Lv\bigr)+\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})-Lv\bigr)\overset{v\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0

(2.3.45)

by continuity of
L
and
\lVert v\rVert\leq 1
for
v\rightarrow 0
. Thus the mapping
f

is also continuous at
v_{0}
, that is to say
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Using the same techniques, one may establish that
\widecheck{P_{n}}
are
the higher order total derivatives of
f
, i.e.
P_{2}
is the total derivative of
v_{0}\mapsto P_{1,v_{0}}
and so on. The symmetry of the inducing maps
\widecheck{P_{n}}

– or equivalently, that we may pass to

P_{n}
in (
2.2.32
) – reflects the symmetry of mixed higher derivatives
with respect to their ordering. We proceed with another characterization of Fréchet
holomorphy by means of only the first derivative.

Proposition 2.3.17
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
W
is sequentially complete,
U\subseteq V
open and
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
. Moreover assume that there
is some some convex open set
U_{0}\subseteq U
such
that the first Taylor polynomial is jointly continuous as a mapping

P_{1,\bullet}\colon U_{0}\times V\longrightarrow W.

(2.3.46)

Then the restriction
f\big|_{U_{0}}
is Fréchet holomorphic.

In fact, there is a version of this result for functions that are only
real

Gâteaux differentiable and
P_{1,\bullet}
is replaced by the continuous
Gâteaux derivative
df\colon U\times V\longrightarrow\widehat{W}
. We
provide a proof that works in this generality, but refrain from making the
additional terminology precise.

Proof
:

Fix
v_{0}\in U_{0}
and let
v\in U_{0}
such that the line segment

??\coloneqq\bigl\{tv+(1-t)(v-v_{0})\colon t\in[0,1]\bigr\}

(2.3.47)

between
v_{0}
and
v
is contained in
U_{0}
. We consider the auxiliary
function

g\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow W,\quad g(t)\coloneqq f\bigl(v_{0}+tv\bigr),

(2.3.48)

which is differentiable by virtue of
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
with
derivative

g^{\prime}(t)=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{g(t+h)-g(t)}{h}=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(v_{0}+tv+hv)-g(v_{0}+tv)}{h}=P_{1,v_{0}+tv}(v)

(2.3.49)

for
t\in[0,1]
. By the vector-valued fundamental theorem of calculus as
it can be found e.g. in
[
113
, 2.6. Corollary (6)]
,
this yields

f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})=g(1)-g(0)=\int_{0}^{1}g^{\prime}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}P_{1,v_{0}+tv}(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(2.3.50)

and thus

\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})\bigr)\leq\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{1,v_{0}+tv}(v)\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

(
\heartsuit
)

Now,
P_{1,v_{0}}(0)=0
by linearity, and the continuity of
(
2.3.46
) implies that given
\varepsilon>0

there exists a
\delta>0
and an open convex neighbourhood
U_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{0}
such that

\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{1,v_{0}+tv}(v)\bigr)\leq\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all $t\in[0,1]$ and $v\in U_{0}^{\prime}$ with $[v_{0},v]\subseteq U_{0}^{\prime}$.}

(2.3.51)

Plugging this into (

♡

‣
Proof
) yields

\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})\bigr)\leq\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all $v\in U_{0}^{\prime}$ with $[v_{0},v]\subseteq U_{0}^{\prime}$.}

(2.3.52)

As
U_{0}^{\prime}
is an open convex neighbourhood of
v_{0}
, this implies
continuity of
f
at
v_{0}
. Variation of
v_{0}
completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Along the way, we have established the following well-known version of the
fundamental theorem of calculus for Fréchet holomorphic functions and Riemann
integrals.

Corollary 2.3.18
(Fundamental Theorem of Calculus).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
W
is sequentially
complete,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)
. Then

f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}P_{1,v_{0}+tv}(v)dt

(2.3.53)

for all
v_{0},v\in U
with
[v_{0},v]\subseteq U
.

Remark 2.3.19
(Bastiani holomorphy).

Recent work such as
[
33
,
86
,
34
]
in algebraic
quantum
field theory have identified the notion of
Bastiani
29
29

29

Andrèe Ehresmann
(born as Andrèe Bastiani in 1935) is a French mathematician specialized in infinite
dimensional differential calculus, category theory and differential geometry. Since the
death of her husband, the differential geometer Charles
Ehresmann (1905–1979), she has been the director of the mathematical journal

Cahiers de Topologie et Géométrie Différentielle Catégoriques
.
smoothness

as
a both sufficiently strong and general notion of differentiability for the
observable algebras: for a function
f\colon U\longrightarrow W
, one
demands the existence of directional
derivatives
d^{n}f
of
all
orders and their continuity as mappings

d^{n}f\colon U\times V^{n}\longrightarrow W

(2.3.54)

with respect to the product topology. In Proposition
2.3.17

we have proved that Bastiani smooth maps are, in particular, continuous
and fulfil a version of the fundamental theorem of calculus. The idea first
appeared in
[
132
]
as
M
-differential and was later refined
significantly in
[
9
]
. Replacing real with complex
differentiability to define
Bastiani holomorphy
, a combination of
Corollary
2.3.3
with
Proposition
2.3.17
proves that this ostensibly new notion
actually coincides with Fréchet holomorphy. It would be interesting to
study whether the functions that appear in the context of algebraic
quantum field theory admit for holomorphic extensions.

2.4
Uniform Convergence on Bounded Sets

Multiple exclamation marks,’ he went on, shaking his head, ’are a sure sign
of a diseased mind.

Eric
– Terry Pratchett

In this section, we study the spaces of polynomials of a fixed degree
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
of bounded Fréchet holomorphic functions, each endowed with the topology of uniform
