convergence on bounded sets. In Lemma
2.1.9
, we
have seen that every continuous polynomial is bounded and that both notions are
equivalent if the domain is bornological. Taking a step back, we first establish that
the space of all bounded mappings

??\coloneqq\big\{f\colon U\longrightarrow W\;\big|\;\forall_{B\subseteq U\textrm{ bounded}}\colon f(B)\subseteq W\textrm{ is bounded}\big\}

(2.4.1)

is complete if endowed with the locally convex topology generated by the seminorms

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}
from (
2.1.51
).
30
30

30

The author
would like to thank Praful Rahangdale for asking about and discussing this.

Here,
U\subseteq V
is some fixed open set of a locally convex space,
B\subseteq U
runs through all of its bounded subsets and

\mathrm{q}
through
\operatorname{cs}(W)
. The results of this section are well known, but
references are sparse as analysts tend to favour coarser topologies. That being said,
the material we are going to present may also be derived from
Mackey’s
31
31

31

George Whitelaw Mackey (1916-2006) was an American representation
theorist and early noncommutative geometer. His principal insight concerning duality
theory was that different topologies may produce the same topological dual space,
which lead him to the notion of vector bornologies, which focus on bounded sets
instead of open ones. Remarkably, this also provides a suitable framework for
homological algebra on locally convex spaces.

[
123
]

more algebraic theory of bornologies, a
comprehensive exposition of which is the monograph
[
94
]
. In view
of our goals, we shall stick to our more analytic approach.

Proposition 2.4.1
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces and
U\subseteq V
be open.

i.)

If
W
is complete Hausdorff, then so is
\mathscr{B}(U,W)
.

ii.)

If
W
is sequentially complete Hausdorff, then so is
\mathscr{B}(U,W)
.

Proof
:

Let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(U,W)
be a Cauchy net. As finite
sets are always bounded, the topology of pointwise convergence is coarser
than the topology of uniform convergence on bounded subsets. Consequently,

\mathscr{B}(U,W)
is Hausdorff and the net
(f_{\alpha})
converges pointwisely to its
pointwise limit

f\colon U\longrightarrow W,\quad f(v)\coloneqq\lim_{\alpha\in J}f_{\alpha}(v).

(2.4.2)

We prove that this convergence is actually uniform on bounded subsets of
U
. To
this end, fix
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
and a bounded subset
B\subseteq U
. As
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq\mathscr{B}(U,W)
is a Cauchy net, we find an index
\alpha_{0}\in J
such
that

\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f_{\alpha}(v)-f_{\beta}(v)\bigr)=\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(f_{\alpha}-f_{\beta}\bigr)\leq\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all }\alpha,\beta\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}.

(2.4.3)

Consequently, the continuity of
\mathrm{q}
implies

\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)-f_{\beta}(v)\bigr)=\lim_{\alpha\in J}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f_{\alpha}(v)-f_{\beta}(v)\bigr)\leq\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in B\textrm{ and }\beta\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}.

(2.4.4)

Taking the supremum over
v\in B
yields

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(f-f_{\beta}\bigr)\leq\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all }\beta\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}.

(
\diamondsuit
)

This establishes the uniform convergence
f_{\alpha}\rightarrow f
on bounded
subsets of
V
. It remains to show that
f
is bounded itself. To prove this,
keep the objects from as above, say for the particular choice
\varepsilon\coloneqq 1
. Then (

♢

‣
Proof
) gives

\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v)\bigr)=\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(f\bigr)\leq\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(f-f_{\alpha_{0}}\bigr)+\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(f_{\alpha_{0}}\bigr)\leq 1+\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(f_{\alpha_{0}}\bigr)<\infty

(2.4.5)

by boundedness of
f_{\alpha_{0}}
. Varying
B
and
\mathrm{q}
proves the
boundedness of
f
, which establishes
i.)
. Replacing
the nets with sequences throughout our considerations provides a proof
of
ii.)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Taking another look at the upper part of our argument, we get the following
somewhat technical, but nevertheless useful, lemma.

Lemma 2.4.2
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and assume that
W
is
complete Hausdorff. Moreover, let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
be a net of
mappings
f_{\alpha}\colon U\longrightarrow W
that is Cauchy with respect to the
topology
\tau
of uniform convergence on some collection of sets containing the
finite subsets of
U
. Then the net
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
converges to
its pointwise limit with respect to
\tau
.

Of course, there is an analogous statement for sequences if
W
is merely sequentially
complete. Two examples for
\tau
are the topologies of uniform convergence on all
compact or all bounded subsets of
U
. Here we use that finite subsets
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}\subseteq V
are compact, and thus in particular bounded, regardless of the
topology of
V
. One should also be mindful that, given any collection
S
of subsets
of
U
, one typically has to include additional sets to ensure that uniform
convergence on all members of
S
induces a topology. A comprehensive list of the
most important constructions within the linear theory can be found in

[
152
, Sec. 3.6]
.

We have already seen that boundedness and continuity are related for certain types of
maps. In the case of
V
being a Montel
32
32

32

Paul Montel (1876-1975) was a French
complex analyst. He introduced and systematized the notion of
normal families

of holomorphic functions, which notably predates the abstract concept of compactness.
His comprehensive
exposition on the field of complex dynamics as pioneered by Pierre Fatou and Gaston
Julia was the first of its kind.

space
33
33

33

Montel’s Theorem
holds: every bounded and closed subset of
V
is compact. Some authors call such
spaces semi-Montel. Roughly speaking, the
Montel property ensures that there is a rich
supply of compact sets, which intertwines topology and bornology.
, we get
relations between both notions for arbitrary globally defined functions.

Proposition 2.4.3
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
V
is Montel.

i.)

The topologies of uniform convergence on bounded and uniform convergence on
compact subsets of
V
coincide on the space of bounded mappings

\mathscr{B}(V,W)
.

ii.)

We have

\mathscr{C}(V,W)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(V,W),

(2.4.6)

where ??denotes the set of continuous functions between
topological spaces
X
and
Y
.

Proof
:

Every compact set
K\subseteq V
is bounded: Indeed,

\bigcup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},n}(0)=V

(2.4.7)

provides an open covering of
K
for every
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Passing to a
finite subcover means that there is some
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
with
K\subseteq\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},n}(0)
. This proves the boundedness of
K
by varying

\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Conversely, let
B\subseteq V
be bounded. The Montel
property implies that its closure
B^{\mathrm{cl}}\subseteq V
is compact. Consequently,
uniform convergence on all compact subsets of
V
implies uniform convergence on
all bounded subsets of
V
. We have shown
i.)
.

To see
ii.)
, let again
B\subseteq V
be
bounded
and
f\in\mathscr{C}(V,W)
. Invoking the Montel property as before, its closure

B^{\mathrm{cl}}
is compact. Hence, the same is true for its image
f(B^{\mathrm{cl}})
under the
continuous mapping
f
. By the first part of the proof, this in particular means

f(B^{\mathrm{cl}})
is a bounded superset of
f(B)
, proving its boundedness. This
establishes
f\in\mathscr{B}(V,W)
and completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

The upshot of Proposition
2.4.3
is that our results on the

\beta
-topology on the polynomial functions
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
pass to the topology of
uniform convergence on compact subsets under the additional assumption that the domain

V
is Montel.

Remark 2.4.4
.

Note that we have restricted ourselves to globally defined bounded mappings in
Proposition
2.4.3
. The boundedness ensures that both topologies are
locally convex. Having global domains of definitions reflects that a
compact subset
K
of
V
needs not have a compact intersection with an open
subset
U
. For a simple example, consider the vector space
V\coloneqq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
with
the
U\coloneqq(-1,1)
and
K\coloneqq[0,2]
. The problem here is, of course,
that
K
is not contained within
U
.

We return to the study of polynomials and note the following continuity estimates
regarding translations on
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)
, which can be found in

[
50
, Lem. 1.10]
and will be useful in various contexts.

Proposition 2.4.5
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
B\subseteq V
be a bounded and balanced
subset,
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
and
P\in\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
for some
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.
Then

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P)\leq\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+B,\mathrm{q}}(P)\qquad\textrm{for all }v_{0}\in V.

(2.4.8)

If
B
is moreover convex and
r>0
as well as
v_{0}\in V
are such that
rv_{0}\in B
, then conversely

\mathrm{p}_{B+v_{0},\mathrm{q}}(P)\leq\bigl(1+1/r\bigr)^{n}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P).

(2.4.9)

Proof
:

As
B
is balanced and
P
is
n
-homogeneous, we have on the one hand

\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+B,\mathrm{q}}(P)=\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v_{0}+v)\bigr)=\sup_{v\in B,t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v_{0}+e^{\mathrm{i}t}v)\bigr)=\sup_{v\in B,t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(e^{-\mathrm{i}t}v_{0}+v)\bigr).

(2.4.10)

On the other hand, utilizing the mean value property (
2.2.42
) around
v\in B
in direction
v_{0}\in V
, we get

P(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{P(zv_{0}+v)}{z}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

(2.4.11)

and thus

\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(v)\bigr)\leq\sup_{t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\mathrm{q}\bigl(P(e^{\mathrm{i}t}v_{0}+v)\bigr).

(2.4.12)

Taking the suprema over
B
on both sides and using the first equality proves
(
2.4.8
). For the second part, we additionally
assume convexity of
B
and fix
r>0
such that
rv_{0}\in B
. Then

v_{0}+B\in B/r+B=\bigl(1+1/r\bigr)B.

(2.4.13)

By homogeneity of
P
, we arrive at desired estimate

\mathrm{p}_{B+v_{0},\mathrm{q}}(P)\leq\mathrm{p}_{(1+1/r)B,\mathrm{q}}(P)=\bigl(1+1/r\bigr)^{n}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P).

(2.4.14)

\ch@irxboxempty

Remark 2.4.6
.

One should think of Proposition
2.4.5
as a
quantitative incarnation of the algebraic fact that a polynomial is uniquely
determined by its values on any balanced zero neighbourhood.
Taking another look at our proof, we see that both the estimates
(
2.4.8
) and (
2.4.9
)
also hold for unbounded subsets
B
with seminorms defined as in
(
2.1.51
), but may degenerate into vacuous statements about
infinities.

Next, we establish completeness of
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)
for any fixed
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}

under mild assumptions, slightly generalizing
[
49
, Prop. 1.30]
.

Theorem 2.4.7
.

Let
V
be a bornological locally convex space and
W

be complete Hausdorff. Then
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)
is complete Hausdorff for every
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

Proof
:

The case
n=0
is just the completeness of
W
. Thus assume
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. By
Lemma
2.1.9
,

i.)
we may view
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)

as endowed with the subspace topology inherited from the inclusion

\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)\subseteq\mathscr{B}(V,W).

(2.4.15)

As the space of bounded mappings
\mathscr{B}(V,W)
is complete Hausdorff by
Proposition
2.4.1
,
i.)
this
means that
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)
is Hausdorff itself and that it suffices to
establish the closedness of
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)
within
\mathscr{B}(V,W)
. To this
end, let

(P_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V,W)

(2.4.16)

be a convergent net with limit
P\in\mathscr{B}(V,W)
. As the pointwise convergence
of
(P_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}

corresponds to pointwise convergence of the corresponding net

(\widecheck{P}_{\alpha})
of
n
-linear mappings by
Proposition
2.1.2
, it is clear that
P
is an
n
-homogeneous
polynomial itself. Finally, invoking
Lemma
2.1.9
,

ii.)
, which we may apply by the
assumption that
V
is bornological, the boundedness of
P
implies its continuity.

\ch@irxboxempty

Dropping the assumption that
V
is bornological, our proof shows that the spaces of

bounded

n
-homogeneous polynomials are complete Hausdorff for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. In other words, having a bornological domain is only required to pass back from
boundedness to continuity. Invoking Proposition
2.4.3
,

i.)
, we get the completeness of
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
also
with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets under the
additional assumption that
V
is Montel.

This completes our considerations on polynomials. In
Example
2.3.8
, we have seen that boundedness typically
singles out a proper subspace of the space of
all Fréchet holomorphic functions. We make this additional assumption, as the
functions of interest in Section
2.5
are always bounded.
Once again, our goal is to establish completeness, this time under suitable
assumptions. We begin by fixing some notation.

Definition 2.4.8
(Bounded Fréchet holomorphic functions).

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces and
U\subseteq V
be open. We write

??\coloneqq\bigl\{f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)\colon f\;\textrm{is bounded}\bigr\}=\mathscr{H}(U,W)\cap\mathscr{B}(U,W)

(2.4.17)

for the space of bounded Fréchet holomorphic functions endowed with the subspace
topology inherited from
\mathscr{B}(U,W)
.

This topology is, of course, the topology of uniform convergence on bounded
subsets. By Proposition
2.4.3
, the following is immediate.

Corollary 2.4.9
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
V
is Montel, and let
U\subseteq V
be open. Then

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,V)=\mathscr{H}(U,V).

(2.4.18)

As indicated in the end of
[
50
, Sec. 3.1]
, there is an alternative
defining set of seminorms for

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(V,W)
based on summability of the Taylor polynomials at the origin.
We generalize this to functions defined on open subsets
U\subseteq V
. As the
correspondence is ultimately based on the existence of Taylor expansions
(
2.2.32
) and the locally convex Cauchy estimates
(
2.2.45
), we introduce a more precise notation for
their typical domains of convergence. Indeed, by what we have
shown
34
34

34

After carefully reverting the additional assumption of
v_{0}=0
.
, the
Taylor series

f(v_{0}+v)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(v)

(2.4.19)

of some
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
converges for all
v\in U
such that the line segment

[v_{0},v]\coloneqq\bigl\{v_{0}+t(v-v_{0})\colon t\in[0,1]\bigr\}

(2.4.20)

between
v_{0}
and
v
is contained in
U
. That is, on any starlike subset of
U
with
starpoint
v_{0}
. As we are interested in bounded sets, we consider

??\coloneqq\bigl\{B\subseteq V\colon B\;\textrm{is an absolutely convex bounded set with}\;v_{0}+B\subseteq U\bigr\}

(2.4.21)

for any open
U\subseteq V
and
v_{0}\in U
. Indeed, if
B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
is
non-empty, then
B
is starlike with starpoint zero by absolute convexity, and
thus
v_{0}+B
is starlike with starpoint
v_{0}
. Note that, by
Lemma
2.0.1
there is finally always a wealth
of absolutely convex bounded sets. One can regard the
collection (
2.4.21
) as a
locally convex adaptation of polydisks. The locally convex Cauchy estimates
(
2.2.45
) now yield the following:

Proposition 2.4.10
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open and
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
with Taylor polynomials
P_{n,v_{0}}
at
v_{0}\in U
.

i.)

The function
f
is bounded if and only if

??(f)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(P_{n,v_{0}}\bigr)<\infty

(2.4.22)

for all
v_{0}\in U
,
B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
and
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
.

ii.)

The functions
\mathrm{r}_{B,\mathrm{q},v_{0}}
are seminorms on

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
for all vectors
v_{0}\in U
, bounded sets
B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
and continuous seminorms
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
.

iii.)

The locally convex topology generated by the system

\bigl\{\mathrm{r}_{B,\mathrm{q},v_{0}}\colon\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W),\,v_{0}\in U,\,B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})\bigr\}

(2.4.23)

is the
\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
topology. More precisely, we have the estimates

\mathrm{r}_{B,\mathrm{q},v_{0}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+rB,\mathrm{q}}}{1-r}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\leq\mathrm{r}_{B,\mathrm{q}}

(2.4.24)

for any
B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
and
r>0
with
v_{0}+rB\subseteq U
.

iv.)

If
U=V
, then the seminorms corresponding to
\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
in
(
2.4.23
) generate the topology of

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
for every
v_{0}\in V
.

v.)

If
U=V
, then the seminorms corresponding to
\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
in
(
2.1.51
) generate the topology of

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)

for every
v_{0}\in V
.

Proof
:

Assume first that
f
is bounded and let
v_{0}\in U
,
B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
and
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
. Applying the locally convex Cauchy estimates (
2.2.45
), we get

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P_{n,v_{0}})\leq\frac{\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+rB,\mathrm{q}}(f)}{r^{n}}

(2.4.25)

for any
r>0
with
v_{0}+rB\subseteq U
and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. This yields the estimate

\mathrm{r}_{B,\mathrm{q},v_{0}}(f)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P_{n,v_{0}})\leq\frac{\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+rB,\mathrm{q}}(f)}{1-r}.

(2.4.26)

This implies
\mathrm{r}_{B,\mathrm{q},v_{0}}(f)<\infty
, as with
B
also

v_{0}+rB
is bounded. Conversely, we know that the series
(
2.2.32
) converges on all of
B
by our definition of

\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
and thus we get

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(f)\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P_{n,v_{0}})=\mathrm{r}_{B,\mathrm{q},v_{0}}(f).

(2.4.27)

This completes the proof of
i.)
and

iii.)
. Moreover,

ii.)
is now clear, as pointwisely convergent
series over seminorms are seminorms. Finally, to see

iv.)
and
v.)
,
note that

\mathfrak{B}(V,v_{0})=\bigl\{B\subseteq V\colon B\;\textrm{is absolutely convex and bounded,}\;v_{0}+B\subseteq V\bigr\}

(2.4.28)

is simply the collection of
all
absolutely convex and bounded subsets of
V
,
as the second condition is vacuous. In particular, we get

\mathfrak{B}(V,v_{0})=\mathfrak{B}(V,v_{0}^{\prime})\qquad\textrm{for all}\quad v_{0},v_{0}^{\prime}\in V.

\ch@irxboxempty

By Corollary
2.3.3
, the Taylor polynomials of a Fréchet
holomorphic function are continuous, which by
Lemma
2.1.9
in particular implies their
boundedness.

Corollary 2.4.11
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
open and
v_{0}\in U
.

i.)

The linear mapping

P_{n,v_{0}}\colon\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{n}(V,\widehat{W})

(2.4.29)

is continuous with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on bounded
subsets.

ii.)

If
(f_{\alpha})\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
converges within

\mathscr{B}(U,W)
to some function

f\colon U\longrightarrow W,

(2.4.30)

then
f
is bounded as well as Gâteaux holomorphic. Furthermore, the Taylor
polynomials
P_{n,v_{0},\alpha}
of
f_{\alpha}
at
v_{0}
converge uniformly
on bounded
subsets to the Taylor polynomials
P_{n,v_{0}}
of
f
at
v_{0}
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

Proof
:

The first part is a direct consequence of the locally convex Cauchy estimates.
Indeed, let
B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
,
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
and
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)
.
Then, by (
2.2.45
) with
r=1
,

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P_{n,v_{0}})\leq\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+B,\mathrm{q}}(f)\qquad\textrm{for all $\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)$ and $n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}$.}

(2.4.31)

If now
B\subseteq V
is an arbitrary bounded, absolutely convex subset of
V
,
then there exists some
r>0
such that

rB\subseteq U-v_{0},\qquad\textrm{that is}\quad rB\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0}).

(2.4.32)

By what we have already shown, this yields the continuity estimate

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}(P_{n,v_{0}})=\frac{1}{r^{n}}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{rB,\mathrm{q}}(P_{n,v_{0}})\leq\frac{1}{r^{n}}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+rB,\mathrm{q}}(f),

(2.4.33)

completing the proof of
i.)
. Note that the second
statement would be an immediate consequence of the first if we assumed the
continuity of the limit

f
, which we do not. Let
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
and
f
as described
in
ii.)
be given. As the topology of uniform
convergence on bounded subsets is finer than the topology of uniform convergence
on intersections of
U
with finite dimensional subspaces, we know that
f
is
Gâteaux holomorphic by Proposition
2.2.6
.
Proposition
2.4.1
,
i.)

moreover implies the boundedness of
f
. Taking another look at the proof of the
first part, we have not used the continuity of
f
, but only its boundedness. Thus,

ii.)
follows.

\ch@irxboxempty

Another remarkable and important consequence of our estimates is that Taylor series of
bounded holomorphic functions converge uniformly on bounded subsets.

Corollary 2.4.12
.

Let
V
and
W
be Hausdorff locally convex spaces,
U_{0}
a balanced
neighbourhood of
zero,
v_{0}\in V
and
f\in\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(v_{0}+U_{0},W)
. Then the Taylor
series

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n,v_{0}}

(2.4.34)

converges towards
f(v_{0}+\,\cdot\,)
in the space

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U_{0},\widehat{W})
.

Proof
:

By Theorem
2.2.12
, the series (
2.4.34
)
converges pointwisely to
f(v_{0}+\,\cdot\,)
on all of
U_{0}
. As the topology
of uniform convergence on bounded sets is finer than the topology of pointwise
convergence, it thus suffices to establish that the series
(
2.4.34
) converges absolutely. To this end, fix

\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
and

B\in\mathfrak{B}(U_{0},0)=\mathfrak{B}\bigl(v_{0}+U_{0},v_{0}\bigr).

(2.4.35)

Then, by the locally convex Cauchy estimates
(
2.2.45
) and (
2.2.32
), we have

\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\biggl(f-\sum_{n=0}^{N}P_{n,v_{0}}\biggr)=\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}_{B,\mathrm{q}}\bigl(P_{n,v_{0}}\bigr)\leq\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}+rB,\mathrm{q}}(f)\cdot\frac{r^{N+1}}{1-r}\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

(2.4.36)

for any
0<r<1
with
rB\subseteq U_{0}
as before.

\ch@irxboxempty

Note that we have to assume
f\in\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(v_{0}+U_{0},W)
to make this
argument work: we do not yet know whether the
\mathscr{H}_{\beta}
-topology is
complete. Which brings us to our next goal: to work out sufficient conditions to
ensure this. As a preliminary consideration, we prove another continuity property of
taking Taylor
polynomials of Fréchet holomorphic functions, which is worth comparing with
Corollary
2.3.3
and
Corollary
2.4.11
.

Proposition 2.4.13
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces,
U\subseteq V
be open,
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)
and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Then the mapping

P_{n,\bullet}\colon U\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}(U,\widehat{W}),

(2.4.37)

which sends
v_{0}\in U
to the
n
-th Taylor polynomial

P_{n,\bullet}
of
f
, is continuous.

Proof
:

By Corollary
2.3.3
, the mapping
(
2.4.37
) is well defined. Let

(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq U
be a convergent net with limit
v_{0}\in U
. Fix moreover
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
and
B\in\mathfrak{B}(U,v_{0})
. By continuity of
f
at
v_{0}
, there exists an absolutely
convex zero neighbourhood
U_{0}\subseteq V
such that

\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+v)-f(v_{0})\bigr)\leq\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{for all $v\in U_{0}$.}

(2.4.38)

The continuity of vector addition provides another absolutely convex zero
neighbourhood

U_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{0}\qquad\textrm{such that}\qquad U_{0}^{\prime}+U_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{0}.

(2.4.39)

By convergence of
(v_{\alpha})
to
v_{0}
we find an index
\alpha_{0}\in J
such that

v_{\alpha}\in U_{0}^{\prime}
for all
\alpha\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}
. As
B
is bounded, there
moreover exists some
0<r<1
such that the rescaled set
rB\subseteq U_{0}^{\prime}

and (
2.2.33
) yields

P_{n,v_{0}}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{r\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(v_{0}+zv)}{z^{n}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\quad\textrm{and}\qquad P_{n,v_{\alpha}}(v)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{r\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}}\frac{f(v_{\alpha}+zv)}{z^{n}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

(2.4.40)

for all
v_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}
and
v\in U_{0}
. Putting
everything together, this provides the estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{n,v_{0}}(v)-P_{n,v_{\alpha}}(v)\bigr)

(2.4.41)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi r^{n}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+re^{\mathrm{i}t}v)-f(v_{\alpha}+re^{\mathrm{i}t}v)\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(2.4.42)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi r^{n}}\biggl(\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+e^{\mathrm{i}t}\underbrace{rv}_{\in rB\subseteq U_{0}^{\prime}})-f(v_{0})\bigr)+\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{\alpha}+e^{\mathrm{i}t}\underbrace{rv}_{\in rB\subseteq U_{0}^{\prime}})-f(v_{0})\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\biggr)

(2.4.43)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi r^{n}}\biggl(\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+\underbrace{re^{\mathrm{i}t}v}_{\in U_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{0}})-f(v_{0})\bigr)+\mathrm{q}\bigl(f(v_{0}+\underbrace{\underbrace{v_{\alpha}-v_{0}}_{\in U_{0}^{\prime}}+\underbrace{re^{\mathrm{i}t}v}_{\in U_{0}^{\prime}}}_{\in U_{0}})-f(v_{0})\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\biggr)

(2.4.44)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{r^{n}}

(2.4.45)

for all
v\in B
and
\alpha\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}
, where we have used the absolute convexity of
U_{0}^{\prime}
several
times.

\ch@irxboxempty

Building on Theorem
2.4.7
, we add another
assumption on the domain space
V
to facilitate completeness.
Recall that a topological space
X
is called
Baire
space if countable
intersections of dense open subsets of
X
are dense. Or, equivalently, if any
countable
union
A
of closed subsets
A_{n}
of
X
has an inner point, then so does one of the

A_{n}
. Crucially, complete metric spaces are always Baire by Baire’s first category
theorem
35
35

35

René-Louis Baire (1874-1932) was a French mathematician, whose
study of pointwise limits of continuous functions lead to him to what the mathematical
community nowadays calls his category theorems, which are at the core of modern
functional analysis. Surprising many of his contemporaries, Baire proved that there is no
function
f\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
, which is continuous
exactly
at all
rational numbers.

and thus the same is true for Banach and Fréchet spaces. We refer to

[
107
, §4.6]
or
[
31
, Ch. IX §5.]
for a systematic
treatment, but cannot resist to mention the
following characterization of the Baire property for topological vector spaces due to
Saxon.
36
36

36

Stephen Apollos Saxon is a Professor Emeritus of Florida State
University and a specialist in locally convex analysis and various notions related to
barrels.

Proposition 2.4.14
(Baire property in topological vector spaces

[
165
, Thm. 1]
).

Let
V
be a topological vector space not endowed with the indiscrete
topology.
37
37

37

The topology
\{\emptyset,V\}
.

Then
V
is Baire if and only if every closed balanced and absorbing subset
A\subseteq V
has non-empty interior.

Proof
(Of the trivial implication):

Let
A\subseteq V
be closed balanced and absorbing. We denote the interior of a
set
S
by
S^{\circ}
. As the multiplication with a fixed scalar is a
homeomorphism of
V
, we have

A^{\circ}\neq\emptyset\iff\exists_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\colon(nA)^{\circ}\neq\emptyset\iff\forall_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\colon(nA)^{\circ}\neq\emptyset.

Moreover, as
A
is absorbing, we may write

V=\bigcup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}(nA),

(2.4.46)

which certainly has an inner point and is a countable union of closed sets, again
as the scaling with
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
is a homeomorphism and
A
is closed by assumption.
Thus, if
V
is Baire, then there exists some
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
with
(nA)^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
and thus
A^{\circ}\neq\emptyset
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Recall that a
barrel
in
V
is a closed, balanced, absorbing and
convex

subset and that
V
is called
barrelled
if every barrel is a neighbourhood of
zero.
The condition in Proposition
2.4.14
is thus reminiscent of
V
being
barrelled: every closed, absolutely convex, absorbing subset of
V
is a zero
neighbourhood. Comparing this with
Proposition
2.4.14
, we see that one trades convexity for asserting that
zero is an inner point – rather than just having nonempty interior. Adapting the proof
of the easy implication yields the following well known lemma.

Lemma 2.4.15
(Baire implies barrelled).

Let
V
be a topological vector space with the Baire property. Then
V
is barrelled.

Proof
:

By the easy implication of Proposition
2.4.14
, every barrel
B\subseteq V
possesses an inner point
v_{0}
, say with open neighbourhood
U\subseteq B
.
By balancedness of
B
and the continuity of the multiplication with scalars, this
implies that
-U\subseteq B
is an open neighbourhood of
-v_{0}
. But then
0=v_{0}+(-v_{0})
is a linear combination of inner points and thus inner itself, with open
neighbourhood given by

U+(-U)=\bigl\{u_{1}-u_{2}\colon u_{1},u_{2}\in U\bigr\}.

(2.4.47)

Thus
V
is indeed barrelled.

\ch@irxboxempty

The converse of Lemma
2.4.15
does not hold, as every LF
space
38
38

38

Countable strict inductive limits of Fréchet spaces
V_{n}
with

V_{n+1}\setminus V_{n}\neq\emptyset
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. One prominent example of such a
space is the space of test functions
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})
.
is
barrelled, but never Baire.
39
39

39

Indeed, as a vector space, a strict inductive limit

\varinjlim V_{n}
is given by the union over all
V_{n}
and as proper subspaces, each of
them has empty interior.
The Baire property supplies a useful sufficient
criterion for when the continuity of the Taylor polynomials passes to the
corresponding Gâteaux holomorphic function. In view of (
2.2.32
), this
is not too surprising, as Baire’s main objective within
[
5
,
6
]
was
the study of pointwise limits of continuous mappings, which ultimately resulted in the
isolation of the Baire property as a useful tool. The following proposition is a
generalization of
[
50
, Example 3.8(a)]
.

Proposition 2.4.16
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
V
is Baire and
W
is complete.
Furthermore let
U\subseteq V
be open as well as connected and
v_{0}\in U
.
Moreover assume that
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
possesses continuous Taylor
polynomials
P_{n,v_{0}}\in\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Then
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)
.

Proof
:

We have to prove continuity of
f
, which is equivalent to continuity of the
compositions
\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ f\colon U\longrightarrow??

for all
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
, where
\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\colon W\longrightarrow W_{\mathrm{q}}
denotes the local Banach
space at
\mathrm{q}
.
40
40

40

The completion of

W/\ker\mathrm{q}
with respect to the norm
\lVert[w]\rVert_{\mathrm{q}}\coloneqq\mathrm{q}(w)
for
w\in W
.
By translating and shrinking
U
if necessary, we
may assume that
v_{0}=0
and that
U
is absolutely convex. Consider

U_{n}\coloneqq\bigcap_{m=0}^{\infty}\bigl\{v\in U\colon\mathrm{q}\bigl(P_{m}(v)\bigr)\leq n\bigr\},

which is closed subset of
U
as an intersection of such, where we use the
continuity of the Taylor polynomials
P_{m}
. By convergence of the Taylor series
(
2.2.32
) for fixed
v\in U
, we know that

(\mathrm{q}(P_{m}(v)))_{m}\subseteq W
is a zero sequence and thus

\bigcup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}U_{n}=U
.

By the Baire property, there thus exists an index

N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
v_{0}^{\prime}\in U_{N}
such that
v_{0}^{\prime}
is an interior point of
U_{N}
. Let

U_{0}\subseteq V
be an absolutely convex zero neighbourhood such that
v_{0}^{\prime}+U_{0}\subseteq V_{N}
. Using homogeneity and
(
2.4.8
) in the extended setting as explained in
Remark
2.4.6
, we estimate

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}_{U_{0}/2,\mathrm{q}}(P_{n})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{-n}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{U_{0},\mathrm{q}}(P_{n})\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{-n}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{v_{0}^{\prime}+U_{0},\mathrm{q}}(P_{n})\leq 2N.

(2.4.48)

Hence, the Taylor series (
2.2.32
) converges uniformly on the open
neighbourhood
v_{0}+U_{0}/2
of
v_{0}
and consequently
\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ f

is continuous on
v_{0}+U_{0}/2
. We have shown

\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ f\in\mathscr{H}\bigl(U_{0}/2,W_{\mathrm{q}}\bigr).

(2.4.49)

Let now
v_{0}^{\prime\prime}\in U
. Then, by Corollary
2.2.17
, and
indicating the function dependence in square brackets, we have

P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime\prime}}[\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ f]=P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime\prime}}\biggl[\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ P_{m}\biggr]=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime\prime}}[\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ P_{m}]=\sum_{m=n}^{\infty}P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime\prime}}[\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ P_{m}],

(2.4.50)

where we have also used (
2.2.61
), and that the series
converges uniformly on intersections of
U_{0}/2
with finite dimensional subspaces,
see again Theorem
2.2.12
,
iv.)
.
By continuity of the
P_{m}
and
Corollary
2.3.3
– or by virtue of the explicit
formula – each of the Taylor polynomials

P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime\prime}}[\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ P_{m}]
is continuous. Invoking Baire’s Theorem
on points of continuity
[
6
]
, we get that the pointwise limit

P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime\prime}}[\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ f]
of continuous functions has a point of continuity. By
Proposition
2.1.8
, this implies continuity of
P_{n,v_{0}^{\prime\prime}}

on all of
V
. By the first part of the proof, we get continuity of
\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ f
on some neighbourhood of
v_{0}^{\prime\prime}
. As
v_{0}^{\prime\prime}
was
arbitrary, this yields
\pi_{\mathrm{q}}\circ f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W_{\mathrm{q}})

and variation of
\mathrm{q}
completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Taking another look at Example
2.2.8
,
Example
2.3.5
and
Example
2.3.8
, all
functions we have considered are Fréchet
holomorphic on the entirety of their domains. By a slight variation of

[
50
, Example 3.8(d)]
, there is another class of
examples with the pleasant property described in Proposition
2.4.16
.

Corollary 2.4.17
.

Let
V=??B_{\alpha}
be a direct limit of Baire locally convex
spaces
B_{\alpha}
both in the category of topological spaces and continuous maps
and in the category of topological vector spaces. If
W
is
a complete Hausdorff locally convex space and
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(V,W)

such that the
Taylor polynomials
P_{n,0}\in\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V,W)
, then
f\in\mathscr{H}(V,W)
.

Proof
:

By the characteristic property of the
topological
inductive limit, the
mapping

f\colon V\longrightarrow W

(2.4.51)

is continuous if and only if all pullbacks

f_{\alpha}\coloneqq f\circ\phi_{\alpha}\colon B_{\alpha}\longrightarrow W

(2.4.52)

are continuous for all
\alpha\in J
. Now, by Gâteaux holomorphy of
f
, we
may
Taylor expand
f
around
0=\phi_{\alpha}(0)
by
Theorem
2.2.12
.
This yields unique
n
-homogeneous polynomials

P_{n,0}\colon V\longrightarrow W\qquad\textrm{for all }n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}

(2.4.53)

such that

\bigl(f\circ\phi_{\alpha}\bigr)(v)=f\bigl(\phi_{\alpha}(v)\bigr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n,0}\bigl(\phi_{\alpha}(v)\bigr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigl(P_{n,0}\circ\phi_{\alpha}\bigr)(v)

(2.4.54)

for all
v\in B_{\alpha}
. By
1
-homogeneity of
\phi_{\alpha}
41
41

41

Here we
use that
V
is the direct limit of the
B_{\alpha}
in the category of topological
vector spaces.
, the mappings

P_{n,0}\circ\phi_{\alpha}
are
n
-homogeneous for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. As Taylor
expansions are unique, this implies that
f_{\alpha}
is Gâteaux holomorphic with
Taylor polynomials
Q_{n}\coloneqq P_{n}\circ\phi_{\alpha}
at
0
. By assumption,
every
Q_{n}
is continuous and as
B_{\alpha}
is Baire, this implies continuity of

f_{\alpha}
by Proposition
2.4.16
. Hence,
f_{\alpha}\in\mathscr{H}(B_{\alpha},W)
and by the characteristic property of the topological
direct limit we are done.

\ch@irxboxempty

Theorem 2.4.18
(Completeness of
\mathscr{H}_{\beta}
).

Let
V
and
W
be locally convex spaces such that
V
is bornological as well as
Baire and
W
is complete Hausdorff. Then
\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
is complete
Hausdorff for any open
U\subseteq V
.

Proof
:

As in the proof of Theorem
2.4.7
, we view

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
as a subspace of
\mathscr{B}(U,W)
. Consequently, the
Hausdorff property is obvious and it suffices to prove the closedness of the
subspace
\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
within
\mathscr{B}(U,W)
by its completeness,
which we have established in Proposition
2.4.1
,

i.)
.

To this end, let

(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
be a convergent net
with limit
f\in\mathscr{B}(U,W)
. Then
f
is Gâteaux holomorphic by virtue of
Proposition
2.2.6
. It remains to establish the continuity of

f
. Fix
v_{0}\in U
and
first note that by Corollary
2.4.11
,

ii.)
, the Taylor polynomials
P_{n,v_{0},\alpha}
of

f_{\alpha}
at
v_{0}
converge uniformly on bounded subsets to the Taylor
polynomials
P_{n,v_{0}}
of
f
at
v_{0}
. The latter indeed exist by a
combination of
f\in\mathscr{H}_{G}(U,W)
and Theorem
2.2.12
.
By Fréchet holomorphy of
f_{\alpha}
, each of the Taylor polynomials

P_{n,v_{0},\alpha}\colon V\longrightarrow W

(2.4.55)

is continuous by virtue of Corollary
2.3.3
. Invoking the
completeness of
\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{n}(V,W)
for every
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
from
Theorem
2.4.7
this, in turn, implies that

P_{n,v_{0}}
is continuous for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. As
V
is Baire by assumption,
this is sufficient to ensure the continuity of
f
by
Proposition
2.4.16
. Thus,
f\in\mathscr{H}(U,W)
and we are
done.

\ch@irxboxempty

As we have noted before, if
V
is Banach and
W
is Fréchet, then the

\beta
-topology may be
induced by countably many seminorms. Combining this with
Theorem
2.4.18
yields the following:

Corollary 2.4.19
.

Let
V
be Banach,
W
be Fréchet and
U\subseteq V
be open. Then the space

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)

(2.4.56)

is Fréchet.

Weakening completeness to sequential completeness once again yields an analogous
statement, essentially by the very same methods.

Corollary 2.4.20
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces such that
V
is bornological as well as Baire
and
W
is a sequentially complete Hausdorff space. Then

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(U,W)
is sequentially complete for any open
U\subseteq V
.

Remark 2.4.21
(Oka principle).

Taking another look at our proof of Theorem
2.4.18
, we
note that the only problematic aspect is the continuity of the pointwise limit. Indeed,
by virtue of constant functions being holomorphic, the completeness of
W
is
certainly a necessary condition. One may view this as an instance of the Oka
principle
42
42

42

Named in honour of the Japanese complex geometer Kiyoshi Oka
(1901-1978), whose seminal coherence Theorems are at the heart of modern
complex analysis of several variables, at least for its sheaf theoretic
incarnation.
: it
constitutes a holomorphic problem that only
possesses a topological
obstruction.

We conclude the section with studying an explicit example of a Fréchet holomorphic
function defined on the LF space of continuous test functions.

Example 2.4.22
(Test functions).

Consider the LF space of continuous test functions

??(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})\coloneqq\varinjlim\mathscr{C}_{K}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})

(2.4.57)

with

\mathscr{C}_{K}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})\coloneqq\bigl\{f\in\mathscr{C}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})\colon f\big|_{\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}\setminus K}\equiv 0\bigr\}

(2.4.58)

for compact
K\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
and the natural inclusions
\mathscr{C}_{K}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{K^{\prime}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})
whenever
K^{\prime}\subseteq K
. We study the
variant of
Dirac’s
43
43

43

Paul Dirac (1902-1984) was a British theoretical and mathematical
physicist, who won the Nobel prize in 1933. He may be regarded as one of the
founding fathers of quantum mechanics and, subsequently, quantum field theory.

comb given by

f\colon\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad f(\phi)\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}\phi(n)^{n}.

(2.4.59)

As the series terminates for every
\phi\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
, the function
f

is well
defined. As
n
-th term is a continuous polynomial of degree
n
, we have thus
defined
f
by its Taylor series and thus it is Gâteaux holomorphic by
Corollary
2.2.7
.

If
B\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
is bounded, then there exists a compact
interval
[-N,N]\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
such that
B
is contained within

\mathscr{C}_{K}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and bounded there by
[
107
, §19.5 (5)]
. That
is to say

\sup_{\phi\in B}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{\phi\in B}\sup_{x\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert<\infty.

(2.4.60)

Hence,

\sup_{\phi\in B}\big\lvert f(\phi)\big\rvert\leq\sup_{\phi\in B}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\phi(n)^{n}\leq N\cdot\max\biggl\{\sup_{\phi\in B}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty},\sup_{\phi\in B}\lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty}^{N}\biggr\},

(2.4.61)

and consequently
f
is bounded.

Next, we prove the continuity of
f
. As already noted before, we may not rely on
Proposition
2.4.16
as LF spaces are never Baire and the condition
in Corollary
2.4.17
is impractical. Instead, we will be using
the explicit description of the continuous seminorms for
\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
as it
can be found in
[
96
, Thm. 2.1.5]
for

??(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
with the
obvious modification of dropping the derivatives. Indeed, using the same ideas it is not
hard to show that if
V=\varinjlim V_{n}
is a countable strict inductive limit
realized as the union
V=\bigcup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}V_{n}
, then the seminorms

\mathrm{p}_{a}\coloneqq\sup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}a_{n}\cdot\mathrm{p_{n}}

(2.4.62)

with arbitrary sequences
a=(a_{n})_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
of weights and

\mathrm{p}_{n}\in\operatorname{cs}(V_{n})
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
provide a defining system of
seminorms for
V
. For
V=\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
, one may take the Banach spaces

V_{n}\coloneqq\mathscr{C}\bigl([-n,n]\bigr)\quad\textrm{with norms}\quad\lVert\phi\rVert_{n}\coloneqq\max_{x\in[-n,n]}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert.

(2.4.63)

Interpolating the sequence
a
to a continuous – or smooth – function on
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}

results in the version of the seminorms provided in
[
96
]
.

Let
a_{n}\coloneqq 2^{n}
and
\phi\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p}_{a},1}(0)
. Then

\lVert\phi\rVert_{n}\leq 1
, i.e.
\lVert\phi\rVert_{n}^{n}\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{n}
, for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
, and we we may estimate

\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}\big\lvert\phi(n)\big\rvert^{n}\leq\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot\lVert\phi\rVert_{n}^{n}\leq\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{a}(\phi)\leq 2^{N}

(2.4.64)

for all
N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Hence, the Taylor series of
f
converges
uniformly

on the open set
\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p}_{a},1}(0)
and
f
is continuous on this
neighbourhood of zero.

Let now
\phi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and
C\coloneqq\max\{\lVert\phi\rVert_{\infty},1\}
. We compute

f(\phi_{0}+\phi)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigl(\phi_{0}(n)+\phi(n)\bigr)^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\phi_{0}(n)^{n-k}\cdot\phi(n)^{k},

(2.4.65)

for
\phi\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. If now
b_{n}\coloneqq(4C)^{n}
for
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
and

\phi\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p}_{b},1}(0)
, then this provides the estimate

\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}\big\lvert\phi_{0}(n)+\phi(n)\big\rvert^{n}\leq\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}C^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\big\lVert\phi\big\rVert_{n}=\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}(2C^{n})\cdot\big\lVert\phi\big\rVert_{n}\leq 2^{N}

(2.4.66)

for
N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
, proving the uniform convergence of the Taylor series of
f
on the
open neighbourhood
\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p}_{b},1}(\phi_{0})
of
\phi_{0}
. Hence,
\phi

is continuous at
\phi_{0}
and thus continuous everywhere.

Summarizing, we have established that
f\in\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(\mathscr{C}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))

and have thus found an example of a continuous and bounded holomorphic
function on a non-Baire space. It is straightforward to generalize this to
series over powers of distributions
\varphi_{n}
with supports, say, contained
in
[-n,n]\setminus[n-1,n-1]
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
.

2.5
Symmetric Tensors as Polynomials

“The truth has got its boots on,” he said. “It’s going to start
kicking.”

The Truth
– Terry Pratchett

Having established a firm control over holomorphic differential calculus on
infinite dimensional locally convex spaces and the topology of uniform
convergence on bounded sets, we return to strict deformation quantization. More
precisely, we investigate the complex analytic properties of the spaces of
tensors central to
[
186
]
, when interpreted as polynomials on the
strong dual space. We begin by fixing our notation. Let
V
be a locally convex
space and denote its
n
-th symmetric tensor power by

V^{\vee n}\coloneqq??\coloneqq\underbrace{V\vee\cdots\vee V}_{n-\text{times}}\qquad\textrm{for }n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}

(2.5.1)

and
V^{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{S}^{0}(V)\coloneqq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. We endow each of the
spaces
\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)
with the
projective tensor product topology
, which is
generated by the seminorms

??(v)\coloneqq\inf\biggl\{\sum_{k}\mathrm{p}\bigl(v_{1}^{(k)}\bigr)\cdots\mathrm{p}\bigl(v_{n}^{(k)}\bigr)\colon v=\sum_{k}v_{1}^{(k)}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}^{(k)}\biggr\},

(2.5.2)

where the summation over
k
is finite, and
\mathrm{p}
varies over
\operatorname{cs}(V)
. The
infimum accounts for the non-uniqueness of the decomposition of a given tensor
v
into
factorizing ones. For
n=0
, one sets
\mathrm{p}^{0}\coloneqq\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert
. We indicate this topology with a subscript
\pi
. That is, we
write
\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)
, and call
??
the
n
-th

projective symmetric tensor power
of
V
.

Note that (
2.5.2
) makes sense regardless of the symmetry of
v

and that one may define tensor product of different locally convex spaces and
seminorms in an analogous fashion.
44
44

44

We will make this precise in
(
2.5.4
).
We collect some well known properties of the
projective tensor powers.

Proposition 2.5.1
(Projective tensor powers).

Let
V
be a locally convex space.

i.)

The space
\mathrm{S}^{1}_{\pi}(V)
coincides with
V
as a locally convex space.

ii.)

If
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
, then

\mathrm{p}^{n}(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})=\mathrm{p}(v_{1})\cdots\mathrm{p}(v_{n}).

(2.5.3)

iii.)

The locally convex space
V
is Hausdorff if and only if
\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)

is for some
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. In this case,
\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)
is Hausdorff for
all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

iv.)

If
\mathrm{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{p}_{n}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
, then

\bigl(\mathrm{p}_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{p}_{n}\bigr)(v)\coloneqq\inf\biggl\{\sum_{k}\mathrm{p}_{1}\bigl(v_{1}^{(k)}\bigr)\cdots\mathrm{p}_{n}\bigl(v_{n}^{(k)}\bigr)\colon v=\sum_{k}v_{1}^{(k)}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}^{(k)}\biggr\}

(2.5.4)

defines a continuous seminorm on
\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)
.

v.)

If
\mathrm{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{p}_{n}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
, then

\bigl(\mathrm{p}_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{p}_{m}\bigr)\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\mathrm{p}_{m+1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{p}_{n}\bigr)=\mathrm{p}_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{p}_{n}

(2.5.5)

for any
m,n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
with
1\leq m<n
.
45
45

45

Indeed, this is what makes
speaking of tensor powers feasible in the first place.

vi.)

The symmetric tensor product is continuous as a bilinear mapping

\vee\colon\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)\times\mathrm{S}_{\pi}^{m}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{S}^{n+m}_{\pi}(V).

(2.5.6)

More precisely, if
v\in\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)
and
w\in\mathrm{S}^{m}_{\pi}(V)
, then

\mathrm{p}^{n+m}(v\vee w)\leq\mathrm{p}^{n}(v)\cdot\mathrm{p}^{m}(w).

(2.5.7)

Proof
:

All presented statements and a systematic treatment of projective tensor products
can be found in the textbooks
[
181
, Ch. 43]
,
[
108
, §41]
or

[
98
, Ch. 15]
.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the context of star products we will actually need a slightly more general construction,
namely the projective tensor product of two different locally convex spaces
V

and
W
. The topology of
V\mathbin{\otimes}W
is then generated by the seminorms
(
2.5.4
) with continuous seminorms
\mathrm{p}_{1}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and
\mathrm{p}_{2}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
. We indicate this topology
by writing
V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}W
, whenever there is any room for confusion, and otherwise
suppress the index. Unsurprisingly, the resulting space has properties
analogous to the projective symmetric tensor powers. Moreover, the following rather
convenient characterization of continuity for
n
-multilinear mappings holds, which
provides significant simplifications for certain technical arguments within strict
deformation quantization. This is exemplified within proof-sketch of
Theorem
3.1.8
and the proof of
Theorem
3.5.17
.

Proposition 2.5.2
(Infimum argument,
[
181
, Prop. 43.4]
).

Let
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n},W
be locally convex spaces and

\phi\colon V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{n}\longrightarrow W

(2.5.8)

be
n
-linear with corresponding linear map

\Phi\colon V_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}V_{n}\longrightarrow W.

(2.5.9)

Endow
V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{n}
with the Cartesian product topology and

V_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}V_{n}
with the projective tensor product topology.
Then
\phi
is continuous if and only if
\Phi
is. More precisely, if for
a continuous seminorm
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
there are
\mathrm{p}_{1}\in\operatorname{cs}(V_{1})
,
\ldots
,

\mathrm{p_{n}}\in\operatorname{cs}(V_{n})
such that

\mathrm{q}\bigl(\phi(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\bigr)\leq\mathrm{p}_{1}(v_{1})\cdots\mathrm{p}_{n}(v_{n})\qquad\textrm{for all }v_{1}\in V_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V_{n},

(2.5.10)

then

\mathrm{q}\bigl(\Phi(v)\bigr)\leq\bigl(\mathrm{p}_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{p}_{n}\bigr)(v)\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in V_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}V_{n},

(2.5.11)

and vice versa.

This means that it suffices to prove continuity estimates for multilinear mappings,
such as star products, on
factorizing tensors
only. In view of the infimum in
(
2.5.3
), this constitutes a drastic simplification.

Remark 2.5.3
(Injective tensor product).

There is another natural way of endowing the algebraic tensor product
V\mathbin{\otimes}W
with a topology, namely by means of
injective tensor products
of
seminorms. Indeed, if
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
,
then their injective tensor product is defined by

\bigl(\mathrm{p}\,??\,\mathrm{q}\bigr)(x)\coloneqq\sup\bigl\{\bigl(v^{\prime}\mathbin{\otimes}w^{\prime}\bigr)(x)\colon v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}},\;w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{\mathrm{q}},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}\bigr\},

(2.5.12)

where

??\coloneqq\bigl\{\varphi\in V^{\prime}\colon\big\lvert\varphi(v)\big\rvert\leq 1\textrm{ for all }v\in A\bigr\}

(2.5.13)

denotes the
polar
of
A\subseteq V
. Remarkably, an analogue of
(
2.5.3
) still holds for the injective tensor product, as we
shall prove in a moment. However, there is no injective version of
Proposition
2.5.2
, a fact we shall make precise in
Remark
2.5.6
after some preliminary
considerations. Having to work with the supremum and the presence of polars
make injective tensor products less convenient to work with than projective
tensor products in the context of strict deformation quantization.

That being said, ultimately, all locally convex spaces
V,W
of observables
utilized in
the strict deformation quantizations
[
186
,
63
,
173
,
90
]
turn out to be
nuclear
: this implies that
the
identity mapping

V??W\longrightarrow V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}W

(2.5.14)

constitutes an isomorphism of locally convex spaces. In general, it is at least
continuous.

Lemma 2.5.4
(Injective vs. projective
\mathbin{\otimes}
,
[
181
, Cor. of
Prop. 43.4]
).

Let
V
and
W
be locally convex spaces. Then the identity mapping

V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}W\longrightarrow V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}W

(2.5.15)

is continuous. More precisely, if
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W)
, then

\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{q}\leq\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}\mathrm{q}.

(2.5.16)

Proof
:

Let
x\in V\mathbin{\otimes}W
and consider a finite decomposition

x=\sum_{k}v_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}w_{k}

(2.5.17)

into factorizing tensors as well as
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}

and
w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{q},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}
. By definition of the polars,
see again (
2.5.13
), we have

\bigg\lvert v^{\prime}\biggl(\frac{v}{\mathrm{p}(v)}\biggr)\bigg\rvert\leq 1,\qquad\textrm{that is}\quad\big\lvert v^{\prime}(v)\big\rvert\leq\mathrm{p}(v)

(2.5.18)

for all
v\in V\setminus\ker\mathrm{p}
. If
\mathrm{p}(v)=0
, then

\mathrm{p}(rv)=0\leq 1
for all
r>0
, as well. By definition of the polar
this in turn means that

r\cdot\big\lvert v^{\prime}(v)\big\rvert=\big\lvert v^{\prime}(rv)\big\rvert\leq 1\qquad\textrm{for all }r>0,

(2.5.19)

which implies
v^{\prime}(v)=0
. Hence,
\lvert v^{\prime}\rvert\leq\mathrm{p}
holds and analogously we get
\lvert w^{\prime}\rvert\leq\mathrm{q}
.
46
46

46

In passing, we note that this proves “
\leq
” in
(
2.5.3
) for injective tensor products. To see the other
inequality, fix vectors
v\in V
,
w\in W
and extend the continuous linear
functionals

\varphi_{v}(\lambda v)\coloneqq\lambda\cdot\mathrm{p}(v)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\varphi_{w}(\lambda w)\coloneqq\lambda\cdot\mathrm{q}(w)

(2.5.20)

from
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\cdot v
resp.
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\cdot w
to
V
resp.
W
by means of the
Hahn-Banach Theorem.

Consequently,

\big\lvert\bigl(v^{\prime}\mathbin{\otimes}w^{\prime}\bigr)(x)\big\rvert\leq\sum_{k}\big\lvert v^{\prime}(v_{k})\cdot w^{\prime}(w_{k})\big\rvert\leq\sum_{k}\mathrm{p}(v_{k})\cdot\mathrm{q}(w_{k}).

(2.5.21)

Note that the left-hand side is independent of the chosen decomposition,
whereas the right-hand side does not depend on the choice of
v^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}
. Thus,
taking the infimum over all decompositions of
x
and the supremum over all
v^{\prime}

and
w^{\prime}
in the respective polars proves the continuity estimate
(
2.5.16
).

\ch@irxboxempty

Along the way, we showed the following alternative description of polars, which
is conceptually pleasing and will be useful later.

Corollary 2.5.5
.

Let
V
be a locally convex space and
p\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Then

\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}=\bigl\{\varphi\in V^{\prime}\colon\lvert\varphi\rvert\leq\mathrm{p}\bigr\}.

(2.5.22)

By
[
181
, Thm. 50.1(f)]
nuclearity of a locally convex space
V
may be
characterized as

V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}W\cong V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}W

(2.5.23)

as locally convex spaces via the canonical mapping
(
2.5.15
) for all locally convex spaces
W
,
which in view of Lemma
2.5.4
is in turn equivalent to the
continuity of the identity mapping

V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}W\longrightarrow V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}W

(2.5.24)

for all locally convex spaces
W
. Indeed, this was also
Grothendieck’s
47
47

47

Alexander Grothendieck (1928-2014) was the father of all
of
algebraic geometry and received the Field’s medal in 1966. Before turning to geometry,
he pursued and largely laid to rest locally convex analysis under his doctoral
advisors
Laurent Schwartz and
Jean Dieudonné.
original definition in
[
78
]
. We may use this to
prove that having an injective version of Proposition
2.5.2
for
bilinear mappings already implies nuclearity in the following sense.

Remark 2.5.6
.

Let
V,W
be locally convex spaces and consider the bilinear mapping

\mathbin{\otimes}\colon V\times W\longrightarrow V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}W,\quad\mathbin{\otimes}(v,w)\coloneqq v\mathbin{\otimes}w.

(2.5.25)

By (
2.5.3
), the mapping
\mathbin{\otimes}
is continuous. If now
Proposition
2.5.2
were to hold for bilinear mappings, then the
same would be true for the induced mapping

V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}W\longrightarrow V\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}W,

(2.5.26)

which is just the identity. Thus we could infer nuclearity of
V
by varying
W
.

Note that the utilization of the seminorms (
2.5.12
) is rather
non-standard in the older literature, which usually defines the topology by
providing a basis of zero neighbourhoods. This has the advantage of capturing the
higher generality of topological vector spaces instead of only locally convex ones,
but is arguably less intuitive to work with. For a comprehensive discussion of
injective and projective tensor products and in particular their interpretation as
continuous
\mathbin{\otimes}
-
\operatorname{\mathrm{Hom}}
-adjunctions, we refer to the textbooks

[
181
, Ch. 43 & 44]
,
[
30
, Ch. II §3-4]
,
[
98
, Ch. 15
& 16]
and in particular
[
184
, Sec. 6]
for a more modern
exposition based on seminorms.

After this short digression, we return to the goal of this section, which is to
investigate the mapping

??\colon??\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}(V^{\prime}),\quad\iota(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})\Big|_{\varphi}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi(v_{j})

(2.5.27)

and its components
\iota_{n}\coloneqq\iota\big|_{\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)}
. Our considerations are
thus similar to the classical study of duality as it can e.g. be found in the textbook

[
50
, Sec. 1.2 & Ch. 2]
, but with
a notably different outlook and focus: the application to strict deformation
quantization. As a preliminary consideration, we prove the injectivity of
\iota
.

Lemma 2.5.7
.

Let
V
be a Hausdorff locally convex space. Then the
mapping
\iota
from (
2.5.27
) and its components
\iota_{n}
are injective for all

n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

Proof
:

As polynomials of different homogeneous degrees are linearly
independent, it suffices to prove the injectivity of the maps

\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.28)

for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. For
n=0
, we simply have

\iota_{0}=\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}{C}

(2.5.29)

and thus there is nothing to be shown. Thus assume
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
and let

0\neq v_{n}\in\mathrm{S}^{n}(V).

(2.5.30)

We commit the cardinal sin
48
48

48

Sorry!
and choose a linear algebraic basis

\{\mathit{e}_{\alpha}\colon\alpha\in I\}\subseteq V
to expand

v_{n}=v^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}\cdot\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{n}},

(2.5.31)

where – and in the sequel – we also adhere to Einstein’s summation
convention. As
v_{n}
is nonzero,
there are indices
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in I
such that the
corresponding
coefficient
v^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}
is nonzero, as well. Moreover, if

\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\in I
is a
different choice
49
49

49

That is,
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})
is not a permutation of
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
.
of
indices such that the coefficient

v^{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}\neq 0
, then

\beta_{j}\notin\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}\qquad\textrm{for some}\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(2.5.32)

Choosing one such index for every other non-vanishing coefficient defines a
finite index set
I_{0}\subseteq I
, as we are working with a liner algebraic
basis. Consider the linear functional

\displaystyle\psi\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{span}}\bigl(\bigl\{\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{n}}\bigr\}\cup\{\mathit{e}_{\beta}\colon\beta\in I_{0}\}\bigr)\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},

(2.5.33)

\displaystyle\psi\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\eta_{k}\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{k}}+\sum_{\beta\in I_{0}}\lambda_{\beta}\mathit{e}_{\beta}\biggr)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\eta_{k}.

(2.5.34)

As the various
\mathit{e}_{\beta}
are linearly independent, our functional
\psi

is well
defined. Moreover, as its domain is finite dimensional and Hausdorff,
\psi
is
continuous. By the Hahn-Banach Theorem, we find a continuous extension
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
of
\psi
. By construction,

\iota_{n}(v_{n})\big|_{\varphi}=v^{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}\cdot\varphi(\mathit{e}_{\beta_{1}})\cdots\varphi(\mathit{e}_{\beta_{n}})=v^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}\neq 0,

as every other term contains a factor
0
. This proves that the linear mapping

\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.35)

is injective and thus the same is true for
\iota
itself, as we are dealing with
direct sums throughout.

\ch@irxboxempty

Notice that
\iota_{1}\colon V\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}(V^{\prime})=V^{\prime\prime}
is the canonical embedding of
V
into its
bidual.
50
50

50

To speak of the continuous bidual
V^{\prime\prime}
or continuous polynomials on

V^{\prime}
, one has to choose a topology for
V^{\prime}
. This is an oversight, which we shall rectify
momentarily.

Consequently,
the mapping
\iota
may be viewed as the algebra morphism
obtained from the universal property of
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(V)
as the
free
commutative algebra
over
V
, see
[
30
, Ch. III,
§6]
or
[
175
, Sec. 10.13]
for a proper discussion of
this
algebraic point of view. This has the pleasant consequence that

\iota_{n}(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})=\iota_{1}(v_{1})\cdots\iota_{1}(v_{n}),

(2.5.36)

where we use the pointwise product
V^{\prime}
inherits from
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
on the right-hand side. As
we are first and foremost interested in the continuity of
\iota
, we have to
endow the
symmetric tensor algebra
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(V)
and its direct summands with a topology; we
have already achieved the latter by means of projective tensor products.

For the purposes of strict deformation quantization
[
186
, Sec. 3.1]

has endowed
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(V)
with a topology finer than the locally convex direct
sum, but coarser than the Cartesian product topology, the so called
R
-topology with
respect to a real parameter
R
. We have encountered this topology for
V=\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
and
R=1/2
in Example
1.2.2
. Both extremal cases may be ruled out a priori,
which has lead to this intermediate choice:

On the one hand, the star products fail to be continuous for the
full Cartesian product, which essentially corresponds to the fact that not every
formal power series converges. And, on the other hand, one wishes to incorporate
certain formal power series, for example
observables of Gaussian decay, in completions of the observable algebras. As the
locally convex direct sum is already complete, this necessitates the passage to a
finer
topology. We refer to the survey
[
187
]
for a comprehensive discussion
of these ideas.

The main result of this section is that these power series and their topology possess
a conceptual interpretation as bounded Fréchet holomorphic functions. To make this
precise, we first recall the definition of the
R
-topologies by means of a defining
system of seminorms. Fix
R\geq 0
. If
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
, then we define

??\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c^{n}\cdot n!^{R}\cdot\mathrm{p}^{n}

(2.5.37)

for any
c\geq 0
, where
\mathrm{p}^{n}
denotes the
n
-th projective tensor power
from
(
2.5.2
). Note that the series in (
2.5.37
)
is only formally infinite, as
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(V)
is a direct sum. This means that
every element
v\in\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(V)
is a finite linear combination of
factorizing tensors. We then write

??\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)

(2.5.38)

for the symmetric algebra of
V
endowed with the locally convex topology generated by
the seminorms (
2.5.37
) with
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and
c\geq 0
. In the sequel, we shall refer to this topology as the
R-topology
. Note
that even in the
case that
V\neq\{0\}
is normable,

\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)
is
not
. Different constants
c\geq 0

result in inequivalent seminorms.
51
51

51

In view of the presence of canonical
commutation relations in many important observable algebras, see again
(
1.1.23
), this is a feature and not a bug if
one wants to achieve the continuity of the star product.
We collect the
properties of
\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)
, which will play a role in our further
considerations.

Proposition 2.5.8
(
R
-Topologies,
[
186
, Lem. 3.6]
or

[
90
, Prop. 3.1 & 3.2]
).

Let
V
be a locally convex space and
R\geq 0
.

i.)

If
T\leq R
, then
\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)\subseteq\mathrm{S}_{T}^{\bullet}(V)
and
the inclusion is continuous.

ii.)

The subspace topology inherited from the inclusion
\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)\subseteq\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)
is the projective tensor power topology for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

iii.)

The space
\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)
is Hausdorff if and only if
V
is.

iv.)

The completion
\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)
of
\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)
is
given by

\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)=\biggl\{v=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}\in??\widehat{\mathrm{S}}^{n}(V)\colon\mathrm{p}_{R,c}(v)<\infty\textrm{ for all }\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V),c\geq 0\biggr\}.

(2.5.39)

Moreover, the infinite series converges in the
R
-topology.

v.)

The symmetric tensor product
\vee
endows
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}_{R}(V)
with the
structure of a locally convex algebra. If
R=0
, then
\vee
is
submultiplicative.

vi.)

For every
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
, the evaluation
functional

\delta_{\varphi}\colon\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\delta_{\varphi}(v)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varphi^{k}(v_{k})

(2.5.40)

is a continuous character, i.e. constitutes an algebra morphism.

One may use the very same seminorms to define a topology on the full tensor
algebra
\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}(V)
. This is sometimes useful, as the symmetrizer

??\colon\mathrm{T}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V),\quad\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n})\coloneqq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}v_{\sigma(1)}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{\sigma(n)}

(2.5.41)

then turns out to be a continuous linear projection by
(
2.5.3
), where ??denotes the permutation group in the letters
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. This more general
point of view is sometimes useful, for instance for our considerations within
Section
2.7
.

In analogy to indicating the topology of uniform convergence on bounded sets with a
subscript
\beta
, we shall in the sequel use the subscript
??to indicate the
topology of pointwise convergence – that is to say, the topology of uniform
convergence on finite sets. For example, we write
V^{\prime}_{\sigma}
and

\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}_{\sigma}
. This yields the
following continuity properties of (
2.5.27
).

Theorem 2.5.9
(Tensors as polynomials on the dual space).

Let
V
be a locally convex space and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

i.)

Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
. The polynomial

\iota(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})\colon V^{\prime}_{\sigma}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.5.42)

is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
n
of finite type
52
52

52

A
polynomial
P
is
of
finite type
if there are
finitely
many
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\in(V^{\prime}_{\beta})^{\prime}
and corresponding coefficients

\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
such that

P(\varphi)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\lambda_{k}a_{k}(\varphi)^{n}\qquad\textrm{for all }\varphi\in V^{\prime}.

(2.5.43)

Note that this linear combination takes place within fixed
homogeneity and is thus different from the decomposition of
\iota
into
its homogeneous degrees
\iota_{n}
.

and continuous. In particular, it is continuous as a polynomial on
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
.

ii.)

The restrictions

\iota_{n}\coloneqq\iota\Big|_{\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)}\colon\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}^{n}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.44)

are continuous for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

iii.)

If
V
is barrelled, then the restrictions

\iota_{n}=\iota\Big|_{\mathrm{S}^{n}}\colon\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{n}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.45)

are continuous.

iv.)

If
V
is barrelled and
R\geq 0
, then the mapping

\iota\colon\mathrm{S}_{R}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}_{\beta}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.46)

is continuous.

Proof
:

The case
n=0
is once again trivial by (
2.5.29
). Let
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
. The homogeneity of
\iota(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})
is clear by (
2.5.27
). Note that the seminorm

\mathrm{p}(\varphi)\coloneqq\max_{j=1,\ldots,n}\big\lvert\varphi(v_{j})\big\rvert

(2.5.47)

is
\sigma
-continuous. Thus the estimate

\big\lvert\iota(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})\varphi\big\rvert=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big\lvert\varphi(v_{j})\big\rvert\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{p}(\varphi)=\mathrm{p}(\varphi)^{n}

(2.5.48)

proves the
\sigma
-continuity of
\iota(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})
by virtue of
Proposition
2.1.8
,

v.)
. As the strong topology is finer than
the
\sigma
-topology, the polynomial
\iota(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})
is thus
also continuous on
V_{\beta}^{\prime}
. We have shown the continuity assertions in

i.)
. Let now

v=\sum_{k}v_{1}^{(k)}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n}^{(k)}\in\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)

(2.5.49)

and
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
with corresponding seminorm

\mathrm{p}_{\varphi}(Q)\coloneqq\lvert Q(\varphi)\rvert
for
Q\in\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}_{\sigma}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
. By continuity of
\varphi
, we moreover note

\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}\coloneqq\lvert\varphi\rvert\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. We arrive at the
estimate

\mathrm{p}_{\varphi}\bigl(\iota_{n}(v)\bigr)=\bigg\lvert\sum_{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi(v_{j}^{(k)})\bigg\rvert\leq\sum_{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big\lvert\varphi(v_{j}^{(k)})\big\rvert=\sum_{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}\bigl(v_{j}^{(k)}\bigr).

(2.5.50)

Taking the infimum over all decompositions of
v
into factorizing tensors thus yields the continuity estimate

\big\lvert\bigl(\iota_{n}(v)\bigr)(\varphi)\big\rvert=\mathrm{p}_{\varphi}\bigl(\iota_{n}(v)\bigr)\leq\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}^{n}(v),

(2.5.51)

where
\mathrm{q}^{n}_{\varphi}
denotes the
n
-th projective tensor power of

\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}
. This proves
ii.)
. Let now
V

be barrelled and
B^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}_{\beta}
a bounded subset. By what we have already
argued, we get (
2.5.50
) for every
\varphi\in B^{\prime}
. Consider

\mathrm{q}\coloneqq\sup_{\varphi\in B^{\prime}}\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}
, which is a
well defined, continuous seminorm on
V
by the Banach-Steinhaus Theorem. We
arrive at the continuity estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}_{B^{\prime}}\bigl(\iota_{n}(v)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\varphi\in B^{\prime}}\mathrm{p}_{\varphi}\bigl(\iota_{n}(v)\bigr)

(2.5.52)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\varphi\in B^{\prime}}\sum_{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}\bigl(v_{j}^{(k)}\bigr)

(2.5.53)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\sup_{\varphi\in B^{\prime}}\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}(v_{j}^{(k)})

(2.5.54)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{q}(v_{j}^{(k)}).

(2.5.55)

This proves
iii.)
by once again taking the infimum over
all decompositions of
v
into factorizing tensors. Next, we prove that

\iota_{n}(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})
is of finite type. As noted before, the
mapping
\iota_{1}\colon V\longrightarrow V^{\prime\prime}
is just the usual canonical
injection, whence there is nothing to be shown in this case. Turning to
higher degrees, let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in V
be given. By the polarization
identity (
2.1.7
), we may write

\varphi(v_{1})\cdots\varphi(v_{n})=\frac{1}{2^{n}\cdot n!}\sum_{\varepsilon_{j}=\pm 1}\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\cdot\varphi\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{k}v_{k}\biggr)^{n}

(2.5.56)

for
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
, as the left-hand side is linear with respect to
v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}
. Consequently, taking the index set
J\coloneqq\{-1,1\}^{n}
,

a_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}j(k)v_{k}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\lambda_{j}\coloneqq\frac{j(1)\cdots j(n)}{2^{n}\cdot n!}

(2.5.57)

for all
j\in J
does the job. Finally, it suffices to consider the
case
R=0
in
iv.)
, as it is the coarsest of the Hausdorff

R
-topologies and biggest as a set by Proposition
2.5.8
. By
what we have already shown, we have

\mathrm{p}_{B^{\prime}}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr)\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{p}_{B^{\prime}}\bigl(\iota_{n}(v_{n})\bigr)\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{q}^{n}\bigl(v_{n})=\mathrm{q}_{0,1}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr)

(2.5.58)

for
v=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}\in\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
with homogeneous
components
v_{n}\in\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Polynomials of finite type have been studied extensively, beginning with the work of
Aron-Schottenloher
[
4
]
and we refer to

[
50
, Sec. 2.6]
for a comprehensive overview over the literature.

Our next goal is to investigate the flavour of functions the unique continuous linear
extension

??\colon\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{0}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Map}(V_{\beta}^{\prime})

(2.5.59)

of (
2.5.27
) produces for locally convex Hausdorff spaces
V
and
R\geq 0
. To
this end, we first observe that

\hat{\iota}\biggl(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}\biggr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{\iota}(v_{n})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{\iota}_{n}(v_{n}).

(2.5.60)

for vectors
v=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}\in\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
with components
v_{n}\in\widehat{\mathrm{S}}^{n}_{\pi}(V)
.
To see this, recall that the partial sums of the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}
actually
converge to
v
in the
\mathrm{S}_{R}
-topology and each of the restrictions
\hat{\iota}_{n}

is continuous, as well, and given by extending the restrictions
\iota_{n}

of
\iota
.
For this it is crucial that the subspace topology induced by the inclusion

\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)\subseteq\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
is the
\pi
-topology by
Proposition
2.5.8
,
ii.)
. Moreover, we
observe that already the homogeneous
components
v_{n}
might not actually be linear combinations of factorizing tensors any
more.

Thus there are two limiting procedures to be understood: a
horizontal

completion within each homogeneous degree and a
vertical
completion of the
symmetric algebra, where the summability condition comes into play. By completeness of

\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}(V_{\beta}^{\prime})
for bornological
V_{\beta}^{\prime}
, see again
Theorem
2.4.7
, we get that the extended
components
\hat{\iota}_{n}
map again into
\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}(V_{\beta}^{\prime})
. Moreover, it turns
out that Gâteaux holomorphy is preserved without any assumptions.

Corollary 2.5.10
.

Let
V
be a Hausdorff locally convex space and
v\in\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{0}(V)
. Then

\hat{\iota}(v)\colon V_{\beta}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.5.61)

is Gâteaux holomorphic with Taylor polynomials at the origin given by

\hat{\iota}_{n}(v_{n})
for
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

Proof
:

This is clear by Corollary
2.2.7
and
Theorem
2.5.9
,
ii.)
, where
we once again use that multilinearity is stable under pointwise limits.

\ch@irxboxempty

Our next goal is to understand when
\iota
is an embedding. We begin with a
preliminary lemma on polars, which is remarkable in its own right.

Lemma 2.5.11
.

Let
V
be a locally convex space and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Then the polar

\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}\subseteq V_{\beta}^{\prime}

(2.5.62)

is bounded with respect to the topology of uniform convergence on bounded sets.

Proof
:

Recall that a subset
S
of a locally convex space
W
is bounded if and only if

\sup_{v\in S}\mathrm{q}(v)<\infty\qquad\textrm{for all }\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(W).

(2.5.63)

For
W=V^{\prime}_{\beta}
the continuous seminorms are given by
\mathrm{p}_{B}
from
(
2.1.51
) for all bounded subsets
B\subseteq V
. Thus let
B\subseteq V
be bounded and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. By boundedness, there
exists some radius
r>0
with
B\subseteq\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},r}(0)
. Thus,
also investing
(
2.5.22
), we get

\sup_{\varphi\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}}\mathrm{p}_{B}(\varphi)=\sup_{\varphi\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}}\sup_{v\in B}\big\lvert\varphi(v)\big\rvert\leq\sup_{v\in B}\mathrm{p}(v)\leq r<\infty,

(2.5.64)

proving the boundedness of
\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

The final ingredient we need to guarantee that
\iota
is an embedding turns out to
be nuclearity. We will use this property mostly as a blackbox. For us, its crucial
consequence is that, on the tensor product of nuclear spaces, the locally convex
topologies generated by projective and injective tensor products coincide, see again
Remark
2.5.3
.

Theorem 2.5.12
(Embedding I).

Let
V
be a barrelled Hausdorff locally convex space. Assume
furthermore that
V
is nuclear. Then the mapping

\iota\colon\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{\bullet}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.65)

is a grading preserving embedding, i.e. a linear injection such that the subspace
topology inherited from the inclusion of its image into

\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}_{\beta}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
coincides with the
0
-topology.

Proof
:

We have just proved the injectivity of
\iota
in Lemma
2.5.7
.
By Theorem
2.5.9
,
iv.)
, our
mapping (
2.5.65
) is continuous. It remains to assert that the
subspace topology inherited from the inclusion
\iota(\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V))\subseteq\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{\bullet}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
is finer than the
\mathrm{S}_{0}
-topology.

To this end, let
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)\setminus\{0\}
. By nuclearity, there exists
another seminorm
\mathrm{p}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
with
\mathrm{p}\leq\mathrm{p}^{\prime}

such that

\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}\mathrm{q}\leq\nu\cdot\bigl(\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{q}\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(V),

(2.5.66)

where
\nu
corresponds to the nuclear norm of the canonical mapping

V_{\mathrm{p^{\prime}}}\longrightarrow V_{\mathrm{p}}

between the local Banach spaces, see
[
184
, Thm. 6.38]
. Crucially,

\nu
thus
only
depends on the seminorm on the left-hand side. For tensor
powers, we claim that this implies

\mathrm{p}^{n}\leq\nu^{n-1}\cdot\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{p}\qquad\textrm{for all }n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}.

(2.5.67)

Indeed, for
n=1
, this is clear and, in view of
Proposition
2.5.1
,
v.)
and the
analogous statement for injective tensor powers
53
53

53

Which, taking another look
at (
2.5.12
), is indeed clear.
, we get by induction

\mathrm{p}^{n}=\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}\mathrm{p}^{n-1}\leq\nu^{n-2}\cdot\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}\bigl(\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{p}\bigr)\leq\nu^{n-1}\cdot\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{p},

(2.5.68)

proving (
2.5.67
). Consider now the polar
B^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{p},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}
as defined
in (
2.5.13
). By Corollary
2.5.5
, every functional
\varphi\in B^{\prime}
fulfils

\lvert\varphi\rvert\leq p
, and by Lemma
2.5.11
the set
B^{\prime}
is
bounded within
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Let

v=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}\in\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}_{0}(V)\qquad\textrm{with homogeneous components}\quad v_{n}\in\mathrm{S}_{\pi}^{n}(V)

(2.5.69)

and write
P_{n}\coloneqq\iota_{n}(v_{n})\in\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
. Using
(
2.5.67
) and (
2.1.34
) for

B^{\prime}
, where we use that polars are always absolutely convex by the triangle
inequality, leads to the estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}^{n}\bigl(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\nu^{n-1}\cdot\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{p}

(2.5.70)

\displaystyle=\nu^{n-1}\sup_{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\in B^{\prime}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi_{n}\bigr)(v_{n})\Big\rvert

(2.5.71)

\displaystyle=\nu^{n-1}\sup_{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\in B^{\prime}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\varphi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\varphi_{n}\bigr)(v_{n})\Big\rvert
