(2.5.72)

\displaystyle=\nu^{n-1}\sup_{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\in B^{\prime}}\Big\lvert\widecheck{P}_{n}\bigl(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\bigr)\Big\rvert

(2.5.73)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu^{n-1}\cdot n^{n}}{n!}\sup_{\varphi\in B^{\prime}}\big\lvert P_{n}(\varphi)\big\rvert

(2.5.74)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu^{n-1}\cdot n^{n}}{n!}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{B^{\prime}}(P_{n})

(2.5.75)

for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. Putting everything together, setting

C\coloneqq\max\{\nu,1\}
and
using (
1.3.12
) yields the desired inequality

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}_{0,c}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c^{n}\cdot\mathrm{p}^{n}(v_{n})

(2.5.76)

\displaystyle\leq\lvert v_{0}\rvert+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(C\cdot c)^{n}\cdot\frac{n^{n}}{n!}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{B^{\prime}}(P_{n})

(2.5.77)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(C\cdot c\cdot e)^{n}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{B^{\prime}}(P_{n})

(2.5.78)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{r}_{mB^{\prime},\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert,0}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr)

(2.5.79)

with
m\coloneqq C\cdot c\cdot e
and the continuous seminorm

\mathrm{r}_{mB^{\prime},\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert,0}

from (
2.4.23
).

\ch@irxboxempty

In the preceding proof we have used the nuclearity of
V
to pass from the projective to
the injective tensor product within each symmetric tensor degree. This raises the
question whether one could have worked with injective tensor powers instead of
projective ones from the start to construct an alternative
\mathrm{S}_{R}
-topology compatible
with
\iota
also without nuclearity. This turns out to be feasible.

In analogy to (
2.5.37
), given
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
, we write

\mathrm{p}^{\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}n}
for its
n
-th injective tensor power and define for
any
c\geq 0
the seminorm

\widetilde{\mathrm{p}}_{0,c}\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c^{n}\cdot\mathrm{p}^{\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}n}

(2.5.80)

on
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(V)
, which like (
2.5.37
) is only formally an
infinite series. Finally, we denote the corresponding locally convex space by
??.
54
54

54

Of course, one can in principle introduce the
parameter
R
as before, but not going to this generality allows us to simplify the
bookkeeping considerably.
By Lemma
2.5.4
, this results in a
coarser topology than the
0
-topology. Taking another look at
Theorem
2.5.9
, we thus have to revisit the continuity of

\iota
, now as a mapping defined on
\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\bullet}(V)
.

Theorem 2.5.13
(Embedding II).

Let
V
be a barrelled Hausdorff locally convex space.
Then the mapping

\iota\colon\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\bullet}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{\bullet}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.81)

is a grading preserving embedding, i.e. a linear injection such that the subspace
topology inherited from the inclusion of its image into

\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}_{\beta}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
coincides with the
0
-topology.

Proof
:

It is instructive to proceed as in the proof of
Theorem
2.5.9
, i.e. by first considering each component
mapping
\iota_{n}
and
\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}^{n}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
. To this end, let
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
with
corresponding seminorms
\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}\coloneqq\lvert\varphi\rvert\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and
\mathrm{p}_{\varphi}\in\operatorname{cs}(\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}(V^{\prime}_{\beta}))

given by
\mathrm{p}_{\varphi}(Q)=\lvert Q(\varphi)\rvert
for
Q\in\mathrm{Pol}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
. Then we have
\varphi\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{q}_{\varphi},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}
by (
2.5.22
) and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}_{\varphi}\bigl(\iota_{n}(v)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Big\lvert\bigl(\varphi\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi\bigr)(v)\Big\rvert

(2.5.82)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\in\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{q}_{\varphi},1}(0)^{\textrm{\tiny\char 94\relax}}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\varphi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi_{n}\bigr)(v)\Big\rvert

(2.5.83)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}_{\varepsilon}\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}\bigr)(v)

(2.5.84)

for all
v\in\mathrm{S}^{n}(V)
. This means that

\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\sigma}^{n}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})

(2.5.85)

is continuous. If now
V
is barrelled and
B^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}
some bounded set, we
may replace
\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}
with
\mathrm{q}\coloneqq\sup_{\varphi\in B^{\prime}}\mathrm{q}_{\varphi}
to prove the continuity also of

\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{n}(V^{\prime}_{\beta}).

(2.5.86)

By (
2.5.80
), the corresponding continuity estimates then
glue to a continuity estimate of (
2.5.81
). As we have already
argued that the remaining statements hold also in this alternative setting, this
completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

It would be interesting to investigate the continuity of the star products in

[
186
,
173
,
90
]
after
replacing
\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
with the appropriately parametrized version of

\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\bullet}(V)
. In view of the absence of the infimum argument, see again
Remark
2.5.3
, this might however be prohibitively difficult.

Question 2.5.14
.

Are the continuous star products from
[
186
]
continuous with
respect to the
R
-topologies constructed from injective tensor products?

We return to the investigation of the vertical completion of

\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
in terms of bounded entire Fréchet holomorphic functions.

Corollary 2.5.15
.

Let
V
be a bornological and barrelled Hausdorff locally convex space. Assume
furthermore that
V
is nuclear and that
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
is Baire. Then each element of

\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{0}(V)
may be identified with a unique entire bounded Fréchet
holomorphic function on
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
.

Proof
:

Composing (
2.5.65
) with the further embedding

\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{\bullet}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(V^{\prime}_{\beta},\ch@airxmathbbm{C})

(2.5.87)

allows us to identify
\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
with a subspace
S
of the complete
Hausdorff space
\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(V^{\prime}_{\beta},\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
. Thus, one may realize the
completion of
\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
as the closure within

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(V^{\prime}_{\beta},\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
.

Alternatively, one may argue as follows. Let
v\in\widehat{\mathrm{S}}^{\bullet}_{0}(V)

with homogeneous components
v_{n}\in\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{\pi}^{n}(V)
. By
Corollary
2.5.10
, we know that
\hat{\iota}(v)
is Gâteaux holomorphic
with Taylor polynomials at the origin given by
\hat{\iota}_{n}(v_{n})
.
Theorem
2.5.9
,
i.)
and

iii.)
in combination with
Theorem
2.4.7
asserts that

\hat{\iota}_{n}(v_{n})
is continuous for every
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. By
Proposition
2.4.16
, this implies Fréchet holomorphy of

\hat{\iota}(v)
on all of
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Our alternative proof shows that the nuclearity is not necessary for the Fréchet
holomorphy of
\hat{\iota}(v)
. However, without nuclearity of
V
, it may in
principle happen
that there are non-convergent nets in
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}_{0}(V)
such that the image net under

\iota
converges in
\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(V^{\prime}_{\beta})
. We have seen in
Theorem
2.4.18
that the Baire property on
the domain ensures completeness of the space of bounded Fréchet holomorphic functions.
To apply this criterion we run into the complication that
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
is a dual
space, which is at odds with the usual sufficient criteria for the Baire property.

Remark 2.5.16
(Duality).

One way around this is to switch the roles of
V
and
V^{\prime}
: As they are in
duality, every
\varphi\in\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(V^{\prime})
with homogeneous components

\varphi_{n}\in\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\pi}(V^{\prime})
defines a polynomial on
V
by
means of

v\quad\mapsto\quad\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{n}\bigl(\underbrace{v\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v}_{n-\textrm{times}}\bigr).

(2.5.88)

As the resulting maps are nothing else than the restrictions of maps obtained by
further dualizing, i.e. by considering

\iota\colon\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}_{0}\bigl(V^{\prime}_{\beta}\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}\bigl((V_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta}^{\prime}\bigr),

(2.5.89)

analogous statements to the ones we have proved hold for
(
2.5.88
). Here one runs into the slight complication that, a priori,
the bounded subsets of
V
and its bidual
(V^{\prime}_{\beta})^{\prime}_{\beta}
may not be the same:
If
B\subseteq V
is a bounded subset of a barrelled and bornological space, then

\iota_{1}(B)\subseteq\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}(V_{\beta}^{\prime})=(V_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta}^{\prime}

(2.5.90)

is bounded by Theorem
2.5.9
,

iii.)
and
Lemma
2.1.9
,

ii.)
. However, in general there are
many more bounded subsets of
(V^{\prime}_{\beta})^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Nevertheless, as the properties
of
V
and
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
are typically vastly different e.g. in the setting of Fréchet
spaces, swapping roles might be useful. Another particular case arises if
V

admits for a pre-dual. Then one does not even have to restrict. We will see these
duality phenomenona in action in Example
2.6.2
and
Example
2.6.3
.

Even in the situation of Theorem
2.5.12
, the immediate
question is however the following:

Question 2.5.17
.

How large is the closure of
\iota(\mathrm{S}_{0}(V))
within

\mathscr{H}_{\beta}(V_{\beta}^{\prime})
?

Remark 2.5.18
(Reflexivity).

Approaching this question, it is notable that it is interesting already for the
component mappings
\iota_{n}
. As we have noted and used before,
\iota_{1}
is
nothing else than the canonical inclusion of
V
into its strong bidual

(V^{\prime}_{\beta})^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Surjectivity of
\iota_{1}
for barrelled space
V
thus
corresponds to
reflexivity
of
V
, as we have already proved the continuity
of
\iota_{1}
at least for barrelled
V
in Theorem
2.5.9
,

iii.)
. As such, the continuity of
\iota_{1}
is well-known
and can be found in
[
166
, 5.5 & 5.6]
.

Combining reflexivity with our considerations on duality from Remark
2.5.16

gives rise to a remarkable class of examples.
55
55

55

I would like to thank Stefan
Waldmann for pointing this out.

Example 2.5.19
(Reflexive nuclear Fréchet spaces).

Let
F
be a nuclear reflexive Fréchet space such as:

i.)

The algebra of smooth functions
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)
defined on some
open subset
\emptyset\neq\Omega\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
with values in
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, see

[
131
, Example 28.9 (1)]
and
[
181
, Prop 36.10]
.

ii.)

The Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})
of complex valued rapidly
decreasing smooth functions on
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
, which we will discuss in the context of
smooth vectors in Example
3.6.17
, see

[
181
, Prop 36.10 & Thm. 51.5]
.

iii.)

The algebra of holomorphic functions
\mathscr{H}(\Omega)
defined on
some complex nonempty domain
\Omega\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
with values in

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, see

[
131
, Example 28.9 (4)]
and
[
181
, Corollary of
Prop. 36.9 or Prop. 36.10]
, both of which are applicable by
virtue of Montel’s Theorem.

Consider
V\coloneqq F^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Then, by reflexivity,
V^{\prime}=(F^{\prime}_{\beta})_{\beta}^{\prime}\cong F
is Baire as a
Fréchet space. Moreover,
V
is nuclear as the strong dual of a nuclear space by

[
181
, Prop. 50.6]
, and bornological as the strong dual of a
reflexive Fréchet space. Finally,
F
is Montel as a nuclear Fréchet space by

[
181
, Prop. 50.2]
and thus its strong dual
F^{\prime}
is also Montel by

[
181
, Prop. 36.10]
, which in particular
implies that
F^{\prime}
is barrelled itself by
[
181
, Cor. of Prop 36.9 &
Prop. 36.4]
. Thus all assumptions of
Theorem
2.5.9
and
Theorem
2.5.12
are fulfilled.

Going to higher orders, surjectivity of

\hat{\iota}_{n}\colon\widehat{\mathrm{S}}^{n}_{\pi}(V)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(V)

(2.5.91)

may then be seen as a natural definition of
n
-reflexivity. This immediately
opens several avenues for further research such as:

Question 2.5.20
.

Does
n
-reflexivity imply
k
-reflexivity for all smaller (greater)
k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
?

Question 2.5.21
.

Does
n
-reflexivity for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
imply surjectivity of
\hat{\iota}
?

Question 2.5.22
.

Does surjectivity of
\hat{\iota}
imply
n
-reflexivity?

As we have seen in the abstract setting in Section
2.3

and as
we will again see in Section
2.6
, Taylor series
need not converge in the
\beta
-topology. Thus the relationship between

\hat{\iota}
and its components (
2.5.91
) not clear: the
polynomials in some approximating net may change completely in every step. One
should think of this like the distinction between having a
convergent power
series
and
approximation by polynomials
. Already on compact subsets of the
real line
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
, the former implies holomorphy, the latter only continuity.

Remark 2.5.23
(Positive
R
and Gelfand-Shilov).

In
[
186
, Sec. 5.4]
the particular case of finite dimensional
V

was investigated. Similarly to what we have done for
\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(V)
in
Theorem
2.5.12
, one may identify
\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(V)
with the
space of entire functions of finite order
1/R
and minimal type for any
R\geq 0
. The order constitutes a growth condition on the Taylor coefficients, which is
most apparent in (
2.5.37
), and may be rephrased as a growth
condition
of the full function by the methods we have employed in
Proposition
2.4.10
.

2.6
A Collection of Examples

Ook!

The Librarian

It is instructive to study how one can understand (
2.5.27
) in examples, where
the continuous dual
V^{\prime}_{\beta}
may be described explicitly. Of particular interest are
the questions of surjectivity of
\hat{\iota}_{n}
and
\hat{\iota}
itself. We begin
with a sanity check.

Example 2.6.1
(Finite dimensions).

Let
V=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
. Then every
n
-linear mapping on the dual space
V^{\prime}\cong V
is
continuous. Consequently,
\mathrm{Pol}^{n}(V^{\prime})\cong\ch@airxmathbbm{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
with

\iota_{n}\bigl(e_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee e_{j_{n}}\bigr)=z_{j_{1}}\cdots z_{j_{n}}.

(2.6.1)

Moreover, the
\beta
-topology reduces to the topology of locally uniform
convergence. Thus the subspace
\iota(\mathrm{S}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n})\subseteq\mathscr{H}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n})
is
dense by virtue of Taylor expansions.

As our next examples will show, approximation by Taylor series does not always work.

Example 2.6.2
(Non reflexive sequence spaces).

We consider the Banach space of zero sequences

??\coloneqq\bigl\{\gamma\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{N}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\colon\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\gamma_{n}=0\bigr\}

(2.6.2)

endowed with the supremum norm
??\coloneqq\sup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\lvert\gamma_{n}\rvert
. By
[
192
, Satz II.2.3]
, we have

c_{0}^{\prime}\cong??\coloneqq\biggl\{\gamma\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{N}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\colon??\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\big\lvert\gamma_{n}\big\rvert<\infty\biggr\}

(2.6.3)

and

(\ell^{1})^{\prime}\cong??\coloneqq\bigl\{\gamma\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{N}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\colon\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\infty}<\infty\bigr\}

(2.6.4)

as Banach spaces by means of the natural pairings

\langle\gamma,s\rangle\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{n}\cdot s_{n}

(2.6.5)

with
\gamma\in c_{0}
resp.
\gamma\in\ell^{\infty}
and
s\in\ell^{1}
. In
particular,
c_{0}
is not reflexive, as the canonical mapping

\iota_{1}\colon c_{0}\longrightarrow\ell^{\infty}
is simply given by the set
inclusion
c_{0}\subseteq\ell^{\infty}
. Consequently,
\iota_{1}
is not surjective.

Investigating the second order, we claim that the completed projective tensor
square

c_{0}??c_{0}

(2.6.6)

may be viewed as a dense, but proper, subspace
of the space
c_{0}(c_{0})
of zero sequences with entries in
c_{0}
. An element

\Lambda\in c_{0}(c_{0})
is a sequence
(\Lambda_{n})_{n}
of zero sequences, which
converges uniformly to the zero sequence. The latter translates to the condition

0=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lVert\Lambda_{n}\rVert_{\infty}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\big\lvert\Lambda_{n}(m)\big\rvert,

(2.6.7)

whereas the former implies

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda_{n}(m)=0\qquad\textrm{for all }n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}.

(2.6.8)

In particular, both limits commute and one may visualize
\Lambda
as the infinite
matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{1}(1)&\Lambda_{1}(2)&\ldots&0\\
\Lambda_{2}(1)&\Lambda_{2}(2)&\ldots&0\\
\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots\\
0&0&\ldots&0\end{pmatrix}

(2.6.9)

such that any path ending on the right or bottom edge corresponds to a zero
sequence. This also means that, a posteriori, both indices exhibit the same
behaviour and thus we may view
\Lambda
as a doubly indexed sequence without
losing information. In particular, the norm of
c_{0}(c_{0})
is by slight abuse of
notation simply given by

\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\lVert\Lambda_{n}\rVert_{\infty}=\sup_{n,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\big\lvert\Lambda_{n,m}\big\rvert.

(2.6.10)

We consider the injective linear mapping

\chi\colon c_{0}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}c_{0}\longrightarrow c_{0}(c_{0}),\quad\chi(\gamma\mathbin{\otimes}\eta)\Big|_{(n,m)}\coloneqq\gamma(n)\cdot\eta(m).

(2.6.11)

Then
\chi(\gamma\mathbin{\otimes}\eta)
fulfils both (
2.6.7
)
and (
2.6.8
), as writing
\Lambda\coloneqq\chi(\gamma\mathbin{\otimes}\eta)
we get

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lVert\Lambda_{n}\rVert_{\infty}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\lvert\Lambda_{n}(m)\rvert=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\big\lvert\gamma_{n}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lvert\eta_{m}\big\rvert=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\big\lvert\gamma_{n}\big\rvert\cdot\lVert\eta\rVert_{\infty}=0

(2.6.12)

and

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda_{n}(m)=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\gamma_{n}\cdot\eta_{m}=\gamma_{n}\cdot\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\eta_{m}=\gamma_{n}\cdot 0=0

(2.6.13)

for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. Thus, (
2.6.11
) is well defined. We
moreover have the continuity estimate

\big\lVert\chi(\gamma\mathbin{\otimes}\eta)\big\rVert_{\infty}=\sup_{n,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\big\lvert\gamma_{n}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lvert\eta_{m}\big\rvert=\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\infty}\cdot\lVert\eta\rVert_{\infty}

(2.6.14)

on factorizing tensors and Proposition
2.5.2
implies the
continuity of
\chi
.

Next, we check that the image of
\chi
is dense. To this end, fix
\Lambda\in c_{0}(c_{0})
and
\varepsilon>0
. By our preliminary considerations, there exists an
index
N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
such that
\lvert\Lambda_{n,m}\rvert\leq\varepsilon
for all
n,m\geq N
.
Using the standard unit vectors
\mathit{e}_{n}\in c_{0}
with entries

(\mathit{e}_{n})(m)\coloneqq\delta_{n,m}
we moreover note

\chi\bigl(\mathit{e}_{n}\mathbin{\otimes}\Lambda_{n,\,\cdot\,}\bigr)\Big|_{(k,\ell)}=\delta_{n,k}\cdot\Lambda_{n,\ell}

(2.6.15)

for all
n,k,\ell\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. In terms of infinite matrices, one may visualize

\chi(\mathit{e}_{n}\mathbin{\otimes}\Lambda_{n,\,\cdot\,})
as the
n
-th row of
\Lambda

with zeros otherwise. Consequently,

\Gamma\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\chi\bigl(\mathit{e}_{n}\mathbin{\otimes}\Lambda_{n,\,\cdot\,}\bigr)=\begin{pNiceArray}

(2.6.16)

fulfils
\lVert\Lambda-\Gamma\rVert_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
by choice of
N
.
Hence, the image of (
2.6.11
) is dense.

Recall now the explicit description of the completion
c_{0}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}c_{0}
as it
can be found in
[
181
, Thm. 45.1]
. That is, for every
\Gamma\in c_{0}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}_{\pi}c_{0}
there are two sequences
\gamma\in\ell^{1}(c_{0})
and
\eta\in\ell^{\infty}(c_{0})

such that

\Gamma=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\eta_{k}.

(2.6.17)

This has two consequences: Firstly, the continuous extension of
\chi
is to
c_{0}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}_{\pi}c_{0}
is still injective. Secondly, the series

\sum_{m,n=1}^{\infty}\chi\bigl(\mathit{e}_{m}\mathbin{\otimes}\Lambda_{n,\,\cdot\,}\bigr)

(2.6.18)

is
not
convergent in
c_{0}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}_{\pi}c_{0}
unless

\Lambda_{n,\,\cdot\,}
is actually absolutely summable. Indeed, replacing zero
sequences with absolutely summable sequences throughout, i.e. dualizing, proves
the well-known identities

\ell^{1}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}_{\pi}\ell^{1}\cong\ell^{1}(\ell^{1})\cong\ell^{1}(\ch@airxmathbbm{N}^{2})\cong\ell^{1}.

(2.6.19)

We will meet some of these identifications from a rather different point of view
and in higher detail in Lemma
3.2.15
and refer to

[
181
, Ch. 46]
and
[
98
, Sec. 15.7]
for a discussion
of completed projective tensor products with
\mathrm{L}^{1}
-spaces associated to some
measure.
Our discussion may then be recovered by considering the particular case of
the counting measure.

Moreover, (
2.6.19
)
gives an abstract reason for
\chi(c_{0}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}_{\pi}c_{0})\subsetneq c_{0}(c_{0})
: strong duals essentially turn injective into projective
tensor products by
[
181
, Ch. 49 & 50]
or
[
108
, §45.3]

and infinite dimensional normed spaces are never nuclear, see again
Remark
2.5.3
and
[
181
, Ch. 50, Cor. 2]
.
Either way, using our identifications,
\iota_{2}
takes the form

\iota_{2}(a)\Big|_{\gamma}=\sum_{n,m=1}^{\infty}\gamma(n)\Lambda(n,m)\gamma(m)\qquad\textrm{for all }\Lambda\in c_{0}(c_{0}),\;\gamma\in\ell^{1}.

(2.6.20)

Once again, this series still converges for
a\in\ell^{\infty}
, so
\iota_{2}
is
also not surjective. As noted before, this does not imply that the full mapping
\iota

is not surjective, as one might be able to approximate by mixing degrees. It is clear
that these considerations generalize to higher degrees without complications.

Example 2.6.3
(Continuous Functions).

Let
V=\mathscr{C}([0,1])
equipped with the norm topology induced by the
supremum norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\infty}
. By the Riesz-Markov Theorem, every
functional
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
is given by a unique regular complex Radon
measure
\mu_{\varphi}
on
[0,1]
, i.e.

\varphi(f)=\int_{[0,1]}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}\qquad\textrm{for all }f\in\mathscr{C}([0,1]).

(2.6.21)

Moreover, it is a straightforward consequence of the Stone-Weierstraß Theorem, as
it can e.g. be found in
[
163
, 5.7, p. 122]
, that

\mathscr{C}\bigl([0,1]\bigr)\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}\mathscr{C}\bigl([0,1]\bigr)\cong\mathscr{C}\bigl([0,1]^{2}\bigr)

(2.6.22)

is just the space of continuous functions on the unit square, again equipped with the
norm topology induced by the corresponding supremum norm: Indeed, the set

\mathscr{A}\coloneqq\biggl\{\Bigl((x,y)\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{N}f_{k}(x)\cdot g_{k}(y)\Bigr)\colon N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N},\;f_{k},g_{k}\in\mathscr{C}([0,1])\biggr\}

(2.6.23)

of finite sums of factorizing functions constitutes a point separating

∗
-subalgebra of the continuous functions on the unit square

\mathscr{C}([0,1]\times[0,1])
. Given a mapping
f\in\mathscr{C}([0,1]^{2})
with
f(x,y)=f(y,x)
for all
x,y\in[0,1]
, it is
thus reasonable to consider the function
\iota(f)
. Taking another look at
(
2.6.21
) suggests that

\iota(f)\Big|_{\varphi}=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi}\times\mu_{\varphi}),\qquad\varphi\in V^{\prime}.

(2.6.24)

Here,
\mu_{\varphi}\times\mu_{\varphi}
denotes the product measure of

\mu_{\varphi}
with itself. Note that (
2.6.24
) makes sense
regardless of the symmetry properties of our function
f
, so we ignore them in the
sequel. If
f
is factorizing, i.e.
f(x,y)=\sum_{k}g_{k}(x)h_{k}(y)
for all points
x,y\in[0,1]
and suitable functions
g_{k},h_{k}\in\mathscr{C}([0,1])
, then
(
2.6.24
) reproduces the expected result
\sum_{k}\varphi(g_{k})\varphi(h_{k})
by Fubini’s Theorem. By density of such factorizing functions and the
continuity of (
2.6.24
) as a linear functional on

\mathscr{C}([0,1]^{2})
, this is thus the correct description also for non-factorizing
elements. In particular, all of our continuity estimates carry over unharmed.

Notably, one may conversely consider
(
2.6.24
) as a linear functional
\Xi
on
\mathscr{C}([0,1]^{2})
by
fixing functionals
\varphi,\xi\in\mathscr{C}([0,1])^{\prime}
and setting

\Xi(f)\coloneqq\int_{[0,1]^{2}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi}\times\mu_{\xi})\qquad\textrm{for all }f\in\mathscr{C}([0,1]^{2}).

(2.6.25)

This is, of course, just the duality of
V
and
V^{\prime}
in action. In particular,
\Xi

extends to the algebraic bidual
V^{**}
. Finally, this also showcases the manner in
which the continuous dual of the projective tensor product
V\otimes_{\pi}V
is
isomorphic to the space of quadratic polynomials on
V
, see

[
50
, Sec. 1.2]
for a comprehensive abstract discussion.

Example 2.6.4
(Sequence Spaces).

This is motivated by
[
50
, Example 1.27]
. Throughout, we equip the
complex numbers
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
with their unique Hausdorff locally convex topology.
That is to say, any seminorm on
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is continuous. Let
J
be an
index set and

V\coloneqq??\coloneqq\prod_{j\in J}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.6.26)

be endowed with the product topology. We
conjecture that the strong dual is given by

\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\bigr)^{\prime}_{\beta}\cong??\coloneqq\bigoplus_{j\in J}\ch@airxmathbbm{C},

(2.6.27)

where the finite sequences
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}
carry the locally convex direct sum
topology. Summing over
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, this results in the finest locally convex topology
on
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}
, i.e. also here any seminorm is continuous.

To prove (
2.6.27
) for countable
56
56

56

We stick
to a general index set
J
until we actually use the countability, namely for
the continuity of the inverse mapping.
index sets
J
, we consider the linear
mapping

\Phi\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}\longrightarrow\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\bigr)^{\prime}_{\beta},\quad\Phi(\gamma)\Big|_{c}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in J}\gamma_{j}\cdot c_{j}.

(2.6.28)

As
\gamma_{j}=0
for almost all
j\in J
, any choice of linear ordering on
J

results in the same
\Phi
, so we suppress this choice in the sequel. The map

\Phi
is clearly injective. Let
\varphi\in(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J})^{\prime}_{\beta}
be given and define

\gamma_{j}\coloneqq\varphi(\mathit{e}_{j})
, where
(\mathit{e}_{j})_{k}=\delta_{j,k}
for
j,k\in J
. All
but finitely many of the coefficients
\gamma_{j}
vanish: Consider the sequence
c\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}
with

c_{j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}1/\gamma_{j}&\;\textrm{if}\;\gamma_{j}\neq 0,\\
0&\;\textrm{if}\;\gamma_{j}=0,\end{cases}

for all
j\in J
and let
J_{0}\coloneqq\{j\in J\colon c_{j}=0\}
. Note
moreover that, ordering
the finite subsets
F
of
J
by inclusion, the net limit

\lim_{F\subseteq J}\sum_{j\in F}c_{j}\mathit{e}_{j}\eqqcolon\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}\mathit{e}_{j}

(2.6.29)

exists and reproduces
c
. Thus, by continuity and linearity of
\varphi
, we have

\infty>\varphi(c)=\sum_{j\in J}\varphi(c_{j}\mathit{e}_{j})=\sum_{j\in J}c_{j}\varphi(\mathit{e}_{j})=\sum_{j\in J\setminus J_{0}}1.

Hence
J\setminus J_{0}
is indeed finite, i.e.
\gamma\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}
. An analogous computation yields
\Phi(\gamma)=\varphi
at once. That is, the mapping
\Phi
is bijective, and we have constructed the inverse explicitly. By the characteristic property of the final topology, it suffices to check the continuity of the mappings

\phi_{j}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\longrightarrow\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\bigr)^{\prime}_{\beta},\quad\phi_{j}(\lambda)\big|_{c}\coloneqq\lambda\cdot c_{j}

for all
j\in J
to infer the continuity of
\Phi
. But this is trivial, as any seminorm on

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is continuous.

We turn towards the continuity of the inverse mapping
\Phi^{-1}
. To facilitate
our proof we make the additional assumption that the index set
J
is
countably infinite. That is, we
assume
J=\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. Let
\mathrm{q}=\sum_{j\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\mathrm{q}_{j}
be a
formal sum of seminorms
\mathrm{q}_{j}
on
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. By continuity, there are
scalars
\lambda_{j}\geq 0
such that
\mathrm{q}_{j}(\lambda)\leq\lambda_{j}\lvert\lambda\rvert
for all
\lambda\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
and
j\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. Recall that the seminorms

\lvert\delta_{j}\rvert
– with the usual Dirac ??functional
\delta_{j}(c)\coloneqq c_{j}
–
form a defining system of seminorms for
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}
. Hence, the set

B\coloneqq\bigl\{2^{j}\cdot\lambda_{j}\cdot\mathit{e}_{j}\colon j\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}\bigr\}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{N}

is bounded.
57
57

57

This is where the countability of the index set
J
is
crucial.
Putting both
observations together yields the estimate

\mathrm{q}\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(\varphi)\bigr)=\sum_{j\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\mathrm{q}_{j}\bigl(\varphi(\mathit{e}_{j})\bigr)\leq\sum_{j\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\lambda_{j}\big\lvert\varphi(\mathit{e}_{j})\big\rvert\leq\sum_{j\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}2^{-j}\cdot\big\lvert\varphi(2^{j}\cdot\lambda_{j}\cdot\mathit{e}_{j})\big\rvert\leq\mathrm{p}_{B}(\varphi)

(2.6.30)

for all
\varphi\in\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\bigr)^{\prime}_{\beta}
. As formal sums of seminorms are a
defining system for the direct sum topology, this finally proves
(
2.6.27
), albeit only for
J=\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
.

Next, we show that

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\cong\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J\times J}.

(2.6.31)

To this end, we consider the linear extension of the mapping

\Psi\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\otimes_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J\times J},\quad\Psi(c\mathbin{\otimes}d)_{jk}\coloneqq c_{j}\cdot d_{k}

(2.6.32)

and first prove that it is continuous with dense image. By the characteristic property of the product topology, the continuity of the linear mappings
\psi_{jk}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\otimes_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
,

\psi_{jk}(c\mathbin{\otimes}d)\coloneqq\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{pr}}_{jk}\circ\Psi\bigr)(c\mathbin{\otimes}d)=c_{j}\cdot d_{k}=\bigl(\delta_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}\delta_{k}\bigr)(c\mathbin{\otimes}d)

for all
j,k\in J
implies continuity of
\Psi
. Here we have used that tensor
product of continuous linear mappings are themselves continuous on the projective
tensor product. This general fact is readily proved by combining
Proposition
2.5.2
with (
2.5.3
).

To see the density of the image, we shift our perspective, disregard the involved
topologies for a moment and consider
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J\times J}
as the continuous
functions
\mathscr{C}(J\times J)
, where we endow the index set
J\times J
with the discrete topology. Notice now that the image
\Psi(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\otimes_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J})
is a unital
∗
-subalgebra of
\mathscr{C}(J\times J)
. By
the Stone-Weierstraß Theorem, it is thus dense in
\mathscr{C}(J\times J)
with respect to the topology of convergence on compact subsets
of
J\times J
. That is, by discreteness, the topology of pointwise convergence.
Passing back to
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J\times J}
, this is exactly the product topology. Hence
our map
\Psi
indeed has dense image.

Alternatively and more explicitly, given
a\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J\times J}
one may
consider the sequences of one index given by
(a_{j\bullet})_{k}\coloneqq a_{jk}

for all
k\in J
. Notice that, in
analogy with (
2.6.15
), we have

\biggl(\sum_{j\in J}\Psi(\mathit{e}_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}a_{j\bullet})\biggr)_{mn}=\sum_{j\in J}\delta_{j,m}\cdot a_{jn}=a_{mn}\qquad\textrm{for all }m,n\in J.

Making once again use of the silently agreed on linear ordering
\succeq
of
J
,
we may view the series
\sum_{j\in J}\mathit{e}_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}a_{j\bullet}
as a net in
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}
. Notice now that,
fixing
j\in J
, the seminorm

\bigl(\lvert\delta_{n}\rvert\mathbin{\otimes}\lvert\delta_{m}\rvert\bigr)(\mathit{e}_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}a_{j\bullet})=\delta_{n,j}\cdot\lvert a_{jm}\rvert

simply vanishes for
j\succ n
. Thus, taking
k\succ\ell\succ n\in J
,

\bigl(\lvert\delta_{n}\rvert\mathbin{\otimes}\lvert\delta_{m}\rvert\bigr)\biggl(\sum_{k\succeq j\succeq\ell}\mathit{e}_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}a_{j\bullet}\biggr)\leq\sum_{k\succeq j\succeq\ell}\delta_{n,j}\cdot\lvert a_{jm}\rvert=0,

i.e.
\sum_{k\succeq j\succeq\ell}\mathit{e}_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}a_{j\bullet}
is
Cauchy in

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\otimes_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}
and thus defines an element of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}
.

We extend
\Psi
to
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}
by continuity
and
denote the resulting map again by
\Psi
. It remains to establish continuity of the
inverse mapping. Using the concrete description of open subsets of the product
topology, one may prove openness of
\Psi
directly. This, however, disregards most
of the linear structure, making the argument quite cumbersome. Having constructed
the inverse, we may instead show continuity by direct estimation. Indeed, this boils
down to the familiar

\bigl(\lvert\delta_{n}\rvert\mathbin{\otimes}\lvert\delta_{m}\rvert\bigr)\biggl(\sum_{j\in J}\mathit{e}_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}a_{j\bullet}\biggr)=\sum_{j\in J}\delta_{j,n}\cdot\lvert a_{jm}\rvert=\lvert a_{nm}\rvert=\big\lvert\delta_{n,m}\big\rvert(a)

for all
a\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J\times J}
and
n,m\in J
.

Having established these preliminaries, guessing the behaviour of our map

\iota
is straightforward: Let
a\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J\times J}
. Suppressing

\Psi
, we claim that

\iota(a)\Big|_{\gamma}=\sum_{j,k\in J}\gamma_{j}\cdot a_{jk}\cdot\gamma_{k}\qquad\textrm{for all }\gamma\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}

(2.6.33)

is just the obvious quadratic form. Indeed, using once again our various constructions yields

\iota(a)\Big|_{\gamma}=\sum_{j\in J}\iota(\mathit{e}_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}a_{j\bullet})\Big|_{\gamma}=\sum_{j\in J}\sum_{\ell\in J}\gamma_{j}\delta_{j,\ell}\sum_{k\in J}\gamma_{k}a_{jk}=\sum_{j,k\in J}\gamma_{j}a_{jk}\gamma_{k}.

Notably, the bilinear mapping
L_{a}(\gamma,\delta)\coloneqq\sum_{j,k\in J}\gamma_{j}a_{jk}\delta_{k}
, which induces the quadratic polynomial
\iota(a)
,
remains well defined if only one argument is a finite sequence. By induction, our
discussion extends to arbitrary finite tensor powers.

Let now
a=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}\in\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{0}^{\bullet}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J})
with

a^{(n)}\in\widehat{\mathrm{S}}^{n}_{\pi}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J})
. The symmetry condition simply means that the

\mathrm{S}_{n}
-action
(\sigma\mathbin{\triangleright}a^{(n)})_{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}=a^{(n)}_{j_{\sigma(1)}\cdots j_{\sigma(n)}}
is trivial for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. By what we have shown,

\iota(a)\Big|_{\gamma}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in J}a_{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}^{(n)}\cdot\gamma_{j_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{j_{n}}\qquad\textrm{for all }\gamma\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)},

(2.6.34)

which already very much looks like a Taylor series. In this language, the vertical
summability condition in
\mathrm{S}_{0}^{\bullet}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J})
takes the form

\lvert\delta_{j}\rvert_{0,c}(a)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c^{n}\lvert\delta_{j}\rvert^{n}\bigl(a^{(n)}\bigr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c^{n}\big\lvert a^{(n)}_{j\cdots j}\big\rvert<\infty

(2.6.35)

for all
c\geq 0
and
j\in J
. That is to say, the functions
z\mapsto\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z^{n}a^{(n)}_{j\cdots j}
of one complex variable have to be entire for
every
j\in J
. As the absolute values of the Dirac functionals are a defining system
of seminorms for
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{J}
, this indeed suffices. As there are continuous
seminorms beyond those, there is actually more structure. Fixing finitely many
indices
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in J
, we consider
\gamma\coloneqq z_{1}\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}+\cdots+z_{n}\mathit{e}_{j_{n}}
with
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. Then (
2.6.34
) gives the convergent
series

\iota(a)\Big|_{\gamma}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m}=1}^{n}a_{j_{\ell_{1}}\cdots j_{\ell_{m}}}^{(m)}z_{\ell_{1}}\cdots z_{\ell_{m}}.

(2.6.36)

That is, restrictions of
\iota(a)
to finite dimensional subspaces are entire functions
in the usual sense. That is exactly the notion of Gâteaux holomorphy.
By Theorem
2.5.9
,

iv.)
we know that
\iota(a)
is bounded on bounded sets
B\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}
. We claim that any such set is contained in some finite
dimensional subspace and bounded there. The boundedness is clear by continuity of

\lvert\delta_{j}\rvert
for every
j\in J
. We prove the contraposition of the first statement.
To this end, let
S\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}
generate an infinite dimensional
subspace. Then we find a countably infinite subset
\widetilde{J}\subseteq J
with
corresponding linearly independent
\gamma_{j}\in S
for every
j\in\widetilde{J}
. We
complete this linearly independent set to some linear algebraic basis of

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}
and write
\widetilde{\delta}_{j}
for the corresponding
coefficient functionals.
Finally, choose some enumeration of
\widetilde{J}=\{j_{n}\;|\;n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}\}
and
consider the formal sum of seminorms

\mathrm{p}(\gamma)\coloneqq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}j_{n}\big\lvert\widetilde{\delta}_{j_{n}}(\gamma_{j_{n}})\big\rvert\qquad\textrm{for all }\gamma\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{(J)}.

By construction,
\mathrm{p}(\gamma_{j_{n}})=n
, i.e.
\mathrm{p}
is
unbounded on
S
. Hence
S
is not bounded. Consequently, having
(
2.6.36
) recovers the boundedness of
\iota(a)
on
bounded sets at once, as holomorphic functions on finite dimensional spaces are
always continuous. Thus the example is consistent with our claims in
Theorem
2.5.9
.

2.7
Hilbert Tensor Product

Time is a drug.

Too much of it kills you.

Small Gods
– Terry
Pratchett

We conclude the chapter by developing a Hilbert
58
58

58

We all know David
Hilbert (1862-1943) and no finite footnote could ever do him justice.
space version of the
considerations from Section
2.5
, replacing projective and
injective tensor products with the Hilbert space tensor product, as it is
discussed in
[
99
, Ch. 11]
. In this setting, many of
the technical problems we have faced so far turn out to be simply absent.

Recall that given Hilbert spaces
(H_{1},\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{1})
and

(H_{2},\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{2})
we may endow their tensor product

H_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}H_{2}
with the structure of a Pre-Hilbert space by
sesquilinearly
59
59

59

We follow the physicist’s convention of constituting
anti-linearity of ??in the
first
argument.
extending

\langle v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}v_{2},w_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}w_{2}\rangle\coloneqq\langle v_{1},w_{1}\rangle_{1}\cdot\langle v_{2},w_{2}\rangle_{2},

(2.7.1)

where
v_{1},w_{1}\in H_{1}
and
v_{2},w_{2}\in H_{2}
. The resulting space is not complete,
whenever both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are infinite dimensional. In the sequel, we write

H_{1}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}H_{2}
for the completion of the algebraic tensor product
H_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}H_{2}
with respect to the norm ??induced by (
2.7.1
).

Lemma 2.7.1
.

Let
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
be Hilbert spaces. The linear extension of

\Xi\colon H_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}H_{2}\longrightarrow\overline{\operatorname{HS}}(H_{1},H_{2}),\quad\Xi(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}v_{2})v\coloneqq\langle v,v_{1}\rangle_{1}\cdot v_{2}

(2.7.2)

is an isometry with dense image, where
\overline{??}(H_{1},H_{2})

denotes the space of antilinear Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
. In
particular,

H_{1}??H_{2}\cong\overline{\operatorname{HS}}(H_{1},H_{2}).

(2.7.3)

Proof
:

Let
v_{1}\in H_{1}
and
v_{2}\in H_{2}
. The operator
\Xi(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}v_{2})
is
linear with finite rank, so
\Xi
is well defined. Let
\{e_{j}\}_{j\in J_{1}}\subseteq H_{1}
be
a Hilbert basis. Invoking Parseval’s identity and (
2.7.1
), we get

\big\lVert\Xi(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}v_{2})\big\rVert_{\operatorname{HS}}^{2}=\sum_{j\in J_{1}}\big\lVert\Xi(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}v_{2})e_{j}\big\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j\in J_{1}}\big\lvert\langle e_{j},v_{1}\rangle_{1}\big\rvert^{2}\cdot\lVert v_{2}\rVert^{2}=\lVert v_{1}\rVert^{2}_{1}\cdot\lVert v_{2}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}v_{2}\rVert^{2}

for any
v_{1}\in H_{1}
and
v_{2}\in H_{2}
.
Thus, our mapping
\Xi
is indeed isometric. Moreover, given a Hilbert basis

\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J_{2}}\subseteq H_{2}
, we claim that the operators
\Xi(e_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}f_{k})
with
j\in J_{1}
and
k\in J_{2}
constitute a Hilbert basis of
\overline{\operatorname{HS}}(H_{1},H_{2})
: Indeed,
if
T\in\overline{\operatorname{HS}}(H_{1},H_{2})
fulfils

0=\big\langle\Xi(e_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}f_{k}),T\big\rangle_{\operatorname{HS}}=\sum_{\ell\in J_{1}}\big\langle\Xi(e_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}f_{k})e_{\ell},Te_{\ell}\big\rangle_{2}=\sum_{\ell\in J_{1}}\langle e_{\ell},e_{j}\rangle_{1}\cdot\big\langle f_{k},Te_{\ell}\big\rangle_{2}=\big\langle f_{k},Te_{j}\big\rangle_{2}

(2.7.4)

for all
j\in J_{1}
and
k\in J_{2}
, then by completeness of
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J_{2}}
it
follows
that
Te_{j}=0
for all
j\in J_{1}
. That is,
T=0
. Hence,
\Xi
does indeed
have dense image. Finally, we note

\big\langle e_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}f_{k},e_{\ell}\mathbin{\otimes}f_{m}\big\rangle=\langle e_{j},e_{\ell}\rangle\cdot\langle f_{k},f_{m}\rangle=\delta_{j,\ell}\cdot\delta_{k,m}\qquad\textrm{for $j,\ell\in J_{1}$ and $k,m\in J_{2}$},

(2.7.5)

proving also the orthonormality by virtue of our first computation.

\ch@irxboxempty

As a by-product, we have proved the following Hilbert basis description of the Hilbert
tensor product, which in particular demystifies the structure of the completion.

Corollary 2.7.2
.

Let
\{e_{j}\}_{j\in J_{1}}\subseteq H_{1}
and
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J_{2}}\subseteq H_{2}
be
Hilbert bases of Hilbert spaces
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. Then the set

\big\{e_{j}\mathbin{\otimes}f_{k}\colon j\in J_{1},k\in J_{2}\big\}

(2.7.6)

constitutes a Hilbert basis of
H_{1}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}H_{2}
.

Reducing the Hilbert spaces
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
to the index sets
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
of their
Hilbert bases, their completed Hilbert tensor product
H_{1}\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}H_{2}
thus
corresponds to the Cartesian product
J_{1}\times J_{2}
of the index sets. In other words,

\ell^{2}(J_{1})\;\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}\;\ell^{2}(J_{2})\cong\ell^{2}(J_{1}\times J_{2})

(2.7.7)

as Hilbert spaces. In particular, tensorizing with yet another Hilbert space
H_{3}
yields

(H_{1}\;\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}\;H_{2})\;\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}\;H_{3}\cong H_{1}\;\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}\;(H_{2}\;\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}\;H_{3})

canonically via the usual identification. Here, continuity is trivially fulfilled, as both ways
of bracketing yield the same norm by (
2.7.1
). In the sequel, we are
interested in
n
-th tensor powers of a single Hilbert space
H
, that is the case
H_{1}=\ldots=H_{n}=H
. By what we have shown and the uniqueness of vector space
completions, any order of tensorizing results, up to unique isometric
isomorphism, in the same Hilbert space

??(H)\coloneqq\underbrace{H\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}\cdots\widehat{\mathbin{\otimes}}H}_{n\;\text{times}}.

(2.7.8)

By (
2.7.1
), its norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
on factorizing tensors is given by

\lVert v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}\rVert=\lVert v_{1}\rVert\cdots\lVert v_{n}\rVert\qquad\textrm{for }v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in H.

(2.7.9)

Moreover, we set
\mathrm{T}^{0}(H)\coloneqq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
endowed with the Euclidean scalar
product. Unwrapping the definition of a Hilbert basis yields the following coordinate
based description of the completion
\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)
of
\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)
by
means of square summable sequences ??.

Corollary 2.7.3
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space with Hilbert basis
\{e_{j}\colon j\in J\}
and
v\in\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)
. Then there exist unique coefficients
(v^{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}})\in\ell^{2}(J^{n})
such that

v=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in J}v^{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}\cdot e_{j_{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}e_{j_{n}}.

(2.7.10)

The series converges absolutely, at most countably many
\alpha^{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}

are nonzero, and they are explicitly given by

v^{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}=\langle e_{j_{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}e_{j_{n}},v\rangle.

(2.7.11)

Conversely, every sequence
\alpha\in\ell^{2}(J^{n})
defines an element
v\in\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)
by (
2.7.10
) and

\lVert v\rVert_{n}^{2}=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in J}\big\lvert v^{j_{1}\cdots j_{n}}\big\rvert^{2}.

(2.7.12)

As for locally convex spaces, an element
v\in\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)
is called symmetric if
it is invariant under the the symmetrization operator

??\colon\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}^{n}(H),\quad\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n})\coloneqq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}v_{\sigma(1)}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{\sigma(n)},

(2.7.13)

which defines subspaces
\mathrm{S}^{n}(H)\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(\mathrm{T}^{n}(H))\subseteq\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
and the corresponding graded vector space

\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(H)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{S}^{n}(H),

(2.7.14)

where we once again set
\mathrm{S}^{0}(H)\coloneqq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. The symmetrizer turns out to be
compatible with the Hilbert space structure.

Lemma 2.7.4
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. Then the symmetrizer extends to an
orthogonal projection

\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}\colon\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathrm{S}}^{n}(H)\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H).

(2.7.15)

In particular, we may endow
\widehat{\mathrm{S}}^{n}(H)
with the structure of a Hilbert
space with scalar product given by the restriction of (
2.7.1
).

Proof
:

To facilitate explicit computations, we begin by studying
(
2.7.13
). The principal difficulty here is that
\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)
is
only pre-Hilbert. The algebraic insight that the symmetrizer
\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}
is a linear
projection is standard and shall not be repeated here. Note

\displaystyle\big\langle v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n},\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(w_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}w_{n})\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\langle v_{1},w_{\sigma(1)}\rangle\cdots\langle v_{n},w_{\sigma(n)}\rangle

(2.7.16)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\langle v_{\sigma(1)},w_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle v_{\sigma(1)},w_{n}\rangle

(2.7.17)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}),w_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}w_{n}\big\rangle

(2.7.18)

for
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in H
. Hence,
P^{*}=P
is a symmetric,
densely defined operator. It remains to assert its continuity, which then means that its
extension to
\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)
is an orthogonal projection. The
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality facilitates the continuity estimate

\displaystyle\big\lVert\mathrm{S}(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n})\big\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!^{2}}\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in S_{n}}\big\langle v_{\sigma(1)},v_{\tau(1)}\big\rangle\cdots\big\langle v_{\sigma(n)},v_{\tau(n)}\big\rangle

(2.7.19)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in S_{n}}\big\langle v_{\sigma(1)},v_{1}\big\rangle\cdots\big\langle v_{\sigma(n)},v_{n}\big\rangle

(2.7.20)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in S_{n}}\big\lvert\big\langle v_{\sigma(1)},v_{1}\big\rangle\big\rvert\cdots\big\lvert\big\langle v_{\sigma(n)},v_{n}\big\rangle\big\rvert

(2.7.21)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in S_{n}}\big\lVert v_{\sigma(1)}\big\rVert\cdot\big\lVert v_{1}\big\rVert\cdots\big\lVert v_{\sigma(n)}\big\rVert\cdot\big\lVert v_{n}\big\rVert

(2.7.22)

\displaystyle=\big\lVert v_{1}\big\rVert^{2}\cdots\big\lVert v_{n}\big\rVert^{2}

(2.7.23)

for
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in H
, completing the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Using the Riesz Representation Theorem, the Hilbert space analogue of our map
(
2.5.27
) may now be decomposed into

\iota_{n}\bigl(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}\bigr)\Big|_{w}=\langle v_{1},w\rangle\cdots\langle v_{n},w\rangle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\langle v_{j},w\rangle=\iota_{n}\bigl(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n}\bigr)\Big|_{w}

(2.7.24)

for
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},w\in H
, where we use the symmetric tensor product

v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n}\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathrm{Sym}}\bigl(v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}\bigr)

(2.7.25)

as in Section
2.5
. Note that
\iota_{n}
is
antilinear

with respect to
v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{n}
in this description. Having
established these prelimiaries, we arrive at the following Hilbertian analogue of
our Theorem
2.5.9
relating tensors to polynomials.

Theorem 2.7.5
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

i.)

Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in H
. Then the mapping

\iota(v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n})\colon H\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(2.7.26)

is a continuous
n
-homogeneous polynomial of finite type.

ii.)

The antilinear mapping
\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{n}(H)
is continuous and fulfils

\big\lvert\iota_{n}(v)w\big\rvert\leq\lVert v\rVert_{n}\cdot\lVert w\rVert^{n}\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in\mathrm{T}^{n}(H),\,w\in H.

(2.7.27)

Proof
:

Let
v\in\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)
and
w\in H
. By Corollary
2.7.3
we
have

\displaystyle\iota_{n}(v)w

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in J}\overline{{v^{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}}}\cdot\iota_{n}(e_{j_{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}e_{j_{n}})w

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in J}\overline{{v^{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}}}\cdot\langle e_{j_{1}},w\rangle\cdots\langle e_{j_{n}},w\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in J}\overline{{v^{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}}}\cdot w^{j_{1}}\cdots w^{j_{n}}

\displaystyle=\langle v,W\rangle,

where the final scalar product is taken in
\ell^{2}(J^{n})
and
W^{j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}\coloneqq w^{j_{1}}\cdots w^{j_{n}}
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality this implies

\big\lvert\iota_{n}(v)w\big\rvert\leq\lVert v\rVert_{n}\cdot\lVert W\rVert_{n}=\lVert v\rVert_{n}\cdot\lVert w\rVert^{n},

proving the continuity in
i.)
and taking a supremum
over
w\in B
for bounded subsets
B\subseteq H
yields

ii.)
at once. The statement about finite type is purely
algebraic and independent of the topology, so it follows from
Theorem
2.5.9
,
i.)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Passing to the full tensor algebra, we introduce the seminorms

\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}(H)\longrightarrow[0,\infty),\quad\mathrm{p}_{R,c}(v)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!^{R}\cdot c^{n}\cdot\lVert v_{n}\rVert_{n}

(2.7.28)

for
R,c\geq 0
, where
v_{n}\in\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)
are the homogeneous components of
v

and
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{n}
is defined as in (
2.7.12
). Fixing
R\geq 0
,
we write
\mathrm{T}_{R}^{\bullet}(H)
for the tensor algebra
\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}(H)

endowed with the locally
convex topology generated by the seminorms
\mathrm{p}_{R,c}
with
c\geq 0
.
While this is technically conflicting with already established notation, we shall
not need the projective or injective
R
-topologies for the remainder of this
section, and should thus cause a bounded amount of confusion.

Corollary 2.7.6
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
R\geq 0
. Then the mapping

\iota\colon\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}_{R}(H)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}_{\beta}(H)

(2.7.29)

and its components
\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{T}^{n}(H)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}^{n}_{\beta}(H)
are
continuous.

Proof
:

It suffices to consider the case
R=0
, as the inclusions
\mathrm{T}_{R}^{\bullet}(H)\subseteq\mathrm{T}_{S}^{\bullet}(H)
for parameters
S\leq R
are continuous. Let

v\in\mathrm{T}^{\bullet}(H)
. By (
2.7.27
), we have

\big\lvert\iota(v)w\big\rvert\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\big\lvert\iota_{n}(v)w_{n}\big\rvert\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lVert v\rVert_{n}\cdot\lVert w\rVert^{n}=\mathrm{p}_{0,\lVert w\rVert}(v)\qquad\textrm{for }w\in H.

Taking suprema over bounded subsets of
H
yields the continuity of

\iota
, which in turn implies continuity of its components at once.

\ch@irxboxempty

By continuity, we extend the mappings
\iota_{n}
and
\iota
to the completions

\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)
and
\widehat{\mathrm{T}}_{R}^{\bullet}(H)
as
\hat{\iota}_{n}

for all

n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
\hat{\iota}
, respectively. By
[
186
, Lemma 3.6,

iii.)
]
, we have

\widehat{\mathrm{T}}_{R}^{\bullet}(H)=\bigg\{v=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}\in\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)\colon\mathrm{p}_{R,c}(v)<\infty\textrm{ for all }c\geq 0\bigg\}

(2.7.30)

in analogy with Proposition
2.5.8
,
iv.)
. As
in the locally convex situation, the extended mapping
\hat{\iota}
and its components
map into the space of Fréchet
holomorphic functions.

Theorem 2.7.7
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
v\in\widehat{\mathrm{T}}_{0}^{\bullet}(H)
with
homogeneous
components
v_{n}\in\widehat{\mathrm{T}}^{n}(H)
for all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.

i.)

The mappings
\hat{\iota}_{n}(v_{n})
and
\hat{\iota}(v)
are Fréchet
holomorphic and
bounded.

ii.)

The
n
-th Taylor polynomial of
\hat{\iota}(v)
is given by

\hat{\iota}_{n}(v_{n})
.

Proof
:

As for projective tensor products, the infinite series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v_{n}

actually converges in the
\mathrm{T}_{0}
-topology, essentially by the definitions of the
seminorms (
2.7.28
). This proves the Gâteaux holomorphy
of
\hat{\iota}(v)
, the second statement and thus also the Gâteaux holomorphy of

\hat{\iota}(v)
by Corollary
2.2.7
and
Theorem
2.4.7
. As each Taylor
polynomial
\hat{\iota}_{n}(v_{n})
is continuous and thus bounded by
Theorem
2.7.5
and
Lemma
2.1.9
, this in
turn implies Fréchet holomorphy and boundedness of
\iota(v)
by
Proposition
2.4.16
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Finally, one may wonder whether an analogue of Theorem
2.5.12
and
Theorem
2.5.13
holds for symmetric Hilbert tensor products.
This turns out to be remarkably delicate. Firstly, one has to restrict to symmetric tensors
to make
\iota_{n}
and
\iota
injective. This then makes the norm more complicated, as

\big\lVert v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n}\big\rVert_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\big\langle v_{1},v_{\sigma(1)}\big\rangle\cdots\big\langle v_{n},v_{\sigma(n)}\big\rangle\qquad\textrm{for all }v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in H.

(2.7.31)

While for factorizing tensors, this provides the estimate

\big\lVert v_{1}\vee\cdots\vee v_{n}\big\rVert_{n}^{2}\leq\lVert v_{1}\rVert^{2}\cdots\lVert v_{n}\rVert^{2}

(2.7.32)

by Lemma
2.7.4
, mixed tensors are much more complicated as a
result. To see this, we consider
v\in\mathrm{S}^{n}(H)
. The polarization estimate
(
2.1.34
) yields

\sup_{w\in\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)}\big\lvert\iota_{n}(v)w\big\rvert\geq\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\sup_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)}\big\lvert\big\langle v,w_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}w_{n}\big\rangle_{n}\big\rvert.

(2.7.33)

One would now like to achieve
w_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}w_{n}=v
, as then

\big\lvert\big\langle v,w_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}w_{n}\big\rangle_{n}\big\rvert=\langle v,v\rangle_{n}=\lVert v\rVert_{n}^{2}.

(2.7.34)

However, unless
v=v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}v_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}v_{1}
for some
v_{1}\in H
,
this is not possible
60
60

60

The symmetry of
v
was essential to apply the polarization
estimate.
and thus the supremum in
(
2.7.33
) may be strictly smaller than
\lVert v\rVert
.

Example 2.7.8
.

For a
simple example, consider
H=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{2}=\operatorname{\mathrm{span}}(\mathit{e}_{1},\mathit{e}_{2})
with

v\coloneqq 2\mathit{e}_{1}\vee(\mathit{e}_{1}+\mathit{e}_{2})=2\mathit{e}_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{1}+\mathit{e}_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{2}+\mathit{e}_{2}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{1}.

(2.7.35)

Making the ansatz
w\coloneqq(\alpha\mathit{e}_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\mathit{e}_{2})\mathbin{\otimes}(\beta\mathit{e}_{1}+\sqrt{1-\beta^{2}}\mathit{e}_{2})

with
0\leq\lvert\alpha\rvert,\lvert\beta\rvert\leq 1
it is not hard to see that

\sup_{w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)^{\mathrm{cl}}}\big\lvert\langle v,w_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}w_{2}\rangle_{2}\big\rvert=1+\sqrt{2}<\sqrt{6}=\lVert v\rVert_{2},

(2.7.36)

where the maximum is attained for
\alpha=\beta=\frac{\sqrt{2+\sqrt{2}}}{2}
.

This does, of course, not rule out the existence of a universal estimate, but it seems
unlikely. We leave this as another question for posterity.

Question 2.7.9
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}\setminus\{1\}
. Is the continuous map

\iota_{n}\colon\mathrm{S}^{n}_{0}(H)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}_{\beta}^{n}(H)

(2.7.37)

an embedding?

The structure of this chapter is as follows. In Section
3.1
, we review a
classical construction of the standard ordered star product
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
for the

of a Lie group
G

3.1.46
).
Along the way, we obtain a left invariant symbol calculus for the algebra
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)

and the Gutt star product for the polynomial functions on the dual of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.
Section
3.2
is concerned with establishing and studying the notion of
entire functions, resulting in the algebras

\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G),\qquad\textrm{where $R\geq 0$},

(3.0.8)

which are modelled as the Lie theoretic analogues of the algebras
\mathscr{E}
from
(
1.2.12
) and
\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{R}^{\bullet}
, which we have discussed in
Section
2.5
. To this end, we first review the Lie-Taylor
formula in one and then multiple variables. As most of the material was already
presented in
[
90
]
, we are going to focus on the underlying
concepts and ideas instead of replicating all details. Of course, there will be precise
references
throughout. Whenever there is an interesting variant of a proof available, we present
it. Our efforts culminate in Theorem
3.2.14
, where the
continuity of
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
is established.

Sections
3.3
,

3.4
and
3.5
then
serve the overarching goal of characterizing the algebra
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
by means of
holomorphic functions on the universal complexification
G{C}
of
G
. That is to say,
we pass from the real analytic picture painted in Section
1.2

to the holomorphic one in Section
1.3
. This endeavour
requires careful study of the geometric properties of the universal complexification,
which we conduct in Section
3.3
. Everything we discuss
seems to be well known, at least as folklore. Having established the abstract theory,
we discuss a number of examples for universal complexifications in
Section
3.4
with a focus on possible
pathologies that may occur in the context of holomorphic extension. With these
preparations, we then prove Theorem
3.5.9
and
Theorem
3.5.13
, which together provide a one-to-one correspondence
between entire functions on
G
and holomorphic functions on the universal
complexification as locally convex algebras in the setting of linear Lie groups.
Afterwards, we establish a complexified version of the standard
ordered star product in Theorem
3.5.17
and prove
its continuity both by real techniques and by direct estimation within the holomorphic
world. Our key auxiliary result throughout is provided by the Lie theoretic
incarnation of the Cauchy estimates
(
3.5.55
).

In Section

, we recast these results in the language of entire and

G

continuous functions. This shift in perspective allows for another proof of

3.5.9
by means of methods from
Chapter
2
and a group theoretic incarnation of the monodromy
theorem. To put these considerations into their natural and conceptual
context, we first give an overview over the most important properties of continuous
and smooth vectors for representations on locally convex spaces in
Section
3.6
. Here, our aims beyond collecting the
hallmarks of the abstract theory are two-fold.

On the one hand, we develop practical criteria to determine the space of smooth
vectors in concrete situations. We showcase the resulting methods in
Example
3.6.17
, where we discuss the Schrödinger
representation of the Heisenberg group, which is at the heart of basic quantum
mechanics. On the other hand, we establish
differentiation of the representation of
G
to a Lie algebra representation of

\mathfrak{g}
. This then naturally leads to the converse problem of integration. That
is, the reconstruction of the group action from the Lie algebraic one.

Looking for a resolution, in turn, motivates the notions of analytic, entire and
strongly entire vectors, whose characterization is the content of
Section
3.7
. Our considerations culminate in
Corollary
3.7.15
, which gives sufficient conditions for
the resolvability of the integration problem, and in a
generalization of
[
90
, Thm. 4.23]
in the form of
Theorem
3.7.19
, which establishes the universality of the
translation representation.

3.1
Standard Ordered Quantization

The presence of those seeking the truth is infinitely to be preferred to the
presence of those who think they’ve found it.

Monstrous Regiment
–

Terry
Pratchett

In this section, we discuss a brief and concrete construction of the standard ordered
quantization map and the Gutt star product on the cotangent bundle
??G
,
which goes
back to
[
80
]

[
80

[

[

calculus, which is utilized as a quantization map in the sense of
(
1.1.3

Recall that the natural manner of differentiation on a Lie group
G
consists in left
invariant derivatives implemented by taking Lie derivatives in direction of left
invariant vector fields. Geometrically, this quantifies the rate of change of a
function along the flow of a left invariant vector field, that is to say along the image of the
Lie exponential. One may encode this as the Lie algebra morphism

??\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{1}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\bigr)\cong\Gamma^{\infty}(TG),\quad\xi\mapsto\mathscr{L}(\xi)=\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}},

(3.1.1)

where we realize tangent vector fields
??(TG)
as linear derivations of
the real-valued smooth functions
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
on
G
. Before continuing, we make
the notion of linear differential operator precise.

The inverse of (

For now, we only need that any

Gerhard

Remark 3.1.2
(Lie brackets).

Note that our considerations did not require the Lie bracket of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
whatsoever. Going back to (
3.1.1
), one may
thus reverse the logic and
define
the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}=T_{\mathrm{e}}G

by declaring the isomorphism
\mathscr{L}
to be bracket preserving. In fact, this is how
the Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}
is typically defined in the first place,
see e.g.
[
92
, Def. 9.1.7]
.

This is also a good opportunity to review the construction of the
complex

Lie algebra of a complex Lie group
G
as it can be found in

[
103
, Ch. X 6.]
. Indeed, using the complex structure, we
may define the holomorphic tangent spaces
T{C}_{g}G^{(1,0)}
as the set of

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear derivations of the space of complex valued holomorphic germs

\mathscr{H}_{g}(G)
at
g\in G
. Their disjoint union

T{C}G^{(1,0)}\coloneqq??T{C}_{g}G^{(1,0)}

(3.1.15)

then constitutes the holomorphic tangent bundle of
G
, which is involutive. Its
complex structure is obtained from the one of
G
by applying the
tangent functor.

By our definition of a complex Lie group, the left translations
\ell_{g}
from
(
3.0.1
) are holomorphic. In particular, they are
almost holomorphic, which means that

T_{\mathrm{e}}\ell_{g}\circ J_{\mathrm{e}}=J_{g}\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\ell_{g}\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in G.

(3.1.16)

Here,
J\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\operatorname{\mathrm{End}}(G))
denotes the almost complex structure induced
by the complex structure of
G
. This implies that the left invariant vector
fields fulfil

X_{\xi}=T_{\mathrm{e}}\ell_{\bullet}\xi\in\Gamma^{\infty}\bigl(T{C}G^{(1,0)}\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }\xi\in T_{\mathrm{e}}{C}G^{(1,0)}.

(3.1.17)

The involutivity of
T{C}G^{(1,0)}
now means that, building on what is known
from the real situation, the
G
-invariant vector fields form a Lie
subalgebra of
\Gamma^{\infty}(T{C}G^{(1,0)})
, which is linearly isomorphic to the

complex Lie algebra

??\coloneqq T_{\mathrm{e}}{C}G^{(1,0)}

(3.1.18)

by means of left translation. Finally, we endow
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
with a Lie
bracket by demanding that this linear isomorphism is even an isomorphism of complex
Lie algebras.

Returning to the setting of a real Lie group
G

\xi\mathbin{\otimes}\chi-\chi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi\sim[\xi,\chi]\qquad\textrm{for all }\xi,\chi\in\mathfrak{g}.

(3.1.19)

As

In fact,

\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k})=\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi_{k}}}

Here, we define the filtration of

As
\mathfrak{g}{C}

filtration induced by the grading of the tensor algebra via

with degree one elements. Note that this does not quite respect the grading, but we
nevertheless get a filtration. In the end, both constructions yield the same filtered
algebra
\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
.

The mapping (
3.1.21

The idea is now that we after applying (

over

for all

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{k}(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi_{k}}}\bigr)

(3.1.24)

\displaystyle=??(\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi_{1}}})\vee\cdots\vee\sigma_{1}(\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi_{k}}})

(3.1.25)

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k}

(3.1.26)

11

Of which Capelli proved a

We provide a proof for this for our particular situation within

of the standard ordered quantization map as
\mathscr{L}\circ\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}
. This
leads us to the following educated guess, where we additionally incorporate a
