Definition 3.1.3
(Standard ordered quantization map).

Let
G
be a Lie group with corresponding Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and
\hbar\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. The standard ordered quantization map is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&??\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{\bullet}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\bigr),\\
&\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k}\bigr)\coloneqq\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\cdot M_{\phi}\circ\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\sigma\mathbin{\triangleright}\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k}\bigr),\end{split}

(3.1.28)

where
M_{\phi}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
is the multiplication
operator with symbol
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
.

Going back to (
3.1.13
), we note the isomorphisms

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\cong\Gamma^{\infty}\bigl(\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}{C}(TG)\bigr)\cong??(T^{*}G),

(3.1.29)

where
\mathrm{Pol}(T^{*}G)
is the set of smooth maps
P\colon T^{*}G\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

that are polynomial in fiber directions. Indeed, restriction to the zero section

??\colon G\longrightarrow T^{*}G
yields
\iota^{*}P\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
and, provided
\iota^{*}P(g)\neq 0
, the map

P\bigl(g,T_{\mathrm{e}}\ell_{g}\,\cdot\,\bigr)\colon\mathfrak{g}\cong T_{\mathrm{e}}G\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(3.1.30)

is in
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})
. If there is no such
g
, we may infer
P=0
. The
inverse process is given by the pullback with the bundle projection
\pi\colon T^{*}G\longrightarrow G
and left translation. Consequently, the standard ordering
(
3.1.28
) provides a symbol calculus for the polynomials on the cotangent
bundle
T^{*}G
.

Proposition 3.1.4
(Standard ordered symbol calculus,

[
90
, Prop. 2.3]
).

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\hbar\in{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\setminus\{0\}}
. Then the
standard ordered quantization map (
3.1.28
) constitutes an
isomorphism of filtered vector spaces, i.e.
\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
is a linear bijection
such that

\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}^{m}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\bigr)=\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{m}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}.

(3.1.31)

Here, we endow
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
with the filtration
corresponding to the grading of
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
and by declaring

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}1
to be degree zero. Moreover, the same is true for its
restriction

\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\colon\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\cong\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}{C}(\mathfrak{g})\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{\bullet}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\bigr)^{G}.

(3.1.32)

Proof
:

We begin with the proof of the second statement. By (
3.1.26
), we have

\bigl(\sigma_{k}\circ\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigr)(\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k})=\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k}

(
\sim
)

for all
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}
and thus
(
3.1.32
) is injective,
matching the surjectivity of the symbol maps
\sigma_{k}
. Taking another look
at (
3.1.28
), we also see that the operator

\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k}\bigr)=\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}(\xi_{\sigma(1)})\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{L}(\xi_{\sigma(k)})

(3.1.33)

is
G
-invariant, as all of its factors
\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1}),\ldots,\mathscr{L}(\xi_{k})
are. To establish its surjectivity, let

D\in\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{k}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\bigr)^{G}\qquad\textrm{with}\quad\widetilde{\sigma}_{k}(D)\in\mathrm{S}^{k}(\mathfrak{g}{C})

(3.1.34)

be given. By the left invariance of
D
, also its leading symbol

\sigma_{k}(D)
is left invariant, which means that

\sigma(D)\Big|_{g}=(T_{\mathrm{e}}\ell_{g})^{\mathbin{\otimes}k}\widetilde{\sigma}_{k}(D)\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in G.

(3.1.35)

Consequently,

D^{\prime}\coloneqq D-\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\biggr)^{k}\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\sigma(D)\bigr)

(3.1.36)

is another
G
-invariant differential operator with

\sigma_{k}(D^{\prime})=\sigma_{k}(D)-\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\biggr)^{k}\Bigl(\sigma_{k}\circ\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\Bigr)\bigl(\sigma(D)\bigr)=0

(3.1.37)

by virtue of (

∼

‣
Proof
), where we also use the assumption
\hbar\neq 0
.
That is to say,
D^{\prime}
has strictly lower degree than
D
. Iterating
this procedure proves the surjectivity of
(
3.1.32
). Taking another look at
(
3.1.28
), it is left
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
-linear. Consequently, it
remains to assert that

\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{\bullet}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\bigr)\cong\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{\bullet}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\bigr)^{G}.

(3.1.38)

By what we have already shown, we know that
1\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
and the operators of the
form
\mathscr{L}(\xi)
generate the algebra
\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G))^{G}
. Thus it
suffices to check the claim in filtration degree one. But this is just the
bijectivity of (
3.1.14
). We have completed the
proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Having established the bijectivity of the quantization map
\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
, we may
define the standard ordered star product by pulling back the product
\circ
of

\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G))
, which is the composition of differential
operators. More precisely, we define a linear map

??\colon\mathrm{Pol}(T^{*}G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{Pol}(T^{*}G)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Pol}(T^{*}G)

(3.1.39)

by setting

P\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}Q\coloneqq\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}^{-1}\bigl(\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(P)\circ\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(Q)\bigr).

(3.1.40)

By construction, the associativity of
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
is clear, as the composition of
functions is always associative. Taking another look at (
3.1.28
), we
moreover see that there are no convergence issues. As the degrees of the polynomials

P
and
Q
are finite, the same is true for the degree of the operator

\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(P)\circ\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(Q)
and thus
P\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}Q
is a polynomial of
degree at most
\deg P+\deg Q
. Of course, we ultimately want to go beyond the
purely polynomial situation regardless, at which point we will have to deal with
convergence questions. The following corollary is immediate from
Proposition
3.1.4
.

Corollary 3.1.5
(Gutt star product).

Let
G
be a Lie group. Then the mapping

??\colon\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\longrightarrow\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C}),\quad p\star_{\mathrm{G}}q\coloneqq(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}p)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)

(3.1.41)

constitutes an associative product on
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
, where
??denotes the function on
G
with constant value equal
to
1\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
.

We call
\star_{\mathrm{G}}
the
Gutt star product
on
\mathfrak{g}
or the
Lie
algebra star product
. By virtue of Lie-Cartan’s Theorem, which we will review in
Theorem
3.3.13
, one may forget about the underlying Lie group
G
and work
with an abstract real or complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
from the start. This is
also clear from the construction we have presented.

Remark 3.1.6
(Gutt star product).

Calling
\star_{\mathrm{G}}
the Gutt
12
12

12

Simone Gutt (born 1956) is a Belgian
differential geometer and professor emeritus at Université libre de Bruxelles. She
has made numerous contributions to symplectic geometry and symplectic formal
deformation quantization, including early existence and classification results.

star product is historically somewhat inaccurate.
The construction in
[
80
]
goes beyond the purely Lie algebraic
situation and
provides a Weyl
13
13

13

Weyl-Wigner-Groenewold-Moyal.
ordering on
T^{*}G
,
which is different from – but equivalent as a
star product to – the standard ordering we have presented so far. We will come
back to the ordering problem in Remark
3.1.13
. However, also the
Weyl-ordered star product descends to the Lie algebra -- and yields a star product
equivalent to the Gutt star product -- which warrants our choice of terminology.
Moreover, it
should be noted that the star product was independently constructed by
Drinfel’d
14
14

14

Vladimir Drinfel’d (born 1954) is a Ukrainian mathematical physicist,
who moved to the United States of America in 1999 and is currently working at the
University of Chicago. His main interest consists in algebraic geometry over finite
fields and their connection to number theory. In the year 1990, he received the Fields
Medal for his contributions to the geometric
Langlands correspondence and the theory of quantum groups.

[
58
]
in the setting of quantum groups. In the surrounding literature,
many authors speak of
h
-deformations instead of star products.

Unwrapping the definitions, one may think of the combinatorics of the Lie algebra star
product of
p,q\in\mathrm{S}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
in the following manner. First, one associates
the invariant and symmetric differential operators
\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(p)
and

\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(q)
to
p
and
q
. Composition results in another invariant and
symmetric differential operator, which is however no longer of the form in
(
3.1.28
). The star product
p\star q
then constitutes the

symmetric polynomial
inducing the composition
\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(p)\circ\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(q)
.

An instructive example is the following: Let
\xi,\chi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
k,\ell\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Then

\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\underbrace{\xi\vee\cdots\vee\xi}_{k-\textrm{times}}\bigr)=\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}}^{k}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\underbrace{\chi\vee\cdots\vee\chi}_{\ell-\textrm{times}}\bigr)=\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{\ell}\mathscr{L}_{X_{\chi}}^{\ell}.

(3.1.42)

From here, it is not at all clear what
\xi^{k}\star_{\mathrm{G}}\chi^{\ell}
might be, even for
k=\ell=1
. The principal issue is that, while
\xi^{k}
and
\chi^{\ell}
are homogeneous, their product will typically longer be. In the degenerate case
\xi=\chi
we of course get that
\xi\star_{\mathrm{G}}\chi=\xi\vee\chi
.

A comprehensive discussion of the Lie algebra star product and its intimate
connection to the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula can be found in

[
176
, Sec. 3.2 & 3.4]
and

[
63
, Sec. 2]
. The inclined reader may
moreover find a proper analysis of our example in terms of the
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series within
[
176
, Lemma 4.1.9]
.

The main result of
[
63
]
is the following continuity
result, which the authors even extended to certain infinite dimensional Lie algebras
with the asymptotic estimate property or fulfilling a nilpotency condition. We confine
ourselves to the finite dimensional situation and use the notation from
Section
2.5
and in particular (
2.5.38
) by
using the fact that a Lie algebra is, in particular, a vector space.

Theorem 3.1.7
(Continuity of the Gutt star product,
[
63
, Sec. 3.1]
).

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite dimensional Lie algebra,
\hbar\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
and
R\geq 1
. The Lie algebra star product

\star_{\mathrm{G}}\colon\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\otimes\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\longrightarrow\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})

(3.1.43)

is well defined and continuous. Moreover,

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\ni\hbar\mapsto p\star_{\mathrm{G}}q\in\mathrm{S}_{R}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})

(3.1.44)

is Fréchet holomorphic for all
p,q\in\mathrm{S}_{R}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
. The condition

R\geq 1
is necessary in general.

Before investigating the Poisson brackets to warrant calling
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
and

\star_{\mathrm{G}}
star products in the sense of Definition
1.2.1
, we
collect some explicit formulas. As before,
\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
denotes the
constant function with value
1\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, i.e. the unit of
(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G),\cdot)
. Recall that the standard ordered quantization map (
3.1.28
)
is left
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
-linear by definition. Thus,

(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)=\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}^{-1}\bigl(M_{\phi}\circ\mathscr{L}(q)\bigr)=\phi\mathbin{\otimes}q=(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\cdot(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)

(3.1.45)

for
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
and
q\in\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}{C}(\mathfrak{g})
by
(
3.1.40
). Here,
\cdot
acts as pointwise multiplication in the first
factor and as the symmetric tensor product
\vee
in the second. The remaining
combination is more interesting.

Theorem 3.1.8
(Standard ordered star product,

[
90
, Prop. 2.4]
).

Let
G
be a Lie group,
\hbar\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\setminus\{0\}
,
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
,
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
and
q\in\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}{C}(\mathfrak{g})
. Then

(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k})\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}q)=(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\cdot(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k})\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)\\
=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{p}\frac{\phi}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\xi_{\sigma(1)}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\sigma(p)}\bigr)\psi\mathbin{\otimes}(\xi_{\sigma(p+1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)})\star_{\mathrm{G}}q.

(3.1.46)

Proof
:

One may obtain the presented formulae from

[
26
, Prop. 11]
by making
(
3.1.29
) explicit. We provide another proof based on
(
3.1.28
). Let
p\coloneqq\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k}
for ease of notation. By associativity of the star product, the
neutrality of
\ch@airxmathbbm{1}
and
1
as well as (
3.1.45
),
we first note

(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}p)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}q)=(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\cdot(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}p)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q).

(3.1.47)

To compute the middle product, we work with a basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of the Lie algebra. By bilinearity of
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
, we may assume
that

p=\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\qquad\textrm{for some}\qquad j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(3.1.48)

The trick is to apply the isomorphism
\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}

on both sides of (
3.1.46
) after setting
\phi=\ch@airxmathbbm{1}
and
q=1
. By definition of the star product in
(
3.1.40
) and
(
3.1.28
), the left-hand side yields on the one hand

\displaystyle\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl((\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}p)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}})\circ\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)

(3.1.49)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr)\circ M_{\psi}

(3.1.50)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\bigr)\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr)\circ M_{\psi}.

(3.1.51)

To arrive back at an expression of the form (
3.1.28
), we need to pull the
multiplication operator
M_{\psi}
to the left. As

\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}),\ldots,\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}})
are
derivations, we may do so at the cost of a Leibniz rule. Each time, this produces
two terms: one where the derivative
acts on
\psi
and another where it does not. Due to the additional symmetrization,
this leads to the noncommutative higher Leibniz rule

\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}D_{\sigma(1)}\circ\cdots\circ D_{\sigma(k)}(ab)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\sum_{p=0}^{k}\binom{k}{p}\bigl(D_{\sigma(1)}\circ\cdots\circ D_{\sigma(p)}a\bigr)\bigl(D_{\sigma(p+1)}\circ\cdots\circ D_{\sigma(k)}b\bigr)

(3.1.52)

for any derivations
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
of some – not necessarily associative –
ring

\mathsf{R}
with
\ch@airxmathbbm{Q}\subseteq\mathsf{R}
and
a,b\in\mathsf{R}
, see

[
90
, Appendix. B]
. In our situation, this yields

\displaystyle\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl((\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}p)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\bigr)

(3.1.53)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\sum_{p=0}^{k}\binom{k}{p}M_{\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p)}})\psi}\circ\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+1)}}\bigr)\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr)

(3.1.54)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k}\frac{1}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}M_{\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p)}})\psi}\circ\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+1)}}\bigr)\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr).

(3.1.55)

Using the left
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
-linearity of
\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
gives on the other hand

\displaystyle\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}

\displaystyle\biggl(\sum_{p=0}^{k}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{p}\frac{1}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p)}}\bigr)\psi\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\biggr)

(3.1.56)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{p}\frac{1}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}M_{\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p)}})\psi}\circ\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr).

(3.1.57)

Dropping the matching prefactors and using the auxiliary variables

\chi^{\sigma}_{j}\coloneqq\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+j)}}\qquad\textrm{for }j=1,\ldots,k-p\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sigma\in S_{k},

(3.1.58)

we finally compute

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}

\displaystyle\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr)

(3.1.59)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\chi_{1}^{\sigma}\vee\cdots\vee\chi_{k-p}^{\sigma}\bigr)

(3.1.60)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k-p}\frac{1}{(k-p)!}\sum_{\tau\in S_{k-p}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\chi_{\tau(1)}^{\sigma}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\vee\chi_{\tau(k-p)}^{\sigma}\bigr)

(3.1.61)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k-p}\frac{1}{(k-p)!}\sum_{\tau\in S_{k-p}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+\tau(1))}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+\tau(k-p))}}\bigr)

(3.1.62)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k-p)!}\sum_{\tau\in S_{k-p}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k-p}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+\tau(1))}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+\tau(k-p))}}\bigr)

(3.1.63)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k-p)!}\sum_{\tau\in S_{k-p}}\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in S_{k}}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k-p}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma^{\prime}(p+1)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma^{\prime}(k))}}\bigr)

(3.1.64)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{k-p}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+1)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr),

(3.1.65)

as
\sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq\sigma\circ(\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}\times\tau)
yields all of
S_{k}
if we fix
\tau\in S_{k-p}
and vary
\sigma\in S_{k}
. Summarizing, we have now shown (
3.1.46
) under the restraint
q=1
. But, going back to the factorization and once more using associativity, we get

\displaystyle(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}p)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)

(3.1.66)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sum_{p=0}^{k}\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{p}\frac{\phi}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\xi_{\sigma(1)}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\sigma(p)}\bigr)\psi}_{\eqqcolon\psi_{p}}\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\xi_{\sigma(p+1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}\bigr)\Bigg)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)

(3.1.67)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\bigl(\psi_{p}\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\sigma(p+1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}\bigr)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)

(3.1.68)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\bigl(\psi_{p}\mathbin{\otimes}1\bigr)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\sigma(p+1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}\bigr)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}q)

(3.1.69)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\bigl(\psi_{p}\mathbin{\otimes}1\bigr)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\sigma(p+1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}\star_{\mathrm{G}}q\bigr)

(3.1.70)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\psi_{p}\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\xi_{\sigma(p+1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}\star_{\mathrm{G}}q\bigr)

(3.1.71)

by virtue of (
3.1.41
). This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

The factorization (
3.1.46
) facilitated the continuity
estimates in
[
90
, Thm. 6.3]
, which build on
Theorem
3.1.7
. We will use similar techniques in
Theorem
3.5.17
to provide a holomorphic version of
this result. Note that (
3.1.46
) makes sense for
\hbar=0
, which gives

\displaystyle(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k})\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\psi\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\xi_{\sigma(1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}\vee q\bigr)

(3.1.72)

\displaystyle=\psi\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k}\vee q\bigr).

(3.1.73)

This means that, despite our construction breaking down for
\hbar=0
, naively
setting
\hbar=0
within the explicit formula (
3.1.46
)
provides an associative product. Taking a closer look, it is nothing else than the

undeformed product
of
\mathrm{Pol}(T^{*}G)
and thus our construction is consistent with
the classical limit (
1.1.16
). In the sequel, this is what we mean
when speaking of the case
\hbar=0
.

Remark 3.1.9
(Interpretation of
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
).

One may think of (
3.1.46
) for
\phi=\ch@airxmathbbm{1}
and

q=1
as arising from the
commutator
of the operators
\varrho_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(p)

and
M_{\psi}
up to some combinatorial factors. In particular, the natural pairing
of
\operatorname{\mathrm{DiffOp}}^{\bullet}(G)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
appears as the term with
p=k
in the
summation.

In
[
90
, Thm. 6.3 & Thm. 6.4]
an extension of
Theorem
3.1.7
to the full standard ordered
star product
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
on a suitable subalgebra was proved. In view of
Theorem
3.1.7
, one expects operators
associated to elements of the form

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\xi^{\vee k}}{k!}=\exp_{\vee}(\xi)

(3.1.74)

for some fixed
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
to appear in the completion of

\mathrm{S}_{1}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
, see again Proposition
2.5.8
,

iv.)
. That is,
actions of operators such as
\exp(\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}})
will be crucial. The first half
of Section
3.2
will thus be dedicated to the study of such
operators, as for sufficiently nice functions, the operator
\exp(\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}})

simply constitutes a pullback with the right translation by
\exp\xi

on
G
. We
will phrase this as the
Lie-Taylor formula
on
G
in
Theorem
3.2.4
.

Note that (
3.1.74
) is
not quite
contained in

\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{1}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
for general Lie algebras, see

[
63
, Ex. 3.3]
and the discussion in

[
176
, Sec. 5.4]
. Indeed, this is only possible if the
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series converges on the entirety of
\mathfrak{g}{C}
,
which limits the geometric complexity and generality severely.
Nevertheless, as elements of slightly slower growth are still relevant, this
provides good
intuition regardless. In particular, we see that
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
is much too large of an
algebra if we want to be able to take star products with the entirety of

\mathrm{S}_{1}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
. This
type of question for natural pairings was investigated by Goodman
15
15

15

Roe
Goodman is a harmonic analyst and distinguished Professor Emeritus at Rutgers
University since 2015. He is a specialist on analytic, entire and strongly entire
vectors of strongly continuous unitary representations.
in

[
74
,
75
,
76
]
, and we will return to the
accompanying concepts in Section
3.6
and
Section
3.7
.

The geometric interpretation of operators associated to non-real Lie algebra
elements is unclear at this point, but we will resolve this in
Theorem
3.5.9
: They provide translations within the universal
complexification
G{C}
of
G
.

We proceed by taking a closer look at the associated Poisson brackets. That is,
we enforce the semiclassical limit (
1.2.10
) by
defining

\bigl\{P,Q\bigr\}\coloneqq\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\hbar}\bigl(P\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}Q-Q\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}P\bigr)\bigg|_{\hbar=0}´

(3.1.75)

and

\bigl\{p,q\bigr\}_{\mathrm{G}}\coloneqq\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\hbar}\bigl(p\star_{\mathrm{G}}q-q\star_{\mathrm{G}}p\bigr)\bigg|_{\hbar=0}

(3.1.76)

for
P,Q\in\mathrm{Pol}(T^{*}G)
and
p,q\in\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
. Note that

\star_{\mathrm{G}}
does indeed have a non-trivial dependence on
\hbar
. Thus
Theorem
3.1.8
yields the following:

Corollary 3.1.10
(Poisson bracket,

[
26
, Prop. 8.2]
).

Let
G
be a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and let
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)

as well as
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
.

i.)

If the second factor is constant in fiber direction, the Poisson bracket is given by

\bigl\{\phi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k},\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1\bigr\}=\phi\cdot\sum_{p=1}^{k}\mathscr{L}(\xi_{p})\psi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\overset{\xi_{p}}{\cdots}\vee\xi_{k},

(3.1.77)

where ??indicates that the factor
\xi_{p}
is absent.

ii.)

The Poisson bracket
\{\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\}_{\mathrm{G}}
on
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})

coincides with the Kostant-Kirillov-Souriau bracket
16
16

16

For a
comprehensive discussion, we refer to
[
102
, Ch. 1]
.

??on
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
if one identifies
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})
with
polynomials on the dual

\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by natural pairing for linear polynomials and
compatibility with
\vee
.
17
17

17

Just like we did earlier in
Section
2.5
.

Proof
:

The idea for the first part is that differentiation by
\hbar
and then plugging in
\hbar=0
singles out the term with
p=1
in (
3.1.46
). Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\hbar}\bigl((\phi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k})\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\bigr)\Big|_{\hbar=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi}{(k-1)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}(\xi_{\sigma(1)})\psi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\sigma(2)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}

(3.1.78)

\displaystyle=\phi\cdot\sum_{p=1}^{k}\mathscr{L}(\xi_{p})\psi\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\overset{\xi_{p}}{\cdots}\vee\xi_{k},

(3.1.79)

where we have used the symmetry of
\vee
. The other contribution vanishes, as
left multiplication with
\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1

reduces to the pointwise product, which is indepedent of
\hbar
. We have proved

i.)
. It suffices to
check
ii.)
on generators, i.e. constant functions and
linear
monomials.
18
18

18

Here, we use that in order
\hbar^{k}
of the star product there
is a bi-differential operator of order
(k,k)
acting on the inputs. For
k=1
,
this guarantees that (
3.1.75
) and (
3.1.76
) define
Poisson brackets, as
(1,1)
bi-differential operators are exactly bi-derivations
and (
3.1.75
) as well as (
3.1.76
) have built-in
antisymmetry.
For the former, there is nothing to be shown, as both sides simply
vanish. Thus let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}
. By
[
176
, Lemma. 4.1]

with
z=\hbar/\mathrm{i}
, we have

\xi_{1}\star_{\mathrm{G}}\xi_{2}=\xi_{1}\vee\xi_{2}+\frac{\hbar}{2\mathrm{i}}[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]

(3.1.80)

and thus by antisymmetry of the Lie bracket

\bigl\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\bigr\}_{G}=[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}].

(3.1.81)

This yields the statement, as suppressing the identification, we simply have

\{\xi,\eta\}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{KKS}}}=[\xi,\eta]
.

\ch@irxboxempty

We conclude this section with some more remarks.

Remark 3.1.11
(Complex Lie algebras).

Let
G
be a complex Lie group. Replacing the complexified Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}{C}
with
the complex Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
from Remark
3.1.2

and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
with
\mathscr{H}(G)
throughout provides a complex version of
the
Gutt and standard ordered star products. To make this precise, one either
establishes a holomorphic version of the left invariant symbol calculus discussed
in Proposition
3.1.4
or observes that the
explicit formulas from
[
63
, Sec. 2.3]
and
(
3.1.46
) respect holomorphy. Either way, one is lead to
defining the
the holomorphic polynomial algebra

??\coloneqq\mathscr{H}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}_{1}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}}),

(3.1.82)

where
\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}
denotes the projective tensor product
19
19

19

See again the
discussion around (
2.5.2
).
and

\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}_{R}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})
is the symmetric algebra endowed with topology
induced by the seminorms (
2.5.37
) with
R=1
. As the
continuity result from Theorem
3.1.7
is
agnostic regarding the base field, the resulting complex Gutt star product is
still continuous. We shall prove that the same is true for the resulting complex
standard ordered star product in

3.5.17
.

Remark 3.1.12
(Fedosov’s construction).

The principal idea of
[
26
, Sec. 8]
is to
identify the star product constructed in
[
80
]
as the
Fedosov star product
[
64
]
associated to the half-commutator
connection

\nabla_{X_{\xi}}X_{\chi}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}X_{[\xi,\chi]}

(3.1.83)

for the tangent bundle
TG
. From Fedosov’s classification results it is clear that
there exists some connection ??for
TG
with this property,
but the abstract
machinery does not provide its exact form. In our formulation, the choice of this
connection is virtually invisible, but may be recovered from (
3.1.28
) by
comparing with the abstract formulas involving symmetrized covariant derivatives.
We refer to
[
185
, Sec. 6.4]
for a comprehensive discussion of
Fedosov’s construction and to
[
47
]
for a reformulation and
subsequent generalization within the framework of algebraic quantum field theory.

Remark 3.1.13
(
\kappa
-orderings and Weyl star product).

In (
3.1.28
), we have chosen an ordering prescription. Namely, we have
put the coefficient function to the very left as a multiplication operator and all
polynomials to the right as derivatives. This leads to particularly simple formulas, as
the derivatives would act on their coefficient functions otherwise. However,
this is not
the only choice, and has the serious flaw of being incompatible with the

∗
-involution on
\mathrm{Pol}(T^{*}G)
given by pointwise complex conjugation. By
additionally choosing a smooth positive density
\mu
on
T^{*}G
– such as the
density associated to the left invariant volume form
\theta^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{n}
– one may pass from the standard ordering to other ordering
prescriptions
by means of a Neumaier operator, which provides a linear bijection of

\mathrm{Pol}^{\bullet}(T^{*}G)

intertwining the products. Introducing an additional real parameter
\kappa
,
this
yields a continuum of equivalent star products, both in the formal and the
strict setting. In the case of
\kappa=1/2
,
this results in the so called Weyl
20
20

20

No, we are not doing this again.

star product, which is compatible with the

∗
-involution. Roughly speaking, it arises in the same fashion as
(
3.1.28
), but with full symmetrization over all possible orderings.
Note
that these considerations are not specific to
T^{*}G
and work for any cotangent
bundle, leading to an astonishing explicit formula for the adjoint operator of

any
differential
operator acting on a suitable
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-space. We refer the interested reader to

[
26
,
27
]

for the algebraic details and
[
90
, Appendix A, Sec. 2.2 &
Prop. 6.7]
, which contains a continuity result for the
Neumaier operators.

3.2
Lie-Taylor Expansions and Entire Functions

Tiffany jumped when she saw a balloon sail up above the trees, catch the
wind, and swoop away, but it turned out to be just a balloon and not a lump of excess
Brian. She could tell this because it was followed by a long scream of rage mixed with a
roar of complaint: “AAaargwannawannaaaagongongonaargggaaaaBLOON!” which is
the traditional sound of a very small child learning that with balloons, as with life itself, it
is important to know when not to let go of the string. The whole point of balloons is to
teach small children this.

A Hat Full of Sky
– Terry Pratchett

This section serves as a review of the most important properties of the algebra of

entire functions
on
G
. That is to say, we are in the realm of strict
deformation quantization of the cotangent bundle
T^{*}G
, as discussed in
Section
1.2
. As such, almost everything we present is already
discussed in
[
90
]
to vastly greater detail. The inclusion
of this section is thus purely for the reader’s convenience and to keep the text as
self-contained as possible.

We begin with the classical Lie-Taylor formulas, as they can be found in the
beginning of
[
91
, Sec. 2.1.4]
for Lie groups and

[
67
, (1.48)]
or
[
90
, Prop. 4.5]
for
arbitrary analytic manifolds. We already specialize to the setting to Lie groups, as
that
is the only situation we are ultimately interested in. In the sequel, we write

\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
for
the real analytic functions on a Lie group
G
, where we use the fact that the
exponential atlas endows
G
with the structure of an analytic manifold, see for
instance

[
59
, (1.6.3) Theorem]
.

Proposition 3.2.1
(One variable Lie-Taylor formula).

Let
G
be a Lie group and fix a real analytic
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
, an expansion
point
g\in G
and a direction
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
.

i.)

There exists a radius
R>0
such that the Lie-Taylor formula

\phi\bigl(g\cdot??(t\xi)\bigr)=\bigl(\exp(t\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}})\phi\bigr)(g)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}}^{k}\phi(g)

(3.2.1)

holds whenever
t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
fulfils
\lvert t\rvert<R
.

ii.)

The series (
3.2.1
) is convergent in the Fréchet
space
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(-R,R)
with respect to
t
. That is to say, each individual
derivative with respect to
t
converges uniformly on compact subsets of the
open interval
??\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
.

One should think of
g\in G
as the expansion point of the power series,
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
the direction and
t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
as the magnitude of the perturbation or,
alternatively, a time parameter. For the additive group
G=\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
, the Lie-Taylor
formula (
3.2.1
) takes the familiar form

\phi(x+t\xi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\frac{\partial^{k}\phi}{\partial\xi^{k}}(x)\qquad\textrm{for all $x,\xi\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}$},

(3.2.2)

where we suppress the Lie exponential as usual and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}

denotes the directional derivative in direction of
\xi
.

Proof
(of Proposition
3.2.1
):

Let
U\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be an open neighbourhood of
0
such that

\exp\colon U\longrightarrow\exp(U)

(3.2.3)

is a diffeomorphism. The idea is to consider the left-hand side of
(
3.2.1
) for
t\xi\in U
as our function
\phi

precomposed with the exponential chart
\ell_{g}\circ\exp
around
g
. Or in
other words, we may view it as a coordinate expression for
\phi
. By
analyticity of
\phi
and by
shrinking
U
if necessary, we may thus assume that
\phi\circ\ell_{g}\circ\exp\colon U\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is globally given by its Taylor series around zero.
Moreover, we may assume that
\xi=\mathit{e}_{1}
by choosing an appropriate basis
of
\mathfrak{g}
. As the origin is an inner point of
U
, we find some
R>0
such
that the open ??-ball
\mathrm{B}_{R}(0)
is contained within
U
. For

\lVert t\xi\rVert=\lvert t\rvert<R
, the classical Taylor formula around the origin then
takes the simple form

\phi\bigl(g\exp(t\xi)\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{k}}\phi\bigl(g\exp(s\xi)\bigr)\Big|_{s=0}.

(
\ast
)

By left invariance, we have

\displaystyle\Bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\phi\Bigr)\bigl(g\exp(s\xi)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\ell_{g\exp(s\xi)}^{*}\circ\mathscr{L}(\xi)\bigr)\phi\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\circ\ell_{g\exp(s\xi)}^{*}\bigr)\phi\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}

(3.2.5)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\phi\bigl(g\exp(s\xi)\exp(t\xi)\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\phi\bigl(g\exp((s+t)\xi)\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\phi\bigl(g\exp(s\xi)\bigr)

(3.2.8)

and thus

\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}}^{k}\phi\Big|_{g\exp(s\xi)}=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{k}}\phi\bigl(g\exp(s\xi)\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all $k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}$.}

(3.2.9)

Plugging this into (

∗

‣
Proof
) and once more invoking left invariance, we obtain

\phi\bigl(g\exp(t\xi)\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}}^{k}\phi\bigl(g\exp(s\xi)\bigr)\Big|_{s=0}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}}^{k}\phi(g)

(3.2.10)

whenever
\lvert t\rvert<R
, which is (
3.2.1
).
The remaining statement follows from the fact that the partial sums of
(
3.2.1
) correspond exactly to the partial sums
of the classical Taylor series of
\phi\circ\ell_{g}\circ\exp
and the latter is
even convergent in the Fréchet space
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

We proceed with some remarks on the construction.

Remark 3.2.2
(A wrong conclusion).

Notably, Proposition
3.2.1
does not imply that the
exponential operator
\exp(\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}})

acts as a well defined endomorphism of
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
, contrary to what was stated
in
[
90
, Prop. 4.5,
iii.)
]
. This is already
wrong in the simplest non-trivial case of the additive reals
G=(\ch@airxmathbbm{R},+)
. Indeed,
consider the family of functions

f_{\varepsilon}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad f_{\varepsilon}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{x-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}\qquad\textrm{for }\varepsilon>0,

(3.2.11)

which may be meromorphically extended to
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
with a simple pole at
x=\mathrm{i}\varepsilon
. By virtue of

\exp(t\mathscr{L}_{X_{1}})f_{\varepsilon}\Big|_{x}=\exp\biggl(t\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}\biggr)f_{\varepsilon}\Big|_{x}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}^{k}f_{\varepsilon}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{k}}(x)

(3.2.12)

the Lie-Taylor series coincides with the usual Taylor series; this also follows
from the Lie exponential of
G
being given by the identity mapping. Consequently,
the maximal choice of the radius
R
for
f_{\varepsilon}
is
\varepsilon
and thus

\exp(t\mathscr{L}_{X_{1}})
does not act on all
f_{\varepsilon}
for any
t\neq 0
.

Remark 3.2.3
(Uniform choices,
[
90
, Cor. 4.9]
).

Let
g\in G
. By what we have shown, the function
R_{g}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow(0,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
,

R_{g}(\xi)\coloneqq\sup\bigl\{R>0\colon\eqref{eq:LieTaylorOneVariable}\;\textrm{holds whenever}\;\lvert t\rvert<R\bigr\}

(3.2.13)

is well defined. Taking another look at our construction for fixed
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, we see that the zero neighbourhood
U
was independent of
\xi
and thus

R_{g}\coloneqq\min_{\lVert\chi\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1}R_{g}(\chi)\geq R_{g}(\xi)>0,

(3.2.14)

where we take the supnorm with respect to some linear algebraic basis of

\mathfrak{g}
with
\xi=\mathit{e}_{1}
. Indeed, given
\chi\in\mathfrak{g}

with
\lVert\chi\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
that is not a
multiple of
\xi
, we may change basis such that
\mathit{e}_{2}=\chi
. Having a
uniform choice
R_{g}>0
reflects the fact that power series converge in open
sets. We will incorporate this fact in our formulation of the multivariable
Lie-Taylor formula. Note that
R_{g}
depends on the choice of our basis and thus
has no intrinsic geometric meaning. However,
R_{g}>0
with respect to some basis
does imply that
R_{g}>0
for
any
basis. This, in turn, is equivalent to

\phi
being real analytic at the point
g
. Indeed, this observation will be at
the core of Proposition
3.7.1
.

Having noted this, we may return to Remark
3.2.2
.
By Proposition
3.2.1
, the operator

\exp\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\bigr)\colon\mathscr{C}^{\omega}_{\lvert\xi\rvert}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)

(3.2.15)

is well defined and corresponds to the pullback with the right translation

r_{\exp(\xi)}
, where

??=\bigl\{\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)\;\big|\;\forall_{g\in G}\colon R_{g}\geq r\bigr\}\qquad\textrm{for any }r>0.

(3.2.16)

As already noted, the spaces
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}_{r}(G)
for finite values of
r
depend on a
choice of basis and is thus not intrinsically interesting. Consequently and up to
some technical subtleties regarding the placement of absolute values, our
and the interest of
[
90
]
lies in the extreme case

r=\infty
, i.e. the intersection

\bigcap_{r>0}\mathscr{C}^{\omega}_{r}(G),

(3.2.17)

where all such operators are well
defined. One may think of the Lie-Taylor formula (
3.2.1
)
as the Lie algebra action (
3.1.1
) integrating to the Lie
group action

g\mathbin{\triangleright}\phi=\phi\circ r_{g}=r_{g}^{*}\phi

(3.2.18)

and as the intersection
\bigcap_{r>0}\mathscr{C}^{\omega}_{r}(G)
as the
space of
entire vectors
for the action. We refer to

[
90
, Rem. 4.16]
and the references
therein for a comprehensive discussion of these concepts, and will return to this
point of view to resolve the integration problem in
Section
3.7
. Note however that the
spaces (
3.2.16
) and also the notion of entire vectors already appeared
and were studied in the series of papers
[
74
,
75
,
76
]
.

To simplify bookkeeping for the multivariable version and the rest of this chapter, we
fix some more notation, which was devised by Oliver Roth and introduced in

[
90
, Sec. 4]
. Recall that we have once and for all fixed a
basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Expanding on
(
3.1.20
), we write

\mathscr{L}_{X_{\alpha}}\coloneqq\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\coloneqq\mathscr{L}_{X_{\alpha_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{L}_{X_{\alpha_{k}}}

(3.2.19)

as well as

\underline{z}^{\alpha}\coloneqq z^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots z^{\alpha_{k}}\quad\textrm{for}\quad\underline{z}=(z^{1},\ldots,z^{n})\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}

(3.2.20)

for any
k
-tuple
\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}\eqqcolon\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}
. For
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
and
g\in G
, we call the formal series

??\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(g)\cdot\underline{z}^{\alpha}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\ch@irxllbbracket\underline{z}\ch@irxrrbbracket

(3.2.21)

the Lie-Taylor series of
\phi
at the point
g\in G
. This is warranted by the
following theorem. Recall that we make use of Einstein’s summation
convention
21
21

21

Which is a marvellous gadget, whose self-correcting properties
make it an indispensable tool for any calculation involving coordinates.
to
implicitly sum over repeated indices.

Theorem 3.2.4
(Multivariable Lie-Taylor formula,

[
90
, Cor. 4.6]
).

Let
G
be a Lie group,
g\in G
and
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
. Fix a basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Then there is a radius
R_{g}>0
such that

\phi\bigl(g\exp(x^{j}\mathit{e}_{j})\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{\phi}(\underline{x};g)

(3.2.22)

for all
\underline{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
with
\lVert\underline{x}\rVert_{\infty}<R_{g}
.

Proof
:

We define
R_{g}
as discussed in Remark
3.2.3
corresponding to
the basis ??. Consequently, we may apply
(
3.2.1
) to the direction
\xi\coloneqq x^{j}\mathit{e}_{j}

for all
\underline{x}=(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
with

\lVert\underline{x}\rVert_{\infty}<R_{g}
. The formula (
3.2.22
) is now purely a
matter of multiplying out
within the universal enveloping algebra
\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})
, see
again (
3.1.19
). Indeed,
we have

\xi^{k}=(x^{j}\mathit{e}_{j})^{k}=\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{k}}\cdots\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\underline{x}^{\alpha}

(3.2.23)

and thus

\mathscr{L}(\xi)^{k}=\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\cdot\underline{x}^{\alpha}.

\ch@irxboxempty

Motivated by Remark
3.1.9
, we now pull the absolute values
all the way inside of (
3.2.22
) to define the
Lie-Taylor majorant
of

\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
at
g\in G
as the formal power series

??\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(\phi;g)\cdot\underline{z}^{k}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\ch@irxllbbracket\underline{z}\ch@irxrrbbracket,

(3.2.24)

where

??\coloneqq\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(g)\Big\rvert\qquad\textrm{for all }k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}.

(3.2.25)

For
g=\mathrm{e}
, we simply write
??\coloneqq\operatorname{F}_{\phi}(\underline{z};\mathrm{e})

and
??\coloneqq c_{k}(\phi;\mathrm{e})
. Note that we have
suppressed the choice of basis in our notation. This is warranted by the following
lemma.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Let
G
be a Lie group,
g\in G
,
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
and
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be bases.

i.)

If
\operatorname{F}^{\mathcal{B}}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;g)
converges on all of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, then also
\operatorname{F}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;g)
converges on all of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
.

ii.)

If
\operatorname{F}^{\mathcal{B}}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;g)
has positive radius of convergence, then so does
\operatorname{F}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;g)
.

Proof
:

Let
\mathcal{B}=(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=(\mathit{e}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}^{\prime})
. Then
\mathit{e}_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}^{\ell}\mathit{e}_{\ell}
and we set

M\coloneqq\max_{j,\ell=1,\ldots,n}\big\lvert\lambda_{j}^{\ell}\big\rvert,

(3.2.26)

which is simply the modulus of the largest entry of the linear change of basis
from
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{B}
. This yields the estimate

c_{k}^{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}(\phi;g)=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{k}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\prime})\phi\bigr)(g)\Big\rvert\leq(Mn)^{k}\cdot c_{k}(\phi;g)

(3.2.27)

for all
k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Crucially, this grows like a constant raised to the power of

k
. Consequently, this factor may be absorbed into the complex variable

\underline{z}
, resulting in a scaling of the radius of convergence, and both
claims follow.

\ch@irxboxempty

The upshot is that the choice of basis is inconsequential in the extremal situations
of infinite and positive but unspecified radius of convergence. Moving on, let

\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
. Then

\big\lvert\operatorname{T}_{\phi}(\underline{z};g)\big\rvert\leq\operatorname{F}_{\phi}\bigl(\lVert\underline{z}\rVert_{\infty};g\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }\underline{z}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n},

(3.2.28)

where either side might be equal to
\infty
and we set
x\leq\infty
for all
x\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\cup\{\infty\}
. This warrants our choice of terminology. In particular, if
the
Lie-Taylor majorant
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}
defines an entire function, then the formal
Lie-Taylor
series
\operatorname{T}_{\phi}(\underline{z};g)
converges for all
\underline{z}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
. As

\phi
was assumed to be real analytic, Theorem
3.2.4
then
implies that
the
multivariable Lie-Taylor formula (
3.2.22
) holds for all
\underline{x}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
. More precisely, we get the following:

Proposition 3.2.6
(Translation invariance,

[
90
, Thm. 4.17,
ii.)
]
).

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
such that

\operatorname{F}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;g_{0})\in\mathscr{H}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
for some
g_{0}\in G
. Then
also
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;g)\in\mathscr{H}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
for all
g\in G
.

Proof
:

We sketch the argument. By left translation, we may assume
g_{0}=\mathrm{e}
by
replacing
\phi
with
\phi\circ\ell_{g_{0}}
. Writing
g=\exp(\xi_{1})\cdots\exp(\xi_{k})
for suitable
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}
adhering the
normalization
\lVert\xi\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
, one may then apply

[
90
, Prop. 4.15, iv.)]

k
-times, which
yields the claim.

\ch@irxboxempty

For this reason, we shall in the sequel always suppose connectedness of the Lie group

G
. Another
pleasant consequence of this assumption is that

\phi=0\quad\iff\quad\exists_{g\in G}\colon\operatorname{F}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;g)=0,

(3.2.29)

as all coefficients (
3.2.25
) are nonnegative. This
leads us to the notion of
entire functions
on
G
. That is to say, we demand
that the Lie-Taylor majorant
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}
at the group unit defines an entire
function of one complex variable.

Definition 3.2.7
(Entire functions,
[
90
, Def. 4.10]
).

Let
G
be a connected Lie group.

i.)

A real analytic function
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
is called entire
if its Lie-Taylor majorant
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}
at the group
unit is holomorphic on all of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
.

ii.)

We denote the set of all entire functions on
G
by

??\coloneqq\bigl\{\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)\colon\operatorname{F}_{\phi}\in\mathscr{H}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\bigr\}.

(3.2.30)

iii.)

For
c\geq 0
, we define a family of norms on
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
by

??(\phi)\coloneqq\operatorname{F}_{\phi}(c)

(3.2.31)

and endow
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
with the corresponding locally convex topology.

The advantage of working with
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}
instead of with the norms
(
3.2.31
) is that
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}
is a holomorphic function. This
simple shift of perspective allows for the utilization of many useful complex analytic
techniques, such as the Cauchy estimates and Montel’s Theorem. We collect the most
important properties and symmetries of
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
. A systematic discussion and
detailed proofs may be found in
[
90
, Sec. 4]
.

Theorem 3.2.8
(Symmetries,
[
90
, Thm. 4.17,
4.18]
).

Let
G
be a connected Lie group.

i.)

The set
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
is a nuclear Fréchet algebra that is moreover
a Montel space
22
22

22

Montel’s Theorem holds: every bounded and
closed subset of
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
is compact.
, separable as well as reflexive.

ii.)

The group inversion
??\colon G\longrightarrow G
acts
isometrically on
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
by pullback. More precisely,

\operatorname{F}_{\phi\circ\operatorname{\mathrm{inv}}}=\operatorname{F}_{\phi}\qquad\textrm{for all }\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G).

(3.2.32)

iii.)

The group
G
acts on
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
via pullbacks with left and right
translations in a strongly continuous fashion, i.e. the functions

G\ni g\mapsto\ell_{g}^{*}\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)\quad\textrm{and}\quad G\ni g\mapsto r_{g}^{*}\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)

(3.2.33)

are continuous for all
\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
.

iv.)

The universal enveloping algebra
\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
acts on

\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
via (
3.1.20
) by continuous
linear mappings.

v.)

The Lie-Taylor series (
3.2.21
) is an absolutely convergent power series in
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
, i.e. converges absolutely in
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
for any
\underline{z}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
.

vi.)

The
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
-topology is finer than the
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
-topology. In particular, the evaluation functionals

\delta_{g,\xi}\colon\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\delta_{g,\xi}(\phi)\coloneqq\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\phi\bigr)(g)

(3.2.34)

are continuous for all
g\in G
and
\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
.

Note that the statement
v.)
relies on

iv.)
, which in particular establishes that every
partial sum corresponding to the formal Lie-Taylor series

\operatorname{T}(\underline{z},\,\cdot\,)
is an element of
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
.

Remark 3.2.9
(
R
-Entire functions,
[
90
, Def. 4.13]
).

Rescaling the coefficients
c_{k}(\phi)
from
(
3.2.25
) with
k!^{R}
for a fixed parameter

yields a spectrum of locally convex spaces

\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\coloneqq\biggl\{\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)\;\Big|\;\forall_{c\geq 0}\colon\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k!^{R}\cdot c_{k}(\phi)\cdot c^{k}<\infty\biggr\}.

(3.2.35)

Note that
\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{S}(G)
whenever
S\leq R
. They may be
viewed as
the Lie theoretic analogue of
entire functions of finite order and minimal
type
or Gelfand
23
23

23

Israïl Moyseyovich Gel’fand (1913-2009) was a
Soviet-American functional analyst specialized in representation theory. For most
of his life, he worked at the Moscow State University, until he emigrated to the
United States in 1989, where he remained until his death.
-Shilov
24
24

24

Georgi
Evgen’evich Shilov (1917-1975) was a Soviet functional analyst and a doctoral

spaces. This is of course motivated by the case
G=\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
, which we have
discussed in Section

at least for

and Example

.
Indeed, in this case it is straightforward to see that

\mathscr{E}_{R}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})=\widehat{\mathrm{S}}_{R}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n})

(3.2.36)

as Fréchet algebras, see
[

. The absence

has lead us to model the completion directly.
The case of

0
. For

with

thus the Taylor data at the group unit might not describe its global behaviour. In
the sequel, we will mostly be interested in the case

, as it results in the
largest algebra with reasonable properties.

Another important observation is that
\mathscr{E}_{R}
constitutes a contravariant functor
from the category of connected Lie groups into the category of Fréchet algebras. That
is to say, there is the following compatibility with group morphisms.

Proposition 3.2.10
(Functoriality of
\mathscr{E}_{R}
,
[

, Prop. 4.21]
).

Let
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
be a Lie group morphism between connected
Lie groups and
R\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. Then the pullback with

is a morphism of Fréchet
algebras

\Phi^{*}\colon\mathscr{E}_{R}(H)\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}_{R}(G).

(3.2.37)

Finally, we note that there always is a wealth of entire functions, at least for

25
25

25

Lie groups, namely the

??\colon G\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\pi_{v,\varphi}(g)\coloneqq\varphi\bigl(\pi(g)v\bigr).

For a systematic discussion of representative functions, we refer to

Theorem 3.2.11
(Representative functions,

Let
