Remark 3.2.12
(Representation theory and entire functions).

The fact that representative functions are entire is not a coincidence. It turns
out that their Lie derivatives are in direct correspondence with the ones of the

orbit mappings

??\colon G\longrightarrow V,\quad\pi_{v}(g)\coloneqq\pi(g)v,

(3.2.41)

where
v\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
is a fixed vector.
Extending Theorem
3.2.11
to suitable representations on
infinite dimensional spaces then naturally leads to the notions of
entire

and
strongly entire vectors
. We will make this precise in the form of the
universality Theorem
3.7.19
.

Remark 3.2.13
(Representative functions in physics).

Physically, Theorem
3.2.11
has the following
interpretation: Going into a representation
\pi
of
G
is the same as choosing a
concrete realization of our system, as it lifts to the cotangent bundle
T^{*}G
as
a point transformation, which then also respects the symplectic structure. That is
to say, it preserves the dynamics. The representative functions

\pi_{v,w}(g)\coloneqq\big\langle v,\pi(g)w\big\rangle

(3.2.42)

then carry the interpretation of
correlations
between states described by
normalized vectors

v,w\in V
. In the case of
v=w
, one moreover obtains the
expectation
value
of
\pi(g)
in the state
v
.
Theorem
3.2.11
therefore asserts that these
quantities are always observable, both in the classical and the quantum situation.
This also makes it clear that for serious applications, we are going to have to
generalize our result to representations on infinite dimensional spaces, which
will ultimately lead us to Theorem
3.7.19
.

The principal purpose of the algebra of entire functions
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
is to serve as
a natural partner of
\mathrm{S}_{1}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
with respect to
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
,
which yields the following extension of
Theorem
3.1.7
.

Theorem 3.2.14
(Continuity of
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
,
[
90
, Thm. 6.3]
).

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and fix
\hbar\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
,
R\geq 0
and
R^{\prime}\geq 1
.
Then the standard ordered star product (
3.1.40
) is a well
defined and
continuous product

\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\colon\bigl(\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{R^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\bigr)\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{R^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{R^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C}),

(3.2.43)

where
\mathbin{\otimes}
denotes the projective tensor product. Moreover,

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\ni\hbar\;\mapsto\;P\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}Q\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{R^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})

(3.2.44)

is an entire Fréchet holomorphic function for all
P,Q\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{R^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
.

Proof
:

We discuss the concepts underlying the proof and profitable technical
simplifications one should employ. The missing details can be found in

[
90
, Thm. 6.3]
.

The first crucial observation is that working with projective tensor products means
that it suffices to estimate products of
factorizing tensors
. This is the

infimum argument
from Proposition
2.5.2
.

The factorization (
3.1.46
), which we have obtained from
the associativity of the star product, then reduces the continuity of
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}

to the continuity of the pointwise product, the Gutt star product
\star_{\mathrm{G}}

and the mixed product

\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\colon\bigl(\mathrm{S}_{R^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\mathbin{\otimes}1\bigr)\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{R^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C}).

(3.2.45)

The continuity of the pointwise product is a straightforward exercise, which was
spelled out in
[
90
, Prop. 4.15, iii.)]
and
the continuity of
\star_{\mathrm{G}}
for
R^{\prime}\geq 1
is the main result of

[
63
, Sec. 3.1]
, see again
Theorem
3.1.7
. Setting
\phi=\ch@airxmathbbm{1}
and

q=1
in (
3.1.46
), we are left with estimating the
norm of the expression

(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{k})\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\psi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\\
=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\biggl(\frac{\hbar}{\mathrm{i}}\biggr)^{p}\frac{1}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\mathscr{L}\bigl(\xi_{\sigma(1)}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\sigma(p)}\bigr)\psi\mathbin{\otimes}(\xi_{\sigma(p+1)}\vee\cdots\vee\xi_{\sigma(k)}),

(3.2.46)

for
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
and
\psi\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)
by means of seminorms of both factors. As
\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)
is a complex vector space, it suffices to consider real
\xi
.

The next useful observation is that we may
choose
which seminorms we use
for
\mathfrak{g}
, as it constitutes a finite dimensional vector space. Here, a
good choice turns out to be the
\ell^{1}
-norm

\mathrm{p}
associated to a fixed basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of

\mathfrak{g}
, as the projective tensor product of two
\ell^{1}
-norms, say
associated to index sets
I
and
J
, is simply given by the
\ell^{1}
-norm
associated to
I\times J
. A proof of this fact can be found in

[
40
, Lem. A.1]
. In our situation, this leads to
“orthogonality within homogeneous degrees”, which manifests in
equality

in the triangle inequality for certain seminorms. As we will need the resulting
estimates later for the continuity of the holomorphic version of the star product, which
we are going to establish within
Theorem
3.5.17
, we make this precise in the
form of Lemma
3.2.15
.

The estimation of the mixed product then roughly goes as follows: First, one uses
the triangle inequality to pull the seminorms
\mathrm{q}_{R,c}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{p}_{R^{\prime},c^{\prime}}
all the way inside. By (
2.5.3
),
we get the factorization

\bigl(\mathrm{p}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{q}\bigr)(v\mathbin{\otimes}w)=\mathrm{p}(v)\cdot\mathrm{q}(w),

which in our case leads to
\mathrm{q}_{R,c}
of a derivative and terms of the
form (
3.2.47
). Spelling everything out, one is left with a
shifted series, which may be estimated by the full series by virtue of the
nonnegativity of all terms; here it is crucial that (
3.2.24
)
converges and not just the one-variable Lie-Taylor series. We will come back to
this problem in Section
3.7
. Finally, having
equalities

in (
3.2.48
) allows to extend the estimate to
arbitrary polynomials in the first factor.

The holomorphic dependence on
\hbar
is then comparatively easy to obtain.
Throughout the estimates, one may treat
\hbar
in a locally uniform way and each
of the partial sums is simply a polynomial in
\hbar
. Our estimate moreover
implies
absolute
convergence of the series in
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
, which overall
yields the claim by Corollary
2.3.9
. We will later see
that, in a purely
complex setting, one may
view
(\hbar,P,Q)\mapsto P\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}Q
as a single Fréchet holomorphic
mapping in the sense of Definition
2.3.1
, matching with the programme
outlined in Section
1.3
.

\ch@irxboxempty

As we are going to need it, we conclude the section by discussing a precise
formulation of the “orthogonality within fixed homogeneous degree” from the proof of

[
90
, Lemma 6.2]
.

Lemma 3.2.15
.

Let
G
be a complex Lie group and fix a basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of its Lie algebra. Let moreover
\mathrm{p}
be the
\ell^{1}
-norm with respect to this basis and denote its
k
-th projective tensor power by
\mathrm{p}^{k}
. Fix
k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\leq n
.
Then

\mathrm{p}^{\ell}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(\ell)}}\bigr)=1=\mathrm{p}^{k}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\bigr)

(3.2.47)

and

\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(\ell)}}\bigr)=\biggl(\frac{\ell!}{k!}\biggr)^{R}c^{\ell-k}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\bigr)

(3.2.48)

hold for all parameters
R,c\geq 0
, indices
1\leq\ell\leq k
and permutations

\sigma\in S_{k}
.

Proof
:

The equality (
3.2.47
) is immediate from

[
40
, Lem. A.1]
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(\ell)}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\ell!^{R}c^{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{p}^{k-j}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(\ell)}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{\ell!}{k!}\biggr)^{R}c^{\ell-k}\cdot k!^{R}c^{k}\cdot\mathrm{p}^{k}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{\ell!}{k!}\biggr)^{R}c^{\ell-k}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\bigr),

which is exactly (
3.2.48
).

\ch@irxboxempty

As noted before, the crucial point is that (
3.2.48
) is an
equality
and not just an estimate.

3.3
Universal Complexification of Lie Groups

Thunder rolled. It rolled a six.

Guards! Guards!
– Terry Pratchett

The principal idea behind complexification of a real Lie group
G
is to find a complex
Lie group
G{C}
, whose properties are in controlled correspondence with the ones of

G
.

Historically, there have been quite a few different notions and constructions of
complexifications. The textbook
[
141
, Ch. X §13-14]
advocates for
a notion of complexification based on the Lie algebra complexification,

[
46
, Ch. VI §VII-IX]

discusses the Chevalley
26
26

26

Claude Chevalley (1909-1984) was a French algebraic
group theorist,
a founding member of the Bourbaki group and the doctoral advisor of Gerhard
Hochschild. He introduced and studied what we nowadays refer to as Chevalley groups,
which are essentially Lie groups over finite fields.

complexification of compact Lie groups
27
27

27

The construction is based on the Hopf
algebra of representative functions. In analogy to Milnor’s exercise, one then
identifies the real characters of this algebra as the points of the underlying real
group by considering evaluation functionals. The complex characters then provide the
complexification.
and

[
92
, Sec. 15.1]
discusses the
universal complexification introduced by Hochschild
[
93
]
, which is
most suited for our needs.

The common denominator is that the Lie algebra of the
complexification should be isomorphic to the complexification of
\operatorname{Lie}(G)
as a
vector space. While this holds in many important cases also for
universal
complexifications, we shall however see in Example
3.4.4
that this
fails in general. This will result in some quite interesting technical complications, and
may be viewed as the price one pays for its guaranteed existence.

Recall that given a – not necessarily finite dimensional – real vector space
V
,
its complexification may be defined as
??\coloneqq V\mathbin{\otimes}{R}\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
with its usual
addition and the linearly extended multiplication by scalars

w\cdot(v\mathbin{\otimes}z)\coloneqq v\mathbin{\otimes}(zw)\eqqcolon(v\mathbin{\otimes}z)\cdot w\qquad\textrm{for $v\in V$ and $z,w\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}$.}

(3.3.1)

By slight abuse of notation, one then writes
zv\coloneqq v\mathbin{\otimes}z
and uses
complex multiples of real vectors. An alternative model is given by
V\oplus V
with

z\cdot(v,v^{\prime})\coloneqq\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Re}}(z)v-\operatorname{\mathrm{Im}}(z)v^{\prime},\operatorname{\mathrm{Re}}(z)v^{\prime}+\operatorname{\mathrm{Im}}(z)v\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for $v,v^{\prime}\in V$ and $z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}$.}

(3.3.2)

This corresponds to the decomposition into real ??and imaginary parts
??, which provides the
complex linear isomorphism

V{C}\ni v\mathbin{\otimes}z\mapsto\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Re}}(z)v,\operatorname{\mathrm{Im}}(z)v\bigr)\in V\oplus V.

(3.3.3)

Note that the original vector space
V
may be identified with the real subspaces
V\mathbin{\otimes}\{1\}
and
V\oplus\{0\}
, respectively. If now
\Phi\colon V\longrightarrow W
is a linear map into a complex vector space
W
, we may set

\Phi{C}\colon V{C}\longrightarrow W,\quad\Phi{C}(v\mathbin{\otimes}z)\coloneqq\Phi(v)\mathbin{\otimes}z,

(3.3.4)

which is the unique
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear extension of
\Phi
to
V{C}
. It turns out that this
universal property characterizes the pair
(V{C},V\hookrightarrow V{C})

uniquely up to unique isomorphism of complex vector spaces. As we are ultimately
interested
in relating entire functions on
G
with holomorphic functions on
G{C}
and in view
of the functoriality of
\mathscr{E}_{0}
from Proposition
3.2.10
, having a
Lie group morphism from the group into its complexification will be crucial. This
leads us to the notion of universal complexification.

Definition 3.3.1
(Universal Complexification of a Lie group,

[
93
]
).

Let
G
be a Lie group. A pair
(??,??)
of a
complex Lie group
G{C}
and a Lie group morphism

\eta_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}

(3.3.5)

is called the universal
complexification of the Lie group
G
if for every complex Lie group
H
and every
Lie group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
there exists a unique
holomorphic group morphism

\Phi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H\qquad\textrm{such that}\qquad\Phi{C}\circ\eta_{G}=\Phi.

(3.3.6)

We suppress the subscript and write
\eta
instead when only one complexification is involved. In terms of a commutative diagram, the universal complexification looks like this:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;\Phi{C}}

(3.3.7)

Note that there are two different kinds of arrows: While all arrows
are morphisms of real Lie groups, the mapping
\Phi{C}
is additionally
holomorphic. The usual argument for universal objects
shows that the universal complexification of a given Lie group is
unique up to a unique biholomorphic group morphism, i.e. an isomorphism of complex Lie
groups. In the sequel, we shall thus speak of
the
universal complexification by
slight abuse of terminology. We confirm that our preliminary considerations
are consistent with Definition
3.3.1
.

Example 3.3.2
(Universal Complexification of vector space groups).

Let
G=(V,+)
be a finite dimensional vector space, viewed as a real Lie group.
Then the pair
(V{C},V\hookrightarrow V{C})
is the universal complexification
of
V
as a Lie group. Indeed, let

\Phi\colon V\longrightarrow H

(3.3.8)

be a Lie group morphism into a complex Lie group
H
. Choosing a basis

(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of
V
, we get real one parameter subgroups

\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\ni t\mapsto\Phi(t\mathit{e}_{k})\in H

(3.3.9)

of
H
for
k=1,\ldots,n
. By
[
92
, Thm. 9.2.15]
there exist
unique corresponding
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{h}
with

\Phi(t\mathit{e}_{k})=\exp_{H}(t\xi_{k})\qquad\textrm{for all }t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\textrm{ and }k=1,\ldots,n.

(3.3.10)

As
H
is a complex Lie group, we may then define

\Phi{C}(\mathit{e}_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}z)\coloneqq\exp_{H}(z\xi_{k})\qquad\textrm{for all }z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\textrm{ and }k=1,\ldots,n,

(3.3.11)

which we extend to all of
V
by demanding additivity. By construction, this
provides a holomorphic extension of
\Phi
to
V{C}
. This extension is unique by
uniqueness of
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
. Thus we have shown that the pair
(V{C},V\hookrightarrow V{C})
is indeed the universal complexification of
V
, also as a
Lie group.

The existence of universal complexifications is more involved, but as we shall see,
always guaranteed by Theorem
3.3.14
. To prove this, we
are going to utilize Lie’s second and third Theorems. Taking its existence for
granted for a
moment, we collect some first abstract properties of universal complexification.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
G,H
be real Lie groups and
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
a Lie group
morphism. Then there exists a unique holomorphic group morphism
\Phi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H{C}
with

\Phi{C}\circ\eta_{G}=\eta_{H}\circ\Phi.

(3.3.12)

Proof
:

The idea is that
\eta_{H}\circ\Phi
constitutes a Lie group morphism from
G
into the complex Lie group
H{C}
. By the universal property of
G{C}
, there exists a a unique holomorphic group morphism
(\eta_{H}\circ\Phi){C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H{C}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{H}\circ\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;(\eta_{H}\circ\Phi){C}}

(3.3.13)

is commutative. That is,
(\eta_{H}\circ\Phi){C}\circ\eta_{G}=\eta_{H}\circ\Phi
, which means that
\Phi{C}\coloneqq(\eta_{H}\circ\Phi){C}
is the
desired holomorphic
group morphism and it is uniquely determined by
\Phi
, as every solution makes
the diagram (
3.3.7
) for
\eta_{H}\circ\Phi
commute.

\ch@irxboxempty

We may rephrase Lemma
3.3.3
as functoriality.
Here we bump into a slight technical complication
28
28

28

I would like to thank Stefan
Waldmann for telling me about a similar problematic, which made me aware of what
follows and how to elegantly resolve it in the first place.
: To
define a functor, we
need to
specify
a concrete realization of the universal complexification for
every given group
G
. That is to say, one needs to pick a representative within the
singular equivalence class. We get around invoking a suitably powerful version
of the
axiom of
choice by defining the functor by means of the
construction
we will provide in
Theorem
3.3.14
to prove the existence of universal
complexifications.

Having dealt with this subtlety, we denote the category of real Lie
groups with Lie group morphisms as morphisms by ??and the category of
complex Lie groups with holomorphic group morphisms as morphisms by
??.

Corollary 3.3.4
.

Universal complexification constitutes a covariant functor

??\colon\mathsf{Lie}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Lie}{C}.

(3.3.14)

In particular, universal complexification
\,\cdot\,{C}
preserves isomorphisms.

Proof
:

Let
G
be a Lie group. Taking another look at (
3.3.87
), it is clear that
\Phi=\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{G}
induces
(\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{G}){C}=\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{G{C}}
. Let moreover be
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
and
\Psi\colon H\longrightarrow K
be Lie group morphisms. By Lemma
3.3.3
, we get the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi\circ\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;\Phi{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;(\Psi\circ\Phi){C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;\Psi{C}}

(3.3.15)

with holomorphic group morphisms
\Phi{C},\Psi{C}
and
(\Psi\circ\Phi){C}
.
By uniqueness,

(\Psi\circ\Phi){C}=\Psi{C}\circ\Phi{C}

(3.3.16)

follows. The additional statement is clear.

\ch@irxboxempty

The next question we investigate is the following: Given Lie groups
G
and
H
with

H\subseteq G
, is the universal complexification
H{C}
of
H
a
subgroup of the universal complexification
G{C}
of
G
? Encoding the subgroup as an
injective group morphism
\iota\colon H\longrightarrow G
,
Lemma
3.3.3
yields a unique holomorphic group
morphism

\iota{C}\colon H{C}\longrightarrow G{C}\qquad\textrm{with}\quad\iota{C}\circ\eta_{H}=\iota.

(3.3.17)

Thus
H{C}
is a subgroup of
G{C}
if
\iota{C}
is injective. In
Example
3.4.4
, we will see that
non-injectivity of
\eta_{G}
may lead to a negative answer to our initial question.
Conversely, assuming injectivity of
\eta_{G}
ensures injectivity of
\eta_{H}
.

Proposition 3.3.5
.

Let
G
be a Lie group with universal complexification
\eta_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
and
\iota\colon H\longrightarrow G
a group morphism.

i.)

If
\eta_{G}
and
\iota
are locally injective at the group unit, then so is

\eta_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow H{C}
.

ii.)

If
\eta_{G}
and
\iota
are injective, then so is
\eta_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow H{C}
.

Proof
:

By Lemma
3.3.3
there exists a unique holomorphic group morphism
\iota{C}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota{C}}

(3.3.18)

commutes. By assumption in
i.)
,
the edge
\eta_{G}\circ\iota
is locally injective at the group unit, as

\iota
preserves the unit as a group morphism. Thus the same is true for the
other edge
\iota{C}\circ\eta_{H}
, necessitating local injectivity at
\mathrm{e}
.
Alternatively, one may also
apply the tangent functor to the diagram (
3.3.18
)
and evaluate at the group unit to argue with injectivity instead of local
injectivity. The proof of part
ii.)

is
analogous.

\ch@irxboxempty

Thus, if
\eta_{G}
is injective, one is within the situation
H\subseteq G\subseteq G{C}

and
H\subseteq H{C}
. The idea is now that the smallest complex closed subgroup
containing
H
within
G{C}
should be isomorphic to
H{C}
. Under certain additional
assumptions this is indeed true; to check the universal property directly, we take a
brief detour to group theory. Recall that a
complement
of some subgroup
H\subseteq G
is another subgroup
K\subseteq G
with
G=HK
and
H\cap K=\{\mathrm{e}\}
. That is to say, every element
g\in G
may be uniquely written as a
product

hk\qquad\textrm{with}\quad h\in H\quad\textrm{and}\quad k\in K.

(3.3.19)

Note that, if
G=H??N
is a semidirect product,
then
N
is always a complement of
H
that is in addition a normal subgroup of
G
.
We will discuss this situation in detail and from a rather different angle as
Example
3.4.2
.

Proposition 3.3.6
(Complemented groups,
[
84
]
).

Let
G
be a group and
H\subseteq G
be a subgroup. Then the following are equivalent:

i.)

The subgroup
H
admits a complement that is normal in
G
.

ii.)

We have
H\cong G/N
for some normal subgroup
N\subseteq G
.

iii.)

Every group morphism
\phi\colon H\longrightarrow L
into another group
L
admits an extension to a group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow L
.

Proof
:

The equivalence of
i.)
and
ii.)
is clear. Let
N\subseteq G
be a normal subgroup that is also a complement of
H
and let
\phi\colon H\longrightarrow L
be a group morphism into another group
L
. We define

\Phi\colon G=HN\longrightarrow L,\quad\Phi(hn)\coloneqq\phi(h),

(3.3.20)

where
h\in H
and
n\in N
. If now
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
and
n_{1},n_{2}\in N
, then
there exists some element
n_{3}\in N
with

h_{1}n_{1}h_{2}n_{2}=h_{1}h_{2}n_{3}

(3.3.21)

as by normality
Nh_{2}=h_{2}N
. Consequently, we have

\Phi\bigl(h_{1}n_{1}h_{2}n_{2}\bigr)=\Phi(h_{1}h_{2}n_{3})=\phi(h_{1}h_{2})=\phi(h_{1})\phi(h_{2})=\Phi(h_{1}n_{1})\Phi(h_{2}n_{2})

(3.3.22)

and thus
\Phi
is a group morphism with
\Phi\big|_{H}=\phi
. Alternatively, one
may use
ii.)
with the quotient projection
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow H
and define
\Phi\coloneqq\phi\circ\pi
as the pullback of

\phi
along
\pi
. This recovers the
\Phi
we have defined. Assume conversely
that
iii.)
holds and let
\phi\colon H\longrightarrow H
be
\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{H}
. By assumption, there exists a group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
with
\Phi(h)=h
for all
h\in H
. We set
N\coloneqq\ker\Phi
, which yields a normal subgroup of
G
. If
g\in H\cap N
, then

g\in\ker\phi=\{\mathrm{e}_{H}\},\qquad\textrm{i.e.}\quad H\cap N=\{\mathrm{e}_{H}\}.

(3.3.23)

If
g\in G
, then
n\coloneqq\Phi(g)^{-1}g
fulfils

\Phi(n)=\mathrm{e}_{H}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Phi(g)n=\Phi(g)\Phi(g)^{-1}g=g

(3.3.24)

and thus
G=HN
. This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Taking
H=\{\mathrm{e}\}
as the trivial group, it is clear that the extension

iii.)
is highly non-unique in general: Any group
morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow L
restricts to the trivial morphism

\phi\colon\{\mathrm{e}\}\longrightarrow L

(3.3.25)

The preceding considerations pass to topological groups and continuous group
morphisms without complications and thus in particular to Lie groups.

Corollary 3.3.7
.

Let
G
be a topological group and
H\subseteq G
be a closed subgroup. Then the following are equivalent:

i.)

The subgroup
H
admits a complement that is normal and closed in
G
.

ii.)

We have
H\cong G/N
as topological groups for some normal subgroup
N\subseteq G
.

iii.)

Every continuous group morphism
\phi\colon H\longrightarrow L
into another group
L
admits an extension to a continuous group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow L
.

Having a normal and closed complement now yields a sufficient condition for
H{C}\subseteq G{C}
.

Proposition 3.3.8
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
H\subseteq G
be a closed subgroup with

0<??H<\dim G

(3.3.26)

admitting a normal as well as closed complement. Assume moreover that
\eta_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is injective. Then
H{C}
is isomorphic to a
subgroup of
G{C}
.

Proof
:

As already noted, injectivity of
\eta_{G}
lets us view
H
as a subgroup of

G{C}
.
This allows us to consider the smallest complex closed subgroup
H{C}

containing

H
within
G{C}

and
\eta_{H}\colon H\longrightarrow H{C}
be the inclusion of
H
into

H{C}
.
29
29

29

Which is, in accordance with what we expect in view of
Proposition
3.3.5
, is indeed
injective.
We verify that
(H{C},\eta_{H})
possesses the universal property of
the universal complexification. To this end, let
\phi\colon H\longrightarrow L

be a Lie group morphism into a complex Lie group
L
. Then, by assumption and
Proposition
3.3.7
, there exists an extension of
\phi

to a Lie group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow L
. By the universal
property of
G{C}
, we may extend
\Phi
even further to a unique holomorphic
group morphism
\Phi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow L
. Setting
\phi{C}\coloneqq\Phi{C}\big|_{H{C}}
yields a holomorphic group morphism with

\phi{C}(h)=\Phi{C}(h)=\Phi(h)=\phi(h)\qquad\textrm{for all }h\in H.

It remains to prove the uniqueness of
\phi{C}
, which turns out to be a bit more
involved. If the subgroup
N\subseteq G
is a closed normal complement of
H
, we
may define
N{C}
in the same manner as
H{C}
. Moreover,

[
92
, Exercise 15.1.3(d)]
asserts that the resulting closed
complex subgroup
N{C}\subseteq G{C}
is normal again. We first establish that

H{C}
and
N{C}
are complementary within
G{C}
. To prove this, we tacitly use
two – rather reasonable – facts about universal complexification, which we
are going to demonstrate in
Theorem
3.3.14
and
Lemma
3.3.30
without using what we are
proving right now.

i.)

If
G
is connected, then so is
G{C}
.

ii.)

If the morphism
\eta
is locally injective, then the Lie algebra of the
universal complexification
G{C}
is given by the complexification

\mathfrak{g}{C}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
G
.

Assuming this and recalling the injectivity of
\eta_{H}
and
\eta_{N}
by
Proposition
3.3.5
, we see that the
condition
H\cap N=\{\mathrm{e}\}
implies
\mathfrak{g}\cap\mathfrak{n}=\{0\}
and thus
\mathfrak{g}{C}\cap\mathfrak{n}{C}=\{0\}
, as well. Exponentiating, this means that there exist
zero neighbourhoods
U_{H}\subseteq H{C}
and
U_{N}\subseteq N{C}
with
U_{H}\cap U_{N}=\{\mathrm{e}\}
, which implies
H{C}\cap N{C}=\{\mathrm{e}\}
.

Similarly, investing moreover
G=HN
implies that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}
as vector spaces and thus
\mathfrak{g}{C}=\mathfrak{h}{C}\oplus\mathfrak{n}{C}
. Consequently, the product
\chi\coloneqq\exp_{H{C}}\times\exp_{N{C}}
maps
\mathfrak{g}{C}
onto a neighbourhood of the group unit
within
G{C}
, as a
suitable restriction constitutes an exponential chart of the second kind by

[
92
, Lem. 9.2.6 (ii.)]
. By connectedness of
G
,
also
G{C}

is connected and thus every element of
G{C}
is a finite product of elements in
the image of
\chi
, proving
G{C}=H{C}N{C}
by normality of
N{C}
as
desired.

Let now
\psi{C}\colon H{C}\longrightarrow L
be another holomorphic
group morphism with
\psi{C}(h)=\phi(h)
for all
h\in H
. By what we have
shown and Proposition
3.3.7
, we may extend

\psi{C}
to a group morphism
\Psi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow L
;
taking another look at (
3.3.20
), we moreover note
that our construction even yields a holomorphic extension
\Psi{C}
. But then

\Psi{C}(hn)=\psi{C}(h)=\phi(h)=\Phi(hn)

(3.3.27)

holds for all
h\in H
and
n\in N
. As
\Phi{C}
was the unique holomorphic
extension of the morphism
{\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow L}
to
G{C}
, this
implies
\Psi{C}=\Phi{C}
and thus
\phi{C}=\psi{C}
by restriction to the
subgroup
H{C}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Corollary 3.3.9
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
H\subseteq G
be a closed subgroup with

0<\dim H<\dim G

(3.3.28)

admitting a normal as well as closed complement
N\subseteq G
. Assume moreover
that
\eta_{G}
is locally injective. Then
H{C}
and
N{C}
are complementary
subgroups of
G{C}
and
N{C}
is normal.

This leaves us with the problem of when
\eta_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is
injective. We postpone this investigation until we have a concrete construction
of
G{C}
. Instead, we proceed with a simple example that
covers the case of discrete subgroups and will prove useful for the understanding of
universal complexifications of disconnected groups.

Example 3.3.10
(Discrete Groups).

Let
G
be a discrete Lie group, i.e. a countable group endowed
with the discrete topology. As
\{g\}
is an open subset of

G
for all
g\in G
, we get an atlas of
G
by means of the maps

z_{g}\colon\{g\}\longrightarrow\{0\},\quad z_{g}(g)\coloneqq 0.

(3.3.29)

The idea is now to view
\{0\}\cong\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{0}
as a
zero dimensional
complex
vector space, so we even
have a holomorphic atlas for
G
. Consequently, we claim that
G=G{C}
for similar reasons: any Lie group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
into some complex Lie group
H
is holomorphic for this atlas:
the compositions
\Phi\circ z_{g}^{-1}
are constant.

While this example is trivial, we are
going encounter this situation in the course of the construction of the universal
complexification. The component groups
G/G_{0}
of any Lie group
G

are discrete, where ??denotes the connected component of the
group unit. The
crucial idea is then going to be that they should not change under
complexification beyond the alteration of perspective just described. These
ideas are
at the core of Remark
3.3.33
.

Our next goal is to prove that every Lie group admits a universal complexification. We begin by restating Lie’s seminal theorems in the incarnations we will need.

Theorem 3.3.11
(Lie’s second Theorem,
[
92
, Thm. 9.5.9]
or
[
59
, Thm. 1.8.3]
).

Let
G
and
H
be Lie groups. Assume furthermore
that
G
is connected as well as simply connected and let
\phi\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
be a morphism of Lie
algebras. Then there exists a unique Lie group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
such that
T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi=\phi
. If
G
and
H
are complex Lie groups and
\phi
is
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear, then
\Phi
is holomorphic.

Diagrammatically, the situation is thus the following:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(3.3.30)

As a first application, we provide a simple criterion to identify universal
complexifications in practice.

Proposition 3.3.12
.

Let
H
be a connected and simply connected complex Lie group and let
G\subseteq H
be a real closed connected subgroup such that
\operatorname{Lie}(G){C}\cong\operatorname{Lie}(H)
.
Then
G{C}\coloneqq H
with the inclusion
\eta\colon G\hookrightarrow H
is
the universal complexification of
G
.

Proof
:

Let
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H^{\prime}
be a Lie group morphism into another complex Lie group
H^{\prime}
. By the universal property of the algebra complexification, its tangent map

\phi\coloneqq T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi\colon\operatorname{Lie}(G)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Lie}(H^{\prime})

(3.3.31)

has a unique extension
\phi{C}\colon\operatorname{Lie}(G){C}=\operatorname{Lie}(H)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Lie}(H^{\prime})
to a morphism of complex Lie algebras. By Theorem
3.3.11
, we may integrate
\phi{C}
to a unique holomorphic group morphism
\Phi{C}\colon H\longrightarrow H^{\prime}
with tangent map
\phi{C}
. By construction, we have

T_{\mathrm{e}}\bigl(\Phi{C}\circ\eta\bigr)=\phi{C}\circ\iota^{\prime}=\phi=T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi

(
\star
)

where
\iota^{\prime}
is the inclusion
\operatorname{Lie}(G)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Lie}(H)
. By
connectedness of
G
, this implies

\Phi{C}\circ\eta=\Phi.

If
\Psi\colon H\longrightarrow H^{\prime}
constitutes another holomorphic group
morphism with

\Psi\circ\eta=\Phi
, then its tangent map fulfils

T_{\mathrm{e}}\Psi\big|_{\operatorname{Lie}(G)}=T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi

by (

⋆

‣
Proof
). As
\operatorname{Lie}(G)

generates
\operatorname{Lie}(H)=\operatorname{Lie}(G){C}
as a complex vector space, this in turn
implies
T_{\mathrm{e}}\Psi=T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi{C}
by
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linearity and thus
\Psi=\Phi{C}
by
connectedness.

\ch@irxboxempty

We resume our review of Lie’s theorems.

Theorem 3.3.13
(Cartan-Lie’s Theorem,
[
92
, Thm. 9.4.11]
,

[
59
, Thm. 1.14.3]
).

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite dimensional Lie algebra.

i.)

There exists a connected and simply connected Lie
group
G
with
\operatorname{Lie}(G)=\mathfrak{g}
.

ii.)

The group
G
from
i.)
is unique up to Lie group
isomorphism.

iii.)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is complex, then
G
from
i.)
may
be endowed with the structure of a complex Lie group. In this case,
G
is
unique up to a biholomorphic group isomorphism.

Putting both of Lie’s theorems together and demanding
H
to be simply
connected gives now the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{1}\;\exp_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(3.3.32)

Going even further, one can integrate any Lie algebra morphism to a
Lie group morphism between the corresponding connected and simply
connected Lie groups. Note that to apply Theorem
3.3.11
,
we needed
G
to be simply connected regardless.

The idea to construct a
universal complexification in the sense of
Definition
3.3.1
is now to
integrate the embedding
\iota\colon\mathfrak{g}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}{C}

of vector spaces in the sense of (
3.3.32
). Here,
??is the vector space complexification of
\mathfrak{g}

endowed with the
Lie bracket

\big[\xi\mathbin{\otimes}z,\chi\mathbin{\otimes}w\big]=[\xi,\chi]\mathbin{\otimes}(z\cdot w)\quad\iff\quad\big[z\xi,w\chi\big]=(z´w)\cdot[\xi,\chi]

(3.3.33)

for
\xi,\eta\in\mathfrak{g}
and
z,w\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
or in the other model

\big[\xi_{1}+\mathrm{i}\xi_{2},\chi_{1}+\mathrm{i}\chi_{2}\big]=[\xi_{1},\chi_{1}]+\mathrm{i}[\xi_{2},\chi_{2}]

(3.3.34)

for
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}
. Note that this is consistent both
with (
3.3.3
) and with extending the adjoint
mappings

??\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g},\quad\operatorname{\mathrm{ad}}_{\xi}(\chi)\coloneqq[\xi,\chi]

as linear maps to
\mathfrak{g}{C}
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
by the universal
property.

However, we only get the diagram (
3.3.32
) if
G
is simply connected, which is a rather strong
assumption we do not want to make. To resolve this, we will first complexify the universal
covering group
\widetilde{G}
of
G
as an intermediate step and quotient out the
redundancy afterwards. Recall that a covering is a locally trivial fiber bundle

??\colon X\longrightarrow Y

in the category of topological spaces, whose fibers are discrete. In this situation,
we say that
X
covers
Y
. Unwrapping the definition, this means that
X,Y
are
topological spaces, the projection
p
is continuous and that the following
holds:

Every
y\in Y
has an open neighbourhood
V
, whose preimage
p^{-1}(V)
is the
disjoint union of finitely many open sets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}\subseteq X
such that
p\big|_{U_{j}}\colon U_{j}\longrightarrow Y
is a homeomorphism
for
j=1,\ldots,k
. One calls
V
is an evenly covered neighbourhood of
y
in this
case. Note that the integer
k
typically depends on the point
y
, but is locally
constant. We are going to utilize both the discreteness of the fibers
and the local bijectivity in the sequel. For a comprehensive discussion of covering
spaces for topological spaces, we refer to the textbooks

[
139
, Sec. 53]
,
[
85
, Sec. 1.3]
and

[
92
, Appendix A]
.

Under mild connectedness assumptions
30
30

30

Namely, path connectedness, local path
connectedness and semi-locally simple connectedness. Many authors refer to this
situation as
well connected
.
on
Y
, there is always a simply connected
topological space
\widetilde{Y}
covering
Y
by

[
92
, Thm. A.2.12]
. It is called universal covering, as it covers
all other covering spaces of
Y
by a straightforward application of the Lifting
Theorem
[
92
, Thm. A.2.9]
, and as it is unique up to
fiber preserving homeomorphism by
[
92
, Cor. A.2.10]
. Taking
Y\coloneqq G
as a connected Lie group suffices to guarantee the existence of a
universal covering space ??, which turns out to be a Lie group itself
by
[
92
, Cor. 9.4.7]
. Moreover, the covering morphism
p\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow G
is then a group morphism. By local injectivity, we
moreover have
\operatorname{Lie}(G)=\operatorname{Lie}(\widetilde{G})
.

Finally, for our purposes the fundamental group ??of
G
is
defined as the Lie subgroup
\pi_{1}(G)\coloneqq\ker p
. It is always a central
discrete
subgroup of
\widetilde{G}
by
[
92
, Thm. 9.5.4]
. We have gathered all
ingredients to prove the existence of universal complexifications.

Theorem 3.3.14
(Universal Complexification,
[
92
, Thm. 15.1.4]
).

Let
G
be a Lie group. Then there exists a universal complexification

(G{C},\eta)
of
G
and it is unique up to a unique biholomorphic group morphism.

Proof
:

We have already noted the uniqueness. Assume
G
to be connected. Denoting the universal covering group
of
G
by
p\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow G
, we have the
commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}{C}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp_{\widetilde{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp_{\widetilde{G}{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(
\#
)

where the right half is (
3.3.32
) with
\phi\coloneqq\iota
, i.e.
\iota(\xi)=\xi
. Note that the universal covering group is simply
connected, so the assumptions of Theorem
3.3.11
are
indeed fulfilled and we get
\widetilde{\eta},\iota
and
\widetilde{G}{C}
such that (

#

‣
Proof
) commutes.

By local bijectivity of the covering
p
, its
kernel
\pi_{1}(G)\subseteq\widetilde{G}
is a discrete normal
subgroup. Indeed, we even have
\pi_{1}(G)\subseteq\mathscr{Z}(\widetilde{G})
, where

??\coloneqq\bigl\{g\in G\;|\;\forall_{h\in G}\colon gh=hg\bigr\}

(3.3.35)

denotes the center of a group
G
, which always constitutes a normal
subgroup:

Given
h\in\pi_{1}(G)
and
g\in\widetilde{G}
we
find a path
\gamma
from
g
to
\mathrm{e}
in
\widetilde{G}
. This induces a path

\sigma(t)\coloneqq\gamma(t)h\gamma^{-1}(t)

(3.3.36)

from
ghg^{-1}
to
h
, which is entirely contained in
\pi_{1}(G)
. But
\pi_{1}(G)

is discrete, so this
implies
\sigma\equiv\mathrm{e}
, i.e.
h\in\mathscr{Z}(\widetilde{G})
.

Let now

g\in\pi_{1}(G)\subseteq\mathscr{Z}(\widetilde{G})
, then we find a Lie algebra
element
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
with
g=\exp_{\widetilde{G}}\xi
and
\operatorname{\mathrm{spec}}(\operatorname{\mathrm{ad}}_{\xi})\subseteq 2\pi\mathrm{i}\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}
by

[
92
, Theorem 14.2.8]
. Consequently,

\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{\widetilde{\eta}(g)}\chi=\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{\widetilde{\eta}(\exp_{\widetilde{G}}\xi)}\chi\overset{\eqref{eq:UniversalComplexificationProof0}}{=}\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{\exp_{\widetilde{G}{C}}(\iota(\xi))}\chi=\exp_{\operatorname{\mathrm{End}}(\mathfrak{g}{C})}\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{ad}}_{\iota(\xi)}\bigr)\chi=\chi

holds for all
\chi\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
. In the final step we have used that the
only nilpotent matrix exponentiating to the identity is the zero matrix.
Summarizing, we have shown

\widetilde{\eta}\bigl(\pi_{1}(G)\bigr)\subseteq\ker\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{\widetilde{G}{C}}\bigr)=\mathscr{Z}(\widetilde{G}{C}),

(3.3.37)

where the equality holds by
[
92
, Theorem 14.2.8]
. We denote
the closure of the
complex group span of some subset
S
of a complex Lie group by
??, which constitutes a normal subgroup whenever

S
was by continuity of the group operations. By

[
92
, Theorem 11.1.5]
we can quotient by such a group to
obtain a complex Lie group. We define

G{C}\coloneqq\widetilde{G}{C}\Big/\big<\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))\big>_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C},\quad\eta\bigl(p(g)\bigr)\coloneqq\pi\bigl(\widetilde{\eta}(g)\bigr)

(3.3.38)

with the quotient projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{G}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
. In
terms of a commutative diagram, the situation is

(3.3.39)

We prove that
(G{C},\eta)
is the universal complexification of
G
, and first
check that
\eta
is smooth. By definition, we have
\eta\circ p=\pi\circ\widetilde{\eta}
. As
\pi
and
\widetilde{\eta}
are smooth, the same is true
for
\eta\circ p
. This in turn implies smoothness of
\eta
, as
p
is a local
diffeomorphism. Existence of local inverses of
p
, i.e. of smooth sections, also
yields that
\eta
is a
group morphism.

It remains to check the universal property. To this end, let

\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
be a Lie group morphism into a
complex Lie group
H
with tangent map
\phi\coloneqq T_{\mathrm{e}_{G}}\Phi\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
. This induces a

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear Lie algebra morphism
\phi{C}\colon\mathfrak{g}{C}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
by universal complexification of vector spaces. This puts us into the position to once again apply
Theorem
3.3.11
to integrate
\phi{C}
, i.e. we
get the commutative diagram

Notice now that

T_{\mathrm{e}_{\widetilde{G}}}\bigl(\Phi\circ p\bigr)=\phi\circ\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\phi=\phi{C}\circ\iota=T_{\mathrm{e}_{\widetilde{G}}}\bigl(\widetilde{\Phi}{C}\circ\widetilde{\eta}\bigr),

(
\star
)

which implies
\Phi\circ p=\widetilde{\Phi}{C}\circ\widetilde{\eta}

by connectedness of
\widetilde{G}
, see

[
92
, Cor. 9.2.12]
. In particular, assuming
g\in\pi_{1}(G)
, this yields

0=(\widetilde{\Phi}{C}\circ\widetilde{\eta})(g),\qquad\textrm{i.e.}\quad\widetilde{\eta}\bigl(\pi_{1}(G)\bigr)\subseteq\ker\widetilde{\Phi}{C}.

(3.3.40)

Consequently,
\widetilde{\Phi}{C}
descends to the quotient
G{C}
, inducing a
holomorphic group morphism
\Phi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H
. We moreover
compute

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{e}_{G}}(\Phi{C}\circ\eta)=T_{\mathrm{e}_{\widetilde{G}}}(\Phi{C}\circ\eta\circ p)=T_{\mathrm{e}_{\widetilde{G}}}(\Phi{C}\circ\pi\circ\widetilde{\eta})=T_{\mathrm{e}_{\widetilde{G}}}(\widetilde{\Phi}{C}\circ\widetilde{\eta})=T_{\mathrm{e}_{\widetilde{G}}}(\Phi\circ p)=T_{\mathrm{e}_{G}}\Phi,

where we have used
T_{\mathrm{e}_{\widetilde{G}}}p=\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, the definition of

\eta
, the characteristic property of the quotient and
(

⋆

‣
Proof
). By connectedness,
\Phi{C}\circ\eta=\Phi

follows, as required in
Definition
3.3.1
. As complex multiples of any basis of

\mathfrak{g}
generate
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})
, the
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear tangent mapping
T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi{C}
is uniquely determined by
\phi
. By connectedness this also determines

\Phi{C}
uniquely. This completes the proof for connected
G
.

Let now
G
be disconnected and write
G_{0}
for the connected
component of the group unit. As
G_{0}
is connected, it possesses a
universal complexification
(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}},\eta_{0})
by what we have already
established. For fixed
g\in G
this gives the commutative diagram

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

by Lemma
3.3.3
applied to the restriction

\operatorname{\mathrm{Conj}}_{g}\big|_{G_{0}}
of the conjugation from (
3.0.2
). The uniqueness
gives us the explicit formula

\operatorname{\mathrm{Conj}}_{g,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(h)=\eta_{0}(g)h\eta_{0}(g^{-1}),\qquad h\in G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}},

as this definition makes (
3.3.13
) commute due to
\eta_{0}
being
a group morphism. In particular, we may view this as a
group morphism

c\colon G_{0}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{Aut}}(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}),\quad c_{g}(h)\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathrm{Conj}}_{g,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(h)=\eta_{0}(g)h\eta_{0}(g^{-1}).

(
*
)

Consider now the outer semidirect product
S\coloneqq G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\ltimes_{c}G
together with the subset

K\coloneqq\bigl\{\bigl(\eta_{0}(g),g^{-1}\bigr)\in S\colon g\in G_{0}\bigr\}.

(
\dagger
)

We claim that
K
is a normal subgroup of
S
. To show this, let
first
g,h\in G_{0}
and compute

\displaystyle\bigl(\eta_{0}(g),g^{-1}\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\eta_{0}(h),h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\eta_{0}(g)c_{g^{-1}}(\eta_{0}(h)),g^{-1}h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\eta_{0}(g)\eta_{0}(g^{-1})\eta_{0}(h)\eta_{0}(g),(hg)^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\eta_{0}(hg),(hg)^{-1}\bigr)\in K,

where we have used (

∗

‣
Proof
) and
that
G_{0}
is a subgroup. Let
g\in G_{0}
and
(z,h)\in S
. Then

\displaystyle(z,h)\cdot\bigl(\eta_{0}(g),g^{-1}\bigr)\cdot\bigl(c_{h^{-1}}(z^{-1}),h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(z,h)\cdot\bigl(\eta_{0}(g),g^{-1}\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\eta_{0}(h^{-1})z^{-1}\eta_{0}(h),h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(z,h)\cdot\bigl(\eta_{0}(g)c_{g^{-1}}(\eta_{0}(h^{-1})z^{-1}\eta_{0}(h)),g^{-1}h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(z,h)\cdot\bigl(\eta_{0}(h^{-1})z^{-1}\eta_{0}(hg),g^{-1}h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(zc_{h}(\eta_{0}(h^{-1})z^{-1}\eta_{0}(hg)),hg^{-1}h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(z\eta_{0}(h)(\eta_{0}(h^{-1})z^{-1}\eta_{0}(hg))\eta_{0}(h^{-1}),hg^{-1}h^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\eta_{0}(hgh^{-1}),hg^{-1}h^{-1}\bigr)\in K,

which
proves the normality. Note furthermore that
K
is closed by continuity of group inversion and
\eta_{0}
. We are thus in a position to define

G{C}\coloneqq S\big/K\quad\textrm{and}\quad\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C},\quad\eta(g)\coloneqq\big[(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}},g)\big]

(3.3.41)

as a real Lie group and claim that
(G{C},\eta)
is the universal complexification of
G
. To this end, we compute

\eta(g)\eta(h)=\big[(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}},g)\cdot(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}},h)\big]=\big[(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}c_{g}(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}),gh)\big]=[(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}},gh)]=\eta(gh)

for
g,h\in G
, i.e.
\eta
is a group morphism. As the map
G\ni g\mapsto(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}},g)\in S
is continuous and induces

\eta
, the smoothness of
\eta
follows from the quotient projection being a surjective submersion.

Next, we construct a holomorphic atlas for
G{C}
. To this end, let
(U_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
be a holomorphic atlas of
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
. Note that setting
h\coloneqq g^{-1}
in (

‡

‣
Proof
) gives
(z,g)\sim(z\eta_{0}(g^{-1}),\mathrm{e}_{G})
, i.e. every equivalence class
[(z,g)]
contains a unique representative of the form
(z^{\prime},e_{G})
. Consequently, the sets
V_{\alpha}\coloneqq[U_{\alpha}\times\{\mathrm{e}_{G}\}]
for
\alpha\in J
cover
G{C}
. Moreover, the mappings

w_{\alpha}\colon V_{\alpha}\longrightarrow z_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha}),\quad w_{\alpha}\bigl([z,\mathrm{e}_{G}]\bigr)\coloneqq z_{\alpha}(z)

are well defined biholomorphisms. This provides the desired holomorphic atlas for

G{C}
.

Let now
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
be a Lie
group morphism into a complex Lie group
H
. Its restriction
\Phi_{0}\coloneqq\Phi\big|_{G_{0}}
induces a unique holomorphic group morphism

\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\colon G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\longrightarrow H

satisfying
\Phi=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\circ\eta_{0}
by the universal property of
(G_{0},\eta_{0})
. We consider

\Phi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H,\quad\Phi{C}\bigl([(z,g)]\bigr)\coloneqq\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(z)\Phi(g),

which is well defined: varying
h\in G_{0}
we get all other representatives of
(z,g)\in S
as

(z,g)\cdot(\eta_{0}(h),h^{-1})=\bigl(zc_{g}(\eta_{0}(h)),gh^{-1}\bigr)\overset{\eqref{eq:UniversalComplexificationProof1}}{=}\bigl(z\eta_{0}(ghg^{-1}),gh^{-1}\bigr).

(
{\ddagger}
)

Now, as
\Phi=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\circ\eta_{0}
, we get

\displaystyle\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\bigl(z\eta_{0}(ghg^{-1})\bigr)\cdot\Phi(gh^{-1})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(z)\cdot\Phi(ghg^{-1})\cdot\Phi(gh^{-1})

(3.3.42)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(z)\cdot\Phi(g)

(3.3.43)

\displaystyle=\Phi{C}(z,g).

(3.3.44)

Thus the value of
\Phi{C}
is indeed independent of
the chosen representative. For
z,z^{\prime}\in G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
and

g,g^{\prime}\in G
we compute

\displaystyle\Phi{C}\bigl([(z,g)]\cdot[(z^{\prime},g^{\prime})]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Phi{C}\bigl([(z\eta_{0}(g)z^{\prime}\eta_{0}(g^{-1}),gg^{\prime})]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\bigl(z\eta_{0}(g)z^{\prime}\eta_{0}(g^{-1})\bigr)\Phi(gg^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(z)\cdot\Phi(g)\cdot\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(z^{\prime})\cdot\Phi(g^{-1})\cdot\Phi(gg^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Phi{C}\bigl([(z,g)]\bigr)\cdot\Phi{C}\bigl([(z^{\prime},g^{\prime})]\bigr),

showing
\Phi{C}
is a group morphism. Moreover, the description of
\Phi{C}
in the atlas
(V_{\alpha},w_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
coincides with the description of
\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
in the atlas
(U_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
, i.e.

\Phi{C}\circ w_{\alpha}^{-1}\Big|_{w_{\alpha}([z,\mathrm{e}_{G}])}=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\circ z_{\alpha}^{-1}\Big|_{z_{\alpha}(z)}

for all
\alpha\in J
and
z\in U_{\alpha}
. This implies holomorphicity of
\Phi{C}
at once.
By definition it is also true that

\Phi{C}\bigl(\eta(g)\bigr)=\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}})\cdot\Phi(g)=\Phi(g).

Thus
\Phi_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
makes the diagram
(
3.3.7
) commute. Conversely, the morphism
property and the preceding condition already determine

\Phi{C}
uniquely. This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Taking a closer look at our construction and keeping our notation, we obtain the
following additional well known properties of the universal complexification, all of
which can be found in
[
92
, Sec. 15.1 & Exercises]
.

Corollary 3.3.15
.

Let
G
be a Lie group with universal complexification

(G{C},\eta)
.

i.)

We have
\dim{C}G{C}\leq\dim{R}G
.

ii.)

If
G
is linear, then
\eta
is injective.

If
G
is connected, we moreover have the following:

iii.)

The subgroup
\eta(\pi_{1}(G))
is central.

iv.)

The pair
(\widetilde{G}{C},\widetilde{\eta})
constitutes the
universal complexification of the universal covering group
\widetilde{G}

of
G
.

v.)

If
G
is simply connected, the same is true for its
universal complexification
G{C}
. Moreover,

T_{\mathrm{e}}\eta\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}{C}

is injective,
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})\cong\mathfrak{g}{C}
and
\dim{C}G{C}=\dim{R}G
.

vi.)

If
G
is semisimple, then
\dim{C}G{C}=\dim{R}G
.

Proof
:

To see the first part, recall first that
\dim{C}(\widetilde{G}{C})=\dim{R}(\widetilde{G})=\dim{R}(G)
. Thus, in the connected case, the
quotient
G{C}
of
\widetilde{G}
as defined in
(
3.3.38
) fulfils

dim{C}(G{C})\leq\dim{R}(G).

(3.3.45)

For the disconnected case we first note

\displaystyle\dim{R}\bigl(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\rtimes G\bigr)

\displaystyle=\dim{R}(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}})\cdot\dim{R}(G)

(3.3.46)

\displaystyle=2\cdot\dim{C}(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}})\cdot\dim{R}(G)

(3.3.47)

\displaystyle\leq 2\dim{R}(G)^{2},

(3.3.48)

as semidirect products and direct products coincide as manifolds and the unit
component
G_{0}
fulfils
\dim{R}(G_{0})=\dim{R}(G)
. Taking another look at
(

†

‣
Proof
), we see that

\dim{R}(K)=\dim{R}(G).

(3.3.49)

Putting everything together and using (
3.3.41
) thus
yields
\dim{C}(G{C})\leq\dim{R}(G)
also in the disconnected case.

\rho\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})

As
\rho
is injective, the same is true for
\eta
regardless of the behaviour of
\rho{C}
.

We have already shown
iii.)
during the proof of Theorem
3.3.14
. Going through the construction with
G=\widetilde{G}
, we note
\pi_{1}(G)
and thus
\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))
are trivial, i.e. we quotient out nothing in (
3.3.38
) and get
G{C}=\widetilde{G}{C}
as well as
T_{\mathrm{e}}\eta=\iota
, proving
iv.)
and
v.)
.

For the final statement recall that a semisimple group
G
has discrete center

\mathscr{Z}(G)
. By
iii.)
, this implies that

\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))\subseteq\widetilde{G}{C}
is discrete and as such both
complex and closed, see again Example
3.3.10
. Thus the
quotient in
(
3.3.38
) does not lose dimensions and from

i.)
we infer

vi.)
.

The observation from
vi.)
is due to
[
93
]
.
Our next goal is to describe holomorphicity on a universal complexification in a
left invariant manner. To this end, we once again choose a basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. This induces the basis

(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n},\mathrm{i}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{i}\mathit{e}_{n})
of the
complexification
\mathfrak{g}{C}
and the canonical complex structure
J_{\mathrm{can}}

becomes multiplication with the imaginary unit
\mathrm{i}
. Alternatively, one may consider
the Wirtinger
31
31

31

Wilhelm Wirtinger (1865-1945) was an Austrian function
theorist, who first proposed generalizing eigenvalues to spectra. His
doctoral students include Wilhelm Blaschke, Kurt Gödel and Leopold Vietoris.
linear
combinations

\mathit{f}_{k}\coloneqq\frac{\mathit{e}_{k}-\mathrm{i}\mathit{e}_{k}}{2}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\overline{{\mathit{f}}}_{k}\coloneqq\frac{\mathit{e}_{k}+\mathrm{i}\mathit{e}_{k}}{2}\qquad\textrm{for }k=1,\ldots,n,

(3.3.50)

which constitute another basis of
\mathfrak{g}{C}
. Note that the left translations of

f_{k}
span the holomorphic tangent bundle, whereas the ones of
\overline{{f}}_{k}
generate the
antiholomorphic one.

Proposition 3.3.16
(Left invariant Cauchy-Riemann equations).

Let
G
be a Lie group such that
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\operatorname{Lie}(G){C}
and let
\phi\colon G{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
be a function on the universal complexification
G{C}
of
G
. Then the following are equivalent:

i.)

The function
\phi
is holomorphic.

ii.)

We have
\mathscr{L}(\mathrm{i}\mathit{e}_{k})\phi=\mathrm{i}\cdot\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{k})\phi
for

k=1,\ldots,n
.

iii.)

We have
\mathscr{L}(\overline{{\mathit{f}}}_{k})\phi=0
for
k=1,\ldots,n
.

If one – and thus all – of the above conditions are fulfilled, then

\mathscr{L}(\mathit{e}_{k})\phi\in\mathscr{H}(G)\qquad\textrm{for }k=1,\ldots,n.

(3.3.51)

Proof
:

The equivalence of
ii.)
and
iii.)
is clear by (
3.3.50
). As the Lie exponential is the flow of the left invariant vector field, we have

\mathscr{L}_{X_{\mathit{e}_{k}}}\phi(g)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\phi\bigl(g\exp_{G{C}}(t\mathit{e}_{k})\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}

(3.3.52)

for all
g\in G
. Now,
ii.)
and
iii.)
are exactly the Cauchy-Riemann equations for

\phi\circ\exp_{G{C}}\colon\operatorname{Lie}(G){C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(3.3.53)

and as we may check holomorphicity in the exponential atlas, this establishes the remaining equivalences. The additional statement is now clear.

\ch@irxboxempty

It turns out that the almost complex structure of a complex Lie group arising from universal complexification
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
always comes with a complex conjugation.

Proposition 3.3.17
(Complex conjugation,

[
92
, Thm. 15.1.4
iv.)
]
).

Let
G
be a Lie group with universal complexification
(G{C},\eta)
. Then there
exists a unique antiholomorphic group morphism

??\colon G{C}\longrightarrow G{C}

(3.3.54)

such that
\sigma^{2}=\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{G{C}}
and fixed point group

\bigl\{g\in G_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C},0}\colon\sigma(g)=g\bigr\}=\eta(G_{0}),

(3.3.55)

where
G_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C},0}\subseteq G{C}
denotes the connected component of the group unit.

We call
\sigma\colon G{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
the
complex conjugation
of
G{C}
and refer to ??as the
complex conjugated group
to
G
. As usual, we indicate the corresponding group with an additional subscript when several complex conjugations are involved. For
G=\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
this reproduces the usual complex conjugation.

Proof
(of Proposition
3.3.17
):

We define a complex Lie algebra
\overline{{\mathfrak{g}}}{C}
by endowing
\mathfrak{g}{C}
with the same addition and Lie bracket, but a new multiplication by scalars

??\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\times\overline{{\mathfrak{g}}}{C}\longrightarrow\overline{{\mathfrak{g}}}{C},\quad z\odot\overline{{\xi}}=\overline{{\overline{{z}}\xi}},

(
\flat
)

where we distinguish the elements of
\overline{{\mathfrak{g}}}{C}
by writing

\overline{{\xi}}
.
32
32

32

I would like to thank Stefan Waldmann for making me aware of
this extraordinarily useful notational convention.
As
\overline{{\mathfrak{g}}}{C}
and

\mathfrak{g}{C}
are
isomorphic as real vector spaces, we may replace the latter with the former in our
construction in Theorem
3.3.14
. This yields another
pair
(\overline{{G}}{C},\overline{{\eta}})
of complex Lie group and Lie group morphism

\overline{{\eta}}\colon G\longrightarrow\overline{{G}}{C}
. As real Lie groups,
\overline{{G}}{C}

and
G{C}
coincide, but
\overline{{G}}{C}
does not fulfil the universal property of the
universal complexification. The universal property of
G{C}
yields the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{G}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\;\overline{\sigma}}

with a unique holomorphic group morphism
\overline{{\sigma}}
. Let now

\sigma\colon G{C}\longrightarrow G{C},\quad\sigma\coloneqq\overline{{\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}}\circ\overline{\sigma},

where
\overline{{\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}}\colon\overline{G}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
is given by

\overline{{\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}}(g)\coloneqq g
. Note that
\overline{{\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}}\circ\overline{{\eta}}=\eta
. By
(

♭

‣
Proof
), the mapping
\overline{{\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}}
is antiholomorphic and
thus the same is true for
\sigma
as a composition of a holomorphic with an
anthiholomorphic map. By construction, we moreover have

\sigma\circ\eta=\overline{{\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}}\circ\overline{\sigma}\circ\eta=\overline{{\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}}\circ\overline{{\eta}}=\eta.

(
\sharp
)

Thus
\sigma^{2}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
is holomorphic and
fulfils
\sigma^{2}\circ\eta=\eta
. The
universal property asserts that the unique holomorphic group
morphism with this feature is given by
\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{G{C}}
, proving that
\sigma
is an
involution.
Moreover, (

♯

‣
Proof
) proves the inclusion “
\supseteq
”
in (
3.3.55
). Let conversely be
g\in G_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C},0}
be such
that
\sigma(g)=g
. As
g
is in the unit component of the group unit, we find

\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
such that
g=\exp(\xi_{1})\cdots\exp(\xi_{n})
. Now,
\sigma(\exp(\xi))=\exp(\xi)
for some Lie algebra element

\xi\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
holds if and only if
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, as

\sigma\circ\exp=\exp\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\sigma=\exp\circ\operatorname{cc}

(3.3.56)

with the complex conjugation ??
(\xi)=\overline{{\xi}}
.
Consequently, we get
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
, which implies
g\in\eta(G_{0})
.

Remark 3.3.18
(Fixed point groups).

Let
G
be simply connected and semisimple Lie group. By
Corollary
3.3.15
,

v.)
,
G{C}
is then also simply
connected and Cartan’s
33
33

33

Élie Cartan (1869-1951) was a French differential
geometer, who shaped much of our modern understanding of analysis on manifolds
and, in particular, Lie groups. His oldest son Henri Cartan (1904-2008) also left
significant marks on mathematics, especially in the field of algebraic topology,
and was a founding member of Bourbaki.

semisimplicity criterion

[
92
, Thm. 5.5.9]
yields its semisimplicity. Under these
assumptions, the fixed point group

\bigl\{g\in G{C}\colon\sigma(g)=g\bigr\}

(3.3.57)

is always connected by a theorem of Borel
34
34

34

Armand Borel (1923-2003) was a
Swiss mathematician specialized in algebraic topology and Lie groups. Not to be

1
.

[
28
, Thm. 3.4.]
. Thus, the
restriction to

that facilitated our proof of

3.3.55
) was not necessary in this case.

Along the way we have shown an antiholomorphic version of the universal property.

Corollary 3.3.19
.

For every group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
into a complex Lie group
H
there is a unique
antiholomorphic group morphism
\overline{{\Phi}}{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H
such that

\overline{{\Phi}}{C}\circ\eta=\Phi.

(3.3.58)

Proof
:

Setting
\overline{{\Phi}}{C}\coloneqq\sigma\circ\Phi{C}
with
\Phi{C}
induced by
(
3.3.7
) does the job. Conversely, every
antiholomorphic solution
\overline{{\Phi}}{C}
of (
3.3.58
)
induces a holomorphic solution
\Phi{C}\coloneqq\overline{{\Phi}}{C}\circ\sigma

of (
3.3.6
), proving the uniqueness of

\overline{{\Phi}}{C}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

By continuity of
\sigma
, the following useful observation is now immediate.

Corollary 3.3.20
.

Let
G
be a Lie group with universal complexification
(G{C},\eta)
.

i.)

The image
\eta(G)\subseteq G{C}
is a closed real subgroup and submanifold.

ii.)

The embedding
\iota\colon\eta(G)\hookrightarrow G{C}
constitutes a universal complexification.

Proof
:

By Proposition
3.3.17
, there exists a complex conjugation

\sigma\colon G{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
. Consider the smooth auxiliary mapping

\Phi\colon G{C}\longrightarrow G{C},\quad\Phi(g)\coloneqq\sigma(g)g^{-1}.

(3.3.59)

By (
3.3.55
),
\eta(G)
is the set of fixed points for

\sigma
intersected with the connected component of the group unit, both of which
are closed. Consequently,
\eta(G)
is closed, as well. As images of group
morphisms are subgroups, this implies that
\eta(G)
is a real Lie group itself
and in particular a submanifold of
G{C}
with exponential charts as submanifold
charts by the closed subgroup Theorem
[
92
, Thm. 9.3.7]
. This
completes the proof of the first part. We proceed with

ii.)
. To this end, let
\Phi\colon\eta(G)\longrightarrow H
be a group morphism into a complex Lie group
H
. Then

\Psi\coloneqq\Phi\circ\eta\colon G\longrightarrow H

(3.3.60)

is a Lie group morphism from
G
into a complex Lie group. By the universal
property of
(G{C},\iota\circ\eta)
,
35
35

35

In this proof,
\eta
was
co-restricted to its image
\eta(G)
, so the usual universal complexification
map for
G
is given by
\iota\circ\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}

instead.

there thus exists a unique holomorphic group morphism
\Psi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H
with

\Psi{C}\circ\iota\circ\eta=\Psi=\Phi\circ\eta.

(3.3.61)

By surjectivity of
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow\eta(G)
, this is equivalent to
\Psi{C}\circ\iota=\Phi
and
\Psi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H
is the only holomorphic group morphism with this property.

\ch@irxboxempty

Our next goal is to prove that

\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\operatorname{Lie}\bigl(\eta(G)\bigr){C},

(3.3.62)

which matches with the intuition behind the existence of unique holomorphic extensions
in
ii.)
. Before we begin working towards the
realization of our goal in the form of
Lemma
3.3.30
, we take a short detour. The
complex conjugation allows us to characterize those holomorphic group morphisms
between universal complexifications, which arise from group morphisms between the
underlying real groups.

Proposition 3.3.21
.

Let
G,H
be connected Lie groups and
\Phi\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H{C}
a group morphism. Then the following are equivalent:

i.)

We have
\Phi=\Psi{C}
in the sense of (
3.3.13
) with
\Psi\coloneqq\Phi\big|_{\eta_{G}(G)}\colon\eta_{G}(G)\longrightarrow\eta_{H}(H)
.

ii.)

The group morphism
\Phi
fulfils

\Phi\circ\sigma_{G}=\sigma_{H}\circ\Phi.

(3.3.63)

iii.)

The tangent map
\phi\coloneqq T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi
fulfils

\phi\circ\operatorname{cc}=\operatorname{cc}\circ\phi.

(3.3.64)

If any – and thus all – of the above conditions are fulfilled, then
\Phi
is
holomorphic.

Proof
:

As for connected group also its universal complexification is connected and thus the equivalence of
ii.)
and
iii.)
is immediate. If
G
is not connected, the latter of course still follows from the former by differentiating. Assuming
i.)
, we compute for
g\in G

\sigma_{H}\circ\Phi\circ\sigma_{G}\Big|_{\eta_{G}(g)}=\sigma_{H}\circ\Phi\Big|_{\eta_{G}(g)}=\sigma_{H}\Big|_{\Phi(\eta_{G}(g))}=\Phi\Big|_{\eta_{G}(g)},

(3.3.65)

where we have used (
3.3.55
) and
\Phi(\eta(G))\subseteq\eta_{H}(H)
. By Corollary
3.3.32
, (
3.3.63
) follows. Assume conversely
ii.)
holds. Then we get

\Phi\bigl(\eta_{G}(g)\bigr)=\Phi\Big|_{\eta_{G}(g)}=\Phi\circ\sigma_{G}\Big|_{\eta_{G}(g)}=\sigma_{H}\circ\Phi\Big|_{\eta_{G}(g)}=\sigma_{H}\Big|_{\Phi(\eta_{G}(g))}=\sigma\bigl(\Phi\bigl(\eta(g)\bigr)\bigr)

(3.3.66)

for all
g\in G
. Consequently,
\Phi\big|_{\eta(g)}\subseteq\eta_{H}(H)
by
connectedness of
H
and (
3.3.55
). It remains to
establish that
\Phi
is holomorphic, then the uniqueness in
Lemma
3.3.3

proves
i.)
. We do this by checking
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linearity of
the tangent map
\phi
. By what we have already shown, we know that
\phi
maps

\mathfrak{g}\coloneqq\operatorname{Lie}{\eta_{G}(G)}
into
\mathfrak{h}\coloneqq\operatorname{Lie}{\eta_{H}(H)}
. Consequently, using
(
3.3.64
), we get

\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi)=\phi\circ\operatorname{cc}\Big|_{-\mathrm{i}\xi}=\operatorname{cc}\circ\phi\Big|_{-\mathrm{i}\xi}=-\overline{{\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi)}}

(3.3.67)

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. That is,
\phi
maps
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{g}
into
\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{h}
and thus splits into
\phi=\phi_{1}\oplus\mathrm{i}\phi_{2}
with
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
-linear
functions
\phi_{1}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
and
\phi_{2}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
. But such mappings are
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear.

\ch@irxboxempty

Note that (
3.3.63
) is a stronger condition than
holomorphicity. There typically exist holomorphic group morphisms between universal
complexifications that do not restrict nicely to
\eta(G)
. Indeed, this already
happens in the simplest case
G=\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. While a generic complex linear – and thus
holomorphic – mapping
\Phi\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is given by
\Phi(z)=az

for some
a\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, only those with
a\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
fulfil
(
3.3.63
), which in this case coincides
with (
3.3.64
). The reason for this is
that
any

one dimensonal real subspace of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
complexifies back to
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. That is to
say, real forms are
typically not unique. We will see a more drastic instance of this phenomenon in
Example
3.4.3
, where two non-isomorphic groups have the same
universal complexification. Strictly speaking, this is impossible, since the
morphism
\eta
is part of the data for a universal complexification. However,
the
involved complex Lie groups
do
indeed coincide. Keeping these
phenomenona in mind disproves a number of reasonably sounding abstract
statements.

Next, we investigate the fundamental groups of
G{C}
for connected Lie groups
G
. In Corollary
3.3.15
,
v.)
, we have already seen that
\pi_{1}(G)=\{\mathrm{e}\}
implies that
\pi_{1}(G{C})=\{\mathrm{e}\}
. It thus remains to cover the multiply connected situation.

Taking another look at the construction of the universal complexification we have
discussed in Theorem
3.3.14
, one might expect

\widetilde{G}{C}
to be the universal covering group of
G{C}
with the quotient
projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{G}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
from
(
3.3.38
) being the universal covering map.
This then would imply the desired equalities of fundamental groups at once. Note that
by uniqueness
\pi=(\eta\circ p){C}
, which is the other natural candidate for a
universal covering projection. As a first ingredient, we use the existence of complex
conjugations to infer that injectivity of
\widetilde{\eta}
guarantees it is actually an
embedding of manifolds, i.e. a diffeomorphism when endowing
\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{G})\subseteq\widetilde{G}{C}
with the subspace topology.

Lemma 3.3.22
.

Let
\widetilde{G}
be a connected and simply connected Lie group such that

\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}{C}

(3.3.68)

is injective. Then
\widetilde{\eta}
is an embedding of manifolds.

Proof
:

By
Corollary
3.3.20
,
\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{G})
constitutes a
closed real subgroup and thus a closed submanifold of
\widetilde{G}{C}
.
Consequently,
\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{G})
is a bijective immersion by virtue of
Corollary
3.3.15
,

v.)
. Applying the smooth inverse
function theorem, we see that immersivity and smoothness imply that
\widetilde{\eta}

is a local diffeomorphism. As smoothness of the inverse may be checked locally,
this proves that
\widetilde{\eta}
is a diffeomorphism.

\ch@irxboxempty

Recall that a subset
D\subseteq X
of a topological space
X
is called discrete if the subspace topology the subset
D
inherits from
X
is the discrete topology.

Lemma 3.3.23
.

Let
\iota\colon M\longrightarrow N
be an embedding of topological spaces and let
D\subseteq M
be a discrete subset. Then
\iota(D)\subseteq N
is discrete.

Proof
:

Let
x\in D
. By discreteness of the set
D\subseteq M
, there thus exists an
open neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
such that
U_{x}\cap D=\{x\}
. As the mapping

\iota
is an embedding, the image
\iota(U_{x})\subseteq\iota(D)
is an open
neighbourhood of

\iota(x)
with respect to the subspace topology
\iota(D)\subseteq N
. By
injectivity of
\iota
, we moreover have

\iota(U_{x})\cap\iota(D)=\iota\bigl(U_{x}\cap\iota(D)\bigr)=\iota\bigl(\{x\}\bigr)=\bigl\{\iota(x)\bigr\}.

(3.3.69)

This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Recall that an action
\Phi\colon H\times M\longrightarrow M
of a topological
group
H
on a topological space
M
is called properly discontinuous if every
x\in M
has a neighbourhood
U_{x}\subseteq M
such that

(h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x})\cap U_{x}\neq\emptyset\qquad\textrm{implies}\quad h=\mathrm{e}\quad\textrm{for all }h\in H.

(3.3.70)

Notably, such an action is always free. We borrow the following elementary lemma from
topology.

Lemma 3.3.24
.

Let
\Phi\colon H\times M\longrightarrow M
be a continuous and properly discontinuous action of a topological group
H
on a connected topological space
M
. Then
H
carries the discrete topology and the quotient projection
\pi\colon M\longrightarrow M/H
is a covering of topological spaces.

Proof
:

Given
x\in M
, proper discontinuity yields a neighbourhood
U_{x}\subseteq M
of

x
such that
(h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x})\cap U_{x}=\emptyset
for all
H\setminus\{\mathrm{e}\}
.
As the map
\Phi
is an action, this in particular implies

(h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x})\cap(g\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x})=\emptyset\qquad\textrm{for all }h,g\in H\textrm{ with }h\neq g.

(3.3.71)

As
\Phi
acts by homeomorphisms,
h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x}
is moreover an open neighbourhood
of
h\mathbin{\triangleright}x
. Consequently, the fiber over
\pi(U_{x})
is given by

\pi^{-1}\bigl(\pi(U_{x})\bigr)=\bigsqcup_{h\in H}h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x},

where
\pi(U_{x})
is an open neighbourhood of
\pi(x)
by openness of quotient
projections. Next, we assert that
H
carries the discrete topology. Assuming the
contrary, we find a net
(h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq H
converging to some

h\in H
with
h_{\alpha}\neq h
for all
\alpha\in J
. Left translation with

h^{-1}
yields a convergent net
(h^{-1}h_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
with limit

\mathrm{e}
. By continuity of
\Phi
, we get

\Phi\bigl(h^{-1}h_{\alpha},x\bigr)\rightarrow\Phi(\mathrm{e},x)=x.

(3.3.72)

Consequently, given an open neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
, there exists an
\alpha_{0}\in J
such that we have
\Phi(h^{-1}h_{\alpha},x)\in U_{x}
for all
\alpha\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}
. In particular,
(h^{-1}h_{\alpha_{0}}\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x})\cap U_{x}\neq\emptyset
and the quotient
h^{-1}h_{\alpha_{0}}\neq\mathrm{e}
, so the action of
H
is
not properly
discontinuous. Hence
H
indeed carries the discrete topology. Now, note that the
restrictions
\pi\big|_{h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x}}
are injective for any fixed
h\in H
: If
\pi(h\mathbin{\triangleright}x_{1})=\pi(h\mathbin{\triangleright}x_{2})
for some
x_{1},x_{2}\in U_{x}
, then
there exists a
g\in H
with

g\mathbin{\triangleright}(h\mathbin{\triangleright}x_{2})=h\mathbin{\triangleright}x_{1}

(3.3.73)

and thus

x_{1}=(h^{-1}gh)\mathbin{\triangleright}x_{2}\in U_{x}\cap(h^{-1}gh\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x}).

(3.3.74)

By proper discontinuity, this means
h^{-1}gh=\mathrm{e}
, which implies
g=\mathrm{e}
by
left multiplication with
h
and right multiplication with
h^{-1}
. But then
x_{1}=x_{2}
and thus the restriction
\pi\big|_{h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x}}
is injective. Using once
more the openness of
\pi
and putting everything together, we obtain that the
restrictions
\pi\big|_{h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{x}}
are homeomorphisms onto their images for all

h\in H
.

\ch@irxboxempty

For this reason and to avoid the arguably awkward terminology, some modern literature
such as
[
119
, Ch. 11 & 12]
, which also contains the above result, refers
actions that are both continuous and properly discontinuous as
covering
actions

instead.
In the special case of a subgroup
H\subseteq G
acting on a topological group
M=G
, it suffices to check proper discontinuity at
x=\mathrm{e}
:

Lemma 3.3.25
.

Let
G
be a topological group and
H\subseteq G
be a subgroup such that there exists a zero neighbourhood
U_{0}\subseteq G
with
(h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{0})\cap U_{0}=\emptyset
for all
h\in H\setminus\{\mathrm{e}\}
. Then the left action of
H
on
G
by left multiplications is properly discontinuous.

Proof
:

Let
g\in G
and define
U_{g}\coloneqq U_{0}g
, which is a neighbourhood of
g
. Then

(h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{g})\cap U_{g}=hU_{0}g\cap U_{0}g=(hU_{0}\cap U_{0})g=\emptyset\cdot g=\emptyset.

\ch@irxboxempty

Next, we prove that discrete subgroups always act properly discontinuously on the ambient group and thus induce covering projections by Lemma
3.3.24
.

Lemma 3.3.26
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be a discrete subgroup of a Lie group
G
. Then the quotient projection
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow G/H
is a covering of Lie groups.

Proof
:

By continuity of the group multiplication and discreteness of
H
, there exists a
zero neighbourhood
U_{0}\subseteq\widetilde{G}{C}
with
(U_{0}\cdot U_{0})\cap H=\{\mathrm{e}\}
and
U_{0}^{-1}=U_{0}
. Let
h\in H
be such that

(h\mathbin{\triangleright}U_{0})\cap U_{0}\neq\emptyset.

Taking a closer look, this means that there exist
g_{1},g_{2}\in U_{0}
with

hg_{1}=g_{2},\quad\textrm{i.e.}\quad h=g_{2}g_{1}^{-1}\in U_{0}\cdot U_{0}^{-1}=U_{0}\cdot U_{0}.

By choice of
U_{0}
, this means
h=\mathrm{e}
. Consequently, the action of
H
is
properly discontinuous in view of
Lemma
3.3.25
and thus the quotient
projection
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow G/H
is a covering of Lie groups by
Lemma
3.3.24
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Putting everything together, we arrive at the following, a variant of which can be found in
[
92
, Exercise 15.1.2(b)]
:

Proposition 3.3.27
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group such that
\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}{C}
is injective. Then the quotient projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{G}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
is the universal covering projection of the universal complexification
G{C}
of
G
. In particular,

\pi_{1}(G{C})\cong\widetilde{\eta}\bigl(\pi_{1}(G)\bigr)\cong\pi_{1}(G).

(3.3.75)

Proof
:

By Lemma
3.3.22
,
\widetilde{\eta}
is an embedding. Thus the image
\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))
of the discrete subgroup
\pi_{1}(G)\subseteq\widetilde{G}
is a discrete complex subgroup of
\widetilde{G}{C}
by Lemma
3.3.23
. Lemma
3.3.26
implies then that the quotient projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{G}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
is a covering projection. As
\widetilde{G}{C}
is simply connected,
\pi
is universal and (
3.3.75
) follows immediately.

\ch@irxboxempty

Summarizing, if
\widetilde{\eta}
injective, passing to universal complexifications and universal coverings are commutative processes, i.e. the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

(3.3.76)

commutes and its vertical arrows are universal covering projections. In particular,
there are no new topological obstructions to analytic continuation to
G{C}
beyond
what is already present within
G
. This will be crucial for our considerations in
Section
3.5
, where we extend entire functions defined on

G
, i.e. functions already abiding by all topological obstructions, to holomorphic
ones
on
G{C}
. As another aside, we once again see that
\eta
is locally injective in
this situation, as all other arrows in (
3.3.76
)
are. Truncating our proof of Proposition
3.3.27
, we
have moreover shown the following.

Corollary 3.3.28
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group such that
\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))\subseteq\widetilde{G}{C}
is discrete. Then the quotient projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{G}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
is the universal covering morphism and

\pi_{1}(G{C})\cong\widetilde{\eta}\bigl(\pi_{1}(G)\bigr).

(3.3.77)

In particular,

\pi_{1}(G)\supseteq\pi_{1}(G{C})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\operatorname{Lie}(\widetilde{G}{C}).

(3.3.78)

In Example
3.4.3
we will see that dropping the assumption that

\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}{C}
is injective in
Proposition
3.3.27
may result in
\pi_{1}(G{C})\subsetneq\pi_{1}(G)
. We are now in a position to revisit
Corollary
3.3.20
, for which we first note the following
lemmas, which are also of independent interest. Recall that a Lie group morphism
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
is called locally injective at a group element
g\in G
if there
exists a neighbourhood
U\subseteq G
of
g
such that the restriction
\Phi\big|_{U}\colon U\longrightarrow H
is injective.

Lemma 3.3.29
.

Let
\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H
be a Lie group morphism. Then
\Phi
is
locally injective at the group unit if and only if its tangent map
T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi
is
injective.

Proof
:

The interesting implication is going from local injectivity at
\mathrm{e}
to injectivity
