of the tangent map, as the converse statement is just the usual inverse
function
Theorem. Thus let
U\subseteq G
be a neighbourhood of
\mathrm{e}
such that

\Phi\big|_{U}\colon U\longrightarrow H
is injective. Moreover, we choose a zero
neighbourhood
V\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
such that
\exp_{G}\colon V\longrightarrow G
is a diffeomorphism with image
\exp(V)
contained within
U
. By
injectivity of
\Phi
, this implies injectivity of

\exp_{H}\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi=\Phi\circ\exp_{G}\colon V\longrightarrow H,

(3.3.79)

which implies injectivity of
T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi
on
V
. But
T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi
is a linear map,
so injectivity on a zero neighbourhood implies its global injectivity.

\ch@irxboxempty

Note that even global injectivity of a generic smooth map does not necessarily lead to
injectivity of the tangent map. For a simple example, consider the mapping
x\mapsto x^{3}
on the real line. The underlying problem is that this type of map is not a
diffeomorphism. As bijective Lie group morphisms are always diffeomorphisms, this
yields another way to prove the preceding lemma.

Lemma 3.3.30
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group with universal complexification
(G{C},\eta)

such that
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is locally injective at the group
unit. Then
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\mathfrak{g}{C}
.

Proof
:

Taking another look at (
3.3.39
), we
see that by our assumption, the composition
\pi\circ\widetilde{\eta}
is locally
injective at
\mathrm{e}
. Let
U\subseteq G
be a neighbourhood of
\mathrm{e}
such that

\pi\circ\widetilde{\eta}\big|_{U}\colon U\longrightarrow G{C}

(3.3.80)

is injective. By (
3.3.38
), this means that

\widetilde{\eta}(U)\cap\widetilde{\eta}\bigl(\pi_{1}(G)\bigr)=\{\mathrm{e}\}.

(3.3.81)

That is,
\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))
is discrete in
\widetilde{G}{C}
. By
Corollary
3.3.28
, this implies
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\mathfrak{g}{C}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

As a special case we obtain the following result about the fine structure of
\eta(G)
for a generic connected Lie group
G
, which will be useful for our considerations in Section
3.5
.

Corollary 3.3.31
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
k\coloneqq\dim{R}(\eta(G)))
. Every basis

\bigl(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{k}\bigr)\subseteq\operatorname{Lie}\bigl(\eta(G)\bigr)

(3.3.82)

induces a basis of
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})
via
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{k},\mathrm{i}\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{i}\mathit{e}_{k})
. In particular,

\dim{C}(G{C})=\dim{R}\bigl(\eta(G)\bigr).

(3.3.83)

Proof
:

By Corollary
3.3.20
,
ii.)
, the embedding
\eta(G)\hookrightarrow G{C}
constitutes a universal complexification. As injective mappings are in particular locally injective at every point, Lemma
3.3.30
yields
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\operatorname{Lie}(\eta(G)){C}
, from which the assertions are immediate.

\ch@irxboxempty

Combining Corollary
3.3.31
with Proposition
3.3.16
yields the following version of the identity principle, which will prove useful in many places.

Corollary 3.3.32
(Identity Principle).

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and let

\phi\colon G{C}\longrightarrow M

(3.3.84)

be a holomorphic map into a complex manifold
M
. If
\phi\big|_{\eta(G)}
is
constant, then
\phi
is constant.

Proof
:

Choosing a holomorphic chart
(U,z)
of
M
, we may consider the component mappings
z^{j}\circ\phi\colon G{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. By assumption, each of them is constant on
\eta(G)
, which by Proposition
3.3.16
implies they are constant on the connected component of the group unit. By connectedness of
G
, the assertion follows.

\ch@irxboxempty

We round out our abstract considerations about universal complexification by taking a closer look at the disconnected case. The idea is that one may recast the construction as a Lie group extension problem.

Remark 3.3.33
(Interpretation of the disconnected case).

Consider the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\big/G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(3.3.85)

with the inclusion
\iota\colon G_{0}\hookrightarrow G
and the
quotient projection
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow G/G_{0}
to the
component group
G/G_{0}
. This group should also
be the component group of the complexification, i.e.

G{C}/G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\cong G/G_{0}

(3.3.86)

By Example
3.3.10
this equality makes sense
both as real and as complex Lie groups if
G
has positive
dimension. Otherwise, the respective unit components are just
given by a point and we obtain
G{C}\cong G
once again in
accordance to Example
3.3.10
. Note now that
by the connected case, we do have a universal complexification

(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}},\eta_{0})
of the connected component
G_{0}
of
the group unit. Thus the situation looks like

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\big/G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\big/G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

(3.3.87)

where we have to construct
G{C}
and the dashed arrows in the appropriate
categories
such that the diagram commutes and the second row is exact. Doing this for the
second line only is known as a
group extension
problem,
which typically has a multitude of inequivalent solutions. It is
now the additional conditions derived from the commutativity of
(
3.3.87
) that make the pair

(G{C},\eta)
unique. This type of problem gets discussed
e.g. in
[
29
, Chapter 3§6, Lemma 7]
in some
more generality. In the proof of
Theorem
3.3.14
the complexified
Lie group was then realized as the quotient
(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\rtimes_{c}G)/K
with respect to an action by conjugations
c

and
K
as in (

†

‣
Proof
). To
obtain the remaining Lie group morphisms, the crucial observation
is that we have as a set
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\rtimes_{c}G=G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\times G
. Notably, products with only one non
trivial factor in
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\rtimes_{c}G
just reduce to
the products of
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
and
G
, respectively. Consequently,
simply embedding
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
or
G
yields Lie group morphisms.
In particular, we can map from
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
and
G
into

G{C}
by taking equivalence classes afterwards, that is

\iota{C}(g)\coloneqq\big[(g,\mathrm{e}_{G})\big]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\eta(g)\coloneqq\big[(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}},g)\big],

which are exactly the maps we used in the proof of
Theorem
3.3.14
. We have

\iota{C}\circ\eta_{0}\big|_{g}=\big[(\eta_{0}(g),\mathrm{e}_{G})\big]=\big[(\mathrm{e}_{G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}},\iota(g))\big]=\eta\circ\iota\big|_{g}

for
g\in G_{0}
by (

‡

‣
Proof
), i.e. the left square
in (
3.3.87
) commutes. Note that
we only used elements
g\in G_{0}
in the definition of
K
and

suppressed
the map
\iota
there. As every equivalence class
[(z,g)]
contains a unique representative of the form
(z^{\prime},e_{G})
, the morphism
\iota{C}
is
injective and thus the diagram
(
3.3.87
) is exact at
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
. An alternative way of obtaining the morphism

\iota{C}
is by means of the universal property of
(G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}},\eta_{0})
: the map
\eta\circ\iota\colon G_{0}\longrightarrow G{C}
is a Lie group morphism into a
complex Lie group. Taking another look at the proof, we then used
Lie’s second Theorem
3.3.11
to integrate the
complexified tangent map, which is not very explicit. By the
uniqueness statement in the universal property, this however
reproduces our
\iota{C}
. Similarly, we can use the
universal property of
(G{C},\eta)
to obtain the final
map
\pi{C}
by once again using
Example
3.3.10
to view
G/G_{0}
as
a complex Lie group: as our notation already suggests, the map

\pi{C}
really is the complexification of the quotient
projection
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow G/G_{0}
. The right
square in (
3.3.87
) thus
commutes by construction, which then means also the outer square
commutes. Taking another look at the construction of

\pi{C}
, we first note that
\pi\circ\iota=[\mathrm{e}_{G}]
by
exactness of (
3.3.85
). Thus

\pi\big|_{G_{0}}
complexifies to

\pi{C}\bigl([(z,g)]\bigr)=\pi(g),

which is well defined, as we have for
(\eta_{0}(h)z,h^{-1}g)\in[(z,g)]
also
h^{-1}g\in G_{0}g
,
i.e. multiplication with an element of
G_{0}
does not change the
connected component. As
\pi
is surjective, the same is thus true
for
\pi{C}
,
i.e. (
3.3.87
) is exact also at
the lower
G/G_{0}
. Finally, we compute

\pi{C}\circ\iota{C}\big|_{g}=\pi{C}\bigl([(g,\mathrm{e}_{G})]\bigr)=\pi(\mathrm{e}_{G})=[\mathrm{e}_{G}]=\mathrm{e}_{G/G_{0}}\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}},

which also proves exactness at
G{C}
.

Summarizing, we have the following:

Corollary 3.3.34
.

Let
G
be a Lie group with universal complexification

(G{C},\eta)
.

i.)

The component groups of
G
and
G{C}
are
isomorphic as both real and complex Lie groups.

ii.)

The universal complexification
G{C}
is a group
extension of
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
relative to the component
group
G/G_{0}
.

iii.)

We have
G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}=G_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C},0}
, i.e. the
universal complexification of the unit component
G_{0}
of

G
is given by the unit component of the universal
complexification
G{C}
of
G
.

iv.)

The diagram of real Lie groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\big/G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\big/G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

(3.3.88)

is commutative with exact lines and
\iota{C}
as well as
\pi{C}
are holomorphic.

3.4
Examples of Universal Complexifications

Moist was, by inclination, a stranger to the concept of two in the morning, a
time that happened to other people.

Raising Steam
– Terry Pratchett

The purpose of this section is to fill the abstract machinery we have developed with life
in the form of concrete constructions and counterexamples. While our proof of
Theorem
3.3.14
is constructive, it is typically difficult to
make all the required structures concrete for a given example. The circle group turns out
to be sufficiently simple.

Example 3.4.1
(Circle group,
[
90
, Ex. 4.19]
).

Let

G\coloneqq??\coloneqq\bigl\{z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\colon\lvert z\rvert=1\bigr\}=\partial\ch@airxmathbbm{D}=\operatorname{U}(1),

(3.4.1)

which is connected, but not simply
connected. Its universal covering group
\widetilde{G}
is given by

p\colon\widetilde{\ch@airxmathbbm{S}}^{1}=\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow G,\quad p(t)=e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t}.

(3.4.2)

Any interval of length less than
1
gives a evenly covered
neighbourhood of all its points in
G
. The complexification of
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
is

\widetilde{\eta}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}=\widetilde{G}{C},\quad\widetilde{\eta}(x)=x+0\mathrm{i},

(3.4.3)

see again Example
3.3.2
.
Taking another look at (
3.4.2
), we see
that
\pi_{1}(G)=\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
and thus also

\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))=\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. This is
already a discrete and thus closed subgroup. That is to say,
\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}}}\right>_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
. Consequently, the universal complexification of the circle
group is

\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C}=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\big/\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\eta\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C},\quad\eta\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t}\bigr)=\big[\widetilde{\eta}(t)\big]=[t],

(3.4.4)

which is indeed well defined. Geometrically,
\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C}
is thus given by a
cylinder
\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}\times\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. Notably, the universal complexification

\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C}
is not compact, even though the circle
group
\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}
was. This is good news for the prospects of interesting
globally defined holomorphic functions. We shall see in
Section
3.5
that this non-compactness is a
general feature, which makes holomorphic extension of entire functions on

\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}
to holomorphic ones on
\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C}
feasible. Ultimately, this
results in Theorem
3.5.5
.

Analogously, one obtains the universal complexification for higher tori as
products of cylinders. Notice that the group morphism

\sigma\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C},\quad\sigma\bigl(e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t},r\bigr)=(e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}t},-r)

(3.4.5)

fixes all
g\in\eta(\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1})=\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}\times\{0\}
.
Moreover, we have

T_{(1,0)}\sigma=\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\times\bigl(-\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\bigr),

(3.4.6)

which is
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-antilinear. Thus
\sigma
is the complex conjugation
of
\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}{C}
from Proposition
3.3.17
.

Example 3.4.2
(Semidirect products,
[
92
, Exercise 15.1.7]
).

Let
G
and
N
be Lie groups and
\gamma\colon G\times N\longrightarrow N

be a smooth action of
G
by automorphisms of
N
. We write

\gamma_{g}\coloneqq\gamma(g,\,\cdot\,)\in??(N)\qquad\textrm{for }g\in G.

(3.4.7)

By the universal property of
N{C}
, the compositions
\eta_{N}\circ\gamma_{g}\colon N\longrightarrow N{C}

induce unique holomorphic group morphisms
\widetilde{\gamma}_{g}\colon N{C}\longrightarrow N{C}
such that

\widetilde{\gamma}_{g}\circ\eta_{N}=\eta_{N}\circ\gamma_{g}\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in G.

(3.4.8)

This yields a smooth action
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon G\times N{C}\longrightarrow N{C}
by holomorphic automorphisms of
N{C}
as

\widetilde{\gamma}_{gh}\circ\eta_{N}=\eta_{N}\circ\gamma_{gh}=\eta_{N}\circ\gamma_{g}\circ\gamma_{h}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{g}\circ\eta_{N}\circ\gamma_{h}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{g}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{h}\circ\eta_{N}

holds for all
g,h\in G
. That is,
\eta_{N}\circ\gamma_{g}\circ\gamma_{h}
and
\eta_{N}\circ\gamma_{gh}
are simply the same morphisms and thus also induce the same holomorphic extensions. For every
n\in N{C}
, we now consider the group morphisms

\psi_{n}\colon G\longrightarrow N{C},\quad\psi_{n}(g)\coloneqq\widetilde{\gamma}_{g}(n).

By the universal property of
G{C}
, there exist unique holomorphic group morphisms

\widetilde{\psi}_{n}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow N{C}\quad\textrm{with}\quad\widetilde{\psi}_{n}\circ\eta_{G}=\psi_{n}.

(3.4.9)

Repeating the arguments above, we see that the
\widetilde{\psi}_{n}
induce a
smooth action

\gamma{C}\colon G{C}\times N{C}\longrightarrow N{C}

(3.4.10)

by biholomorphic morphisms of
N{C}
. By construction, both of the partial maps

\gamma{C}(g,\,\cdot\,)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{g}\colon N{C}\longrightarrow N{C}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\gamma{C}(\,\cdot\,,n)=\widetilde{\psi}_{n}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow N{C}

(3.4.11)

are holomorphic for all
g\in G{C}
and
n\in N{C}
. By Hartogs’ Theorem, see
again Remark
2.2.3
, this implies that the full map
\gamma{C}
is
holomorphic, as well. Consequently, the semidirect product
N{C}\rtimes_{\gamma{C}}G{C}
endowed with the product atlas carries the structure of a
complex Lie group: its multiplication may be decomposed into the multiplications of
the complex Lie groups
G{C}
,
N{C}
and applications of
\gamma{C}
. Finally,
we prove that

\eta\colon N\rtimes_{\gamma}G\longrightarrow N{C}\rtimes_{\gamma{C}}G{C},\quad\eta\coloneqq\eta_{N}\times\eta_{G}

(3.4.12)

constitutes a universal complexification. To show this, let
\Phi\colon N\rtimes_{\gamma}G\longrightarrow H
be a group morphism into a complex Lie group

H
. Denoting the canonical inclusions by

\iota_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow N\rtimes_{\gamma}G\quad\textrm{and}\quad\iota_{N}\colon N\longrightarrow N\rtimes_{\gamma}G,

(3.4.13)

we apply the universal properties of
G{C}
resp.
N{C}
to

\Phi_{G}\coloneqq\iota_{G}^{*}\Phi\colon G\longrightarrow H\quad\textrm{resp.}\quad\Phi_{N}\coloneqq\iota^{*}_{N}\Phi\colon N\longrightarrow H.

(3.4.14)

This provides holomorphic group morphisms
\Phi_{G,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow H
fulfilling
\Phi_{G,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\circ\eta_{G}=\Phi_{G}
and
\Phi_{N,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\colon N{C}\longrightarrow H
fulfilling
\Phi_{N,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\circ\eta_{N}=\Phi_{N}
. Using
both maps, we define

\Phi{C}\colon N{C}\rtimes_{\gamma{C}}G{C}\longrightarrow H,\quad\Phi{C}(n,g)\coloneqq\Phi_{N,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(n)\cdot\Phi_{G,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}(g),

(3.4.15)

which is holomorphic. By construction, we have for
g\in G
and
n\in N
that

\Phi{C}\circ\eta\Big|_{(g,n)}=\Phi_{N,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\bigl(\eta_{N}(n)\bigr)\Phi_{G,\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}\bigl(\eta_{G}(g)\bigr)=\Phi_{N}(n)\Phi_{G}(g)=\Phi(e_{G},n)\Phi(g,e_{N})=\Phi(g,e).

(3.4.16)

As
\eta(N\rtimes_{\gamma}G)=\eta_{N}(N)\times\eta_{G}(G)
, the identity principle
from Corollary
3.3.32
implies that
\Phi{C}
is the group
morphism uniquely determined by
\Phi{C}\circ\eta=\Phi
.

Note that (
3.4.12
) implies that
\eta
is
(locally) injective if and only if both
\eta_{N}
and
\eta_{G}
are. Thus twisting
with a group action can never lose dimensions beyond what was already lost upon
complexifying the factors.

As a special case, namely taking
\gamma
as the trivial action, we see that universal
complexification distributes over finite Cartesian products. Note also that, if the
automorphism group
\operatorname{\mathrm{Aut}}(N)
carries a Lie group structure, then the complex
automorphism group
\operatorname{\mathrm{Aut}}{C}(N{C})
may be endowed with the structure of a complex Lie
group by
[
92
, Prop. 15.4.1]
. In this case, one may
construct
\gamma{C}
from
\widetilde{\gamma}
directly by applying the universal
property of
G{C}
, which simplifies the preceding construction somewhat. We continue
with the principal example within Lie theory.

Example 3.4.3
(Special linear group).

We consider the special linear group

G\coloneqq??=\bigl\{M\in??(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\colon\det(M)=1\bigr\},

(3.4.17)

which is connected, but not simply connected. Its universal covering group is

\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
, which is a
non linear
group
by
[
92
, Example 9.5.18]
. We denote the universal covering
projection by
p\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\longrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
.
The Lie algebras of both groups are given by the traceless matrices

??\coloneqq\bigl\{M\in\mathrm{M}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{tr}}(M)=0\bigr\},

(3.4.18)

whose complexification is simply

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}){C}=\bigl\{M\in\mathrm{M}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{tr}}(M)=0\bigr\}\eqqcolon\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}).

(3.4.19)

Consequently, by our construction within
Theorem
3.3.14
, we have

\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}){C}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})=\bigl\{M\in\mathrm{M}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\colon\det(M)=1\bigr\},

(3.4.20)

as
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
is simply connected by

[
92
, Exercise 15.1.1]
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
. Next,
we prove that
\widetilde{\eta}=\iota\circ p
, where
\iota\colon\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
simply reinterprets real matrices as complex
ones. Indeed, by construction its tangent map is given by

T_{\mathrm{e}}\widetilde{\eta}=T_{\mathrm{e}}\iota=T_{\mathrm{e}}\iota\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}p,

(3.4.21)

where
T_{\mathrm{e}}\iota
is the inclusion
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})

and by connectedness the claimed form of
\widetilde{\eta}
follows. Notably,

\widetilde{\eta}
is not injective, but locally injective in accordance with
Corollary
3.3.15
,
v.)
.
Moreover, we get
\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))=\{\mathrm{e}\}
, as
\pi_{1}(G)=\ker p
.
Consequently, (
3.3.38
) yields that

\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}){C}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
with
\eta\coloneqq\iota
in
accordance with Proposition
3.3.12
. This example is
remarkable in at least two regards: firstly, we see that non isomorphic Lie groups may
have isomorphic universal complexifications. Secondly, the conclusion in
Proposition
3.3.27
may fail if
\widetilde{\eta}
is not
injective. Indeed, we in particular have

\pi_{1}(G{C})=\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}))=\{\mathrm{e}\}\subsetneq\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}=\pi_{1}\bigl(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\bigr)=\pi_{1}(G).

(3.4.22)

On the other hand, the example matches what we expect from
Corollary
3.3.28
.

Keeping some of our notation and combining all three of the preceding
examples allows us to show that the inequality
\dim{C}G{C}\leq\dim{R}G

may be strict.

Example 3.4.4
(Dimension drop,
[
92
, Exercise 15.1.4]
).

Consider the quotient

G\coloneqq\bigl(\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\times\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\bigr)\big/D

(3.4.23)

by the subgroup

D\coloneqq\big<(g_{0},1),(\mathrm{e},\sqrt{2})\big>\subseteq\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\times\ch@airxmathbbm{R},

(3.4.24)

where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<g_{0}}}\right>=\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))
is a generator. Explicitly, one may choose

u\coloneqq(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix})\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})

and
g_{0}\coloneqq\exp_{\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})}(2\pi u)
. Crucially,

\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
is an infinite cyclic covering
36
36

36

That is
to say, the
fibers
p^{-1}(\{A\})
are countably infinite cyclic groups for all
A\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. To see this, consider the polar decomposition into the circle
group
\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}
times positive matrices, which form a cone and are as such
contractible.
and
thus the powers of
g_{0}
are distinct. Consequently,

D=\bigl\{\bigl(g_{0}^{k},k+\ell\sqrt{2}\bigr)\colon k,\ell\in\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}\bigr\}

(3.4.25)

is indeed discrete, second countable and in particular closed. Thus
G
carries the
structure of a Lie group itself. Its universal covering group is given by the quotient
projection

\pi\colon\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\times\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow G

(3.4.26)

by Lemma
3.3.26
. By the compatibility of universal complexification with direct products,

\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}{C}\coloneqq\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\times\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\widetilde{\eta}\coloneqq p\times\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}

(3.4.27)

is the universal complexification of
\widetilde{G}
, see Example
3.4.3
. By construction, we arrive at

\widetilde{\eta}\bigl(\pi_{1}(G)\bigr)=\widetilde{\eta}(D)=\Bigl\{\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}_{2},k+\ell\sqrt{2}\bigr)\colon k,\ell\in\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}\Bigr\}

(3.4.28)

with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))}}\right>_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}=\{\ch@airxmathbbm{1}_{2}\}\times\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. We have shown

G{C}=\widetilde{G}{C}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))}}\right>_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})/\{\ch@airxmathbbm{1}_{2}\}\times\ch@airxmathbbm{C}/\ch@airxmathbbm{C}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\times\{\mathrm{e}\}\cong\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})

(3.4.29)

with universal complexification map inherited from
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
. In particular, we see that

\dim{C}G{C}<\dim{R}G\quad\textrm{and}\quad\pi_{1}(G{C})=\{\mathrm{e}\}\subsetneq D=\pi_{1}(G).

(3.4.30)

Example 3.4.5
(Complexifying complex groups,

[
92
, Exercise 15.2.3]
).

Let
\widetilde{G}
be a connected and simply connected – an assumption we shall
drop in a second step –
complex
Lie group. The following provides a model
of

\widetilde{G}
within
\widetilde{G}{C}
as the diagonal. That is, we define

\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}\times\overline{{\widetilde{G}}},\quad\widetilde{\eta}(g)\coloneqq(g,g),

(3.4.31)

where
\overline{{\widetilde{G}}}
is the complex conjugated group to
\widetilde{G}
. The idea is to solve the extension problem posed by the universal property on the level of the Lie algebras and then to integrate by means of Theorem
3.3.11
. We thus begin with some preliminary considerations pertaining the linear algebraic situation.

Note that one should not expect complexification to be idempotent. This already
occurs for vector spaces, as there are
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
-linear mappings that fail to be

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear, e.g. the complex conjugation
\operatorname{cc}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, see again our considerations surrounding
Proposition
3.3.21
.
Here, the two instances of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
play different roles. We consider the domain of

\operatorname{cc}
as the
real
group we want to complexify and the image
as a generic
complex
group.

We double the dimension and consider
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{C}\coloneqq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\oplus\overline{{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}
, where

\overline{{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
as an additive group endowed with the opposite scalar multiplication

z\odot\overline{{\xi}}\coloneqq\overline{{\overline{{z}}\xi}}
for scalars
z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
and vectors
\overline{{\xi}}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
as we have done in the proof of Proposition
3.3.17
. Then
we may embed
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
as the diagonal

\bigl\{(\xi,\xi)\colon\xi\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\bigr\}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{C},

(3.4.32)

which yields a candidate for the universal complexification, namely
\eta(\xi)\coloneqq(\xi,\xi)
. And indeed, for the specific example of
\operatorname{cc}
, setting

\operatorname{cc}{C}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\operatorname{cc}{C}(\xi,\eta)\coloneqq\overline{{\eta}}

(3.4.33)

yields a
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear and thus holomorphic extension of
\operatorname{cc}
. Analogously, the holomorphic extension a of
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear mapping
\phi\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is simply given by
\phi{C}(\xi,\eta)\coloneqq\phi(\xi)
.

For general
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
-linear maps, i.e. maps that are neither
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear nor

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-antilinear, the situation is more complicated. We denote the spaces of

\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
-linear,
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear, respectively
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-antilinear mappings between two complex
vector spaces
V
and
W
by ??, ??and ??.
Then

\operatorname{L}(V,W)\cong\operatorname{L}{C}(V,W)\oplus\overline{{\operatorname{L}}}{C}(V,W)

(3.4.34)

forms a direct decomposition with projections

\displaystyle\pi{C}\colon\operatorname{L}(V,W)\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}{C}(V,W),\quad\bigl(\pi{C}\phi\bigr)(v)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\phi(v)-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}v)\bigr),

(3.4.35)

\displaystyle\overline{{\pi}}{C}\colon\operatorname{L}(V,W)\longrightarrow\overline{{\operatorname{L}}}{C}(V,W),\quad\bigl(\overline{{\pi}}{C}\phi\bigr)(v)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\phi(v)+\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}v)\bigr).

(3.4.36)

Note that we use that both
V
and
W
are complex vector spaces, so the
multiplications with the imaginary unit are well defined both within and outside of

\phi
. By construction, we moreover have
\pi{C}+\overline{{\pi}}{C}=\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}_{\operatorname{L}(V,W)}
,

\bigl(\pi{C}\circ\overline{{\pi}}{C}\bigr)\phi(v)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl((\overline{{\pi}}{C}\phi)(v)-\mathrm{i}(\overline{{\pi}}{C}\phi)(\mathrm{i}v)\bigr)=\frac{1}{4}\bigl((\phi(v)+\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}v))-\mathrm{i}(\phi(\mathrm{i}v)+\mathrm{i}\phi(-v))\bigr)=0

(3.4.37)

and

\bigl(\overline{{\pi}}{C}\circ\pi{C}\bigr)\phi(v)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl((\pi{C}\phi)(v)+\mathrm{i}(\pi{C}\phi)(\mathrm{i}v)\bigr)=\frac{1}{4}\bigl((\phi(v)-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}v))+\mathrm{i}(\phi(\mathrm{i}v)-\mathrm{i}\phi(-v))\bigr)=0.

(3.4.38)

We return to the more specific problem of extending an
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
-linear mapping

\phi\colon V\longrightarrow W,

(3.4.39)

between complex vector spaces
V
and
W
to a
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear mapping defined on

V\oplus\overline{{V}}
. Our considerations yield the extension

\phi{C}\colon V\oplus\overline{{V}}\longrightarrow W,\quad\phi{C}(\xi,\chi)\coloneqq\pi{C}\phi(\xi,\chi)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\phi(\xi+\chi)-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi-\mathrm{i}\chi)\bigr),

(3.4.40)

where the final minus sign arises from the conjugated scalar multiplication
\odot
on
\overline{{V}}
, which is defined exactly as in the case of
\overline{{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}
.

Let now
\Phi\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow H
be a group morphism into
another complex Lie group
H
. We study its
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
-linear tangent map
\phi\coloneqq T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
and define
\phi{C}

by (
3.4.40
). By our preliminary
considerations, this yields a
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear mapping with

\bigl(\phi{C}\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\widetilde{\eta}\bigr)(\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\phi(\xi+\xi)-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi-\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)=\phi(\xi)

(3.4.41)

for
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
by
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
-linearity of
\phi
. It remains to check that
\phi{C}

respects the Lie bracket, which by
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linearity of
\phi
and
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-bilinearity of the
bracket suffices to be done on generators of the form
(\xi,0)
and
(0,\chi)
with

\xi,\chi\in\mathfrak{g}
. To this end, we first note that

\phi{C}\bigl([(\xi,0),(0,\chi)]\bigr)=\phi{C}\bigl([\xi,0],[0,\chi]\bigr)=\phi{C}(0)=0

(3.4.42)

as well as

\displaystyle\big[\phi{C}(\xi,0),\phi{C}(0,\chi)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\big[\phi(\xi)-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi),\phi(\chi)+\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}\chi)\big]

(3.4.43)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\bigl([\phi(\xi),\phi(\chi)]+[\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi),\phi(\mathrm{i}\chi)]+\mathrm{i}(-[\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi),\phi(\chi)]+[\phi(\xi),\phi(\mathrm{i}\chi)])\bigr)

(3.4.44)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\bigl(\phi([\xi,\phi])+\phi([\mathrm{i}\xi,\mathrm{i}\chi])+\mathrm{i}(-\phi([\mathrm{i}\xi,\chi])+\phi([\xi,\mathrm{i}\chi]))\bigr)

(3.4.45)

\displaystyle=0,

(3.4.46)

where we have used that
\phi
is a Lie algebra morphism and that the brackets of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{h}
are
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-bilinear. Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle\phi{C}\bigl([(\xi,0),(\chi,0)]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\phi{C}\bigl(([\xi,\chi],0)\bigr)

(3.4.47)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\phi([\xi,\chi])-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}[\xi,\chi])\bigr)

(3.4.48)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\bigl(2[\phi(\xi),\phi(\chi)]-\mathrm{i}[\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi),\phi(\chi)]-\mathrm{i}[\phi(\xi),\phi(\mathrm{i}\chi)]\bigr)

(3.4.49)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\big[\phi(\xi)-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}\xi),\phi(\chi)-\mathrm{i}\phi(\mathrm{i}\chi)\big]

(3.4.50)

\displaystyle=\big[\phi{C}(\xi,0),\phi{C}(\chi,0)\big].

(3.4.51)

The antisymmetry of the Lie bracket yields the remaining combinations, so we have shown that
\phi{C}\colon\mathfrak{g}\oplus\overline{{\mathfrak{g}}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}
is a
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear Lie algebra morphism.

Invoking Lie’s second Theorem
3.3.11
, there thus exists a unique
holomorphic Lie group
morphism

\Phi{C}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow H

(3.4.52)

such that
T_{\mathrm{e}}\Phi{C}=\phi{C}
. By connectedness of
\widetilde{G}
and
(
3.4.41
), we have
\Phi{C}\circ\widetilde{\eta}=\Phi
. As in the proof of Theorem
3.3.14
,
\Phi{C}

is uniquely determined by its tangent map
\phi{C}
, which in turn is the unique

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-linear extension of
\phi
. That is,
\Phi{C}
is unique. Consequently
(
3.4.31
) is a universal complexification of

\widetilde{G}
.

Let now
G
a be connected, but not necessarily simply connected Lie group. Using
what we have already shown, we may mimic our proof of
Theorem
3.3.14
to obtain a description of the universal
complexification of
G
as a quotient of the universal complexification of its universal
covering group
p\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow G
. In the notation of
Theorem
3.3.14
, we get that
\widetilde{\eta}(g)=(g,g)

and thus

\widetilde{\eta}\bigl(\pi_{1}(G)\bigr)=\bigl\{(g,g)\colon g\in\pi_{1}(G)\bigr\}\subseteq\widetilde{G}\times\overline{{\widetilde{G}}}

(3.4.53)

is already a complex subgroup. Consequently,

\eta\colon G\longrightarrow\bigl(\widetilde{G}\times\overline{{\widetilde{G}}}\bigr)\big/\{(g,g)\colon g\in\pi_{1}(G)\},\quad\eta\bigl(p(h)\bigr)=\bigl[(h,h)\bigr]

(3.4.54)

is a universal complexification of
G
by
(
3.3.38
). One may thus consider repeated
universal complexification as a tower of complex Lie groups obtained by starting with
a complex group, such as the complexification of some Lie group. Note that the
mapping

\widetilde{\eta}
from (
3.4.31
) is injective, so we may
apply
Proposition
3.3.27
. Indeed, taking another look
at (
3.4.54
), we see that

\pi_{1}(G{C})=\bigl\{(g,g)\colon g\in\pi_{1}(G)\bigr\}\cong\pi_{1}(G)

in accordance with (
3.3.75
), as direct products of
simply connected groups are simply connected. These considerations match with the
form of the homogeneous space (
1.3.3
).

Example 3.4.6
(The tower of cylinders).

Let
G\coloneqq(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{\times},\cdot)
be the cylinder, which is the universal complexification of the circle group
\ch@airxmathbbm{S}^{1}
by Example
3.4.1
. Its universal covering projection is given by the complex exponential function

\exp\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{\times}

(3.4.55)

and
\pi_{1}(G)\cong\{2\pi\mathrm{i}k\colon k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}\}
.
By (
3.4.5
), the universal complexification is given by

\eta\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{\times}\longrightarrow G{C}\coloneqq\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\times\overline{{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}}\bigr)\big/\{(2\pi\mathrm{i}k,2\pi\mathrm{i}k)\colon k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}\},\quad\eta\bigl(\exp(z)\bigr)=\bigl[(z,z)\bigr]

(3.4.56)

Two points
(z_{1},w_{1}),(z_{2},w_{2})\in G{C}
are thus identified if and only if

z_{1}-z_{2}=2\pi\mathrm{i}k=w_{1}-w_{2}\qquad\textrm{for some }k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{Z}.

(3.4.57)

That is,
\operatorname{\mathrm{Re}}(z_{1})=\operatorname{\mathrm{Re}}(z_{2})
,
\operatorname{\mathrm{Re}}(w_{1})=\operatorname{\mathrm{Re}}(w_{2})
and

\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Im}}(z_{1}),\operatorname{\mathrm{Im}}(w_{1})\bigr)=\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Im}}(w_{1})+2\pi\mathrm{i}k,\operatorname{\mathrm{Im}}(w_{2})+2\pi\mathrm{i}k\bigr).

(3.4.58)

Grouping together real and imaginary parts,

G{C}
is thus geometrically given by
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
(the real parts) times a cylinder (the
imaginary parts), which matches nicely with
Proposition
3.3.27
. Using the compatibility of direct
products from Example
3.4.2
with universal
complexification, we see that the
n
-fold complexification of
G=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{\times}
is
given by

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{2n-1}\times\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{\times}\cong\widetilde{G}^{2n-1}\times G.

(3.4.59)

3.5
Extension and Restriction of Entire Functions

Wir müssen wissen.

Wir werden wissen.

David Hilbert

The purpose of this section is to establish a one-to-one correspondence between entire
functions on
G
and holomorphic ones on
G{C}
for connected linear Lie groups.
These are
the problems of
extension
and
restriction
. We begin with the former,
which we approach by treating two somewhat redundant but nevertheless instructive
special cases first: Simply connected nilpotent and compact groups.

Understanding these in many regards simpler situations first lets us develop machinery
suitable for the proof of Theorem
3.5.9
and its generalization
Proposition
3.5.10
, which then subsume
Theorem
3.5.3
and Theorem
3.5.5
.
Starting out, we are guided by the general assumption that
the universal complexification morphism
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is
injective. This means that
G
constitutes a closed Lie subgroup of
G{C}
by virtue
of Corollary
3.3.20
. Having a set inclusion is of
course necessary to speak of “extensions” of functions in a strict way. However, we
shall convince ourselves shortly that pullback with
\eta
provides a natural
replacement in the general situation. Revisiting the particular case of representative
functions motivates the indicated program quite nicely.

Example 3.5.1
(Representative functions).

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a representation of a connected Lie
group
G
on a finite dimensional vector space
V
. By
Theorem
3.2.11
, every choice of
v\in V
and
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
induces an entire function

??\colon G\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\pi_{v,\varphi}(g)\coloneqq\varphi\bigl(\pi(g)v\bigr),

(3.5.1)

which is even
R
-entire for
0\leq R<1
by
Theorem
3.2.11
. Complexifying
V
, we may extend

\pi
to a Lie group morphism taking values in the complex Lie group
\operatorname{GL}(V{C})
.
We denote this extension also as
\pi
. By the universal property of
G{C}
,
there in turn exists a unique holomorphic representation
\pi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V{C})
with
\pi{C}\circ\eta=\pi
. Setting

\Pi_{v,\varphi}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\Pi_{v,\varphi}(g)\coloneqq\varphi\bigl(\pi{C}(g)v\bigr),

(3.5.2)

where we also extend the linear mapping
\varphi
to
V{C}
, thus yields a
holomorphic function with

\Pi_{v,\varphi}\circ\eta=\pi_{v,\varphi}.

(3.5.3)

Note that asking for the condition (
3.5.3
) makes sense
regardless of injectivity of
\eta
and thus yields the aforementioned natural
generalization of the extension problem. In Example
3.4.3
, we will
see that the failure of injectivity of
\eta
then enforces certain periodicity
conditions for the extensions. We shall encounter a quite drastic instance of this
phenomenon in Example
3.5.11
.

The first class of geometrically quite simple groups we want to investigate are
connected and simply connected groups, which are in addition nilpotent. A concrete
example is then provided by the Heisenberg groups, which we will investigate in
Example
3.6.17
and a comprehensive discussion of which can be found
in the form of the monograph
[
20
]
. By

[
92
, Cor. 11.2.7]
these assumptions imply that the Lie
exponential

\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow G

(3.5.4)

is a diffeomorphism, which is the property we are interested in. It turns out that
this feature, along with many others, passes to the universal complexification.

Proposition 3.5.2
.

Let
G
be a nilpotent connected and simply connected Lie group with universal complexification
(G{C},\eta)
.

i.)

The universal complexification
G{C}
is connected and simply connected.

ii.)

The mapping
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is injective.

iii.)

We have
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})\cong\mathfrak{g}{C}
as Lie algebras.

iv.)

The universal complexification
G{C}
is a nilpotent group.

v.)

The exponential mapping
\exp_{G{C}}\colon\mathfrak{g}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
is a diffeomorphism.

Proof
:

By Corollary
3.3.15
,

v.)
, the claims

i.)
and

iii.)
hold and
T_{\mathrm{e}}\eta
is injective. But
then

\eta=\exp_{G{C}}\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\eta\circ\log_{G}

(3.5.5)

is injective itself as a composition of injective maps, where we write
\log_{G}
for the
inverse of
\exp_{G}
. We have thus
shown
ii.)
. To prove

iv.)
, we check that
\mathfrak{g}{C}
is nilpotent, which
by
[
92
, Thm. 11.2.5]
then implies that
G{C}
is. Conversely, we
may use that
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent by assumption. Invoking
Engel’s
37
37

37

Friedrich Engel (1861-1941) was a German
mathematician. Together with Sophus Lie, he wrote the series of books “Theory of
transformation groups”
[
61
]
, which may be viewed as the
inception of modern Lie theory.
Not to be confused with the philosopher Friedrich Engels.
Theorem

[
92
, Thm. 5.2.8]
it suffices to check that the linear mappings

\operatorname{\mathrm{ad}}_{\xi}\colon\mathfrak{g}{C}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}{C}
are nilpotent for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
. That is, for every
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
there exists some integer
k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
such that
\operatorname{\mathrm{ad}}_{\xi}^{k}=0
. But
this is clear by either (
3.3.33
)
or (
3.3.34
) and the nilpotency of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Finally,

v.)
follows from

[
92
, Cor. 11.2.7]
in the same manner as it did for
G
itself.

\ch@irxboxempty

The upshot is that
G{C}
is, just like
G
, geometrically a vector space.

Theorem 3.5.3
(Extension from nilpotent groups).

Let
G
be a connected, simply connected and nilpotent Lie group with universal
complexification
(G{C},\eta)
. Moreover, fix a parameter
R\geq 0
and
f\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)
. Then there exists a unique

F\in\mathscr{H}(G{C})\cap\mathscr{E}_{R}(G{C})\qquad\textrm{such that}\qquad\eta^{*}F=f.

(3.5.6)

More precisely,

F\bigl(\eta(g)\exp\xi\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\xi};g\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{F}\bigl(\underline{\xi};\eta(g)\bigr)

(3.5.7)

holds for all
g\in G
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
.

Proof
:

By Proposition
3.5.2
,

v.)
, the exponential map
\exp_{G{C}}\colon\mathfrak{g}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}
constitutes a global chart of
G{C}
with

\exp_{G{C}}(\mathfrak{g})=\eta(G)
. Moreover, the formal Lie-Taylor series

\operatorname{T}_{f}(\underline{\xi};\mathrm{e})
converges for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
by
Theorem
3.2.4
. We define
F\colon G{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
by

F\bigl(\exp_{G{C}}(\xi)\bigr)\coloneqq\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\xi};\mathrm{e}\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)f\bigr)(\mathrm{e})\cdot\underline{\xi}^{\alpha},

(3.5.8)

which is holomorphic with respect to
\xi
and thus holomorphic on
G{C}
. For
g=\exp_{G}(\xi)\in G
, we get

F\bigl(\eta(g)\bigr)=F\bigl(\exp_{G{C}}(\xi)\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\xi};\mathrm{e}\bigr)=f\bigl(\exp_{G}(\xi)\bigr)=f(g).

(3.5.9)

Finally, (
3.5.7
) holds by construction and the fact that
\mathrm{e}\in G{C}\cap\eta(G)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Remark 3.5.4
(Nilpotent groups).

To prove Theorem
3.5.3
, it would suffice to demand that

\exp_{G{C}}\colon\mathfrak{g}{C}\longrightarrow G{C}

(3.5.10)

is a diffeomorphism. By a combination of
[
92
, Thm. 14.4.3]

and Dixmier-Saito’s Theorem
38
38

38

Morihiko Saitō (born 1961) is a Japenese
algebraic geometer and algebraic analyst working at Kyoto University. Dixmier and
Saitō discovered their theorem independently.

[
53
,
164
]
, a
modern formulation of which can be found in

[
92
, Thm. 14.4.8]
, this however
already implies that
G{C}
and thus in particular any of its subgroups are
nilpotent. As our general assumption is the injectivity of
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
, we have thus not lost any generality by dealing with the
nilpotent situation directly.

Next, we investigate connected compact Lie groups
K
by applying the
techniques from the nilpotent case locally. By

[
92
, Prop. 15.2.1(c)]
, the polar decomposition

\Phi\colon K\times\mathfrak{k}\longrightarrow K{C},\quad\Phi(g,\xi)\coloneqq\eta(g)\cdot\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)

(3.5.11)

is a diffeomorphism. Geometrically, one may thus view
K{C}
as a cylinder over

\eta(K)\cong K
, see also
[
92
, Prop. 15.2.1(a)]
. In particular, this
implies
\pi_{1}(K{C})\cong\pi_{1}(K)
. Consequently, all topological obstructions for
analytic continuation are already present within
K
.

Theorem 3.5.5
(Extension from compact groups).

Let
K
be a connected as well as compact Lie group with universal
complexification
(K{C},\eta)
,
R\geq 0
and
f\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(K)
. Then there
exists a unique

F\in\mathscr{H}(K{C})\cap\mathscr{E}_{R}(K{C})\qquad\textrm{such that}\qquad\eta^{*}F=f.

(3.5.12)

More precisely,

F\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{K}(\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi};g\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{F}\bigl(\underline{\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi};\eta(g)\bigr)

(3.5.13)

holds for all
g\in K
and
\chi,\xi\in\mathfrak{k}
.

Proof
:

By
[
92
, Prop. 9.2.5]
there exists a zero neighbourhood

U\subseteq\mathfrak{k}
such that
\exp_{K}\big|_{U}
is a diffeomorphism onto
its image. Combining this with (
3.5.11
) and the injectivity of

\eta
yields a holomorphic atlas
(z_{g},V_{g})_{g\in K}
of
K{C}

with
V_{g}\coloneqq\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(U)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\mathfrak{k})
and

z_{g}^{-1}(\chi,\xi)\coloneqq\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi).

(3.5.14)

A schematic picture of
V_{g}
can be found in
Figure
2
, where only a finite section of the
vertical lines is pictured and
\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)
identified with
\mathrm{i}\xi

by means of (
3.5.11
) for simplicity.
By Theorem
3.2.8
,
v.)

the Lie-Taylor series
\operatorname{T}_{f}(\underline{\xi};g)
of
f
from
(
3.2.21
) around
g
converges for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{k}{C}
. For every
g\in G
, we define
functions
F_{g}\colon V_{g}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
by

F_{g}\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)\coloneqq\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi};g\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)f\bigr)(g)\cdot(\underline{\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi})^{\alpha},

(3.5.15)

which is holomorphic with respect to the complex variable
\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi
. Moreover, we
note that

V_{g}\cap\eta(K)=\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(U)=\eta\bigl(g\exp_{K}(U)\bigr)

(3.5.16)

and thus

\eta^{*}F_{g}\bigl(g\exp_{K}\chi\bigr)=F_{g}\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}\chi\bigr)=T_{f}(\underline{\chi};g)=f\bigl(g\exp_{K}\chi\bigr).

(3.5.17)

Consequently,
F_{g}
constitutes a holomorphic extension of
f\big|_{g\exp(U)}
.
Moreover, if
V_{g}\cap V_{h}\neq\emptyset
for some
g,h\in K
as sketched in
Figure
3
, then

F_{g}\Big|_{\eta(g\exp U\cap h\exp U)}=f\Big|_{g\exp U\cap h\exp U}=F_{h}\Big|_{\eta(g\exp U\cap h\exp U)}

(3.5.18)

and thus
F_{g}=F_{h}
by virtue of the identity principle from
Corollary
3.3.32
. Here we use that

\mathfrak{k}{C}
is the
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
-span of
\mathfrak{k}
. Consequently and as
\bigcup_{g\in G}V_{g}=K{C}
, the family
(F_{g})_{g\in G}
glues to a globally defined holomorphic
function
F
on
K{C}
fulfilling
\eta^{*}F=f
and (
3.5.13
).
Once again invoking the identity principle from
Corollary
3.3.32
establishes the uniqueness of
F
. Finally,
(
3.5.13
) implies that the Lie-Taylor coefficients of
F
at

\eta(g)
simply coincide with those of
f
at
g
for all
g\in G
. This means that
F\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(K{C})
by virtue of
\mathrm{e}_{K{C}}=\mathrm{e}_{K}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Figure 2:
Schematic picture of
V_{g}

Figure 3:
Schematic picture of
V_{g}\cap V_{h}

Notably, using the notation of Theorem
3.5.5
and considering

\widetilde{F}\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)\coloneqq\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\mathrm{i}\xi};g\exp_{K}(\chi)\bigr)

(3.5.19)

also yields an entire function
\widetilde{F}\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(K{C})
with
\eta^{*}\widetilde{F}=f
. However,
\widetilde{F}
fails to be holomorphic in general. It
coincides with (
3.5.13
) whenever
[\chi,\xi]=0
, as in this case

\exp_{K{C}}(\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)=\exp_{K{C}}(\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi)

(3.5.20)

and thus by Theorem
3.2.4
and (
3.5.13
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\mathrm{i}\xi};g\exp(\chi)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{T}_{F}\bigl(\underline{\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi};\eta(g)\bigr)

(3.5.21)

\displaystyle=F\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)

(3.5.22)

\displaystyle=F\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)

(3.5.23)

\displaystyle=F\bigl(\eta(g\exp_{K}\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)

(3.5.24)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)F\bigr)\bigl(\eta(g\exp_{K}\chi)\bigr)\cdot(\underline{\mathrm{i}\xi})^{\alpha}

(3.5.25)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)f\bigr)(g\exp_{K}\chi)\cdot(\underline{\mathrm{i}\xi})^{\alpha}

(3.5.26)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{T}_{f}\bigl(\underline{\mathrm{i}\xi};g\exp_{K}\chi\bigr)

(3.5.27)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{F}\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{K{C}}(\chi)\exp_{K{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr).

(3.5.28)

This completes our preliminary – and ultimately redundant – considerations on
extension. Guided by these particular cases, we will now first use the Lie-Taylor series
(
3.2.22
) to obtain a holomorphic extension of a given entire function around
the group unit. In a second step, we then analytically continue along any path in
G{C}

using essentially Steiner
39
39

39

Jakob Steiner (1796-1863) was a Swiss synthetic
geometer infamous for never preparing his lectures beforehand, and then suffering the
consequences. His treatment of geometry was entirely algebraic, as he harboured a
deep disdain for analysis and, in particular, coordinates.
chains of balls within charts.
Finally, our considerations from
Section
3.3
will allow us to turn these local statements
into global ones, which may be seen as an instance of the Oka principle, see again
Remark
2.4.21
: In the absence of topological obstructions, analytic
continuation is always possible.

We slightly alter our notation for the original function and its extension from
f

and
F
to
\phi
and
\Phi
, respectively, as we shall work with the Lie-Taylor
Majorant
\operatorname{F}
in the sequel. Before putting our plan into practice, we
recall the notion of analytic continuations along paths, which notably need not
be differentiable but only continuous. Indeed, let
M
and
N
be complex
manifolds and
\gamma
be a path within
M
. We say that a holomorphic
mapping
g_{0}\colon U_{0}\longrightarrow N
, where
U_{0}\subseteq M
is a
connected
open neighbourhood of
\gamma(0)
, may be analytically continued along

\gamma
if there are
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}=1
, open connected
neighbourhoods
U_{j}\subseteq M
of
\gamma(t_{j})
and
holomorphic mappings
g_{j}\colon U_{j}\longrightarrow N
with

\gamma(t_{j})\in U_{j-1}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad g_{j-1}\Big|_{U_{j-1}\cap U_{j}}=g_{j}\Big|_{U_{j-1}\cap U_{j}}\qquad\textrm{for }j=1,\ldots,n.

(3.5.29)

Note that this does not mean that the
f_{j}
glue to a globally defined
holomorphic function, as e.g. the principal branch of the holomorphic logarithm
may be analytically continued along any path within
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{\times}
.

Proposition 3.5.6
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group such that the universal complexification morphism

\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is injective, and let
\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
.

i.)

There exists zero neighbourhood
U\subseteq G{C}
and a holomorphic
function

\phi_{0}\colon U\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(3.5.30)

such that
\phi_{0}\big|_{U\cap G}=\phi
.

ii.)

The function
\phi_{0}
may be analytically continued along any path in

G{C}
starting at
\mathrm{e}
.

Proof
:

Throughout, we use the notation from (
3.2.24
) and (
3.2.25
).
By Theorem
3.2.4
we may set
U\coloneqq\exp(\mathfrak{g}{C})
and

\phi_{0}\coloneqq\operatorname{T}_{\phi}(\,\cdot\,;\mathrm{e})
. Let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow G{C}
be a path starting at the group unit. To show the second
statement, we first replace
U=\exp(V)
by a sufficiently small zero neighbourhood
such that
\exp\colon V\longrightarrow U
is a diffeomorphism. By continuity
of
\gamma
, its trace
\gamma([0,1])\subseteq G{C}
is compact. Hence, there are

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{k}=1
with

\gamma(t_{\ell+1})\in\gamma(t_{\ell})\exp(\mathfrak{g}{C})\qquad\textrm{for}\quad\ell=1,\ldots,k-1

(3.5.31)

and

\gamma\bigl([0,1]\bigr)\subseteq\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{k}\gamma(t_{\ell})U=U\cup\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}\gamma(t_{\ell})U.

(3.5.32)

We prove that the Lie-Taylor series
\operatorname{T}_{\phi_{0}}(\,\cdot\,;\gamma(t_{1}))
yields
an entire function on
\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
by generalizing Theorem
3.2.8
,

iii.)
to
complex translations
. By construction,

\gamma(t_{1})\in U
, so there exists a
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}{C}
with
\gamma(t_{1})=\exp\xi
. Recall that
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\mathfrak{g}{C}
by construction or
Lemma
3.3.30
and thus choosing a basis of

\mathfrak{g}
yields a basis of
\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})
, see
Corollary
3.3.31
. Unwrapping the definitions and plugging in
(
3.2.1
) for
\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi_{0}
leads to

\displaystyle\operatorname{F}_{\phi_{0}}\bigl(\lvert z\rvert;\gamma(t_{1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi_{0}\bigr)(\exp\xi)\Big\rvert\cdot\lvert z\rvert^{k}

(3.5.33)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k,\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{k}}{k!\ell!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)^{\ell}\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(\mathrm{e})\Big\rvert.

(3.5.34)

Decomposing
\xi=\xi_{1}+\mathrm{i}\xi_{2}
with
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}

yields by Proposition
3.3.16

\mathscr{L}(\xi)\psi=\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1})\psi+\mathrm{i}\cdot\mathscr{L}(\xi_{2})\psi\qquad\textrm{for all }\psi\in\mathscr{H}(U).

(3.5.35)

Plugging in
\psi\coloneqq\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi_{0}
for any
\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}
, we
estimate

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)^{\ell}\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(\mathrm{e})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\Bigl(\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1})+\mathrm{i}\cdot\mathscr{L}(\xi_{2})\bigr)^{\ell}\mathscr{L}_{X_{\alpha}}\phi\Bigr)(\mathrm{e})\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\sum_{\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{\ell}=1}^{2}\Big\lvert\Bigl(\mathscr{L}\bigl(\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\varepsilon_{\ell}}\bigr)\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\Bigr)(\mathrm{e})\Big\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\sum_{\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{\ell}=1}^{2}\big\lvert\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{m_{1}}\cdots\xi_{\varepsilon_{\ell}}^{m_{\ell}}\big\rvert\cdot\Big\lvert\Bigl(\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{m_{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{m_{\ell}}\bigr)\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\Bigr)(\mathrm{e})\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert 2\xi\rVert_{\infty}^{\ell}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}=1}^{n}\Big\lvert\Bigl(\mathscr{L}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{m_{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{m_{\ell}}\bigr)\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\Bigr)(\mathrm{e})\Big\rvert

\displaystyle=\lVert 2\xi\rVert_{\infty}^{\ell}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k+\ell}}\big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(\mathrm{e})\big\rvert

\displaystyle=\lVert 2\xi\rVert_{\infty}^{\ell}\cdot c_{k+\ell}(\phi)\cdot(k+\ell)!

for all
k,\ell\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Plugging this into (
3.5.34
) with
z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\big\lvert\operatorname{F}_{\phi_{0}}\bigl(z;\gamma(t_{1})\bigr)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k,\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{k}}{k!\ell!}\bigg\lvert\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)^{\ell}\mathscr{L}(\beta)\phi\bigr)(\mathrm{e})\bigg\rvert

(3.5.36)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\lVert\xi\rVert_{\infty})^{\ell}}{\ell!}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(k+\ell)!}{k!}\cdot c_{k+\ell}(\phi)\cdot\lvert z\rvert^{k}

(3.5.37)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\lVert\xi\rVert_{\infty})^{\ell}}{\ell!}\operatorname{F}_{\phi}^{(\ell)}\bigl(\lvert z\rvert\bigr)

(3.5.38)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{F}_{\phi}\bigl(\lvert z\rvert+2\lVert\xi\rVert_{\infty}\bigr),

(3.5.39)

where
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}^{(\ell)}
denotes the
\ell
-th derivative of the Lie-Taylor
majorant
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}\in\mathscr{H}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
. In the final two steps, we have used
that the Lie-Taylor majorant
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}
is entire. Thus

\operatorname{T}_{\phi_{0}}(\,\cdot\,;\gamma(t_{1}))
indeed constitutes an entire function on

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{n}
. With other words, using that
\exp\colon V\longrightarrow U
is bijective, we
may define

\phi_{1}\colon\gamma(t_{1})U\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\phi_{1}\bigl(\gamma(t_{1})\exp\chi\bigr)\coloneqq\operatorname{T}_{\phi_{0}}\bigl(\underline{\chi};\gamma(t_{1})\bigr).

(3.5.40)

Note that
U\cap\gamma(t_{1})U\neq\emptyset
, as
\gamma(t_{1})
is within
by construction. Hence, the intersection even contains an open connected
neighbourhood
W
of
\gamma(t_{1})
, on which
\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}

coincide. By the identity principle, we thus have
\phi_{0}=\phi_{1}
on the
intersection of their domains. We may now iterate the procedure and define

\phi_{2}\coloneqq\operatorname{T}_{\phi_{1}}(\,\cdot\,;\gamma(t_{2}))
and so on.
Crucially, we have shown that the Lie-Taylor majorant

\operatorname{F}_{\phi_{1}}(\,\cdot\,;\gamma(t_{1}))
at
\gamma(t_{1})
is entire, as
well. This yields the desired analytic continuation of
\phi_{0}
along

\gamma
after finitely many steps.

\ch@irxboxempty

Remark 3.5.7
.

Note that it suffices to know the values of
\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
in some
neighbourhood of the group unit to set off this process. This observation will be
crucial to drop a somewhat unnatural assumption we are going to make in our initial
version of Theorem
3.5.9
to simplify the proof. Moreover, a combination
of our construction, the uniqueness of the extension, the identity principle and
Theorem
3.2.4
implies that an analogue of
(
3.5.13
) holds for
\phi
and
\Phi
.

Investing the monodromy theorem, as it can be found in the textbook

[
109
, Sec. 10.3.4]
, yields the existence of a globally defined holomorphic
extensions under the additional assumption that
G{C}
is simply connected.

Corollary 3.5.8
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group such that
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is
injective and
G{C}
is simply connected. Then, for every
\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
,
there is a unique holomorphic function
\Phi\in\mathscr{H}(G{C})
such that
\Phi\circ\eta=\phi
.

Taking another look at (
3.3.75
), the idea is now that
this describes the situation on the universal covering group, at least when

\widetilde{\eta}
is injective.

Theorem 3.5.9
(Extension).

Let
G
be a connected Lie group such that the universal complexification morphism

\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}{C}
of the universal
covering group
\widetilde{G}
is injective. Then, for every
\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)
, where
R\geq 0
,
there exists a unique holomorphic function

\Phi\in\mathscr{H}(G{C})\cap\mathscr{E}_{R}(G{C})\qquad\textrm{such that}\qquad\eta^{*}\Phi=\phi.

(3.5.41)

Moreover,

\Phi\bigl(\eta(g)\exp_{G{C}}(\chi)\exp_{G{C}}(\mathrm{i}\xi)\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{\phi}\bigl(\underline{\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi};g\bigr)=\operatorname{T}_{\Phi}\bigl(\underline{\chi+\mathrm{i}\xi};\eta(g)\bigr)

(3.5.42)

holds for all
g\in G{C}
,
\chi,\xi\in\operatorname{Lie}(\eta(G))\subseteq\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\operatorname{Lie}(\eta(G)){C}
.

Proof
:

The idea is to combine Proposition
3.3.27
with
(
3.3.75
). Denoting the universal covering projection by

p\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow G
, we define
\psi\coloneqq\phi\circ p
, which is

\pi_{1}(G)
-invariant and an element of
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
by
Proposition
3.2.10
. Applying
Corollary
3.5.8
, we obtain a unique holomorphic
function
\Psi\in\mathscr{H}(\widetilde{G}{C})
with
\Psi\circ\widetilde{\eta}=\psi
. Next,
we show that
\Psi
is invariant under the action of
\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))
. To
this end, fix
h\in\pi_{1}(G)
and consider the auxiliary function
\Psi_{h}(g)\coloneqq\Psi(gh)
for
some
g\in G{C}
. Notice that

\Psi\bigl(\widetilde{\eta}(g)\widetilde{\eta}(h)\bigr)=\psi(gh)=\psi(g)=\Psi\bigl(\widetilde{\eta}(g)\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in\widetilde{G}.

By Proposition
3.3.16
, which we may apply by virtue of

\operatorname{Lie}(G{C})=\mathfrak{g}{C}
, this means that the Lie-Taylor series of the
difference
\Psi-\Psi_{h}

around the group unit vanishes and thus we get
\Psi=\Psi_{h}
by a
combination of the Lie-Taylor formula (
3.2.21
) with the
usual identity principle.
Consequently,
\Psi
is indeed invariant under the action of

\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))
and thus factors
through the quotient projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow G{C}
, yielding the
desired extension
\Phi
with
\Psi=\Phi\circ\pi
: Indeed, recall that
\eta\circ p=\pi\circ\widetilde{\eta}
by virtue of (
3.3.38
)
and thus

\Phi\circ\eta\circ p=\Phi\circ\pi\circ\widetilde{\eta}=\Psi\circ\widetilde{\eta}=\psi=\phi\circ p.

Invoking the local bijectivity of
p
, this implies
\Phi\circ\eta=\phi
as
desired. The uniqueness of
\Phi
is clear by
Corollary
3.3.32
and we have already noted the validity of
(
3.5.42
) in Remark
3.5.7
. This also proves the
remaining statement.

\ch@irxboxempty

Being more careful, we can do even better.

Proposition 3.5.10
.

The assertions of Theorem
3.5.9
also hold if
\widetilde{\eta}
is not
injective.

Proof
:

Let
\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
and
\psi\coloneqq\phi\circ p
as before. By
Corollary
3.3.15
,

v.)
, the universal complexification
morphism
\widetilde{\eta}\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\widetilde{G}{C}
is locally injective.
Thus, there exists a neighbourhood
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\widetilde{G}
of the group unit
such that
\widetilde{\eta}\big|_{\widetilde{U}}
is a diffeomorphism with inverse
\Xi
defined in
the neighbourhood
\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{U})
of the group unit in the image group

\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{G})
. Here we use the fact that
\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{G})\subseteq\widetilde{G}{C}
is a closed submanifold by
Corollary
3.3.20
. Consider the function

\widetilde{\psi}\colon\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{U})\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\widetilde{\psi}\coloneqq\psi\circ\Xi.

(3.5.43)

By continuity of the group multiplication, we find another neighbourhood
V\subseteq\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{U})
of the group unit in
\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{G})
such that
V\cdot V\subseteq\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{U})
. As
\Xi
is a group morphism as the inverse of a
group morphism, we thus have
\Xi(gh)=\Xi(g)\Xi(h)
for all
g,h\in V
.
Consequently, for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, we have

\mathscr{L}(\xi)\widetilde{\psi}\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathscr{L}_{X_{\xi}}\bigl(\Xi^{*}\psi\bigr)\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}=\Xi^{*}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(T_{\mathrm{e}}\Xi\xi)\psi\bigr)\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathscr{L}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{e}}\Xi\xi\bigr)\psi\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}.

(3.5.44)

As in
[
90
, Prop. 4.3, ii.)]
, it follows that the
Lie-Taylor majorant
T_{\widetilde{\psi}}(\,\cdot\,;\mathrm{e})
is entire. By
Remark
3.5.7
, we may thus use the techniques from
Proposition
3.5.6
to obtain another zero neighbourhood

V_{0}\subseteq\widetilde{G}{C}
and a holomorphic

\psi_{0}\colon V_{0}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\quad\textrm{with}\quad\psi_{0}\Big|_{V_{0}\cap V}=\widetilde{\psi},

(3.5.45)

which may be analytically continued along any path in
\widetilde{G}{C}
starting at the
group unit. By the simple connectedness of
\widetilde{G}{C}
, which follows from
Corollary
3.3.15
,

v.)
, and the monodromy theorem,
there thus exists a unique globally defined holomorphic extension

\widetilde{\psi}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}

(3.5.46)

of
\psi_{0}
. From here, one may proceed as in the proof of
Theorem
3.5.9
to check that
\psi
factors through the quotient
projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{G}{C}\longrightarrow G
and that this provides the
unique

\Phi\in\mathscr{H}(G{C})\qquad\textrm{with}\quad\Phi\circ\eta=\phi.

(3.5.47)

Here, we use that
\widetilde{\psi}
is
locally

\widetilde{\eta}(\pi_{1}(G))
-invariant: To see this, we fix
h\in\pi_{1}(G)
and if necessary shrink
V
further to achieve
\widetilde{\eta}(h)\cdot V\in\widetilde{\eta}(\widetilde{U})
. By virtue of

\widetilde{\psi}\big|_{g\widetilde{\eta}(h)}=\psi\circ\Xi\big|_{g\widetilde{\eta}(h)}=\psi\bigl(\Xi(g)h\bigr)=\psi(g)

(3.5.48)

for
g\in V
this then implies
\pi_{1}(G)
-invariance of
\psi_{0}
and thus of
\psi
by
the identity principle.

\ch@irxboxempty

Example 3.5.11
(Automatic periodicity).

We revisit the special linear group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and its
universal covering group
p\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\longrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})

from Example
3.4.3
. Recall that

\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}){C}\cong\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\cong\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}){C}

(3.5.49)

and let
f\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))
. By
Theorem
3.5.9
, there
exists a unique holomorphic extension

F\in\mathscr{H}\bigl(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\bigr)\cap\mathscr{E}_{0}\bigl(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\bigr)

(3.5.50)

such that
F\circ p=f
. This means that
F
factors through
p
and is thus
necessarily periodic with respect to the fundamental group

\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))
. Consequently, the algebra

\mathscr{E}_{0}(\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))
does not separate points in this
case. In Theorem
3.5.13
we will see that

\mathscr{H}\bigl(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\bigr)\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{0}\bigl(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})\bigr),

(3.5.51)

where we view
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
as a real Lie group to define entire functions
as before. This matches nicely with the well-known fact that every representation
of

\widetilde{\operatorname{SL}}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
factors over the fundamental group

\pi_{1}(\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
, which in particular implies the same for all
representative functions, which are entire by
Theorem
3.2.11
and always possess holomorphic
extensions by Example
3.5.1
. Indeed,
the analogous statement holds for any covering group
of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. For
a comprehensive discussion and a proof of these observations we refer to

[
92
, Ex. 9.5.18]
.

This completes the resolution of the extension problem: Every entire function
defined on a Lie group possesses – for better and for worse – a unique
holomorphic extension to the universal complexification.
We turn towards the problem of restriction. That is, we investigate, whether

\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)\cap\mathscr{H}(G)=\mathscr{H}(G)

(3.5.52)

for a complex Lie group
G
and how the corresponding topologies are related. In
particular, we may consider this problem for general complex groups, not just groups
arising as universal complexifications.

As it has been the theme throughout, the key towards a resolution of the problem
consists in a suitable version of the
Cauchy estimates. To formulate them, we fix some notation. For
continuous functions
\phi\colon G\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
and compact subsets

K\subseteq G
, we write

??\coloneqq\max_{g\in K}\big\lvert\phi(g)\big\rvert.

(3.5.53)

To facilitate the estimate, we make the additional assumption of working with a matrix
Lie group. That is, we assume that
G
is linear and choose a faithful finite
dimensional representation as matrices. In this context, we denote the operator
norm ??of a matrix
M\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
simply by
\lVert M\rVert
. Moreover, it makes
sense to speak
both
of operator
norms of group elements and of Lie algebra elements. Thus there is a direct way to
compare sizes of infinitesimal and exponentiated elements. Moreover, as the Lie
exponential is simply given by the exponential series for matrix Lie groups, we have
the estimate

\big\lVert\exp\xi\big\rVert\leq e^{\lVert\xi\rVert}\qquad\textrm{for all }\xi\in\mathfrak{g},

(3.5.54)

where
e
denotes Euler’s number.
40
40

40

Leonhard Euler (1707-1783) was an
extraordinarily
productive Swiss polymath. So much so that many of his discoveries were not named
after him, but rather after the second scientist to observe them.

This may be thought of as a continuity estimate for the Lie exponential.
Keeping this in mind,
we arrive at the following Lie theoretic incarnation of the Cauchy estimates.

Lemma 3.5.12
(Lie theoretic Cauchy estimates).

Let
G
be a complex connected matrix Lie group,
\phi\in\mathscr{H}(G)
,
K_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
a compact neighbourhood of zero,
r>0
and
k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
.
Then, writing
K_{r}\coloneqq\{g\in G\colon\lVert g\rVert\leq??^{r}\}
, we
have

\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k})\phi\bigr)(g\exp\xi)\Big\rvert\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{g\exp(K_{0})K_{r}}\cdot\frac{k^{k}}{r^{k}}

(3.5.55)

for all
g\in G
,
\xi\in K_{0}
and
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}
with
\lVert\xi_{j}\rVert\leq 1
for
j=1,\ldots,k
.

Proof
:

As the Lie exponential
\exp(z\xi_{j})
is the flow of the left invariant vector field
X_{\xi_{j}}
, we have

\mathscr{L}(\xi_{j})\phi(g)=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\phi\bigl(g\exp(\xi)\exp(z\xi_{j})\bigr)\Big|_{z=0}

(3.5.56)

for
j=1,\ldots,k
,
g\in G
and
\xi\in K_{0}
. Alternatively, this readily
follows from (
3.2.1
). As the Lie exponential induces
a holomorphic atlas, the composition

\phi\circ\ell_{g\exp(\xi)}\circ\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
is
holomorphic. By induction, we get

\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k})\phi(g\exp(\xi))=\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}\phi\bigl(g\exp(\xi)\exp(z^{k}\xi_{k})\cdots\exp(z^{1}\xi_{1})\bigr)\Big|_{z^{1}=\ldots=z^{k}=0},

where

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k}\ni z\mapsto\phi\bigl(g\exp(\xi)\exp(z^{k}\xi_{k})\cdots\exp(z^{1}\xi_{1})\bigr)

(3.5.57)

is holomorphic. Notably, there are only first derivatives involved. Applying the
usual
Cauchy estimates for first derivatives with all radii equal to
r/k
yields

\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k})\phi\bigr)(g\exp\xi)\Big\rvert\leq\frac{k^{k}}{r^{k}}\max_{\lvert z^{1}\rvert=\cdots=\lvert z^{k}\rvert=r/k}\Big\lvert\phi(g\exp(\xi)\exp(z^{k}\xi_{k})\cdots\exp(z^{1}\xi_{1}))\Big\rvert.

(
\star
)

By submultiplicativity of the operator norm and (
3.5.54
),
we note

\displaystyle\big\lVert\exp(z^{k}\xi_{k})\cdots\exp(z^{1}\xi_{1})\big\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\big\lVert\exp(z^{k}\xi_{k})\big\rVert\cdots\big\lVert\exp(z^{1}\xi_{1})\big\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigl(\lvert z^{k}\rvert\cdot\lVert\xi_{k}\rVert\bigr)\cdots\exp\bigl(\lvert z^{1}\rvert\cdot\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(\lvert z^{k}\rvert\cdot\lVert\xi_{k}\rVert+\cdots+\lvert z^{k}\rvert\cdot\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(r)

for
\lvert z^{1}\rvert=\cdots=\lvert z^{k}\rvert=r/k
. That is, we have

\exp(z^{k}\xi_{k})\cdots\exp(z^{1}\xi_{1})\in K_{r}\qquad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert z^{1}\rvert=\ldots=\lvert z^{k}\rvert=r/k.

(3.5.58)

Consequently, (
3.5.55
) follows from
(

⋆

‣
Proof
) by varying
z
and
\xi
.

\ch@irxboxempty

This simple inequality allows us to resolve the restriction problem with a positive
answer for linear groups. Here, we tacitly – and in the sequel without further
mention – use the functoriality of
\mathscr{E}_{0}
and
\mathscr{H}
, to pass from a
linear group to a concrete realization as matrices to apply the Lie theoretic Cauchy
estimates.

Theorem 3.5.13
(Restriction).

Let
G
be a complex connected linear Lie group. Then

\mathscr{H}(G)\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)

(3.5.59)

induces an embedding of Fréchet algebras, where we endow
\mathscr{H}(G)
with the topology of locally uniform convergence.

Proof
:

Let
\phi\in\mathscr{H}(G)
and fix a basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\lVert\mathit{e}_{j}\rVert=1
for
j=1,\ldots,n
. We once again use the Lie-Taylor majorants from (
3.2.24
). By Lemma
3.5.12
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{F}_{\phi}\bigl(\lvert z\rvert\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(\mathrm{e})\Big\rvert

(3.5.60)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{K_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\frac{k^{k}}{r^{k}}

(3.5.61)

\displaystyle=\lVert\phi\rVert_{K_{r}}\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\biggl(\frac{n}{r}\biggr)^{k}\frac{k^{k}}{k!}\cdot\lvert z\rvert^{k}

(3.5.62)

for
any

r>0
and
z\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. The power series on the right-hand side has a positive radius of convergence
R
for any
r>0
, namely

R=\frac{r}{n}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sqrt[k]{k!}}{k}=\frac{r}{ne},

where
e
denotes again Euler’s number. Taking the limit
r\rightarrow\infty
, we
see that
\operatorname{F}_{\phi}

is indeed entire. This moreover proves that the seminorms
\mathrm{q}_{0,c}
are
continuous with respect to the topology of locally uniform convergence on

\mathscr{H}(G)
; here one first fixes
c>0
and then chooses
r
sufficiently
large. Conversely, let
K_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be a compact zero neighbourhood
such that the restriction
\exp\big|_{K_{0}}
is bijective and
\lVert\xi\rVert\leq 1
for all
\xi\in K
. The Lie-Taylor formula (
3.2.22
) yields then

\big\lVert\phi\big\rVert_{g\exp(K_{0})}\leq\max_{\xi\in K_{0}}\big\lvert\phi(g\exp(\xi))\big\rvert\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(g)\Big\rvert\eqqcolon\mathrm{q}_{0,1,g}(\phi)

for any
g\in G
. By the translation invariance from Theorem
3.2.8
,

iii.)
, the seminorm
\mathrm{q}_{0,1,g}
is
continuous on
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
, completing the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Having established both an extension and a restriction result, the natural question is
what happens during a roundtrip. Astonishingly, the ultimately rather pleasing answer
is
nothing whatsoever
, including the topologies.

Corollary 3.5.14
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group such that its universal
complexification
(G{C},\eta)
is a linear Lie group.
41
41

41

Note that, by the
universal property from Definition
3.3.1
, every
linear Lie
group complexifies to a linear complex Lie group.
Then the pullback

\eta^{*}\colon\mathscr{H}(G{C})\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}_{0}(G),\quad\eta^{*}\Phi\coloneqq\Phi\circ\eta

(3.5.63)

with the universal complexification morphism
\eta
is well defined and an isomorphism of Fréchet algebras, where the products are defined pointwisely.

Proof
:

We check that extension and restriction, which is modelled by
(
3.5.63
), are inverse processes. Given
\Phi\in\mathscr{H}(G{C})
, Theorem
3.5.13
yields
\Phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
and thus
\eta^{*}\Phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
by
Proposition
3.2.10
. Invoking Theorem
3.5.9
,
the function
\eta^{*}\Phi
induces a unique holomorphic function
\Psi\in\mathscr{H}(G{C})
such that

\eta^{*}\Psi=\Psi\circ\eta=\eta^{*}\Phi.

(3.5.64)

But
\Phi
also has this property, so
\Psi=\Phi
by uniqueness. Conversely, let
\phi\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
be given. By Theorem
3.5.9
, there exists a unique holomorphic function
\Phi\in\mathscr{H}(G{C})\cap\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
such that
\eta^{*}\Phi=\phi
. Consequently, (
3.5.63
) is well defined and bijective. It is clear that (
3.5.63
) preserves sums and pointwise products.

\ch@irxboxempty

On the level of the observable algebra
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
, we have thus put the
program of holomorphic deformation quantization as
outlined in Section
1.3
into practice
successfully. The observable algebra
allows for a holomorphic description and the conditions on the Lie-Taylor
coefficients
simply translate to locally uniform convergence within the domains of the
holomorphic extensions.

Using our results, one may now transfer the continuous star products on

\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{1}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
to star products on the
holomorphic polynomial algebra

\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)\coloneqq\mathscr{H}(G{C})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\pi}\mathrm{S}_{1}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})

(3.5.65)

from (
3.1.82
) by declaring
(
3.5.63
) to be an
isomorphism of Fréchet algebras, now with respect to the star products. In view
of the following remark this is however not satisfactory in general:

Remark 3.5.15
(Real forms).

Not every complex Lie group
G
is the universal complexification of a real Lie group
??. Real subgroups
H\subseteq G
with
H{C}\cong G
are called

real
forms
. The obstructions already arise on the Lie algebraic level. For an example of
minimal dimension – namely, three – we refer to

[
92
, Ex. 5.1.24]
. In view of
Corollary
3.3.20
and
Lemma
3.3.30
, one may not obtain the
corresponding connected and simply connected Lie group by means of universal
complexification.

Indeed, assume
H{C}\cong G
for some Lie group
H
. Then
H

is connected and its image
\eta(H)
complexifies to
H{C}
, as well, by
Corollary
3.3.20
. But
\operatorname{Lie}(\eta(H)){C}=\operatorname{Lie}(G)
by
Lemma
3.3.30
, which is a contradiction.

However, by Remark
3.1.11
we may follow the general philosophy
outlined
in Section
1.3
and use our explicit formulas
(
3.1.46
) for the standard ordered star product to treat
complex linear Lie groups
G
and their
complex
Lie algebras
??, see again Remark
3.1.2

directly.
42
42

42

The author would like to sincerely
thank Matthias Schötz for discussing until the construction was no longer both
unnecessarily complicated and eminently inelegant.
By virtue
of the Lie theoretic Cauchy estimates from Lemma
3.5.12
, this
turns out to be considerably simpler than in
[
90
, Sec. 6]
,
see also the general strategy sketched after
Theorem
3.2.14
. Note that by
Corollary
3.5.14
, working with all holomorphic functions in
(
3.1.82
) corresponds to
R=0
.
43
43

43

This loses
some generality for the mixed products,
but if
G
arises as a universal complexification, we ultimately recover the biggest
of the algebras from
[
90
]
for the full product
regardless.
We use Lemma
3.5.12
and
Lemma
3.2.15
to establish the continuity of mixed products
first.

Proposition 3.5.16
.

Let
G
be a complex connected linear Lie group.

i.)

The restricted standard ordered star product

\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\colon\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{1}^{\bullet}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})\bigr)\mathbin{\otimes}\bigl(\mathscr{H}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}1\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)

(3.5.66)

is well defined and continuous.

ii.)

The mapping

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\times\mathscr{H}(G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{S}_{1}^{\bullet}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})\ni(\hbar,\phi,P)\mapsto(\ch@airxmathbbm{1}\mathbin{\otimes}P)\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}(\phi\mathbin{\otimes}1)\in\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)

(3.5.67)

is Fréchet holomorphic.

Proof
:

Fix a complex basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})

of
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
and let
\mathrm{p}
be the corresponding
\ell^{1}
-norm.
Let moreover
K_{0}\subseteq\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
be a compact neighbourhood of zero,
g\in G
,
\phi\in\mathscr{H}(G)
,
c\geq 0
and indices

1\leq j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\leq n
. Motivated by the explicit formula
(
3.1.46
), we use
Lemma
3.5.12
and (
3.2.48
)
with
R=1
to estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{k}\frac{\lvert\hbar\rvert^{p}}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\Big\lVert\mathscr{L}(f_{j_{\sigma(p)}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}f_{j_{\sigma(1)}})\phi\Big\rVert_{g\exp(K_{0})}\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(p+1)}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{\sigma(k)}}\bigr)

(3.5.68)

\displaystyle\leq
