\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{k}\frac{\lvert\hbar\rvert^{p}}{p!(k-p)!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\lVert\phi\rVert_{g\exp(K_{0})K_{r}}\frac{p^{p}}{r^{p}}\biggl(\frac{(k-p)!}{k!}\biggr)^{R}c^{-p}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\bigr)

(3.5.69)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\phi\rVert_{g\exp(K_{0})K_{r}}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\bigr)\sum_{p=0}^{k}\biggl(\frac{(k-p)!}{k!}\biggr)^{R-1}\frac{\lvert\hbar\rvert^{p}}{p!}\frac{p^{p}}{r^{p}\cdot c^{p}}

(3.5.70)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\phi\rVert_{g\exp(K_{0})K_{r}}\cdot\mathrm{p}_{R,c}\bigl(\mathit{e}_{j_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\bigr)\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lvert\hbar\rvert^{p}}{p!}\frac{p^{p}}{r^{p}\cdot c^{p}}

(3.5.71)

for any
r>0
. Taking
r\coloneqq e\lvert\hbar\rvert/c
with Euler’s number
e
yields a
convergent series

M_{r}\coloneqq\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lvert\hbar\rvert^{p}}{p!}\frac{p^{p}}{r^{p}\cdot c^{p}}<\infty,

(3.5.72)

which may be chosen locally uniformly with respect to
\hbar\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
. This proves
both claims by a combination of the triangle inequality with
Corollary
2.3.9
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Mutatis mutandis, this should also work for holomorphic functions of fixed order
tensorized with
\mathrm{S}^{\bullet}_{R}(\hat{\mathfrak{g}})
with parameters chosen as in

[
90
, Lem. 6.2]
. However, one would not gain anything for
the full product, as
R=1
is optimal in
Theorem
3.1.7
. This is also why we
have defined (
3.1.82
) with
R=1
in the first
place.

Theorem 3.5.17
(Complex Standard Ordered Star Product).

Let
G
be a complex connected linear Lie group.

i.)

The standard ordered star product

\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\colon\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)\mathbin{\otimes}\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)

(3.5.73)

is well defined and continuous.

ii.)

The mapping

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\times\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)\otimes\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)\ni(\hbar,\Phi,\Psi)\mapsto\Phi\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}\Psi\in\mathscr{H}_{\mathrm{Pol}}(T^{*}G)

(3.5.74)

is Fréchet holomorphic.

Proof
:

The proof is almost identical to
[
90
, Proof of
Thm. 6.3]
, where our Proposition
3.5.16

replaces
[
90
, Lem. 6.2]
. The key idea is to use the
associativity of
\star_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{std}}}
to separate the full product into the pointwise
product, mixed products and the Lie algebra star product, which we have noted in
(
3.1.46
). Each may then be estimated with what we have
already shown, where we also use the elementary estimate

\lVert\phi\cdot\psi\rVert_{K}\leq\lVert\phi\rVert_{K}\cdot\lVert\psi\rVert_{K}

(3.5.75)

for compact subsets
K\subseteq G
and
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{H}(G)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

From here, one may in principle use Corollary
3.5.14
to
establish a star product on real forms, reversing our earlier logic. This of course
yields nothing new, but the circle closes nicely. It would be interesting to describe
the holomorphic analogues of the spaces
\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)
with
R>0
. By what we have
shown, they are tautologically given by

\mathscr{E}_{R}(G)=\mathscr{H}(G{C})\cap\mathscr{E}_{R}(G{C})

(3.5.76)

for Lie groups
G
such that the universal complexification
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
is a linear Lie group. As in Example
1.3.2
, the
condition on the Lie-Taylor majorant
may be rephrased as its holomorphic extensions being
of finite order
1/R
and
minimal type
. The precise correspondence can be found in

[
90
, Prop. 4.15]
. In the case of
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{n}
, this construction
yields the classical
Gelfand-Shilov spaces and it would be interesting to characterize the
spaces (
3.5.76
) without referring to their Lie-Taylor
majorants, say by means of an intrinsic growth condition on the universal
complexification
G{C}
. See also
Remark
3.2.9
for our prior discussion on the algebra of

R
-entire functions.

3.6
Continuous and Smooth Vectors

Slake was one of those places, Moist thought, that you put on the map
because it was embarrassing to to have a map with holes in it.

Raising Steam

– Terry Pratchett

This section serves as an elementary and self contained discussion of the notions of
continuous and smooth vectors for Lie algebra and Lie group representations on locally
convex spaces.
Everything we cover is standard, but the presentation is catered towards our ultimate
goal: Understanding analytic, entire and strongly entire vectors in
Section
3.7
. As these notions turn out to be stronger than
continuity and smoothness, we thus get to distinguish between smooth and holomorphic
phenomena more easily. Moreover, we put particular emphasis in developing
criteria to
decide the regularity of a particular vector, whose power we showcase in
Example
3.6.17
at the end of the section.

Throughout, we assume that
V
is a
complex
vector space by complexifying if
necessary, as this does not complicate our considerations whatsoever and thus
essentially comes along for free. Indeed, once again, some arguments even simplify.

In the sequel, we shall use the terminology and symbols from
Section
2.4
.
Moreover, we call a group representation
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
of a
Lie group
G
on a topological vector space
V

strongly continuous
if
it is continuous with respect to the
\sigma
-topology -- that is, the weak
topology
44
44

44

This is a rather unfortunate clash of terminology, which
historically arose from the presence of even weaker topologies in the setting of
representations on Hilbert spaces
V
. Instead of fighting these particular windmills,
we join them and rejoice in rotational bliss.
– on

\operatorname{GL}(V)\coloneqq\bigl\{L\in\operatorname{L}(V)\colon L\textrm{ is invertible}\bigr\}\subseteq\operatorname{L}_{\sigma}(V).

(3.6.1)

This means that the
orbit
maps

G\ni g\;\mapsto\;??(g)\coloneqq\pi(g)v\in V

(3.6.2)

are continuous for all
v\in V
. Notably, having a topological group would
suffice for the study of continuity. However, many of our arguments require
G

to be a topological manifold, and thus working with Lie groups from the start is
no loss of generality by the positive resolution of Hilbert’s fifth problem. For a
comprehensive discussion, we refer to the monograph
[
179
, §1]
.

The analogous notion for Lie algebra representations
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
would demand the continuity of the corresponding
orbit maps

\mathfrak{g}\ni\xi\;\mapsto\;\varrho(\xi)v\in V

(3.6.3)

for all
v\in V
. However, by finite dimensionality of
\mathfrak{g}
and linear
dependence of (
3.6.3
) on
\xi
, this is always fulfilled.
Hence, we refrain from using this terminology and speak of Lie algebra
representations instead. The non-trivial assumption is then that
\varrho(\xi)
is
continuous for every
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. Similarly, we implicitly assume the
continuity of the linear mappings
\pi(g)
for all
g\in G
by
assuming
\pi
maps into
\operatorname{GL}(V)
. Whenever both types of orbit maps are
involved, we sometimes refer to (
3.6.3
) as

infinitesimal
orbit maps to distinguish both concepts. If
V
is barrelled,
the Banach-Steinhaus Theorem yields the following by

[
188
, Beginning of Sec. 4.1]
.

Lemma 3.6.1
.

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow V
be a representation of a Lie group

G
on a barrelled topological space
V
. Then
\pi
is strongly continuous
if and only if the action mapping

\Phi\colon G\times V\longrightarrow V,\quad\Phi(g,v)\coloneqq\pi(g)v

(3.6.4)

is continuous with respect to the product topology.

Proof
:

By virtue of
\pi_{v}=\Phi(\,\cdot\,,v)
it is clear that continuity of
(
3.6.4
) implies the strong continuity of
\pi
. To see the
converse implication, let
(g_{n})_{n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}}\subseteq G
and

(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}
be convergent
with limits
g\in G
and
v\in V
. Fix a compact neighbourhood
K\subseteq G

of
g
. Then there exists an index
N\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
such that
g_{n}\in K
for all
n\geq N
. Consider the set

\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\bigl\{\pi(k)\colon k\in K\bigr\}\subseteq\operatorname{L}(V).

(3.6.5)

By continuity of (
3.6.2
), the set
\mathcal{T}
is weakly bounded, i.e. bounded in
\operatorname{L}_{\sigma}(V)
. By the Banach-Steinhaus Theorem,
\mathcal{T}
is thus equicontinuous. Let now
O\subseteq V
be some zero neighbourhood and use the continuity of the vector space addition to choose another zero neighbourhood
U\subseteq V
such that
U+U\subseteq V
. By equicontinuity of
\mathcal{T}
, there exists an index
\alpha_{0}\in J
such that

\Phi(k,v)-\Phi(k,v_{\alpha})=\pi(k)\bigl(v-v_{\alpha}\bigr)\in U\qquad\textrm{for all }k\in K\textrm{ and }\alpha\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}.

(3.6.6)

By continuity of
\pi_{v}
, we moreover find an index
M\geq N
such that

\Phi(g_{n},v)-\Phi(g,v)\in U\qquad\textrm{for all }n\geq M.

(3.6.7)

Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle\Phi(g,v)-\Phi(g_{n},v_{\alpha})=\Phi(g,v)-\Phi(g_{n},v)+\Phi(g_{n},v)-\Phi(g_{n},v_{\alpha})\in U+U\subseteq O

(3.6.8)

for all
n\geq M
and
\alpha\mathrel{\succcurlyeq}\alpha_{0}
. This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

We proceed by establishing some more or less obvious, but useful technical simplifications for checking strong continuity. Having a group representation first of all implies that it suffices to check continuity of (
3.6.2
) at the group unit.

Lemma 3.6.2
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a
representation on some locally convex space
V
and
v\in V
. Then the orbit map

\pi_{v}\colon G\longrightarrow V
is continuous if and only if it is continuous
at the group unit.

Proof
:

Let
v\in V
be such that
\pi_{v}
is continuous at the group unit
\mathrm{e}
. If
g_{n}\rightarrow g
in
G
, then
g^{-1}g_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}
and thus

\pi_{v}(g_{n})=\pi(g_{n})v=\pi(g)\pi(g^{-1}g_{n})v=\pi(g)\pi_{v}(g^{-1}g_{n})\overset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\pi(g)\pi_{v}(\mathrm{e})=\pi(g)v=\pi_{v}(g).

Consequently, the orbit map
\pi_{v}
is continuous at
g
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Moreover, strong continuity may be checked on generators.

Lemma 3.6.3
.

Let
G
and
N
be Lie groups and
\varphi\colon G\times N\longrightarrow N
be a smooth action of
G
by automorphisms of
N
. Assume moreover
that

\pi\colon N\rtimes_{\varphi}G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)

is a group representation on some locally convex space
V
such that the
restrictions
\pi\big|_{N}
and
\pi\big|_{G}
are strongly continuous. Then
\pi
is
strongly continuous.

Proof
:

Let
v\in V
. By Lemma
3.6.2
it suffices to check
continuity of
\pi_{v}
at the group unit. To this end, let
(h_{n},g_{n})_{n}\in N\rtimes_{\varphi}G
be a sequence converging to the group unit
(\mathrm{e}_{N},\mathrm{e}_{G})
. As

N\rtimes_{\varphi}G
carries the product topology, this implies that
h_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}_{N}
and
g_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}_{G}
. Now, writing
\pi^{N}\coloneqq\pi\big|_{N}
and
\pi^{G}\coloneqq\pi\big|_{G}
, we note

\pi_{v}(h_{n},g_{n})=\pi(h_{n},g_{n})v=\pi^{N}(h_{n})\pi^{G}(g_{n})v=\pi^{N}_{\pi^{G}(g_{n})v}(h_{n})=\pi^{N}_{\pi_{v}^{G}(g_{n})}(h_{n}),

(3.6.9)

which implies
\pi_{v}(h_{n},g_{n})\rightarrow v=\pi_{v}(\mathrm{e}_{N},\mathrm{e}_{G})
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Along the way, we have shown the following factorization of the orbit maps, which is occasionally useful.

Corollary 3.6.4
.

Let
G
and
N
be Lie groups and
\varphi\colon G\times N\longrightarrow N
be a smooth action of
G
by automorphisms of
N
. If

\pi\colon N\rtimes_{\varphi}G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)

is a group representation on some locally convex space
V
, then the orbit
maps fulfil

\pi_{v}(h,g)=\pi^{N}_{\pi_{v}^{G}(g)}(h)\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in V,\,g\in G,\,h\in N,

(3.6.10)

where
\pi^{N}\coloneqq\pi\big|_{N}
and
\pi^{G}\coloneqq\pi\big|_{G}
.

Beyond Lemma
3.6.1
, the Banach-Steinhaus
Theorem moreover yields closedness of the set of all continuous vectors within
barrelled locally convex spaces
V
.

Proposition 3.6.5
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a
representation on some barrelled locally convex space
V
. Assume
moreover that for every compact set
K\subseteq G
, the image
\pi(K)\subseteq\operatorname{GL}(V)
is weakly bounded.
45
45

45

The sets
\pi(K)v\subseteq V
are bounded for all
v\in V
.
Then

\mathscr{C}(\pi)\coloneqq\bigl\{v\in V\colon\pi_{v}\in\mathscr{C}(G,V)\bigr\}\subseteq V

(3.6.11)

is closed.

Proof
:

Let
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathscr{C}(\pi)
be a convergent net
with limit
v\in V
. Consider the corresponding net
(\pi_{v_{\alpha}})_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathscr{C}(G,V)
. We prove that this net converges to the orbit
map
\pi_{v}
in the space
\mathscr{C}(G,V)
– that is, uniformly on
compact subsets of
G
. To
this end, let
K\subseteq G
be compact
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. By assumption,
\pi(K)\subseteq\operatorname{GL}(V)
is then
weakly bounded and thus equicontinuous by the Banach-Steinhaus Theorem.
Thus, there is a
\mathrm{q}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
with

\mathrm{q}\bigl(\pi(g)w\bigr)\leq\mathrm{q}(w)\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in K\textrm{ and }w\in V.

Consequently,

\max_{g\in K}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\pi_{v_{\alpha}}(g)-\pi_{v}(g)\bigr)=\max_{g\in K}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\pi(g)(v-v_{\alpha})\bigr)\leq\mathrm{q}\bigl(v_{\alpha}-v\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all $\alpha$},

which implies the convergence of the net
(\pi_{v_{\alpha}})_{\alpha}
to
\pi_{v}

within
\mathscr{C}(G,V)
. In particular, the limit
\pi_{v}
is continuous and thus

v\in\mathscr{C}(\pi)
by (
3.6.11
).

\ch@irxboxempty

An important special instance is given by unitary representations
\pi
on some
Hilbert space
V
. Indeed, in this case each of the operators
\pi(g)
is an isometry,
which implies the continuity estimate
\lVert\pi(g)v\rVert=\lVert v\rVert
for all
v\in V
.
The closedness of
\mathscr{C}(\pi)
now means that it suffices to check strong
continuity
on a dense subspace.

Corollary 3.6.6
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a
representation on some barrelled locally convex space
V
. Assume
moreover that for every compact subset
K\subseteq G
, the image
\pi(K)\subseteq\operatorname{GL}(V)
is weakly bounded. If
\mathscr{C}(\pi)
is dense in
V
,
then
\pi
is strongly continuous.

Proof
:

Closed and dense subsets coincide with the whole space.

\ch@irxboxempty

Going one step further, we use the smooth structure of
G
to ask whether the
orbit mappings (
3.6.2
) and (
3.6.3
) are
smooth, i.e. have
Fréchet derivatives of all orders with values in the completion of
V
. In
Section
2.3
we have seen that sequential completeness
suffices
to take Fréchet derivatives. In the sequel we shall therefore always assume that
V

is sequentially complete and Hausdorff to avoid certain
technical complications.
46
46

46

For instance, without the Hausdorff property of
V

the mere definition of the first derivative as a function could already entail a

choice
. And even after making this choice, one immediately runs into questions
of the following unpleasant flavour: If
\phi\colon V\longrightarrow V
is
continuously differentiable, can the existence of the second derivative depend on
which limit points one uses to define the first?
We denote the subspace of all such

v\in V
by ??and ??, respectively, and
call them
smooth vectors
. As the domains of
\pi_{v}
and
(
3.6.3
) are finite dimensional, it suffices to consider left
invariant or partial derivatives to check their smoothness. For a comprehensive
discussion of smooth vectors, we refer to
[
188
, Sec. 4.4]
.

Remark 3.6.7
(Gårding space).

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
a representation on
a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally convex space
V
. Then space of
smooth
vectors
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\subseteq V
is typically not sequentially complete itself.
In fact, essentially the opposite is true, as
Gårding’s Theorem
[
68
]
asserts that
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
is always

sequentially dense
in
V
-- and thus in particular dense. He proved this
by considering the
Gårding
47
47

47

Lars Gårding (1919-2014) was a Swedish mathematician specialized
in partial differential equations. His doctoral advisor was Marcel Riesz and he
supervised Lars Hörmander.
space

??\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathrm{span}}\biggl\{\int_{G}\phi(g)\pi(g)v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g\;\Big|\;\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(G),v\in V\biggr\},

(3.6.12)

where
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(G)
denotes the space of smooth functions with compact support
and the integral is most easily handled as a weak integral in the sense of
Gelfand-Pettis
48
48

48

Billy James Pettis (1913-1979) was an American functional
analyst specialized on infinite dimensional integration and measure theory. He
generalized Gelfand’s work on weak integrals from the Lebesgue measure on an
interval to general measure spaces.

[
69
,
156
]
, see also

[
163
, Ch. 3.5]
. Alternatively, assuming
V
to be Fréchet, one may
treat it as a vector-valued Riemann integral by
[
163
, Ch. 3,
Exercise 23]
. The integrals in (
3.6.12
) are closely related
to the convolution algebra of
G
49
49

49

In fact, taking
\pi
as the
translation representation on the Banach space
\mathrm{L}^{1}(G)
, the integral reduces to
the usual convolution product. In general, sending
\phi
to

\int_{G}\phi(g)\pi(g)v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g
yields a homomorphism from the convolution algebra

\mathrm{L}^{1}(G)
to
\operatorname{L}(V)
.
and using standard convolution techniques it is not
hard to prove that
\mathfrak{G}
consists entirely of smooth vectors and is
sequentially dense in
V
. Astonishingly, Dixmier
50
50

50

Jacques Dixmier (born
1924) is a French operator algebraist, who introduced numerous fundamental objects
within the theory such as the Dixmier trace and the Dixmier mapping. He also
appears to be impervious to the passage of time.
and
Malliavin
51
51

51

Paul Malliavin (1925-2010) was a French harmonic and stochastic
analyst. He devised what the Malliavin calculus, which is central
to Monte Carlo simulations and mathematical finance.

[
54
]
proved that one even has
\mathfrak{G}=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
for strongly continuous representations on Fréchet spaces.

Example 3.6.8
(Translation Representation I: Continuous & smooth vectors).

We shall mostly discuss right translations, but of course, analogous statements hold
for left translations.
52
52

52

The opposite group is, after all, itself a Lie group.

Recall our notation
r_{g}
for the
left translation by
g
from (
3.0.1
). They induce a
representation

r^{*}\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}\bigl(??(G)\bigr),\quad r^{*}_{g}\phi(h)\coloneqq\phi(hg)

(3.6.13)

on the space
\mathrm{Map}(G)
of all complex valued functions defined on
G
,
endowed with the
locally convex and complete topology of pointwise convergence. To investigate the
strong continuity of
r^{*}
at some
\phi\in\mathrm{Map}(G)
, we have to consider the
continuity of the mappings

G\ni g\;\mapsto\;r^{*}\phi\in\mathrm{Map}(G),

(3.6.14)

which by first countability of
G
is equivalent to sequential continuity. If now
(g_{n})\subseteq G
is a sequence with limit
g\in G
, then we have to check that

\phi(hg_{n})=r_{g_{n}}^{*}\phi(h)\longrightarrow r_{g}^{*}\phi(h)=\phi(hg)\qquad\textrm{for all }h\in G.

(3.6.15)

That is, the function
\phi
needs to be continuous itself.
Thus
r^{*}
restricts to a strongly continuous representation on the space of
complex valued continuous functions
\mathscr{C}(G)
, endowed with the topology of
pointwise convergence.

However, this space is not even sequentially complete and its natural locally
convex topology is instead given by uniform convergence on compact subsets of
G
.
This raises the question whether this more natural topology yields the same
continuous vectors. Proceeding as before, we have to check when
\phi(hg_{n})\rightarrow\phi(hg)
, but now uniformly on compact subsets of
G
. This is
equivalent to the
uniform continuity
of
\phi
on every compact subset,
which is automatic if
\phi
is continuous at all. Thus
r^{*}
is also a strongly
continuous representation on the space
\mathscr{C}(G)
, endowed with the topology
of uniform convergence on compact sets.

Let now
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(r^{*})
. By smoothness, the difference quotient fulfils

\frac{r^{*}_{\exp(t\xi)}\phi-\phi}{t}\bigg|_{g}=\frac{\phi(g\exp(t\xi))-\phi(g)}{t}\overset{t\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{L}(\xi)\phi(g)

(3.6.16)

for all
g\in G
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. Again, we may either consider the limits pointwisely or locally uniformly on compact subsets by means of uniform continuity, this time also of the derivatives. Consequently, every smooth vector of
r^{*}
is continuously differentiable as a function. Applying this reasoning inductively yields that smooth vectors are necessarily smooth as functions and conversely it is clear that every smooth function constitutes a smooth vector. Thus,

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(r^{*})=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G).

(3.6.17)

Note that (
3.6.16
) recovers the Lie algebra action (
3.1.1
) on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
and its higher orders yield (
3.1.20
). In particular, we recover

\mathscr{L}(\xi)\circ r^{*}_{g}=r^{*}_{g}\circ\mathscr{L}\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g^{-1}}\xi\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in G\textrm{ and }\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}(\mathfrak{g}{C})

(3.6.18)

with the adjoint action
\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
from
(
3.0.3
). Indeed, on the one hand, we have

\mathscr{L}(\xi)r_{g}^{*}\phi\Big|_{h}=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\bigl(r^{*}_{\exp(t\xi)}(r_{g}^{*}\phi)\bigr)(h)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}r_{\exp(t\xi)g}^{*}\phi(h)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\phi\bigl(h\exp(t\xi)g\bigr)

(3.6.19)

for all
g,h\in G
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. Now,

\exp(t\xi)g=gg^{-1}\exp(t\xi)g=g\operatorname{\mathrm{Conj}}_{g}^{-1}(\exp(t\xi))=g\exp(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}t\xi)

(3.6.20)

and thus

\mathscr{L}(\xi)r_{g}^{*}\phi\Big|_{h}=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\phi\bigl(hg\exp(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}t\xi)\bigr)=\mathscr{L}(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1})\phi\Big|_{hg}=r_{g}^{*}\mathscr{L}(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1})\phi\Big|_{h}.

(3.6.21)

This proves (
3.6.18
) by
(
3.1.20
) and an induction.

The space of smooth vectors thus provides a natural domain for the

infinitesimal
counterpart of the translation action. However,
(
3.1.1
) is
not
continuous with respect to the
subspace topology induced by the inclusion
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)\subseteq\mathscr{C}(G)
.
For
G=\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
, this is the well-known issue of discontinuity of infinitessimal
generators of semigroups of operators, say acting on a Hilbert space. That being
said, it
is
continuous if we endow
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
with its natural Fréchet
topology.

Taking another look a Remark
3.6.7
and in particular
(
3.6.12
) for
v=\psi\in\mathscr{C}(G)
, we may evaluate
\pi(g)v=\ell_{g^{-1}}^{*}\phi
at some
h\in G
, which yields the left convolution product

\int_{G}\phi(g)\pi(g)v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g\Big|_{h}=\int_{G}\phi(g)\psi(g^{-1}h)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g=\phi\ast\psi\Big|_{h}.

The aforementioned compatibility of the Gårding space
\mathfrak{G}
with the
convolution algebra
(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(G),\ast)
reduces to the associativity of

\ast
in
this case. Moreover, Gårding’s Theorem
[
68
]
reflects the fact that

\phi\ast\psi
is smooth for all
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(G)
and
\psi\in\mathscr{C}(G)
. Coupled with the Dixmier-Malliavin Theorem

[
54
]
, this finally has the not at all obvious
consequence that every smooth function on
G
may be written as a
finite

linear combination of convolution products, even though the convolution algebra
lacks a unit if
G
is not discrete.

As we will see in Theorem
3.6.11
, the preceding observations
on the differentiated action are a general feature of strongly continuous group
representations. To make this precise, including its continuity, we give

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
its own locally convex topology, where we follow the discussion in

[
188
, Sec. 4.4.1]
. The idea is to identify
v
with the mapping
\pi_{v}

from (
3.6.2
), i.e. we realize
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
as a subspace of

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
. We have used this idea in the continuous setting to prove
Proposition
3.6.5
. Recall that the seminorms

??(\phi)\coloneqq\max_{g\in K}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\phi(g)\bigr)

(3.6.22)

for
\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
, compact sets
K\subseteq G
and

\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
yield a defining system of seminorms for
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
that
endow it with the structure of a Hausdorff locally convex space. It is
Fréchet if and only if
V
is, as
V\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
as the constant
functions.

Lemma 3.6.9
.

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a strongly continuous
representation of a Lie group
G
on a complete Hausdorff locally convex
space
V
. Then

??\coloneqq\bigl\{\pi_{v}\colon G\longrightarrow V\;\big|\;v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\bigr\}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)

(3.6.23)

is a closed subspace, which is linearly isomorphic to
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
by means of

??\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\pi}(G,V),\quad\Pi(v)\coloneqq\pi_{v}.

(3.6.24)

If
V
is merely sequentially complete, then the same is true for
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

Proof
:

Let
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
be such that
\pi_{v}=0
. By (
3.6.2
) for

g=\mathrm{e}
, this implies

0=\pi(\mathrm{e})v=v.

(3.6.25)

This establishes the linear isomorphy of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
and
(
3.6.23
) by means of (
3.6.24
). Let now

(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
be such that
\pi_{v_{\alpha}}\rightarrow\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
. Notice that

\pi_{v_{\alpha}}(g)=\pi(g)v_{\alpha}=\pi(g)\pi(\mathrm{e})v_{\alpha}=\pi(g)\pi_{v_{\alpha}}(\mathrm{e})\qquad\textrm{for all }\alpha\in J\textrm{ and }g\in G.

(3.6.26)

Taking limits on both sides yields

\phi(g)=\pi(g)\phi(\mathrm{e})=\pi_{\phi(\mathrm{e})}(g)\qquad\textrm{for all }g\in G.

(3.6.27)

By smoothness of
\phi
, this in particular implies
\phi(\mathrm{e})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.
Thus we have shown that

\phi=\pi_{\phi(\mathrm{e})}\qquad\textrm{with}\quad\phi(\mathrm{e})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi),

(3.6.28)

which completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the sequel, we identify vectors
v
with their orbit maps
\pi_{v}
and endow

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
with the subspace topology inherited from the inclusion

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\pi}(G,V)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
. That is to say, we demand that
(
3.6.24
) is an isomorphism of locally convex spaces. Using

\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
and
K\coloneqq\{\mathrm{e}\}
in
(
3.6.22
) yields that this topology is finer than the one
inherited from the inclusion
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\subseteq V
.
Lemma
3.6.9
moreover states that we obtain a Fréchet space
in this manner whenever
V
is Fréchet itself.

Proposition 3.6.10
(Invariance of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
).

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a strongly continuous
representation of a Lie group
G
on a complete Hausdorff locally convex
space
V
.

i.)

We have
\pi(g)v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
for all
g\in G
and
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

ii.)

The representation
\pi
restricts to a strongly continuous representation on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
, endowed with the subspace topology inherited from
V
.

iii.)

The representation
\pi
restricts to a strongly continuous
representation on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
, endowed with the subspace topology inherited
from
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
.

iv.)

The smooth vectors of both restrictions coincide with
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

Proof
:

Let
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
,
g\in G
and write
w\coloneqq\pi(g)v
. As
\pi
is a representation, we have

\pi_{w}(h)=\pi(h)w=\pi(h)\pi(g)v=\pi(hg)v=\pi\bigl(r_{g}(h)\bigr)v=\bigl(\pi_{v}\circ r_{g}\bigr)(h)

(3.6.29)

for all
h\in G
. Thus
\pi_{w}
is smooth as composition of the smooth maps

\pi_{v}
and
r_{g}
, which means
w\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
. The strong continuity of
\pi\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)}
with respect to the inclusion
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\subseteq V
is
then clear. Next, we study the topology inherited from
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(V,G)
. Indeed,
combining (
3.6.29
) and
(
3.6.18
) we compute

\displaystyle\mathrm{q}_{\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k},K,\mathrm{p}}\bigl(\pi(g)v\bigr)

\displaystyle=\max_{h\in K}\mathrm{p}\Bigl(\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})\pi_{w}\bigr)(h)\Bigr)

(3.6.30)

\displaystyle=\max_{h\in K}\mathrm{p}\Bigl(\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})r_{g}^{*}\pi_{v}\bigr)(h)\Bigr)

(3.6.31)

\displaystyle=\max_{h\in K}\mathrm{p}\Bigl(\bigl(r_{g}^{*}\mathscr{L}(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi_{1})\pi_{v}\bigr)(h)\Bigr)

(3.6.32)

\displaystyle=\max_{h^{\prime}\in Kg}\mathrm{p}\Bigl(\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi_{1})\pi_{v}\bigr)(h^{\prime})\Bigr)

(3.6.33)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{q}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi_{k},Kg,\mathrm{p}}(v)

(3.6.34)

for all
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}
, compact
K\subseteq G
and

\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Thus
\pi(g)
is continuous also in the topology
inherited from
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
. As strong continuity and smoothness are both based
on the properties of the same map
\pi_{v}
, it suffices to prove that every
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
induces a smooth orbit map
\pi_{v}\colon G\longrightarrow V
, i.e.

\pi_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi))
. Equivalently, by means of currying, it
suffices to assert

\Pi(\pi_{v})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\bigl(G,\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\pi}(G,V)\bigr)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\bigl(G,\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)\bigr)\cong\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G\times G,V).

(3.6.35)

Now, keeping the ordering of arguments,
\Pi(\pi_{v})(g,h)=\pi(h)\pi(g)v
holds for all
g,h\in G
and thus we indeed have
\Pi(\pi_{v})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G\times G,V)
. Finally,
\Pi(\pi_{v})
comes from
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
, which means that
v
is a smooth vector also for the restriction of
\pi
to
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

With these preliminaries, we may now realize our hopes and dreams of differentiating

\pi
to a strongly continuous Lie algebra representation.

Theorem 3.6.11
(Differentiation on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
).

Let
G
be a Lie group and

\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)

(3.6.36)

a strongly continuous representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff
locally convex space
V
. Then

T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\bigr),\quad T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi\Big|_{v}\coloneqq\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\pi\bigl(\exp(t\xi)\bigr)v\Big|_{t=0}=\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}\bigr)(\mathrm{e})

(3.6.37)

is a Lie algebra representation with
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi)=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
. In particular, the tangent map
T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
extends to an action of
the universal enveloping algebra

\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

Proof
:

The crucial point is that the limit in (
3.6.37
) exists for all
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
and yields another smooth vector, as
t\mapsto\pi(\exp(t\xi))v
is a coordinate expression of (
3.6.2
). To see the latter, note that

\pi_{T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi v}(g)=\pi(g)\bigl((T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi)(v)\bigr)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\pi\bigl(g\exp(t\xi)\bigr)v\Big|_{t=0}=\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}(g)

(3.6.38)

for all
g\in G
by continuity of
\pi(g)
and the fact that
\pi
is a group representation. Having established this, it follows that
T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
is a Lie algebra morphism at once, as
\mathscr{L}
is. By the universal property of the universal enveloping algebra
\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})
, the Lie algebra representation
T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
has a unique extension to
\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g})
as an algebra morphism, which we denote by the same symbol. Iterating (
3.6.37
), the extension is explicitly given by

T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\bigl(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k}\bigr)v=\mathscr{L}(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k})\pi_{v}(\mathrm{e})

(
\ast
)

for
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}
, which is indeed well defined by
smoothness of
v
. As
T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
is a homomorphism, this moreover implies

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi)=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
. Let now
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. Taking another
look at (
3.6.22
) and (
3.6.37
), we get the
continuity estimate

\mathrm{q}_{\chi,K,\mathrm{p}}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi v\bigr)=\max_{g\in K}\mathrm{p}\Bigl(\mathscr{L}(\chi)\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}(g)\Bigr)=\mathrm{q}_{\xi\mathbin{\otimes}\chi,K,\mathrm{p}}(v)

(3.6.39)

for all
\chi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
, compact sets
K\subseteq G
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Thus the linear map
T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi
is a
continuous self-map of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

We proceed with several corollaries of our considerations.

Corollary 3.6.12
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
a strongly continuous representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally convex space
V
. Then

\pi(g)\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi\Big|_{v}=\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}(g)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Pi\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi=\mathscr{L}(\xi)\circ\Pi

(3.6.40)

for all
g\in G
,
\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
and
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

Proof
:

The first equality is (
3.6.38
). For the second, note that

\Pi\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi\in\operatorname{L}\bigl(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi),\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)\bigr)

(3.6.41)

and so we may evaluate both sides at first at
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
and
subsequently at
g\in G
. Starting with the left-hand side in
(
3.6.40
) and using the first identity, this yields

\Pi\bigl(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi v\bigr)\Big|_{g}=\pi(g)T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi v=\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}(g)=\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\circ\Pi\bigr)(v)\Big|_{g}.

\ch@irxboxempty

The interpretation of (
3.6.40
) is that the isomorphism
\Pi
acts as an
intertwiner
between the representations (
3.6.37
) and (
3.1.20
) of
\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
. Next, we generalize (
3.6.18
).

Corollary 3.6.13
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
a strongly continuous representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally convex space
V
. Then

T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi\circ\pi(g)=\pi(g)\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi\bigr)

(3.6.42)

as operators on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
for all
g\in G
and
\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
.

Proof
:

The reasoning is essentially identical to the proof of
(
3.6.18
). Let
g\in G
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}

and
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
. Using (
3.6.37
), (
3.6.20
)
and (
3.6.40
) we compute

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi\circ\pi(g)\Big|_{v}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{\pi(g)v}(\mathrm{e})

(3.6.43)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\pi\bigl(\exp(t\xi)\bigr)\pi(g)v\bigg|_{t=0}

(3.6.44)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\pi\bigl(\exp(t\xi)g\bigr)v\bigg|_{t=0}

(3.6.45)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\pi\bigl(g\exp(t\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi)\bigr)v\bigg|_{t=0}

(3.6.46)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}^{-1}\xi)\pi_{v}\bigr)(g)

(3.6.47)

\displaystyle=\pi(g)\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}^{-1}_{g}\xi\bigr)\Big|_{v}.

(3.6.48)

This proves (
3.6.42
) by inductive application.

\ch@irxboxempty

Corollary 3.6.14
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
a representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally convex space
V
. The seminorms

\mathrm{r}_{\xi,K,\mathrm{p}}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\longrightarrow[0,\infty),\quad\mathrm{r}_{\xi,K,\mathrm{p}}(v)\coloneqq\max_{g\in K}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\pi(g)T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi v\bigr)

(3.6.49)

for
\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
, compact sets
K\subseteq G
and

\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
constitute a defining system of seminorms for the
topology
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
inherits from
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G,V)
.

Proof
:

Insert (
3.6.40
) into (
3.6.22
).

\ch@irxboxempty

Next, we prove variants of Lemma
3.6.2
and
Lemma
3.6.3
for smoothness, which provide
effective methods for determining the space
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
for a given strongly
continuous Lie group representation
\pi
.

Lemma 3.6.15
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a
representation on some sequentially complete locally convex space
V
and
fix
v\in V
. Then
\pi_{v}\colon G\longrightarrow V
is smooth if and only if it
is smooth at the group unit. In this case,

\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}(g)=\pi(g)\circ\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}

(3.6.50)

holds for all
g\in G
and
\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
.

Proof
:

We check the claim for a single derivative first.
To this end, let
g\in G
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
.
Consider the difference quotient

\frac{\pi_{v}(g\exp(t\xi))-\pi_{v}(g)}{t}=\pi(g)\biggl(\frac{\pi_{v}(\exp(t\xi))-\pi_{v}(\mathrm{e})}{t}\biggr)=\pi(g)\biggl(\frac{\pi_{v}(\exp(t\xi))-v}{t}\biggr),

(3.6.51)

which is convergent in
V
by differentiablity of
\pi_{v}
at the group unit and
continuity of

\pi(g)\colon V\longrightarrow V.

(3.6.52)

Consequently, differentiability of
\pi_{v}
at
\mathrm{e}
implies
\pi_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(G,V)
. Combining (
3.6.37
) with (
3.6.40
)
– both of which indeed require only differentiability and not smoothness – then
leads to

\pi(g)\circ\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}=\pi(g)\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\xi\Big|_{v}=\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}(g),

(3.6.53)

which entails (
3.6.50
). For higher derivatives, we may proceed inductively and replace
\pi_{v}
with
\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v}
throughout the discussion, which completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

As typically not all continuous vectors are smooth by
Example
3.6.8
, we may not naively copy
and paste Lemma
3.6.3
into the
smooth category. The idea is that, if we have a sufficiently invariant candidate

W\subseteq V
for a space consisting of smooth vectors, it suffices to check
smoothness for each factor of a semidirect product.

Lemma 3.6.16
.

Let
G
and
N
be Lie groups and
\varphi\colon G\times N\longrightarrow N
be a smooth action of
G
by automorphisms of
N
.
Moreover, assume that

\pi\colon N\rtimes_{\varphi}G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)

is a strongly continuous group representation on some sequentially complete
locally convex space
V
with a
\pi\big|_{G}
-invariant subspace
W\subseteq V
. If

W\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\bigl(\pi\big|_{N}\bigr)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\bigl(\pi\big|_{G}\bigr),

(3.6.54)

then
W\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

Proof
:

By (
3.6.10
), we have

\pi_{v}(h,g)=\pi^{N}_{\pi_{v}^{G}(g)}(h)\qquad\textrm{for all }v\in V,g\in G,h\in N,

(3.6.55)

where
\pi^{N}\coloneqq\pi\big|_{N}
and
\pi^{G}\coloneqq\pi\big|_{G}
. If
v\in W
, then the
\pi^{G}
-invariance of
W
yields

\pi_{v}(g)=\pi(g)v\in W\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi^{N})

(3.6.56)

and thus
\pi_{v}
is smooth at
(h,g)
by smoothness of
\pi^{G}_{v}
at
g

and
\pi^{N}_{\pi_{v}^{G}(g)}
at
h
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Note that
W=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
is always
\pi
-invariant by
Proposition
3.6.10
,

i.)
. Thus a sufficiently educated guess
for
W
does always recover all smooth vectors in
Lemma
3.6.16
. In concrete examples, one typically
has good candidates for
W
and may then use the finiteness of the seminorms from
Corollary
3.6.14
to prove the converse inclusion. We
conclude our discussion of smooth vectors with a somewhat lengthy example, which is
well-known and discussed using machinery from Fourier analysis in

[
74
, Sec. 6]
. We shall instead treat it by showcasing the abstract
machinery we have developed throughout this section combined with some
rather elementary means.

Example 3.6.17
(Schrödinger representation and Schwartz functions).

Let
G\coloneqq??
be the Heisenberg group in one spacial
dimension, see e.g.
[
92
, Ex. 9.5.20]
for a concrete
representation. As a set,
G
is given by
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{3}
with basis
(q,p,1)
. We
shall use the multiplication

(q,p,\alpha)\cdot(q^{\prime},p^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\coloneqq(q+q^{\prime},p+p^{\prime},qp^{\prime}+\alpha+\alpha^{\prime})

(3.6.57)

with unit
(0,0,0)
and inverses

(q,p,\alpha)^{-1}=(-q,-p,-\alpha+qp^{\prime}).

(3.6.58)

Alternatively, one may view
H_{1}
as the semidirect product
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\ltimes_{\gamma}\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{2}
twisted by the group morphism

\gamma\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{Aut}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{2}),\quad\gamma(q)(p,\alpha)\coloneqq(p,qp+\alpha).

(3.6.59)

Indeed,

\displaystyle\gamma(q)\circ\gamma(q^{\prime})\Big|_{(p,\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\gamma(q)(p,pq^{\prime}+\alpha)=(p,pq+pq^{\prime}+\alpha)=\gamma(q+q^{\prime})\Big|_{(p,\alpha)},

(3.6.60)

\displaystyle\gamma(q)(p+p^{\prime},\alpha+\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(p+p^{\prime},q(p+p^{\prime})+\alpha+\alpha^{\prime})=\gamma(q)(p,\alpha)+\gamma(q)(p^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

(3.6.61)

and

\bigl(q,(p,\alpha)\bigr)\cdot\bigl(q^{\prime},(p^{\prime},\alpha)\bigr)=\bigl(q+q^{\prime},(\gamma(q)(p^{\prime},\alpha))+\alpha^{\prime}\bigr)=\bigl(q+q^{\prime},qp^{\prime}+\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}\bigr)

(3.6.62)

hold for all
q,q^{\prime},p,p^{\prime},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. This observation is convenient, as
it allows us to utilize both Lemma
3.6.3
and
Lemma
3.6.16
. We suppress the superfluous
brackets in the sequel. Note also that
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{2}
is itself a semidirect product over
the identity – a direct product – and so we may decompose even
further.

The Lie algebra of
H_{1}
is again
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}^{3}
, say with basis
(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{1})
with only non-trivial Lie bracket given by

[\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}]=\mathfrak{1}.

(3.6.63)

In particular, the Heisenberg group is nilpotent of step
2
. We consider its
Schrödinger representations

\pi\colon H_{1}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}\bigl(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\bigr),\quad\pi(q,p,\alpha)\psi\Big|_{x}\coloneqq e^{\mathrm{i}(\hbar\alpha+px)}\cdot\psi(x+q)

(3.6.64)

on the Hilbert space
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
of equivance classes of square integrable
functions on the real line. Note that the representations are indexed by a
parameter
\hbar\neq 0
. Clearly, each of the linear mappings
\pi(q,p,\alpha)

is a unitary transformation of
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. Notably, restricting
\pi
to the
subgroup
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}
yields the translation representation
r^{*}
of the
group
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
from Example
3.6.8
, except that it
now acts on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
instead of
\mathscr{C}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
.

Our first goal is to prove that
\pi
is strongly continuous. To this end, we consider the subrepresentations
\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}},\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}}}
and
\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}
of
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
defined by

\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}(q)\coloneqq\pi(q,0,0),\quad\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}}}(p)\coloneqq\pi(0,p,0)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha)\coloneqq\pi(0,0,\alpha),

(3.6.65)

which directly correspond to the decomposition of
H_{1}
into a semidirect product
of three copies of
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. By Lemma
3.6.3
it
suffices to check strong continuity of the three subrepresentations. As
\pi
is
unitary, the sets
\pi(K)\subseteq\operatorname{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))
are even bounded in
operator norm. Namely, contained in the closed unit ball. By
Corollary
3.6.6
it suffices to check that
\pi_{\phi}

is continuous for
\phi
from the dense subspace
??(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})

of test
functions
on
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
.
53
53

53

The space
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
is defined as the space of smooth
functions
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
such that the
support

\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)\coloneqq\bigl\{x\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\colon f(x)\neq 0\bigr\}^{\mathrm{cl}}

(3.6.66)

is compact. Here, the support
\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)
is the smallest closed subset of

\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
such that
\phi
vanishes identically on its complement.

We fix
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and study its restricted orbit maps

\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}},\phi}
,
\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}},\phi}
and
\pi_{\mathscr{Z},\phi}
.

We begin with
\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}
. By compactness of
\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)
, the restriction
\phi\big|_{K}
is uniformly continuous. As
\phi
vanishes identically outside of
K
, this implies uniform continuity of
\phi
on all of
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. Thus, given
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0
such that
\lvert\phi(x)-\phi(y)\rvert\leq\varepsilon
for all
x,y\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
with
\lvert x-y\rvert\leq\delta
. If now
(q_{n})_{n}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
is a zero sequence, then
\lvert q_{n}\rvert\leq\delta
for almost all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. This yields the estimate

\big\lVert\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}(q_{n})\phi-\phi\big\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int{R}\big\lvert\phi(x+q_{n})-\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\varepsilon^{2}\cdot??\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)+\mathrm{B}_{\delta}(0)\bigr)

(3.6.67)

for almost all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
, where
\operatorname{meas}(M)
denotes the Lebesgue
measure of a measurable subset
M\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. Consequently,

\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}(q_{n})\phi\rightarrow\phi
in

\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. That is, the orbit map
\pi_{\phi}
is continuous at the group unit. By
Lemma
3.6.2
, this implies continuity of
\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}},\phi}
on all of
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. Note that these arguments possess straightforward
generalizions to the square integrable functions
\mathrm{L}^{2}(G)
on any Lie group

G
and with respect to the Haar measure without complications.

Next, we consider
\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}}}
. Let
(p_{n})_{n}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
be a zero sequence and note

\big\lVert\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}}}(p_{n})\phi-\phi\big\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int_{\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\cdot\big\lvert 1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{n}x}\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\cdot\big\lvert 1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{n}x}\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.68)

for
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. It thus remains to estimate

\big\lvert 1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{n}x}\big\rvert^{2}=\big\lvert\mathrm{e}^{0}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{n}x}\big\rvert^{2}

(3.6.69)

for almost all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
and
x\in\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)
. Consider the family
\{f_{x}\}_{x\in\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)}
with

f_{x}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad f_{x}(p)\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}px}.

(3.6.70)

Each of the
f_{x}
is smooth with derivative
f^{\prime}_{x}(p)=\mathrm{i}xf_{x}(p)
and thus we may estimate

\big\lvert 1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{n}x}\big\rvert=\big\lvert f_{x}(0)-f_{x}(p_{n})\big\rvert\leq\int_{0}^{p_{n}}\big\lvert f_{x}^{\prime}(t)\big\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{p_{n}}\lvert x\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\lvert p_{n}x\rvert

(3.6.71)

for almost all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. Returning to (
3.6.68
), we arrive at

\big\lVert\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}}}(p_{n})\phi-\phi\big\rVert^{2}_{2}\leq\lvert p_{n}\rvert^{2}\int_{\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)}\lvert x\rvert^{2}\cdot\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\lvert p_{n}\rvert^{2}\cdot\big\lVert x^{2}\phi\big\rVert_{2}^{2}

(3.6.72)

for almost all
n\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}
. As
x^{2}\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
again, this completes the proof of the strong continuity of
\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}
.

The central subrepresentation
\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}
is much simpler. Setting
x=1
and

p_{n}=\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
in (
3.6.71
) leads to

\big\lvert 1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\hbar\alpha}\big\rvert\leq\lvert\hbar\alpha\rvert.

(3.6.73)

Thus, if
(\alpha_{n})_{n}\subseteq\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
is a zero sequence, then

\big\lVert\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha_{n})\phi-\phi\big\rVert^{2}=\big\lvert 1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\hbar\alpha_{n}}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lVert\phi\big\rVert_{2}\leq\lvert\hbar\rvert\cdot\lvert\alpha_{n}\rvert\cdot\big\lVert\phi\big\rVert_{2}.

(3.6.74)

Consequently,
\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}
is also strongly continuous. Putting everything
together, Lemma
3.6.3
yields the strong
continuity of the Schrödinger representation (
3.6.64
).

Having established strong continuity, our next goal is to prove

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)=\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})

(3.6.75)

as locally convex spaces, where we endow
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
with the topology
inherited from the space
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R},\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))
and

??(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\coloneqq\Bigl\{\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\;\big|\;\forall_{n,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}\colon\mathrm{s}_{n,m}(\phi)\coloneqq\sup_{x\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\big\lvert x^{m}f^{(n)}(x)\big\rvert<\infty\Bigr\}

(3.6.76)

is the usual Schwartz
54
54

54

Laurent Schwartz (1915-2002) was a French
functional
analyst. His theory of distributions – in the sense of generalized functions –
allows for a mathematically coherent and elegant treatment of theoretical
electrodynamics and other classical field theories. They moreover serve as an
indispensable tool in the explicit solution theory of partial differential
equations.
space on
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
with defining
system of seminorms

\{\mathrm{s}_{n,m}\}_{n,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}
. Strictly speaking, as
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
, the equality (
3.6.75
) does
not reference
\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
as defined in (
3.6.76
), but rather the
corresponding
equivalence classes
within
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
, i.e. up to equality
almost everywhere. In particular, one should think of the suprema in

\mathrm{s}_{n,m}
as essential suprema instead and demand smoothness only
almost everywhere. We shall suppress this subtlety in the sequel.

As before, we split
\pi
into the subrepresentations
(
3.6.65
) and apply
Lemma
3.6.16
with the candidate
W\coloneqq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
first. Taking another look at
(
3.6.64
), it is clear that
\pi
and thus in
particular all three subrepresentations leave
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
invariant. Note that
we may suppress the Lie exponential when considering each subrepresentation, as it
reduces to the identity mapping. Fix again
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
.

Motivated by (
3.6.16
) we prove that the difference quotient

D_{q}\phi\coloneqq\frac{\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}(q)\phi-\phi}{q}

(3.6.77)

converges to the derivative
\phi^{\prime}
for
q\rightarrow 0
within
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. To see this, let
\varepsilon>0
and use the uniform continuity of
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
to choose
\delta>0
such that

\phi^{\prime}(t)-\phi^{\prime}(x)\leq\big\lvert\phi^{\prime}(t)-\phi^{\prime}(x)\big\rvert\leq\varepsilon\qquad\textrm{whenever}\quad\lvert t-x\rvert\leq\delta.

(3.6.78)

Writing
K\coloneqq\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)+B_{\delta}(0)^{\mathrm{cl}}
and

D_{q}(x)-\phi^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{q}\int_{x}^{x+q}\phi^{\prime}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\phi^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{q}\int_{x}^{x+q}\phi^{\prime}(t)-\phi^{\prime}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

for
x,q\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
yields the estimate

\big\lVert D_{q}\phi-\phi^{\prime}\big\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\int_{K}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\biggl(\int_{x}^{x+q}\big\lvert\phi^{\prime}(t)-\phi^{\prime}(x)\big\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\biggr)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\operatorname{meas}(K)\cdot\varepsilon^{2}

(3.6.79)

for
\lvert q\rvert\leq\delta
. Hence,
D_{q}\phi\rightarrow\phi^{\prime}
for
q\rightarrow 0
within
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. That is,
\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}},\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R},\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}))
by Lemma
3.6.15
and we get the generator

\mathscr{L}(1)\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}},\phi}(\mathrm{e})=T_{0}\pi_{\operatorname{pos}}1\phi=\phi^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}).

(3.6.80)

Consequently, we get
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}})
by induction and we have
proved the inclusion

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}).

(3.6.81)

Next, we check that the difference quotient

M_{p}(\phi)\coloneqq\frac{\pi_{\operatorname{mom}}(p)\phi-\phi}{p}\bigg|_{x}=\frac{e^{\mathrm{i}px}\phi(x)-\phi(x)}{p}=\frac{e^{\mathrm{i}px}-1}{p}\cdot\phi(x)

(3.6.82)

converges to
x\mapsto\mathrm{i}x\phi(x)
within
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. To see this, we estimate

\displaystyle\big\lVert M_{p}(\phi)-\mathrm{i}x\phi\big\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle=\int{R}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\cdot\bigg\lvert\frac{e^{\mathrm{i}px}-1}{p}-\mathrm{i}x\bigg\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.83)

\displaystyle\leq\int{R}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\cdot\bigg\lvert\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{i}px)^{k}}{k!}\bigg\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.84)

\displaystyle\leq\int{R}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\cdot\bigg\lvert\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{i}x)^{2k}}{k!}\bigg\rvert\cdot\bigg\lvert\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}p^{2k}\bigg\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.85)

\displaystyle\leq\int{R}\big\lvert\phi(x)\big\rvert^{2}\cdot\exp(2\lvert x\rvert)\cdot\frac{\lvert p\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert p\rvert^{2}}

(3.6.86)

\displaystyle=\big\lVert\phi\cdot\exp\big\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\frac{\lvert p\rvert^{2}}{1-\lvert p\rvert^{2}}

(3.6.87)

\displaystyle\overset{p\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0

(3.6.88)

for
0<\lvert p\rvert<1
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Our assumption
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
ensures that
\phi\cdot\exp
is still square integrable. By
Lemma
3.6.15
, we have shown

\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}},\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}\bigl(\ch@airxmathbbm{R},\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\bigr)

(3.6.89)

and it follows that the corresponding infinitesimal generator is given by

\mathscr{L}(1)\pi_{{\operatorname{mom}},\phi}(\mathrm{e})=T_{0}\pi_{\operatorname{mom}}1\phi=\mathrm{i}x\phi.

(3.6.90)

As
\mathrm{i}x\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
again, induction yields
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi_{\operatorname{mom}})
.

Repeating this type of consideration for the central subrepresentation
moreover leads to

\bigg\lVert\frac{\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha)\phi-\phi}{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\hbar\phi\bigg\rVert^{2}_{2}=\lVert\phi\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\bigg\lvert\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{i}\hbar\alpha)^{k}}{k!}\bigg\rvert\overset{\alpha\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0

(3.6.91)

by virtue of the continuity of the entire function defined by the power series. In
particular, this works for any
\phi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and thus
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi_{\mathscr{Z}})
with generator given by
\phi\mapsto\mathrm{i}\hbar\phi
, i.e. a
multiple of the identity. In passing, we note that the infinitesimal generators
implement the canonical commutation relation

[T_{0}\pi_{\operatorname{pos}}1,T_{0}\pi_{\operatorname{mom}}1]=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}\circ M_{\mathrm{i}x}-M_{\mathrm{i}x}\circ\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}=\mathrm{i}\cdot\operatorname{\mathrm{id}}=\frac{1}{\hbar}T_{0}\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}1

(3.6.92)

in accordance with (
3.6.63
).

Putting everything together, Lemma
3.6.16
proves
the inclusion

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi).

(3.6.93)

We proceed by taking a closer look at the
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
-seminorms from
(
3.6.22
). Indeed, as
\pi
is a unitary representation
and by virtue of (
3.6.80
),
(
3.6.90
) and
Corollary
3.6.14
, they are explicitly given by

\biggl(\int{R}\big\lvert\bigl(\hbar^{m}x^{\ell}\phi^{(k)}(x)\bigr)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\biggr)^{1/2}=\biggl(\int{R}\big\lvert\bigl(\hbar^{m}x^{\ell}\phi^{(k)}(x)\bigr)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\biggr)^{1/2}

(3.6.94)

for
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
and
k,\ell,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. We may reduce this to the defining system

\mathrm{r}_{n,m}(\phi)\coloneqq\biggl(\int{R}\big\lvert x^{m}\phi^{(n)}(x)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\biggr)^{1/2}

(3.6.95)

for
n,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
, which already resembles the seminorms
\mathrm{s}_{m,n}
from (
3.6.76
). Indeed, we check that both systems generate the same topology on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. To this end, we estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{r}_{n,m}(\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int{R}\big\lvert x^{m}\phi^{(n)}(x)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.96)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}}\big\lvert(1+t^{2})t^{m}\phi^{(n)}(t)\big\rvert^{2}\int{R}\frac{1}{(1+x^{2})^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.97)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\bigl(\mathrm{s}_{n,m}(\phi)+\mathrm{s}_{n,m+2}(\phi)\bigr)^{2}.

(3.6.98)

For the converse estimate, we fix
n,m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
as well as
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and write

x^{m}\cdot\phi^{(n)}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}(t^{m}\phi^{(n)}(t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=m\int_{-\infty}^{x}t^{m-1}\phi^{(n)}(t)+t^{m}\phi^{(n+1)}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(3.6.99)

for
x\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{n,m}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq m\int{R}\big\lvert{x}^{m-1}\phi^{(n)}(x)\big\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int{R}\big\lvert{x}^{m}\phi^{(n+1)}(x)\big\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.100)

\displaystyle=m\int{R}\big\lvert(1+x)^{2}{x}^{m-1}\phi^{(n)}(x)\big\rvert\cdot\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int{R}\big\lvert(1+x^{2}){x}^{m}\phi^{(n+1)}(x)\big\rvert\cdot\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(3.6.101)

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{m\pi}{2}\int{R}\big\lvert(1+x^{2})x^{m-1}\phi^{(n)}(x)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\biggr)^{1/2}+\biggl(\frac{\pi}{2}\int{R}\big\lvert(1+x^{2})x^{m}\phi^{(n+1)}(x)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\biggr)^{1/2}

(3.6.102)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m\pi}{2}\cdot\bigl(\mathrm{r}_{m-1,n}(\phi)+\mathrm{r}_{m+1,n}(\phi)\bigr)+\frac{\pi}{2}\cdot\bigl(\mathrm{r}_{m,n+1}(\phi)+\mathrm{r}_{m+2,n+1}(\phi)\bigr),

(3.6.103)

where we have used the Cauchy-Schwarz and the Minkowski inequalities.
Having established estimates in both directions, we have thus shown that the

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
-topology coincides with the
\mathscr{S}
-topology on

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. But
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
is dense in
\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
with respect
to the
\mathscr{S}
-topology and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
is complete by
Lemma
3.6.9
, so the desired inclusion

\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)

(3.6.104)

follows. Conversely, every
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
necessarily
fulfils
\mathrm{r}_{n,m}(\phi)<\infty
and thus by our estimate also

\mathrm{s}_{n,m}(\phi)<\infty
.
55
55

55

And is in particular, necessarily,
almost everywhere smooth as a
function.
This yields the converse inclusion
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\subseteq\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and finally completes the proof of
(
3.6.75
).

This yields a conceptually quite pleasing interpretation of the Schwartz space as
the natural habitat of naive quantum mechanics. Indeed, by the Stone-von Neumann
Theorem
[
83
, Thm. 14.8]
, the Schrödinger representations are, up to
unitary equivalence, the only irreducible strongly continuous unitary
representations of
H_{1}
, for which the center

\mathscr{Z}(H_{1})=\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\ch@airxmathbbm{R}

(3.6.105)

acts in a non-trivial manner. Moreover, representations corresponding to
different
\hbar
are unitarily inequivalent.

We conclude the discussion of the Schrödinger representation with some remarks on
the corresponding Gårding spaces. As in terms of formulas,
\pi_{\operatorname{pos}}
is
essentially the translation representation from
Example
3.6.8
, the integrals (
3.6.12
)
for
\pi_{\operatorname{pos}}
reproduce convolution with
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\phi)
. The
inclusion
\mathfrak{G}(\pi_{\operatorname{pos}})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi_{\operatorname{pos}})
then corresponds
– up to replacing the kernel function
\phi
with its reflection

\phi(-\,\cdot\,)
– to the fact that the convolution product

??\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi_{\operatorname{pos}})=W^{\infty,2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})

(3.6.106)

is well defined, where

W^{\infty,2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\coloneqq\bigl\{\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\;\big|\;\forall_{k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}}\colon\phi^{(k)}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\bigr\}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})

(3.6.107)

is the Fréchet space obtained from intersecting all Sobolev
56
56

56

Sergei
Lvovich Sobolev (1908-1989) was a Soviet functional analyst working on partial
differential equations. He introduced the notion of weak derivatives, laying the
foundation for Laurent Schwartz’s theory of distributions.
-Hilbert spaces

W^{k,2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
, a discussion of which can be found in

[
192
, Sec. V.2]
.
57
57

57

By virtue of the Sobolev embedding
Theorem
[
96
]
all functions in
W^{\infty,2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})

always possess a smooth representative. This space was introduced and studied by

[
174
, Chapter VI,
§8]
in the context of general
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-spaces, where it is denoted by

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
.
For the momentum representation, we get the integrals

\int{R}\phi(p)\cdot\bigl(\pi_{\operatorname{mom}}(p)\psi\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\Big|_{x}=\int{R}\phi(p)\cdot e^{ipx}\cdot\psi(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p=\hat{\phi}(x)\cdot\psi(x)

(3.6.108)

for
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
,
\psi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and
x\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
, where
\hat{\phi}
is one of the many conventions for the Fourier transform of
\phi
. That is, the operator associated to
\phi
acts by multiplication with
\hat{\phi}
; in particular,
\psi
only has a minor role.
In passing, we note that
\hat{\phi}
has an extension to an entire function of
finite order by virtue of the Paley-Wiener Theorem
[
153
, Thm. I &
IV]
or

[
96
, Thm. 7.1.3]
.
58
58

58

Which, in particular, states that
any such
function may be obtained as the Fourier transformation of a suitable test function
and relates the order of the resulting entire function with the geometry of the
support of the corresponding test function. We will return to this in
Example
3.7.7
.

The fact that
\hat{\phi}\cdot\psi
is smooth with respect to
\pi_{\operatorname{mom}}
implies that the corresponding seminorms are finite, i.e.

\int{R}\big\lvert x^{m}\cdot\hat{\phi}(x)\cdot\psi(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\big\rvert^{2}=\big\lVert x^{m}\cdot\hat{\phi}\cdot\psi\big\rVert_{2}^{2}<\infty

(3.6.109)

for
m\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. One may prove this by elementary means by using that

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\subseteq\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{\mathscr{S}}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})=\mathscr{S}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}).

(3.6.110)

Finally and similarly, we get

\int{R}\phi(\alpha)\cdot\bigl(\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}(\alpha)\psi\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha=\psi\cdot\int{R}\phi(\alpha)e^{i\hbar\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha=\psi\cdot\hat{\phi}(\hbar)

(3.6.111)

for
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
and
\psi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. This reflects the fact that any element of
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
is already smooth with respect to
\pi_{\mathscr{Z}}
and that
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
is indeed a vector space.

Throughout our considerations about the smooth vectors of the Schrödinger representation, we came across and utilized the following criterion.

Corollary 3.6.18
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a strongly
continuous representation on some sequentially complete locally convex space
V
.
If
W\subseteq V
is subspace invariant under
\pi
such that
\pi_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(G,V)
for all
w\in W
, then
W\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
.

3.7
Entire Functions as Strongly Entire Vectors

Once you had a good excuse, you opened the door to bad
excuses.

Thud!
– Terry Pratchett

In Theorem
3.6.11
, we have seen that every strongly
continuous Lie group representation induces a Lie algebra representation on its
space of smooth vectors, which is automatically continuous by finite
dimensionality. Our next goal is to investigate when we
can reverse this process. That is, we seek conditions, under which a Lie algebra
representation comes from a strongly continuous group representation.

Here, one principal problem is that
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
is typically strictly smaller than
the original space
V
. This means that one should try to extend the integrated action
to a larger space as a second step. These questions naturally lead to the concept of

analytic
vectors, as it was conceived in
[
145
]
. We begin with the
well-known equivalence of several feasible definitions, which is an amalgamation of

[
74
, Sec. 2]
,
[
188
, Ch. 4.4]
and

[
148
, Prop. 6.2.21]
.

Proposition 3.7.1
(Analytic vectors).

Let
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
be a Lie algebra
representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally
convex space
V
. Then the following are equivalent for a vector
v\in V
:

i.)

There exists a zero neighbourhood
U
in
\mathfrak{g}
such that the
exponential series

\exp\bigl(\varrho(\xi)\bigr)v\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v

(3.7.1)

converges in
V
for every
\xi\in U
.

ii.)

There exists a zero neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
such that the one
variable Taylor majorants

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi)^{k}v\bigr)

(3.7.2)

are finite for all
\xi\in U
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
.

iii.)

There exists a zero neighbourhood
U{C}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}{C}
such that
(
3.7.1
) extends to a holomorphic function with
respect to
\xi\in U_{\ch@airxmathbbm{C}}
.
59
59

59

By
Proposition
2.3.14
we do not have to distinguish between
Gâteaux and Fréchet holomorphy here.

If
\varrho=T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
and
V\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
for some strongly
continuous group representation

\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(W)

(3.7.3)

of a connected Lie group
G
on a locally convex space
W
, then

i.)
–
iii.)

are equivalent to:

iv.)

The orbit map
\pi_{v}\colon G\longrightarrow V
from
(
3.6.2
) is real analytic.

v.)

The one variable Taylor formula

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v=\pi(\xi)v=\pi_{v}(\exp\xi)

(3.7.4)

holds for every
\xi
in some zero neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof
:

By sequential completeness of
V
,
ii.)
implies

i.)
. Assume now

i.)
and note
that (
3.7.1
) is a series of

k
-homogeneous polynomials by linearity of
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
and thus may be viewed as a power series with values
in
V
in the sense of Corollary
2.2.7
, proving

iii.)
. Conversely, assuming

iii.)
and denoting the holomorphic extension of
(
3.7.1
) by
f\colon U{C}\longrightarrow V
, the
identity principle implies that

f(\xi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}P_{k}(\xi)

(3.7.5)

with Taylor polynomials
P_{k}(\xi)=\tfrac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v
for all
k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
. Invoking the vector valued version of Hartogs’ Theorem from
Proposition
2.3.14
, we get Fréchet holomorphy and thus continuity
of the mapping
f
. We apply the locally convex Cauchy estimates
from Proposition
2.2.16
to infer the convergence
of (
3.7.2
). Indeed, let
B\subseteq U{C}
be
a closed ball with respect to some auxiliary norm. Then
B
is balanced as well as
bounded. Thus, (
2.2.45
) yields the estimate

\sup_{\xi\in B/2}\mathrm{p}\bigl(P_{k}(\xi)\bigr)\leq 2^{-k}\cdot\sup_{\xi\in B}\mathrm{p}\bigl(f(\xi)\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all $k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}$.}

(3.7.6)

This proves the convergence of (
3.7.2
) for all
\xi\in U\coloneqq(B/2)^{\circ}\cap\mathfrak{g}
at once, completing the first
part of the proof.

Assume now that
\varrho=T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
as described above. As the
Lie exponential constitutes a chart of
G
,

v.)
implies the analyticity of
\pi_{v}
at
the group unit. By the classical version of Taylor’s Theorem, we get smoothness of

v
at the group unit and thus
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
by virtue of
Lemma
3.6.15
.
Continuity and linearity of
\pi(g)
for any
g\in G
and
(
3.7.4
) yield

\displaystyle\pi_{v}\bigl(g\exp\xi\bigr)

\displaystyle=\pi(g)\pi_{v}(\exp\xi)

(3.7.7)

\displaystyle=\pi(g)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v

(3.7.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\bigl(\pi(g)\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi(\xi)^{k}\bigr)v

(3.7.9)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)^{k}\pi_{v}\bigr)(g)

(3.7.10)

for all
\xi\in U
, where we have also invested (
3.6.40
). Thus

iv.)
and

v.)
are equivalent. Finally, the
equivalence of
i.)
and

v.)
is obvious in view of
\varrho=T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the sequel, we call a vector analytic for
\varrho
resp.
\pi
if one – and thus
all – of the conditions in Proposition
3.7.1
are fulfilled. The
spaces of all such vectors are denoted by ??and ??, respectively. Our proof reveals that

\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi).

(3.7.11)

By definition, we moreover have
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)=\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi)
. Along the way,
we have proved the following version of the one variable Lie-Taylor formula for
orbit maps.

Corollary 3.7.2
(Lie-Taylor, orbit maps).

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a strongly continuous representation
of a Lie group
G
on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally convex space
V
.
Then

\pi_{v}\bigl(g\exp\xi\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)^{k}\pi_{v}\bigr)(g)=\exp\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)\bigr)\pi_{v}(g)

(3.7.12)

holds for all
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)
,
g\in G
and
\xi
in some sufficiently small
zero neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
.

We cannot resist to at least state Nelson’s Theorem, which may
be viewed as a real analytic version of Gårding’s Theorem for unitary representations,
see again Remark
3.6.7
.

Theorem 3.7.3
(Nelson’s Theorem,
[
145
, Theorem 3]
).

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(H)
be a unitary representation of a Lie
group
G
on a Hilbert space
H
. Then the set of analytic vectors
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)

is dense in
H
.

Consequently, one may often reduce considerations concerning unitary representations
to analytic vectors. We proceed by studying the restrictions of the representations
\pi

and
\varrho
to their spaces of analytic vectors.

Corollary 3.7.4
(Invariance of
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)
).

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a strongly continuous Lie group
representation on a sequentially complete locally convex Hausdorff space
V
. Then

\pi
and
T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
leave
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)
invariant, i.e. restrict to
representations on
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)
.

Proof
:

Let
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)
,
g\in G
and write
w\coloneqq\pi(g)v
. Then, taking
another look at (
3.6.29
) and using
Proposition
3.7.1
,
iv.)
, we see that

\pi_{w}
is real analytic as the composition of real analytic maps. Let now
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\varrho)
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
. Using
(
3.6.42
), we compute

\pi_{\varrho(\xi)v}(g)=\pi(g)T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi(\eta)v=T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}\xi\bigr)\pi(g)v=\bigl(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\bigl(\operatorname{\mathrm{Ad}}_{g}\xi\bigr)\circ\pi_{v}\bigr)(g)

(3.7.13)

for
g\in G
. Thus,
\pi_{\varrho(\xi)v}
is real analytic as a composition of
such maps; the additional dependence on
g
is indeed harmless as the
adjoint
representation is always real analytic.
60
60

60

The correspondence of Lie groups
and Lie algebras even provides the explicit power series expansion in terms of an
exponential series.

\ch@irxboxempty

In principle, one could now proceed as for smooth vectors and identify
v\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)
with
\pi_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G,V)
to obtain a locally convex topology, see
again Lemma
3.6.9
. However, as the natural topology

\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G,V)
is rather unpleasant from a locally convex point of view
61
61

61

It
involves an inductive limit, essentially over spaces of the type (
3.2.16
),
which is typically not strict.
, we refrain from doing so.

Example 3.7.5
(Translation Representation, II: Analytic vectors).

We return to the translation representation, see again
Example
3.6.8
.
In analogy with (
3.6.17
) we claim that

\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(r^{*})=\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G).

(3.7.14)

Indeed, it is the content of (
3.2.1
) that
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(r^{*})
. If, conversely,
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(r^{*})
, then
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(G)
by a combination of (
3.7.11
) and
(
3.6.17
). Moreover,
(
3.7.2
) takes the form

\phi\bigl(g\exp(\xi)\bigr)=r^{*}_{\phi}\bigl(\exp(\xi)\bigr)\Big|_{g}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}T_{\mathrm{e}}r^{*}(\xi)^{k}\phi\Big|_{g}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\xi)^{k}\phi\bigr)(g),

(3.7.15)

which we recognize as (
3.2.1
). This yields
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(G)
and provides an alternative proof of the Lie-Taylor formula.

Taking another look at Proposition
3.7.1
, we see that the
conditions are still too weak to integrate a general Lie algebra representation

\varrho
to a Lie group representation on all of
G
. The problem is already visible
for the translation representation and exactly what we have described in
Remark
3.2.2
. A more sophisticated example can be found in

[
74
, Sec. 8, Example]
. The resolution of this particular obstruction
also comes in the same form: Demanding that we may choose
U=\mathfrak{g}
throughout
Proposition
3.7.1
yields the notion of
entire
vectors as
it was first introduced in
[
74
, Sec. 2]
for Banach spaces
V
.

Proposition 3.7.6
(Entire vectors,
[
147
, Prop. 2.2.7]
).

Let
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
be a Lie algebra
representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally
convex space
V
. Then the following are equivalent for a vector
v\in V
:

i.)

The exponential series

\exp\bigl(\varrho(\xi)\bigr)v=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v

(3.7.16)

converges in
V
for every
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
.

ii.)

The one variable Taylor majorants

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi)^{k}v\bigr)

(3.7.17)

are finite for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
.

iii.)

The function (
3.7.1
) extends to an entire
function with respect to
\xi
on
\mathfrak{g}{C}
.

If
\varrho=T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
and
V\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)
for some strongly
continuous group representation

\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(W)

(3.7.18)

of a connected Lie group
G
on a locally convex space
W
, then

i.)
through

iii.)
are moreover equivalent to:

iv.)

For every
g\in G
, the infinitesimal orbit map

\mathfrak{g}{C}\ni\xi\longrightarrow\pi\bigl(g\exp\xi\bigr)v\in V

(3.7.19)

is an entire function.
62
62

62

Again, either in the sense of Gâteaux or Fréchet by
Proposition
2.3.14
, see also Corollary
2.2.15
.

v.)

The infinitesimal orbit map (
3.7.19
) for
g=\mathrm{e}
is an
entire function.

vi.)

The one variable Taylor formula

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v=\pi(\exp\xi)v=\pi_{v}(\exp\xi)

(3.7.20)

holds for every
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof
:

Identical to Proposition
3.7.1
with
U=\mathfrak{g}
throughout.

\ch@irxboxempty

In the sequel, we call a vector
v\in V

entire
for
\varrho
resp.
\pi
if
it fulfils one – and thus all – of the conditions in
Proposition
3.7.6
. Moreover, we denote the spaces of all such
vectors by ??and ??, respectively.
Notably, this somewhat baroque notation differs from the one we use for the
entire functions
\mathscr{E}(G)
on
G
. As we shall see, this is not an oversight.

The following example arises from a combination of the discussion in

[
74
, Prop. 5.1]
and

[
101
, Ch. VI, §7]
, which also contains a detailed proof.

Example 3.7.7
(Translations on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
).

Consider the space of square integrable functions
V\coloneqq\mathrm{L}^{2}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
on
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}

endowed with the translation action, that is
\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}}
from
Example
3.6.17
. Then
f\in\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\pi_{{\operatorname{pos}}})

if and only if is the restriction of a holomorphic function
F
defined on a strip
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\times(-r,r)
fulfilling

\sup_{\lvert y\rvert\leq s}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big\lvert F(x+\mathrm{i}y)\big\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x<\infty\qquad\textrm{for all }s<t.

(3.7.21)

Indeed, this boils down to a version of the classical
Paley
63
63

63

Raymond Paley (1907-1933) was a British
mathematician, who tragically died in a skiing accident on Deception Pass. He
nevertheless managed to publish a total of 26 papers, making contributions to many
areas of analysis.
-Wiener
64
64

64

Norbert Wiener (1894-1964) was an American
mathematician working on stochastic processes with applications in electronic
engineering and communications. He coined the term

cybernetics
in an attempt to bring together the different sciences.

Theorem
[
153
, Thm. I & IV]
, a modern treatment
of which is
[
96
, Thm. 7.3.1]
.

Returning to (
3.7.17
), we have also
already found a natural system of seminorms for
\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
, namely

??(v)\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\sup_{\ell=1,\ldots,n}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\mathit{e}_{\ell})^{k}v\bigr),

(3.7.22)

where
c\geq 0
,
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
is
a fixed basis of
\mathfrak{g}
. These seminorms also appear as

[
74
, (2.1)]
and one readily verifies that the resulting topology is
independent of the chosen basis by retracing Lemma
3.2.5
in this
more general situation. In particular, this also endows

\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\pi)
with a locally convex topology by differentiation. That is,
by considering
\varrho=T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
, see again Theorem
3.6.11
.
The system may be chosen countable if
V
