admits a countable defining system of
seminorms. Mimicking the proof of Proposition
3.7.1
allows us
to identify the locally convex topology induced by the seminorms
(
3.7.22
) as the topology of locally uniform convergence of the
associated mappings (
3.7.16
) on the complexified
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}{C}
. This is essentially a version of
Proposition
3.6.9
for entire vectors. In analogy to
(
2.1.51
), we write

??(f)\coloneqq\sup_{\xi\in K}\mathrm{p}\bigl(f(\xi)\bigr)

(3.7.23)

for compact subsets
K\subseteq\mathfrak{g}{C}
,
f\in\mathscr{C}(\mathfrak{g}{C},V)

and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
.

Proposition 3.7.8
.

Let
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
be a Lie algebra
representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally
convex space
V
. Then the mapping

\iota\colon\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}(\mathfrak{g}{C},V),\quad\iota(v)\big|_{\xi}\coloneqq\exp\bigl(\varrho(\xi)\bigr)v

(3.7.24)

is a linear embedding, that is a continuous injective linear mapping such that the
subspace topology inherited from the inclusion

\iota\bigl(\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)\bigr)\subseteq\mathscr{H}(\mathfrak{g}{C},V)

(3.7.25)

coincides with the
\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
-topology.

Proof
:

The linearity of
\iota
is clear and injectivity follows at once from
\iota(v)\big|_{0}=v
. Let now
v\in\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
,
K\coloneqq\mathrm{B}_{r}(0)^{\mathrm{cl}}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}{C}
the closed
\ell^{\infty}
-ball of radius
r>0
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{q}_{K,\mathrm{p}}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\xi\in K}\mathrm{p}\biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v\biggr)

(3.7.26)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi\in K}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi)^{k}v\bigr)

(3.7.27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi\in K}\mathrm{p}\biggl(\varrho\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varrho(\mathit{e}_{j}\xi^{j})\Bigr)^{k}v\biggr)

(3.7.28)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(nr)^{k}}{k!}\sup_{j=1,\ldots,n}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\mathit{e}_{j})^{k}v\bigr)

(3.7.29)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{q}_{nr,\mathrm{p}}(v).

(3.7.30)

For the converse inequality, recall that
\iota(v)
is holomorphic with Taylor
polynomials

P_{k}(\xi)=\frac{1}{k!}\varrho(\xi)^{k}v\qquad\textrm{for all }k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}.

(3.7.31)

Thus, the locally convex Cauchy estimates (
2.2.45
)
with
B\coloneqq K
from above yield

\sup_{\xi\in K}\mathrm{p}\bigl(P_{k}(\xi)\bigr)\leq r^{-k}\sup_{\xi\in rK}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr)=r^{-k}\cdot\mathrm{q}_{rK,\mathrm{p}}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr)

(3.7.32)

for all
k\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}
and
r>0
. As
\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\in K
, we
therefore have

\mathrm{q}_{c,\mathrm{p}}(v)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\sup_{\ell=1,\ldots,n}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\mathit{e}_{\ell})^{k}v\bigr)\leq\mathrm{q}_{(2c)K,\mathrm{p}}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{(2c)^{k}}=2\cdot\mathrm{q}_{(2c)K,\mathrm{p}}\bigl(\iota(v)\bigr).

(3.7.33)

This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Consequently,
\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
inherits most of the pleasant functional
analytic properties of
\mathscr{H}(\mathfrak{g}{C},V)
such as nuclearity by

[
181
, (50.3)]
and completeness for complete
V
.

Corollary 3.7.9
.

Let
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
be a Lie algebra
representation on a complete Hausdorff locally convex space
V
. Then the
space of entire vectors
\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
is complete with respect
to the locally convex topology induced by the
seminorms (
3.7.22
). If
V
is merely sequentially
complete, the same is true for
\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
.

Proof
:

By Proposition
3.7.8
, it suffices to prove the
closedness of
\iota(\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho))
within the ambient complete
space
\mathscr{H}(\mathfrak{g}{C},V)
. To this end, let
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in J}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
be such that

\iota(v_{\alpha})\longrightarrow\phi\in\mathscr{H}(\mathfrak{g}{C},V)

(3.7.34)

locally uniformly. As this entails convergence at the origin, we get
v_{\alpha}\rightarrow\phi(0)
. By continuity of (
3.7.24
), this
in turn means that
\iota(v_{\alpha})\rightarrow\iota(\phi(0))
and thus

\phi(\xi)=\exp\bigl(\varrho(\xi)\bigr)\phi(0)\qquad\textrm{for all }\xi\in\mathfrak{g}{C},

(3.7.35)

proving
\phi\in\iota(\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho))
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Remark 3.7.10
(Existence of entire vectors).

Unlike for smooth and analytic vectors, see again Remark
3.6.7
and
Theorem
3.7.3
, there are quite general situations prohibiting the
existence of
any
entire vectors besides the zero vector. Indeed, by

[
74
, Thm. 8.1]
any unitary representation
\pi
of a
non compact simple Lie group
65
65

65

Such as the projective special unitary
groups.
,
which does not contain the identity representation as a
subrepresentation
66
66

66

This essentially states that all
elements of the group act in a non trivial fashion.
, fulfils

\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\pi)=\{0\}
. This pathology in particular includes any
group containing such a group as a subgroup. For another concrete example without
non trivial entire vectors, we refer to
[
74
, Sec. 7]
. In this sense,
Nelson’s Theorem
3.7.3
is best possible.

Remark 3.7.11
(Entire vectors on nilpotent Lie groups).

If
G
is nilpotent and connected, see again
Proposition
3.5.2
,
this point of view moreover provides a positive answer to the integration problem

\varrho\leadsto\pi
. Indeed, in this case
\mathfrak{g}
and
G
are
diffeomorphic by means of the Lie exponential and
(
3.7.16
) thus defines a function on all of

G
. As
\varrho
is a Lie algebra morphism by assumption, this indeed yields a
group morphism
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
. This may be viewed as the
infinite dimensional Lie correspondence for the group
\operatorname{GL}(V)
and Lie algebra

\operatorname{L}(V)
. As an aside, we note that this also matches nicely with complexification:
We have already seen that with
G
, also
G{C}
is nilpotent and thus
\pi
may be
defined directly on
G{C}
instead by Proposition
3.7.6
,

iii.)
. Formally, one may derive this by the naive
application of the the universal property (
3.3.87
)
of the universal
G{C}
, now for the infinite dimensional target
\operatorname{GL}(V)
of invertible
continuous linear self-maps of
V
.

Outside of this very particular situation, one may proceed as follows for connected
groups
G
. Choose an open neighbourhood
U
of the origin in
\mathfrak{g}{C}
such
that
\exp\big|_{U}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image. By openness of
\exp(U)\subseteq G
, every
g\in G
may then be written as

g=\exp(\xi_{1})\cdots\exp(\xi_{k})

(3.7.36)

with – not at all unique –
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in U
. Then one would
be lead to define

\pi(g)v\coloneqq\exp\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{1})\bigr)\cdots\exp\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k})\bigr)v\qquad\textrm{for }v\in\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho).

(3.7.37)

This approach has two problems. Firstly, it is
not at all clear whether this depends on the choice of
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
.
This is ultimately a purely topological obstruction, which may be avoided by
assuming simple connectedness of
G
, i.e. the absence of monodromy.
Concretely, we will resolve this by the Lie theoretic incarnation of the Monodromy
Theorem, as it can be found in
[
92
, Prop. 9.5.8]
; this particular
formulation has the advantage that no differentiable structure on the codomain is
required.
67
67

67

The
group
\operatorname{GL}(V)
surprisingly ill-behaved for general locally convex spaces
V
. Indeed, if

V
is not normable, then
\operatorname{GL}(V)\subseteq\operatorname{L}(V)
is not open and there exists no
topological vector space structure such that composition becomes continuous, see

[
124
, Satz 2]
for the latter result and
[
143
]
for a
comprehensive discussion. That being said, if
V
is Banach, then
\operatorname{GL}(V)
indeed
carries the structure of a
linear
infinite dimensional Lie group.

We return to the problem at hand: Secondly, one needs the invariance of
the space of entire vectors
\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)
under the exponentiated
action

\exp(\varrho(\,\cdot\,))
to formulate the composition. Indeed, our proof of
Corollary
3.7.4
relied heavily on the presence of
\pi
.

This brings us to our final notion of regularity:
Strongly
entire vectors.
To facilitate comprehensive formulas, we extend our Lie algebra representation

\varrho
to an algebra morphism

\varrho\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)

(3.7.38)

by means of the universal property of the universal enveloping algebra

\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
, as we did for the Lie
derivative in (
3.1.20
) and
(
3.2.19
). Moreover, we write

\varrho(\alpha)\coloneqq\varrho\bigl(\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{k}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{1}}\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for }\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}.

(3.7.39)

Definition 3.7.12
(Strongly entire vector).

Let
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
be a Lie algebra
representation on a sequentially complete Hausdorff locally
convex space
V
.

i.)

A vector
v\in V
is called strongly entire if

\mathrm{q}_{c,\mathrm{p}}(v)\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\alpha)v\bigr)

(3.7.40)

is finite for all
c\geq 0
,
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
and some basis

(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of
\mathfrak{g}
.

ii.)

We denote the space of strongly entire vectors by
??and endow it with the locally convex
topology induced by the seminorms (
3.7.40
).

iii.)

If
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
is a strongly continuous Lie
group representation, then we define

??\coloneqq\mathscr{E}(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi).

(3.7.41)

Retracing our argument in Lemma
3.2.5
, we see that
the resulting notion is independent on the choice of basis. By what we have shown and
the definitions, we arrive at the – disregarding the lack of topology for the analytic
vectors – continuous inclusions

V=\mathscr{C}(\varrho)\supseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\varrho)\supseteq\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\varrho)\supseteq\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\varrho)\supseteq\mathscr{E}(\varrho)

(3.7.42)

and

V=\mathscr{C}(\pi)\supseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\pi)\supseteq\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\pi)\supseteq\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\pi)\supseteq\mathscr{E}(\pi).

(3.7.43)

We chose this particular version of the definition to stress the connection with

\mathscr{E}(G)
. Thus, our first goal is to connect our definition with alternatives used
in the literature such as
[
74
,
75
,
76
]
,

[
154
]
or

[
148
]
. To this end, we note the following elementary lemma, which
states that we may replace the inner
summation in (
3.7.40
) with a supremum over any bounded
subset within
\mathfrak{g}
.

Lemma 3.7.13
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and

\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B,\mathrm{p}}(v)\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in B}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.44)

for bounded subsets
B\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
.
Then the collection of all
\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B,\mathrm{p}}
generates the
topology of
\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
.

Proof
:

Let
v\in\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
,
B\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be a bounded set and

\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
B
is
contained in the unit ball
\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)
for the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm corresponding
to some basis
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})
of
\mathfrak{g}
by virtue of
its finite dimensionality. We estimate

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B,\mathrm{p}}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.45)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}=1}^{n}\big\lvert\xi^{j_{1}}\cdots\xi^{j_{k}}\big\rvert\cdot\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{1}})v\bigr)

(3.7.46)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}=1}^{n}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)}\big\lvert\xi^{j_{1}}\cdots\xi^{j_{k}}\big\rvert\cdot\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{1}})v\bigr)

(3.7.47)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}=1}^{n}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\mathit{e}_{j_{k}}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{j_{1}})v\bigr)

(3.7.48)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{q}_{1,\mathrm{p}}(v).

(3.7.49)

For the converse inequality, we note
\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n}\in\mathrm{B}_{1}(0)
, which is bounded. Hence, if
c\geq 0
, then

\mathrm{q}_{c,\mathrm{p}}(v)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}=1}^{n}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathrm{B}_{c}(0)}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)=\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathrm{B}_{nc}(0),\mathrm{p}}(v).

\ch@irxboxempty

In the setting of infinite dimensional groups, this leads to two versions of entire
vectors, both of which turn out to be useful: One with respect to bounded sets
B
in
(
3.7.44
) and another for compact ones. In

[
148
, Def. 6.2.19]
the resulting spaces are denoted by
the symbols
\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}^{\mathcal{O}_{b}}

and
\mathcal{H}_{\varrho}^{\mathcal{O}}
,
respectively. We refer to
[
148
, Sec. 6.3]
for a comprehensive
discussion of these spaces in the setting of
unitary representations
of regular
infinite dimensional Lie groups. It
turns out that quite a few of the results hold also in our slightly different situation, where
we can however no longer guarantee the existence of strongly entire vectors. In
particular, our definition is consistent with the original one given in

[
74
, p. 61]
. The following is a generalization of

[
148
, Thm 6.3.10 (3)]
and crucially does not assume the presence of a
integrated representation
\pi
.

Proposition 3.7.14
(Invariance of
\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
).

Let
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
be a Lie algebra
representation on a sequentially complete locally convex
Hausdorff space
V
. Then
\varrho
leaves
\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
invariant and
restricts to a Lie algebra representation

\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}\bigl(\mathscr{E}(\varrho)\bigr),

(3.7.50)

whose space of strongly entire vectors is precisely
\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
.

Proof
:

Let
v\in\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
,
B\subseteq\mathfrak{g}

be a bounded subset and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Then

B_{\xi}\coloneqq B\cup\{\xi\}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}

(3.7.51)

is still bounded and we may estimate

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B,\mathrm{p}}\bigl(\varrho(\xi)v\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in B}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})\varrho(\xi)v\bigr)

(3.7.52)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{k+1}{k+1}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k+1}\in B_{\xi}}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k+1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.53)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k+1}\in B_{\xi}}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k+1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.54)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in B_{\xi}}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.55)

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{2B_{\xi},\mathrm{p}}(v),

(3.7.56)

which proves
\varrho(\xi)v\in\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
and the continuity of the linear
mapping

\varrho(\xi)\colon\mathscr{E}(\varrho)\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}(\varrho).

(3.7.57)

Taking
c=0
in (
3.7.40
) shows the inclusion

\operatorname{cs}(V)\Big|_{\mathscr{E}(\varrho)}\subseteq\operatorname{cs}\bigl(\mathscr{E}(\varrho)\bigr),

(3.7.58)

which implies that every strongly entire vector for
(
3.7.50
) is strongly entire for
\varrho
as a
representation on
V
. It remains to establish that every
v\in\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
is
strongly entire also with respect to the
\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
-topology.
We once again make use of the alternative
seminorms (
3.7.44
). To this end, let
B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V
be bounded and
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Then
B\coloneqq B^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime\prime}
is bounded, as well, and by assumption

\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B,\mathrm{p}}(v)<\infty
. This implies that the
function
f\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
defined by the power series

f(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in B}\mathrm{p}\Bigl(\varrho\bigl(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{k}\bigr)v\Bigr)

(3.7.59)

is entire in the classical sense. Taylor expanding around
z_{0}=1
leads to the expression

f(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(z-1)^{k}}{k!}f^{(k)}(1)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(z-1)^{k}\sum_{\ell=k}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(\ell-k)!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell}\in B}\mathrm{p}\Bigl(\varrho\bigl(\xi_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{\ell}\bigr)v\Bigr).

(3.7.60)

Consequently, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B^{\prime},\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B^{\prime\prime},\mathrm{p}}}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in B^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathrm{q}}_{B^{\prime\prime},\mathrm{p}}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.61)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in B^{\prime}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell!}\sup_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}\in B^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\eta_{\ell}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\eta_{1})\varrho(\xi_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.62)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k+\ell}\in B}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{k+\ell}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.63)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{m=k}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(m-k)!}\sup_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m}\in B}\mathrm{p}\bigl(\varrho(\xi_{m}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\xi_{1})v\bigr)

(3.7.64)

\displaystyle\leq f(2)

(3.7.65)

\displaystyle<\infty.

(3.7.66)

This completes the proof.

\ch@irxboxempty

Corollary 3.7.15
(Integration on
\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
).

Let
\varrho\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(V)
be a Lie algebra
representation on a sequentially complete locally convex
Hausdorff space
V
. Then there
exists a unique strongly continuous group representation

\pi\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}\bigl(\mathscr{E}(\varrho)\bigr)

(3.7.67)

such that
T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi=\varrho\big|_{\mathscr{E}(\varrho)}
, where
\widetilde{G}

denotes the universal covering group of
G
.

Proof
:

We have discussed the existence of
\widetilde{G}
in Theorem
3.3.13
. Let

U\subseteq\mathfrak{g}{C}
be an absolutely convex neighbourhood of zero such that
the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series converges on
U\times U
and define

\pi(\exp\xi)
by (
3.7.16
) for all
\xi\in U
. By
[
148
, Cor. 6.3.11]
, we get

\pi\bigl(\exp\xi\exp\eta\bigr)=\pi\bigl(\exp\operatorname{BCH}(\xi,\eta)\bigr)=\pi(\xi)\circ\pi(\eta)\qquad\textrm{for all }\xi,\eta\in U.

(3.7.68)

By simple connectedness of
\widetilde{G}
and the
Monodromy Theorem as formulated in
[
92
, Prop. 9.5.8]
, our
mapping
\pi
thus extends to a unique group morphism

\pi\colon\widetilde{G}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}\bigl(\mathscr{E}(\varrho)\bigr).

(3.7.69)

By construction,
\pi
is real analytic with Taylor series around the group unit
given by (
3.7.16
). This implies that
\pi
is
smooth with derivative
\varrho
by a combination of (
3.6.37
)
with (
3.7.16
).

\ch@irxboxempty

Thus the notion of strong entirety turns out to be powerful enough to solve the
integration problem that motivated our study of analytic vectors. As
\pi
is a group
morphism, (
3.7.37
) turns out to be correct a posteriori. In the
sequel, we will work with the group representation directly. Incorporating our
considerations on universal complexifications from
Section
3.3
, we may now complexify by utilizing that
(
3.7.67
) may be represented by the power series
(
3.7.16
). This generalizes the universal property
(
3.3.87
) to the infinite dimensional situation.

Corollary 3.7.16
(Complexification on
\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
).

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a strongly continuous
representation of a connected and simply connected Lie group on a sequentially
complete locally convex space
V
. Write
\eta\colon G\longrightarrow G{C}
for
the universal complexification of
G
. Then there exists a unique strongly
continuous and holomorphic group representation

\pi{C}\colon G{C}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}\bigl(\mathscr{E}(\varrho)\bigr)

(3.7.70)

such that
\pi{C}\circ\eta=\pi
.

Proof
:

By Corollary
3.3.15
,

v.)
the universal
complexification
G{C}
is again connected and simply connected and

T_{\mathrm{e}}\eta\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}{C}

(3.7.71)

is the canonical embedding. Retracing the proof of
Corollary
3.7.15

and using that (
3.7.16
) converges on all of

\mathfrak{g}{C}
, the Monodromy Theorem
[
92
, Prop. 9.5.8]

yields a unique group morphism as in (
3.7.70
). It
is holomorphic, as the Lie exponential constitutes a holomorphic chart and
(
3.7.16
) is a power series. Finally,

T_{\mathrm{e}}\bigl(\pi{C}\circ\eta\bigr)=(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi){C}\circ T_{\mathrm{e}}\eta=T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi

(3.7.72)

by the universal property of vector space complexification. By connectedness of

G
, this implies
\pi{C}\circ\eta=\pi
as desired.

\ch@irxboxempty

The following considerations warrant our notation and generalize
Theorem
3.2.11
from representations on finite dimensional
spaces to representations on locally convex spaces, but comes at the cost of demanding
strong entirety of the involved vectors. Indeed, this is automatic if
V
is finite
dimensional.

Lemma 3.7.17
.

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_{k}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C})
be a representation.
68
68

68

One
should keep in mind that representations on finite dimensional spaces are always
continuous group morphisms by their very definition. As finite dimensional spaces
are always barrelled, Lemma
3.6.1
moreover
implies that strong continuity and continuity coincide in this case.

Then
\mathscr{E}(\pi)=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k}
. In particular, the inclusions
(
3.7.43
) are equalities of locally convex spaces.
69
69

69

Once
again, disregarding the absence of a topology on
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k})
.

Proof
:

We first note that for every
g\in G
, the corresponding mapping
\pi(g)\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k}
is automatically continuous by its linearity. The
crucial point is that the Lie algebra morphism

\varrho\coloneqq T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\operatorname{L}(\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k})

(3.7.73)

is, in particular, a linear mapping between finite dimensional vector spaces.
Choosing a suitable auxiliary norm on
\mathfrak{g}
and operator norms otherwise,
we may achieve that the operator norm
\lVert\varrho\rVert\leq 1
and
(\mathit{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{e}_{n})\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
is a basis with
\lVert\mathit{e}_{k}\rVert=1
for
k=1,\ldots,n
. Consequently, the submultiplicativity of both operator
norms yields

\big\lVert\varrho(\alpha)v\big\rVert=\big\lVert\bigl(\varrho(\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{k}})\circ\cdots\circ\varrho(\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{1}})\bigr)v\big\rVert\leq\lVert\varrho\rVert\cdot\big\lVert\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{k}}\big\rVert\cdots\lVert\varrho\rVert\cdot\big\lVert\mathit{e}_{\alpha_{1}}\big\rVert\cdot\lVert v\rVert\leq\lVert v\rVert

(3.7.74)

for any
\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}
and
v\in\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k}
. Plugging this into the seminorms
(
3.7.40
), we get

\mathrm{q}_{c,\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert}(v)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\big\lVert\varrho(\alpha)v\big\rVert\leq\lVert v\rVert\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(n\cdot c)^{k}}{k!}=\lVert v\rVert\cdot\exp(nc)<\infty

(3.7.75)

for
c\geq 0
and
v\in V
. Thus,
\mathscr{E}(\pi)=\mathscr{E}(\varrho)=V
.
For the final statement, we note that
V=\ch@airxmathbbm{C}^{k}
is certainly Hausdorff and thus
the same is true for all the spaces in (
3.7.43
). Hence, all of
them carry the unique Hausdorff locally convex topology of finite dimensional
vector spaces, see
[
166
, I. 3.2]
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Lemma
3.7.17
may be seen as a generalization of
the fact that every continuous group morphism between finite dimensional Lie groups is
already smooth by
[
92
, Thm. 9.2.16]
and, indeed, analytic
by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula.

Example 3.7.18
.

The continuity assumption in Lemma
3.7.17
is
necessary. Indeed, consider the additive reals
G\coloneqq(\ch@airxmathbbm{R},+)
and let
\Phi\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
be a discontinuous
\ch@airxmathbbm{Q}
-linear mapping. Then

\Phi
is a discontinuous group morphism, say at the point
x_{0}\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
. Moreover,
identifying
x\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
with the multiplication operator

m_{x}\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{R},\quad m_{x}(y)\coloneqq x\cdot y

(3.7.76)

provides a group isomorphism
m\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_{1}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R})
. Combining
both morphisms thus yields a representation

\pi\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}_{1}(\ch@airxmathbbm{R}),\quad\pi(x)y\coloneqq m_{\Phi(x)}(y)

(3.7.77)

of
\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
on itself, as
\pi(x)\colon\ch@airxmathbbm{R}\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
is continuous by its
linearity. The orbit map corresponding to
y\in\ch@airxmathbbm{R}
is however given by

\pi_{y}(x)=m_{\Phi(x)}y,

(3.7.78)

which is discontinuous at
x=x_{0}
for
y\coloneqq 1
. Complexifying all
structures and extending
\Phi
by the identity on the imaginary parts provides a
complex version of this phenomenon.

We turn towards the aforementioned generalization of
Theorem
3.2.11
.

Theorem 3.7.19
(Universality of
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
).

Let
\pi\colon G\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(V)
be a strongly continuous Lie group
representation on a sequentially complete locally convex Hausdorff space
V
and
v\in\mathscr{E}(\pi)
a strongly entire vector. Then the corresponding representative
functions

\pi_{v,\varphi}\colon G\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C},\quad\pi_{v,\varphi}(g)\coloneqq\varphi\bigl(\pi(g)v\bigr)=\bigl(\varphi\circ\pi_{v}\bigr)(g)

(3.7.79)

are elements of
\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{E}(T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi)^{\prime}
.

Proof
:

As usual, we write
\varrho\coloneqq T_{\mathrm{e}}\pi
for the differentiated
representation, see again Theorem
3.6.11
.
Let
\varphi\in\mathscr{E}(\varrho)^{\prime}
.
70
70

70

This includes, in particular, all of
V^{\prime}
. As

\mathscr{E}(\varrho)
typically constitutes a proper subspace of
V
, there may however
be
additional continuous linear functionals available.
Using the continuity of
\varphi

and
(
3.6.40
), we compute

\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v,\varphi}\Big|_{\mathrm{e}}=\varphi\bigl((\mathscr{L}(\xi)\pi_{v})(\mathrm{e})\bigr)=\varphi\bigl((\pi(\mathrm{e})\circ\varrho(\xi))v\bigr)=\varphi\bigl(\varrho(\xi)v\bigr)

(3.7.80)

for
\xi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{U}}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}{C})
. If now
v\in\mathscr{E}(\pi)
, then
\mathrm{p}\coloneqq\lvert\varphi\rvert\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
. Consequently, we get for
c\geq 0

\mathrm{q}_{0,c}\bigl(\pi_{v,\varphi}\bigr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\pi_{v,\varphi}\bigr)(\mathrm{e})\Big\rvert\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{k}}\Big\lvert\varphi\bigl(\varrho(\alpha)v\bigr)\Big\rvert=\mathrm{q}_{c,\mathrm{p}}(v)<\infty

(3.7.81)

by virtue of
v\in\mathscr{E}(\pi)
. Hence,
\pi_{v,\varphi}\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
.

\ch@irxboxempty

Combining Theorem
3.7.19
with
Lemma
3.7.17
provides another proof of
Theorem
3.2.11
.

Conversely, one may wonder whether
\pi_{v,\varphi}\in\mathscr{E}_{0}(G)
for all
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
– or
\varphi\in\mathscr{E}(\varrho)^{\prime}
– already implies
v\in\mathscr{E}(\pi)
. One
may think of this condition as a
weak
version of strong entirety. The principal
problem one is faced with is the fact that a general seminorm
\mathrm{p}\in\operatorname{cs}(V)
cannot be majorized by the modulus of any
\varphi\in V^{\prime}
. We leave the
problem as a question.

Question 3.7.20
.

Does the converse of Theorem
3.7.19
hold?

For strongly continuous unitary representations of matrix Lie groups on Hilbert
spaces, there is a positive answer by a combination of
[
154
, Cor. I.6]

and Theorem
3.5.13
. The particular case
\pi=r^{*}
yields now the
following.

Example 3.7.21
(Translation representation, III: Strongly entire vectors).

Let
G
be a connected Lie group and consider
V\coloneqq\mathscr{C}(G)
, for now
endowed with the topology of pointwise convergence. Then
(
3.7.40
) becomes

\mathrm{q}_{c,g}(\phi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{c^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{0}^{k}}\Big\lvert\bigl(\mathscr{L}(\alpha)\phi\bigr)(g)\Big\rvert

(3.7.82)

for any
c\geq 0
and
g\in G
by virtue of
(
3.6.16
). In particular, we recognize

\mathrm{q}_{c,\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{q}_{0,c}
as the seminorms
inducing the
0
-topology from (
3.2.31
). This implies

\mathscr{E}(r^{*})\subseteq\mathscr{E}(G)
. Combining Theorem
3.2.8
,

iii.)
with (
3.6.18
) we
actually get equality

\mathscr{E}(r^{*})=\mathscr{E}(G).

(3.7.83)

This statement is essentially
[
90
, Theorem 4.17,
v.)
]
.
Remarkably, (
3.7.83
) also holds if one endows

\mathscr{C}(G)
with its natural topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets by

[
90
, Theorem 4.17,
vi.)
]
. Using again the Dirac
functionals
71
71

71

That is,
\delta_{g}\colon\mathscr{C}(G)\longrightarrow\ch@airxmathbbm{C}
defined
by
\delta_{g}(\phi)\coloneqq\phi(g)
.
, the representative
functions corresponding to
\phi\in\mathscr{E}(r^{*})
are given by

r^{*}_{\phi,g_{0}}(g)=\phi\bigl(r_{g_{0}}(g)\bigr)=\phi\bigl(gg_{0}\bigr)\qquad\textrm{for all }g,g_{0}\in G.

(3.7.84)

Taking
g_{0}=\mathrm{e}
thus reproduces
\phi
, now as an element of

\mathscr{E}(G)
, via the universality statement in Theorem
3.7.19
.
This also provides another proof of the inclusion
\mathscr{E}(r^{*})\subseteq\mathscr{E}(G)
.

The attentive reader will have noticed that we have not discussed the entire vectors
of the translation representation. The reason for this is that it is generally not at
all clear whether

\mathscr{E}(\pi)\subsetneq\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\pi).

(3.7.85)

Indeed, under certain assumptions, equality holds in (
3.7.85
)
by Penney’s
72
72

72

Richard Penney is a harmonic analyst and
Professor Emeritus at Purdue University. His doctoral thesis studied entire vectors
under the supervision of Roe Goodman, who initiated their
study in
[
74
,
75
,
76
]
.
Theorem.

Theorem 3.7.22
(Penney’s Theorem,
[
154
, Thm. I.3]
).

Let
G
be a connected, simply connected and solvable
73
73

73

There are subgroups

G=G_{k}\mathbin{\triangleright}G_{k-1}\mathbin{\triangleright}\cdots\mathbin{\triangleright}G_{1}=\{\mathrm{e}\}

(3.7.86)

such that the quotients
G_{j}/G_{j-1}
are abelian for
j=2,\ldots,k
.
Lie
group. Let moreover
H
be a Hilbert space and

\pi\colon G\longrightarrow U(H)

(3.7.87)

be a strongly continuous unitary representation of
G
. Then

\underline{\mathscr{E}}(\pi)=\mathscr{E}(\pi).

(3.7.88)

Proof
(Idea):

First of all one may use Corollary
3.7.16
to
extend
\pi
to a holomorphic representation of
G{C}
. The principal idea is then
that one may reorder

\varrho(\alpha)=\varrho\bigl(\mathit{e}_{k}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{k}+\mathit{e}_{k-1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{k-1}+\cdots+\mathit{e}_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\cdots\mathbin{\otimes}\mathit{e}_{1}\bigr)+\varrho(\textrm{lower order})

(3.7.89)

for any
\alpha\in\ch@airxmathbbm{N}_{n}^{N}
by utilizing that
\varrho
is a Lie algebra morphism.
In general, this results in an extraordinary amount of terms and the bookkeeping
becomes unfeasible quickly.
74
74

74

This may be wonderfully illustrated by working
out the combinatorics for the
ax+b
group in one dimension, a pleasure the author
will not indulge in again within this lifetime.
The additional
assumption of solvability makes this approach viable. The concrete statement of

[
154
, Thm. I.3]
then subsumes the Lie theoretic Cauchy
estimates, as we have used them in the proof of Theorem
3.5.13
for

\pi=r^{*}
.

\ch@irxboxempty

For more on this problem and its analogue for representations of infinite dimensional
Fréchet Lie groups, we refer to
[
144
, Rem. 3.7]
. Assuming the uniform form
of the
Cauchy estimates as concluded in
[
154
, Thm. I.3]
notably suffices to
construct the holomorphic extensions by
[
154
, Rem. I.7]
, at least if the
universal complexification morphism
\eta
is injective. For this reason, some authors
use this then as the definition for strong entirety.
Consequently, the extension and restriction statements we have derived within
Theorem
3.5.9

and
Theorem
3.5.13
may be viewed as variants of Penney’s results on
holomorphic extensions
[
154
, Cor. I.2]
to not necessarily unitary
representations of matrix Lie groups.
